High-Dimensional Data Analysis for Elliptically Symmetric Distributions
Long Feng

Abstract




Preface

High-dimensional data analysis has become one of the central themes of modern
statistics. In contemporary applications arising from genomics, finance,
macroeconomics, imaging, network analysis, and machine learning, the ambient
dimension is often comparable to or much larger than the available sample size.
Over roughly the last two decades, statistical methodology has therefore moved
far beyond the classical fixed-dimension paradigm. New theories have been
developed for location inference, covariance and precision matrix estimation,
multiple testing, factor modelling, classification, change-point analysis, and
dimension reduction in regimes where conventional multivariate procedures are no
longer applicable. Much of this literature began under Gaussian or light-tailed
assumptions, but practical data sets frequently exhibit heavy tails, outliers,
scale heterogeneity, and dependence structures that are poorly captured by the
multivariate normal model.

The purpose of this monograph is to provide a systematic account of
high-dimensional data analysis under
elliptical symmetry
. The elliptical
family occupies a particularly important position in robust multivariate
statistics: it preserves the geometry of location and scatter while allowing a
much broader range of tail behaviours than the Gaussian model. This makes it a
natural framework for studying spatial signs, spatial ranks, multivariate
Kendall’s tau matrices, Tyler-type shape estimators, and other methods that are
stable under heavy tails. At the same time, many classical high-dimensional
questions — mean testing, covariance comparison, alpha testing, classification,
principal component analysis, and factor modelling — can be reformulated in a
coherent way once the distinction between covariance, scatter, and shape is made
explicit. The present book is written from precisely this viewpoint.

The organization of the book reflects that objective. Chapter 1 introduces the
basic theory of elliptically symmetric distributions, together with spatial
signs, spatial ranks, multivariate Kendall’s tau matrices, Tyler’s shape
estimator, Hettmansperger–Randles estimation, and the closure properties of the
elliptical family under linear transformation and conditioning. Chapter 2 turns
to high-dimensional location estimation and testing. It begins with the classical
low-dimensional theory, reviews the main Gaussian and light-tailed benchmark
procedures, and then develops the spatial-sign, spatial-rank, weighted-sign,
max-type, max-sum, and strong-correlation methods that are central to the
elliptical program. Chapter 3 studies matrix estimation and testing, including
covariance, shape, inverse shape, proportionality testing, shrinkage methods,
and high-dimensional Hettmansperger–Randles estimation. Chapter 4 covers other
important testing problems, including alpha tests in factor pricing models,
change-point analysis, white-noise testing, independence testing, and related
adaptive procedures. Chapter 5 is devoted to classification, with particular
emphasis on robust high-dimensional LDA and QDA. Chapter 6 treats principal
component analysis, factor models, elliptical component analysis, generalized
spatial-sign PCA, and Kendall-based robust factor methods.

This book is written as a research monograph rather than as a purely pedagogical
textbook. We have tried to make the statistical constructions, assumptions, and
main proof ideas as transparent as possible, but we have not attempted to write
out every technical proof in complete detail. Doing so for every theorem would
make the book excessively long and, in many places, unnecessarily repetitive.
Instead, we emphasize the principal reductions, the main probabilistic tools, and
the structural ideas behind the arguments. Readers interested in the full details
are encouraged to consult the original papers cited in the bibliographic notes.

A computational companion is also planned. In a later stage, the author intends
to prepare an
R
package, together with reproducible code, so that the
methods in this monograph can be implemented more directly in practice. That
companion will be developed with the aid of modern code-generation tools,
including Codex.

Finally, this monograph is also a personal synthesis of the author’s research
program over the past decade, together with the closely related literature to
which it naturally connects. It is offered as a reference for researchers and
students who wish to understand how high-dimensional methodology can be rebuilt
under elliptical symmetry. Comments, corrections, and suggestions are welcome and
may be sent directly to the author. Future revisions of the manuscript will
continue to be updated, including with the assistance of ChatGPT for editorial
refinement.

Notation and conventions

Throughout the book, vectors are denoted by bold lower-case letters, matrices by
bold upper-case letters, and scalars by italic letters. For a symmetric matrix

\bm{A}
, we write
\lambda_{1}(\bm{A})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}(\bm{A})
for its ordered
eigenvalues,
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A})
for its trace,
\det(\bm{A})
for its determinant,

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
for its operator norm, and
\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}
for its Frobenius norm.
The Euclidean norm of a vector
\bm{x}
is written as
\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}
. The symbol

a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
means that
a_{n}/b_{n}
is bounded away from zero and infinity, and

\overset{d}{\to}
denotes convergence in distribution.

The basic elliptical model is written as

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u},

(0.1)

where
\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1})
,
\xi\geq 0
is independent of
\bm{u}
,
and
\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}
is a scatter matrix. Since
\bm{S}
is identifiable only
up to a positive scalar factor, the normalized shape matrix is defined by

\bm{V}=\frac{p\bm{S}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S})},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p.

(0.2)

Only when second moments exist do we identify the covariance matrix as

\bm{\Sigma}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})}{p}\bm{S}.

(0.3)

For a nonzero vector
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the spatial sign is

U(\bm{x})=\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}},

(0.4)

with the convention
U(\bm{0})=\bm{0}
. The population spatial rank at
\bm{x}

with respect to a distribution
F
is

R(\bm{x};F)=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{U(\bm{x}-\bm{X})\bigr\},\qquad\bm{X}\sim F.

(0.5)

Table 1:
Core notation used throughout the book.

Symbol

Meaning

\bm{X},\bm{Y}

Random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}

\bm{\mu},\bm{\theta},\bm{\alpha}

Location-type parameters

\bm{S}

Scatter matrix

\bm{V}

Normalized shape matrix, usually with
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p

\bm{\Sigma}

Covariance matrix when second moments exist

\bm{\Omega}

Precision matrix or shape-based inverse

U(\bm{x})

Spatial sign of
\bm{x}

R(\bm{x};F)

Population spatial rank of
\bm{x}
under
F

\bar{\bm{X}}

Sample mean

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{SM}}

Sample spatial median

\widehat{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{sign}}

Sample spatial sign covariance matrix

T_{\mathrm{sum}}

Generic sum-type statistic

T_{\max}

Generic max-type statistic

s

Sparsity level

p,n,T

Dimension, sample size, and time horizon

To simplify later exposition, every chapter follows the same internal order
whenever possible:

1.

model and hypothesis;

2.

benchmark Gaussian or light-tailed procedures;

3.

robust elliptical procedure;

4.

asymptotic null theory and power;

5.

proof strategy and technical lemmas.

Chapter 1
Elliptically Symmetric Distributions: Definitions, Geometry, and Robust Building Blocks

1.1
Introduction

The Gaussian model has served as the default point of departure for much of
classical multivariate analysis and, later, for high-dimensional statistics.
This preference is understandable. Under multivariate normality, covariance
matrices capture the full second-order structure, likelihood methods are
available, and many statistics admit tractable large-sample approximations.
However, the Gaussian paradigm is often too narrow for modern data analysis.
Financial returns, genomics measurements, imaging summaries, signal features,
and network-derived covariates frequently exhibit heavy tails, heteroscedastic
scale variation, or latent mixture behavior. In such settings, the covariance
matrix may be unstable or even undefined, whereas the underlying geometry of the
data cloud remains informative. Elliptical symmetry provides a convenient way to
retain that geometry without insisting on normal tails

(
104
;
127
;
35
)
.

The main thesis of this book is that elliptical symmetry is more than a robust
alternative to Gaussianity. It is a structural language in which location,
shape, direction, and radial magnitude are separated in a mathematically clean
manner. That separation is particularly useful in high dimensions. Many of the
procedures studied later in this monograph depend primarily on directional
information, normalized pairwise contrasts, or shape functionals that remain
well behaved under heavy tails. For that reason, Chapter 1 establishes the
geometric and probabilistic foundation on which the rest of the book is built.

This chapter has four goals. First, we introduce spherical and elliptical
symmetry and clarify the distinction between scatter, shape, and covariance.
Second, we present the spatial sign, spatial rank, and spatial median, which are
the fundamental building blocks of robust multivariate inference in the sense of

104
. Third, we discuss sign and rank covariance matrices and
Tyler-type shape estimation, both of which play a central role in later
chapters. Fourth, we summarize the high-dimensional implications of the
elliptical framework and fix a notation system that will remain stable
throughout the book.

1.2
Spherical and elliptical symmetry

1.2.1
Spherical symmetry

We begin with the rotationally invariant case.

Definition 1.1

(Spherical symmetry)
.

A random vector
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is said to be
spherically symmetric
about
the origin if

\bm{Q}\bm{Z}\overset{d}{=}\bm{Z}

for every orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
satisfying

\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{I}_{p}
.

Spherical symmetry implies that the distribution of
\bm{Z}
depends only on the
Euclidean norm
\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{2}
. Equivalently, whenever
\bm{Z}
is nondegenerate,
there exist a nonnegative random variable
\xi
and an independent random
direction
\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1})
such that

\bm{Z}=\xi\bm{u}.

(1.1)

The random variable
\xi
is called the
radial part
, and
\bm{u}
is the

angular part
. This radial-angular decomposition is conceptually basic:
the radial part controls tail behavior, while the angular part captures the
underlying geometry.

If a spherical distribution admits a density, then the density must be of the
form
f(\bm{z})=g(\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})
for a suitable nonnegative function
g
. The
special role of the squared norm in this display foreshadows the Mahalanobis
geometry that appears under elliptical symmetry.

1.2.2
Elliptical symmetry

Elliptical symmetry is obtained by a linear transformation of spherical
symmetry.

Definition 1.2

(Elliptically symmetric distribution)
.

A random vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is said to follow an elliptically symmetric
distribution if there exist
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, a nonnegative random variable

\xi
, a deterministic matrix
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
, and a random direction

\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1})
, independent of
\xi
, such that

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}.

(1.2)

We call
\bm{\mu}
the
location parameter
and

\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}

a
scatter matrix
.

We shall often say simply that
\bm{X}
is
elliptical
with location

\bm{\mu}
and scatter
\bm{S}
. Equation (
1.2
) shows
that the level sets of the distribution are affine images of spheres, hence the
terminology.

Remark 1.1

(Scatter is only defined up to scale)
.

If
c>0
, then

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}=\bm{\mu}+(\xi/c)(c\bm{A})\bm{u}.

Therefore
\bm{S}
is not identifiable on an absolute scale. The intrinsic object
is the
shape
of
\bm{S}
, not its magnitude. This observation is central in
both robust multivariate analysis and high-dimensional inference.

Remark 1.2

(Singular elliptical models)
.

The matrix
\bm{A}
in (
1.2
) need not be full rank.
If
\operatorname{rank}(\bm{A})=q<p
, then
\bm{X}
is supported on an affine subspace of
dimension
q
. Such singular elliptical models are relevant for latent factor
structures and low-rank approximations. In Chapters 5 and 6 we will return to
this viewpoint when discussing discriminant analysis, principal components, and
elliptical factor models
(
35
)
.

The next proposition records the usual density representation for the
nonsingular absolutely continuous case.

Proposition 1.1

(Density representation)
.

Suppose
\bm{S}
is positive definite and
\bm{X}
admits a density. Then
\bm{X}
is
elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and scatter
\bm{S}
if and only if its
density can be written as

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=(\det\bm{S})^{-1/2}g\!\left\{(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\right\},\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},

(1.3)

for some nonnegative function
g
.

Proof.

If
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{A}\bm{Z}
with
\bm{Z}
spherically symmetric and

\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}
, then
\bm{A}
is nonsingular and the change of variables

\bm{z}=\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})
yields

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=|\det(\bm{A})|^{-1}f_{\bm{Z}}\{\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\}.

Since
f_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{z})=g_{0}(\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{z})
for some
g_{0}
under spherical symmetry,

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=|\det(\bm{A})|^{-1}g_{0}\!\left[(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{A}^{-1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\right].

Because
(\bm{A}^{-1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}=\bm{S}^{-1}
and

|\det(\bm{A})|=(\det\bm{S})^{1/2}
, (
1.3
) follows.

Conversely, if (
1.3
) holds, then after the linear
transformation
\bm{z}=\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})
the density of
\bm{z}
depends only on

\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{z}
, hence
\bm{Z}
is spherical. Therefore
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{A}\bm{Z}
is
elliptical.
∎

Example 1.1

(Basic examples)
.

The most important examples are:

(i)

Multivariate normal distributions.
If

\bm{X}\sim N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
, then

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{p/2}\{\det(\bm{\Sigma})\}^{1/2}}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\right\},\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

Hence
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with scatter matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{\Sigma}
.

(ii)

Multivariate
t
distributions.
If

\bm{X}\sim t_{p,\nu}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
with
\nu>0
, then

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=\frac{\Gamma\{(\nu+p)/2\}}{\Gamma(\nu/2)(\nu\pi)^{p/2}\{\det(\bm{\Sigma})\}^{1/2}}\left[1+\frac{1}{\nu}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\right]^{-(\nu+p)/2},\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

Hence
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with scatter matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{\Sigma}
.
Moreover, when
\nu>2
,

\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\frac{\nu}{\nu-2}\bm{\Sigma}.

(iii)

Normal mixture distributions.
Let
H
be a probability
distribution on
(0,\infty)
, and suppose that, conditional on
\tau
,

\bm{X}\mid\tau\sim N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\tau\bm{\Sigma}).

Then
\bm{X}
has density

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2\pi\tau)^{p/2}\{\det(\bm{\Sigma})\}^{1/2}}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{2\tau}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\right\}\,dH(\tau),\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

Hence
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with scatter matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{\Sigma}
.
This class includes the multivariate
t
distribution as a special case for a
suitable choice of the mixing law
H
.

1.2.3
Affine equivariance

A key reason for working with elliptical models is their stability under affine
transformations.

Proposition 1.2

(Affine equivariance)
.

If
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and scatter
\bm{S}
, then
for every nonsingular matrix
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
and vector

\bm{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\bm{B}\bm{X}+\bm{b}

is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{B}\bm{\mu}+\bm{b}
and scatter

\bm{B}\bm{S}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Proof.

Write
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
. Then

\bm{B}\bm{X}+\bm{b}=(\bm{B}\bm{\mu}+\bm{b})+\xi(\bm{B}\bm{A})\bm{u}.

The transformed vector is therefore elliptical, and its scatter matrix is

(\bm{B}\bm{A})(\bm{B}\bm{A})^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{B}\bm{S}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
∎

Affine equivariance is essential for statistical interpretation. A change of
units, a whitening transformation, or an orthogonal rotation should not alter
the inferential content of a procedure except through the corresponding
transformation of parameters. Later chapters repeatedly exploit this principle
when comparing covariance-based procedures with shape-based robust alternatives.

1.2.4
Linear images, marginals, and conditional laws

One of the most important closure properties of the elliptical family is that it
is stable under linear transformations and, in the nonsingular case, under
conditioning. This makes elliptical models analytically tractable in regression,
factor models, discriminant analysis, and portfolio problems

(
36
;
35
)
.

Corollary 1.1

(Linear images, marginals, and linear combinations)
.

Let
\bm{X}
be elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and scatter matrix

\bm{S}
. Then, for any deterministic matrix
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times p}
,

\bm{B}\bm{X}=\bm{B}\bm{\mu}+\xi(\bm{B}\bm{A})\bm{u}

(1.4)

and therefore
\bm{B}\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{B}\bm{\mu}
and
scatter matrix
\bm{B}\bm{S}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}
. In particular:

(i)

every subvector of
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric;

(ii)

for every
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the scalar
\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}
is univariate
elliptical, hence centrally symmetric, with location
\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mu}

and scale
\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}\bm{a}
.

Proof.

Using the stochastic representation
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
, we obtain

\bm{B}\bm{X}=\bm{B}\bm{\mu}+\xi(\bm{B}\bm{A})\bm{u}.

This is again an elliptical representation, with scatter matrix

(\bm{B}\bm{A})(\bm{B}\bm{A})^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{B}\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{B}\bm{S}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}.

Choosing
\bm{B}
to be a coordinate projection yields the marginal statement, and
choosing
\bm{B}=\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
yields the one-dimensional linear combination.
∎

Proposition 1.3

(Conditional distributions under ellipticity)
.

Assume that
\bm{X}
has density representation (
1.3
) with
positive definite scatter matrix
\bm{S}
. Partition

\bm{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{X}_{1}\\
\bm{X}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bm{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{1}\\
\bm{\mu}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bm{S}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{S}_{11}&\bm{S}_{12}\\
\bm{S}_{21}&\bm{S}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

where
\bm{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\bm{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-q}
, and
\bm{S}_{22}
is nonsingular. Let

\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}=\bm{S}_{11}-\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}\bm{S}_{21}

(1.5)

and

\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2})=\bm{\mu}_{1}+\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}).

(1.6)

Then, for every
\bm{x}_{2}
such that
f_{\bm{X}_{2}}(\bm{x}_{2})>0
, the conditional density of

\bm{X}_{1}\mid\bm{X}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}
can be written as

f_{\bm{X}_{1}\mid\bm{X}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}}(\bm{x}_{1})=c(\bm{x}_{2})\,\{\det(\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2})\}^{-1/2}g\!\left[\delta_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})+(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))\right],

(1.7)

where

\delta_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})=(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})

(1.8)

and
c(\bm{x}_{2})
is a normalizing constant depending on
\bm{x}_{2}
only. Hence

\bm{X}_{1}\mid\bm{X}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}
is elliptically symmetric with conditional location

\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2})
and conditional scatter matrix proportional to

\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}
.

Proof.

By the block inverse formula,

\bm{S}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}&-\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}\\
-\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}\bm{S}_{21}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}&\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}+\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}\bm{S}_{21}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore

\displaystyle(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle=\{\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}\{\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{S}_{12}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{22}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))+\delta_{2}(\bm{x}_{2}).

Using also
\det(\bm{S})=\det(\bm{S}_{22})\det(\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2})
, the joint density becomes

f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})=\{\det(\bm{S}_{22})\}^{-1/2}\{\det(\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2})\}^{-1/2}g\!\left[\delta_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})+(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1\mid 2}(\bm{x}_{2}))\right].

For fixed
\bm{x}_{2}
, the factor
\{\det(\bm{S}_{22})\}^{-1/2}
is constant in
\bm{x}_{1}
.
Hence the conditional density is proportional in
\bm{x}_{1}
to the right-hand side
of (
1.7
). After normalization we obtain
(
1.7
), proving the claim.
∎

Remark 1.3

(Generator changes under conditioning)
.

Proposition
1.3
shows that the conditional scatter
structure is determined by the Schur complement
\bm{S}_{11\cdot 2}
. The
conditional generator, however, changes from
g(t)
to a shifted and normalized
version
g\{t+\delta_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\}
. In the Gaussian case this remains Gaussian;
in the multivariate
t
case the conditional law is again multivariate
t
with
updated degrees of freedom.

1.3
Location, scatter, shape, covariance, and precision

1.3.1
Mean and covariance

The scatter matrix
\bm{S}
remains meaningful even when the second moment of the
distribution does not exist. When moments do exist, scatter and covariance are
proportional.

Proposition 1.4

(Scatter versus covariance)
.

Suppose
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
is elliptically symmetric and

\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})<\infty
. Then

\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})}{p}\bm{S}.

(1.9)

If, in addition,
\mathbb{E}(\xi)<\infty
, then
\mathbb{E}(\bm{X})=\bm{\mu}
.

Proof.

Because
\mathbb{E}(\bm{u})=\bm{0}
for the uniform distribution on the sphere,

\mathbb{E}(\bm{X})=\bm{\mu}
whenever
\mathbb{E}(\xi)<\infty
. Moreover,

\mathbb{E}(\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}})=p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
, so

\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\xi^{2}\bm{A}\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})\bm{A}\mathbb{E}(\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})}{p}\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})}{p}\bm{S}.

∎

Proposition
1.4
explains why covariance-based
reasoning can become conceptually misleading under heavy tails. Even when

\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})
is undefined, the shape of the distribution is still encoded by

\bm{S}
and remains inferentially useful.

1.3.2
Normalized shape

Since the scale of
\bm{S}
is not identifiable, we introduce the normalized shape
matrix

\bm{V}=\frac{p\bm{S}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S})},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p.

(1.10)

Whenever convenient we also write

\bm{S}=\sigma^{2}\bm{V},\qquad\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{p}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S}),

so that
\sigma^{2}
plays the role of a global scale and
\bm{V}
records relative
dispersion.

If

\bm{V}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}},

where
\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}_{p})
is orthogonal and

\bm{\Lambda}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})
with

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}>0
, then

\bm{\gamma}_{j}
describes a principal direction and
\lambda_{j}
describes relative
spread along that direction. This spectral decomposition will appear throughout
the book in testing, classification, principal component analysis, and factor
models.

Remark 1.4

(Generalized correlation and inverse shape)
.

If the diagonal entries of
\bm{V}
are positive, we define the shape-based
correlation matrix

\bm{R}=\operatorname{diag}(\bm{V})^{-1/2}\bm{V}\operatorname{diag}(\bm{V})^{-1/2}.

Likewise, whenever
\bm{V}
is positive definite we may work with its inverse

\bm{V}^{-1}
, which is the natural
inverse shape
matrix. Only when second
moments exist and an absolute scale has been fixed do these objects coincide
with the usual correlation and precision matrices derived from covariance.

1.3.3
A notation table

Table
1.1
records the main population-level objects that will
be used throughout this monograph.

Symbol

Name

Role in this book

\bm{\mu}

location parameter

Center of the elliptical distribution; target in Chapter 2 and a nuisance parameter in several later testing problems.

\bm{S}

scatter matrix

Affine-equivariant second-order shape object; meaningful even when covariance does not exist.

\bm{V}

normalized shape matrix

Scale-free version of scatter, defined by
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p
; used to unify testing, PCA, factor models, and discriminant analysis.

\bm{\Sigma}

covariance matrix

Used only when second moments exist; proportional to
\bm{S}
under Proposition
1.4
.

\bm{\Omega}

precision or inverse shape

Inverse of
\bm{\Sigma}
or
\bm{V}
when the corresponding matrix is well defined and nonsingular.

\bm{K}(F)

multivariate Kendall’s tau matrix

Pairwise directional scatter functional; under ellipticity it shares eigenvectors with
\bm{S}
and is insensitive to radial tails.

\bm{\Gamma},\bm{\Lambda}

eigenvectors and eigenvalues

Encode principal directions and relative dispersion; central in Chapters 3, 5, and 6.

Table 1.1:
Population objects in the elliptical framework.

1.4
Radial-angular decomposition and directional geometry

1.4.1
Whitening and angular structure

Let
\bm{S}^{1/2}
be any symmetric square root of
\bm{S}
. Then

\bm{S}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\xi\bm{u}

whenever
\bm{S}
is positive definite. In other words, after whitening by

\bm{S}^{-1/2}
, the distribution becomes spherical. This is the simplest way to
separate angular information from radial magnitude.

The geometric content of elliptical symmetry is therefore encoded in the
dependence of directions on
\bm{S}
. To make this explicit, write the spectral
decomposition
\bm{S}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}
. Then

\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}=\xi\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{u}.

Rotating into the eigenbasis of
\bm{S}
yields coordinates

\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\xi\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{u}
. Thus the radial variable
\xi

controls overall magnitude, while the eigenvalues of
\bm{S}
deform the angular
distribution through
\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}
.

1.4.2
Spatial signs depend only on direction

The spatial sign map

U(\bm{x})=\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}}\mathbbm{1}(\bm{x}\neq\bm{0})

(1.11)

extracts only the direction of a vector and discards its length. This is the
basic reason why sign-based methods are robust to heavy tails.

Proposition 1.5

(Sign representation under ellipticity)
.

Suppose
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
with
\mathbb{P}(\xi=0)=0
. Then

U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{\bm{A}\bm{u}}{\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}}.

(1.12)

Consequently, the distribution of
U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})
depends on
\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}

but not on the radial variable
\xi
.

Proof.

Since
\xi\geq 0
and
\mathbb{P}(\xi=0)=0
,

U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=U(\xi\bm{A}\bm{u})=\frac{\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}}{\left\lVert\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}}=\frac{\bm{A}\bm{u}}{\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}}.

The radial factor cancels identically.
∎

Proposition
1.5
explains why sign-based procedures remain
meaningful even under infinite-variance models: they require directional
structure but not moment existence. This idea will be used heavily in
Chapter 2, where tests for high-dimensional location are built from spatial
signs and their weighted variants.

Remark 1.5

(Central symmetry)
.

If
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
, then

\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}\overset{d}{=}-(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})
. Hence

\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}=\bm{0}

whenever the expectation exists. The null distributions of many sign-based test
statistics ultimately rely on this elementary symmetry.

1.5
Spatial median, spatial signs, and spatial ranks

1.5.1
Spatial median

The spatial median is the most basic robust multivariate location functional in
the sign-rank framework of
104
.

Definition 1.3

(Spatial median)
.

For a random vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with distribution
F
, any minimizer of

Q_{F}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}-\left\lVert\bm{X}\right\rVert_{2}\bigr]

(1.13)

is called a
spatial median
of
F
. Given observations

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, any minimizer of

Q_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}

(1.14)

is called a
sample spatial median
and is denoted by

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}
.

The spatial median is translation equivariant and robust to outliers in
magnitude. Unlike the sample mean, it is well defined without any moment
assumption. Under mild regularity conditions, it is the unique solution to the
estimating equation

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{0}.

(1.15)

Proposition 1.6

(Centering property of the spatial median)
.

If
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and is not supported on
a line through
\bm{\mu}
, then
\bm{\mu}
is the unique spatial median.

Proof.

By elliptical symmetry,
\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}\overset{d}{=}-(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})
. Therefore

\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}=\bm{0},

which means that
\bm{\mu}
is a stationary point of
Q_{F}
. Since

\bm{\theta}\mapsto\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}
is convex for every realization of

\bm{X}
, the functional
Q_{F}(\bm{\theta})
is convex. The nondegeneracy condition
rules out flat directions, so the minimizer is unique and equals
\bm{\mu}
.
∎

1.5.2
Spatial rank

The multivariate spatial rank generalizes the univariate notion of rank by
replacing scalar order information with average directions.

Definition 1.4

(Spatial rank)
.

For a point
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and a distribution
F
, the
spatial rank
of

\bm{x}
with respect to
F
is

R(\bm{x};F)=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{x}-\bm{X})\},\qquad\bm{X}\sim F.

(1.16)

Given a sample
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, the empirical spatial rank of
\bm{X}_{i}
is

\widehat{\bm{R}}_{i}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j}).

(1.17)

Two properties of spatial ranks are worth emphasizing.

First, they are
translation invariant
: adding a common vector
\bm{b}
to
every observation leaves all pairwise differences unchanged. This is the reason
why two-sample spatial rank statistics can often avoid direct location
estimation. Second, they blend information on relative location and direction,
which is why rank-based tests often achieve higher efficiency than pure sign
tests under dense alternatives.

Remark 1.6

(Signs versus ranks)
.

Spatial signs use only one observation at a time after centering and therefore
focus on directional stability. Spatial ranks are based on pairwise differences,
so they are automatically centered and can exploit more of the relative location
structure. In Chapter 2, this difference will correspond roughly to a trade-off
between maximal robustness and improved efficiency.

1.6
Sign and rank covariance matrices

1.6.1
The spatial sign covariance matrix

Sign and rank covariance matrices provide robust alternatives to the sample
covariance matrix
(
128
)
. After centering at a suitable
location estimator, the
sample spatial sign covariance matrix
is

\widehat{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{sign}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(1.18)

At the population level, the corresponding matrix is

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)=\mathbb{E}\left[U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\right].

(1.19)

The matrix
\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)
is orthogonally equivariant, positive
semidefinite, and trace-normalized:

\operatorname{tr}\{\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)\}=1.

When
F
is spherical,
\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)=p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
.

Proposition 1.7

(Eigenvectors of the population SSCM)
.

Let
\bm{X}
be elliptical with location
\bm{\mu}
and positive definite scatter
matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}
. Then

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}

(1.20)

for some diagonal matrix
\bm{\Delta}=\operatorname{diag}(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{p})
with

\delta_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{j}=1
. Hence

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)
and
\bm{S}
have the same eigenvectors.

Proof.

By Proposition
1.5
,

U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{u}}{\sqrt{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}}}.

Therefore

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)=\bm{\Gamma}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{1/2}}{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}}\right]\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}.

It suffices to show that the middle expectation is diagonal. Its
(j,k)
entry
for
j\neq k
is

\sqrt{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{u_{j}u_{k}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{2}}\right).

Because the uniform distribution on the sphere is symmetric under sign changes
of any single coordinate, the integrand is odd in
u_{j}
(or
u_{k}
), so the
expectation vanishes. Hence the matrix is diagonal, proving
(
1.20
). The trace statement follows from

\operatorname{tr}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\}=1
.
∎

Proposition
1.7
is one of the most useful structural
facts in this chapter. Although the eigenvalues of the SSCM are nonlinear
transformations of the eigenvalues of
\bm{S}
, the principal directions are
preserved. This provides a direct bridge from robust directional methods to
eigenspace estimation, principal components, and factor models.

1.6.2
The multivariate Kendall’s tau matrix

A closely related pairwise directional functional is the
multivariate
Kendall’s tau matrix
, also called the

spatial Kendall’s tau matrix
in parts of the literature

(
104
;
60
;
35
)
. Let
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
be an
independent copy of
\bm{X}
. The population version is defined by

\bm{K}(F)=\mathbb{E}\left[U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}}{\left\lVert\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right\}.

(1.21)

Given observations
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, the sample multivariate Kendall’s tau
matrix is the U-statistic

\widehat{\bm{K}}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(1.22)

By construction,
\bm{K}(F)
and
\widehat{\bm{K}}
are positive semidefinite with
unit trace. The pairwise differencing removes the location parameter
automatically, which makes
\widehat{\bm{K}}
especially attractive when robust
centering is difficult.

Proposition 1.8

(Eigenvectors of the multivariate Kendall’s tau matrix)
.

Let
\bm{X}
be elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and positive definite
scatter matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}
. Then

\bm{K}(F)=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{K}}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{K}}=\operatorname{diag}(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{p}),

(1.23)

where

\kappa_{j}=\lambda_{j}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{u_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{2}}\right),\qquad j=1,\ldots,p,

(1.24)

and
\sum_{j=1}^{p}\kappa_{j}=1
. Hence
\bm{K}(F)
and
\bm{S}
have the same
eigenvectors.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
be an independent copy of
\bm{X}
. Since
\bm{X}
is
elliptically symmetric, its characteristic function has the form

\phi_{\bm{X}}(\bm{t})=\exp(\mathrm{i}\bm{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mu})\psi(\bm{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}\bm{t})
for some
scalar function
\psi

(
36
)
. Therefore

\phi_{\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}}}(\bm{t})=\phi_{\bm{X}}(\bm{t})\phi_{\widetilde{\bm{X}}}(-\bm{t})=\psi(\bm{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}\bm{t})^{2},

which depends on
\bm{t}
only through the quadratic form

\bm{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}\bm{t}
. Hence
\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}}
is elliptically symmetric with
location
\bm{0}
and scatter matrix
\bm{S}
. Applying
Proposition
1.7
to the difference vector

\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}}
yields

\bm{K}(F)=\mathbb{E}\left[U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right]=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{K}}\bm{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}.

The diagonal entries of
\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{K}}
are given by
(
1.24
), and the trace identity follows from

\operatorname{tr}\{U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\}=1
.
∎

The matrices
\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sign}}(F)
and
\bm{K}(F)
are similar in spirit but not
identical. The SSCM is built from one-point directions after centering, whereas

\bm{K}(F)
is built from pairwise directions and therefore eliminates the location
parameter automatically. In Chapter 6 this distinction will matter when we turn
to robust eigenspace estimation and elliptical principal component analysis.

1.6.3
Rank covariance matrices

Spatial ranks lead to analogous covariance-type functionals. For example, one
may consider

\widehat{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{rank}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{R}}_{i}\widehat{\bm{R}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}},

(1.25)

or, depending on the application, versions based directly on pairwise
differences. Rank covariance matrices are typically less crude than sign
covariance matrices because they use relative rather than purely directional
information. In later chapters they reappear in matrix testing, independence
testing, and principal component analysis.

Remark 1.7

(Orthogonal versus affine equivariance)
.

The SSCM is only orthogonally equivariant, not fully affine equivariant. This
is not a defect; it is the price of stripping away radial magnitude completely.
When full affine equivariance is needed, Tyler’s shape estimator becomes the
natural replacement.

1.7
Tyler’s shape matrix and affine-equivariant estimation

1.7.1
Population equation and sample estimator

Among affine-equivariant robust shape functionals, Tyler’s

M
-estimator is the most important in the elliptical
setting
(
127
)
. At the sample level, after centering by a location
estimator
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
, it is defined as a positive definite solution to

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}=\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}}{(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}^{-1}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}})=p.

(1.26)

The trace constraint fixes the otherwise arbitrary scale. The corresponding
population equation is

\bm{V}=p\,\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}}{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})}\right],\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p.

(1.27)

Equation (
1.27
) shows that Tyler’s functional depends only
on directions standardized by the current shape estimate. Unlike the sample
covariance matrix, it is intrinsically scale free and remains meaningful even
when second moments do not exist.

Proposition 1.9

(Angular representation of Tyler’s equation)
.

For every positive definite matrix
\bm{V}
and every
\bm{x}\neq\bm{0}
,

\frac{\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{x}}=\bm{V}^{1/2}U(\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x})U(\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{1/2}.

(1.28)

Consequently, if
\bm{Y}_{i}=\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
and

\widehat{\bm{W}}_{i}(\bm{V})=U(\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{Y}_{i}),

(1.29)

then (
1.26
) is equivalent to

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}=\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}^{1/2}\widehat{\bm{W}}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}})\widehat{\bm{W}}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}^{1/2}.

(1.30)

Proof.

Since
\left\lVert\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{x}
,

\bm{V}^{1/2}U(\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x})U(\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{1/2}=\bm{V}^{1/2}\frac{\bm{V}^{-1/2}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1/2}}{\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{x}}\bm{V}^{1/2}=\frac{\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{x}},

which proves (
1.28
). Substituting

\bm{x}=\bm{Y}_{i}
into (
1.28
) and summing over
i

yields (
1.30
).
∎

Proposition
1.9
shows that Tyler’s matrix is
not an ordinary second-moment estimator. It is a
self-consistent covariance
matrix of whitened directions
. The matrix
\bm{V}
is chosen so that, after
whitening by
\bm{V}^{-1/2}
, the average outer product of the resulting spatial
signs becomes isotropic.

Proposition 1.10

(Tyler’s population equation recovers the normalized shape)
.

Suppose that
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
is elliptically symmetric with positive
definite scatter matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}
, and let

\bm{V}_{0}=\frac{p\bm{S}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S})}.

(1.31)

Then
\bm{V}_{0}
satisfies the population fixed-point equation
(
1.27
). Hence, whenever the population solution is unique
under the trace normalization, Tyler’s functional coincides with the normalized
shape matrix.

Proof.

Write
\bm{V}_{0}=c\bm{S}
with
c=p/\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S})
. Then

\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}=c^{-1}\bm{S}^{-1}
. Since
\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}=\xi\bm{A}\bm{u}
,

(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\xi^{2}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}(c^{-1}\bm{S}^{-1})\bm{A}\bm{u}=c^{-1}\xi^{2}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}=c^{-1}\xi^{2}.

Therefore

\frac{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}}{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})}=c\bm{A}\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}.

Taking expectation and using
\mathbb{E}(\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}})=p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
, we obtain

p\,\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}}{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})}\right]=pc\bm{A}\mathbb{E}(\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=c\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=c\bm{S}=\bm{V}_{0}.

Also
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V}_{0})=p
by construction. Hence
\bm{V}_{0}
solves
(
1.27
).
∎

Proposition 1.11

(Basic invariance properties of Tyler’s estimator)
.

Let
\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n})
denote a solution of
(
1.26
). Then:

(i)

for every
c>0
,

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}(c\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,c\bm{X}_{n})=\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n});

(ii)

for every nonsingular
\bm{B}
,

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{B}\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{B}\bm{X}_{n})\propto\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n})\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}},

where the proportionality constant is fixed by the trace normalization.

Proof.

Property (i) follows because multiplying every centered observation by
c

multiplies the numerator and denominator of each summand in
(
1.26
) by the same factor
c^{2}
. For (ii), substitute

\bm{B}\bm{X}_{i}
into (
1.26
) and use the identity

(\bm{B}\bm{V}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1}=(\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{B}^{-1}
. The transformed
equation is satisfied by a positive multiple of

\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}
, and the constant is determined by

\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{T}})=p
.
∎

1.7.2
The angular central Gaussian viewpoint

Tyler’s estimator is closely connected with the

angular central Gaussian
(ACG) distribution.
A random direction
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
is said to follow an ACG distribution
with shape matrix
\bm{V}
if it has density

f_{\mathrm{ACG}}(\bm{w};\bm{V})=\frac{\Gamma(p/2)}{2\pi^{p/2}}\{\det(\bm{V})\}^{-1/2}(\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{w})^{-p/2},\qquad\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}.

(1.32)

This density is scale invariant in the sense that

f_{\mathrm{ACG}}(\bm{w};c\bm{V})=f_{\mathrm{ACG}}(\bm{w};\bm{V})
for every
c>0
. Under an
elliptical model, the centered direction
U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})
depends only on the
shape matrix and has an ACG distribution with that shape.

Proposition 1.12

(Likelihood equation under the ACG model)
.

Let
\bm{W}_{1},\ldots,\bm{W}_{n}\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
be observations from the ACG model with
shape matrix
\bm{V}
, normalized by
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p
. Up to an additive constant, the
log-likelihood is

\ell_{n}(\bm{V})=-\frac{n}{2}\log\det(\bm{V})-\frac{p}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}).

(1.33)

Any interior maximizer satisfies the likelihood equation

\bm{V}=\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{W}_{i}\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p,

(1.34)

which is precisely Tyler’s estimating equation written in terms of directions.

Proof.

Differentiating (
1.33
) with respect to
\bm{V}
gives

d\ell_{n}(\bm{V})=-\frac{n}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V}^{-1}d\bm{V})+\frac{p}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}(d\bm{V})\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}}{\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}}.

Using

\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}(d\bm{V})\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}=\operatorname{tr}\{\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}d\bm{V}\}
,
we can rewrite the differential as

d\ell_{n}(\bm{V})=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\!\left[\left\{-n\bm{V}^{-1}+p\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}}{\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}}\right\}d\bm{V}\right].

Hence the score equation is

n\bm{V}^{-1}=p\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}}{\bm{W}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{W}_{i}}.

Multiplying from the left and right by
\bm{V}
yields
(
1.34
). The trace constraint then fixes the scale.
∎

The ACG representation is conceptually important. It shows that Tyler’s estimator
is the likelihood estimator of the
angular distribution
and therefore a
natural affine-equivariant counterpart of the SSCM and multivariate Kendall’s
tau matrix.

1.7.3
Existence, uniqueness, and computation

The existence theory of Tyler’s estimator is more delicate than that of the SSCM
because the defining equation is nonlinear. Let

\bm{Y}_{i}=\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
denote centered observations. Tyler

(
127
)

showed that a positive definite solution to (
1.26
) exists and
is unique up to scale if the sample is not overly concentrated on any proper
linear subspace, namely if

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}(\bm{Y}_{i}\in L)<\frac{\dim(L)}{p}

(1.35)

for every proper linear subspace
L\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{p}
. In particular, when
n>p

and the observations are in general position, the trace-normalized solution is
unique.

A practical fixed-point algorithm is obtained by iterating

\bm{H}(\bm{V})=\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{Y}_{i}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{i}},\qquad\bm{V}^{(m+1)}=\frac{p\,\bm{H}\{\bm{V}^{(m)}\}}{\operatorname{tr}[\bm{H}\{\bm{V}^{(m)}\}]},

(1.36)

started from any positive definite matrix
\bm{V}^{(0)}
.
Under the existence condition (
1.35
), the sequence
converges to the Tyler solution up to the chosen normalization. This iterative
form makes transparent that Tyler’s estimator is obtained by repeated
reweighting: observations aligned with currently over-dispersed directions are
downweighted through the denominator
\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{i}
.

1.7.4
Location-shape pairs and affine-equivariant medians

The spatial median is orthogonally equivariant and robust, but it is not fully
affine equivariant. When the coordinate system is changed by a general
nonsingular linear transformation, the transformed spatial median need not equal
that transformation applied to the original estimate. In low and moderate
dimensions, this drawback can be removed by estimating location and shape
simultaneously. The classical solution is the
Hettmansperger--Randles estimator
of
64
, which couples a location equation with a
Tyler-type shape equation.

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be observations, and let

(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})
denote a solution of

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U\!\left\{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}})\right\}=\bm{0},

(1.37)

and

\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U\!\left\{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}})\right\}U\!\left\{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}})\right\}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{I}_{p},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})=p.

(1.38)

The first equation centers the whitened data by a spatial-sign balance
condition, whereas the second equation enforces isotropy of the whitened
signs. Equation (
1.38
) is exactly Tyler’s fixed-point
equation written after centering at
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}
.

Remark 1.8

(Relation to the spatial median and Tyler’s estimator)
.

If the shape matrix is held fixed at
\bm{I}_{p}
, then
(
1.37
) reduces to the ordinary spatial median equation.
If the location is held fixed, then (
1.38
) reduces to
Tyler’s shape equation. The HR estimator therefore interpolates between the two
basic robust building blocks introduced earlier in this chapter.

Proposition 1.13

(Population HR equations under ellipticity)
.

Suppose that
\bm{X}
is elliptically symmetric with location
\bm{\mu}
and positive
definite normalized shape matrix
\bm{V}_{0}
, where
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V}_{0})=p
. Then the pair

(\bm{\mu},\bm{V}_{0})
satisfies the population equations

\mathbb{E}\left[U\{\bm{V}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\theta})\}\right]=\bm{0}

(1.39)

and

p\,\mathbb{E}\left[U\{\bm{V}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\theta})\}U\{\bm{V}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\theta})\}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]=\bm{I}_{p}

(1.40)

at
(\bm{\theta},\bm{V})=(\bm{\mu},\bm{V}_{0})
.

Proof.

Write

\bm{Z}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=R\bm{U},

where
R\geq 0
and
\bm{U}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1})
are independent. Then

U\{\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}=\bm{U}
, so

\mathbb{E}\left[U\{\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}\right]=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U})=\bm{0}.

Moreover,

p\,\mathbb{E}\left[U\{\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}U\{\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})\}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]=p\,\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\mathsf{T}})=p\cdot p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}=\bm{I}_{p}.

Hence
(\bm{\mu},\bm{V}_{0})
satisfies
(
1.39
)–(
1.40
).
∎

Proposition 1.14

(Affine equivariance of the HR estimator)
.

Let
\bm{Y}_{i}=\bm{a}+\bm{B}\bm{X}_{i}
, where
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\bm{B}
is nonsingular. If

(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})
satisfies
(
1.37
)–(
1.38
) for the sample

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, then

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n})=\bm{a}+\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n})

(1.41)

and

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n})=\frac{p\,\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n})\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\operatorname{tr}\{\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n})\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}\}}.

(1.42)

Proof.

Set

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y}=\bm{a}+\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\qquad\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y}=c\,\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad c=\frac{p}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})}.

Define

\bm{O}=(\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{1/2}.

Then

\bm{O}\bm{O}^{\mathsf{T}}=(\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}=\bm{I}_{p},

so
\bm{O}
is orthogonal. Therefore

(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y})^{-1/2}(\bm{Y}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y})=\bm{O}\,\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}),

and hence

U\!\left\{(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y})^{-1/2}(\bm{Y}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{\,Y})\right\}=\bm{O}\,U\!\left\{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}})\right\}.

Define

U_{i}=U\!\left\{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}})\right\}.

Because orthogonal transformations preserve the zero vector and the identity
matrix,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{O}\,U_{i}=\bm{O}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i},\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{O}U_{i})(\bm{O}U_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{O}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}U_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\bm{O}^{\mathsf{T}}.

Hence the transformed sample satisfies the same estimating equations, and the
trace normalization determines
c
uniquely.
∎

The HR estimator can be computed by alternately updating whitened residuals,
location, and shape. If
(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{(m)},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m)})
is the
current iterate, define

\widehat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}=\{\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m)}\}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{(m)}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n.

(1.43)

Then the location update is

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{(m+1)}=\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{(m)}+\{\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m)}\}^{1/2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\widehat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}},

(1.44)

and the shape update is

\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m+1)}=p\,\{\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m)}\}^{1/2}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\widehat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})U(\widehat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\}\{\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m)}\}^{1/2},

(1.45)

followed by the normalization

\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m+1)}\leftarrow\frac{p\,\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m+1)}}{\operatorname{tr}\{\widehat{\bm{V}}^{(m+1)}\}}.

(1.46)

Equation (
1.44
) is a one-step Newton update for the location
equation, whereas (
1.45
) is the Tyler update applied to the
currently centered data.

To state the asymptotic result, let

\bm{Z}_{i}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})=R_{i}\bm{U}_{i},\qquad c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}=\mathbb{E}(R_{i}^{-1}).

(1.47)

Because
\bm{U}_{i}
is uniformly distributed on
\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
, we have

\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i})=\bm{0}
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{U}_{i})=p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
.

Assumption 1.1

(Classical fixed-
p
regularity for the HR estimator)
.

Assume that:

(HR1)

p
is fixed and
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
are i.i.d. from an elliptically
symmetric distribution with location
\bm{\mu}
and positive definite normalized
shape matrix
\bm{V}_{0}
, where
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V}_{0})=p
.

(HR2)

The inverse radial moments satisfy

\mathbb{E}(R_{i}^{-1})<\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}(R_{i}^{-2})<\infty.

(1.48)

(HR3)

The joint sample equations
(
1.37
)–(
1.38
) admit a local
solution
(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})
such that

\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(1.49)

Theorem 1.1

(Bahadur representation and asymptotic normality of the HR location estimator)
.

Suppose Assumption
1.1
holds. Then

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{p}{(p-1)c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(1.50)

Consequently,

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})\overset{d}{\to}N_{p}\!\left(\bm{0},\frac{p}{(p-1)^{2}c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}^{2}}\bm{V}_{0}\right).

(1.51)

Equivalently, for every fixed
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\frac{\sqrt{n}\,\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})}{\left\{\frac{p}{(p-1)^{2}c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}^{2}}\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{0}\bm{a}\right\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1).

(1.52)

Proof.

Let

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta},\bm{V})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U\{\bm{V}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\}.

Since
(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})
solves
(
1.37
),

\Psi_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}},\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}})=\bm{0}.

(1.53)

Write

\widehat{\bm{H}}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}-\bm{I}_{p}.

Then by (
1.49
),

\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{H}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\bm{H}})=0.

(1.54)

Also,

\widehat{\bm{V}}_{\mathrm{HR}}^{-1/2}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\bm{I}_{p}+\widehat{\bm{H}})^{-1/2}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}\left(\bm{I}_{p}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\bm{H}}\right)+O_{p}(n^{-1}),

(1.55)

where the remainder is in Frobenius norm because
p
is fixed.

Next, for each
i
define
\bm{Z}_{i}=R_{i}\bm{U}_{i}
as in
(
1.47
) and let

\bm{\delta}_{i}=-\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\bm{H}}\bm{Z}_{i}.

By (
1.49
) and (
1.54
),

\left\lVert\bm{\delta}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
uniformly in
i
on events of probability
approaching one. Using the first-order expansion of the spatial sign map,

U(\bm{Z}_{i}+\bm{\delta}_{i})=\bm{U}_{i}+R_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{\delta}_{i}+O_{p}(n^{-1}),

(1.56)

where the last remainder is uniform in
i
because
p
is fixed and

\mathbb{E}(R_{i}^{-2})<\infty
.
Substituting (
1.55
) into
(
1.53
) and averaging (
1.56
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bm{0}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}-\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\right\}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\widehat{\bm{H}}\bm{U}_{i}\right\}+O_{p}(n^{-1}).

(1.57)

The second line in (
1.57
) is of order
O_{p}(n^{-1})
.
Indeed,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\widehat{\bm{H}}\bm{U}_{i}=\widehat{\bm{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\widehat{\bm{H}}\bm{U}_{i})\bm{U}_{i},

and each average on the right-hand side is
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by the multivariate
central limit theorem and spherical symmetry, while

\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{H}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
.

For the Jacobian term, since
R_{i}
and
\bm{U}_{i}
are independent,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\{R_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}\left(\bm{I}_{p}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\right)+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\frac{p-1}{p}c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}\bm{I}_{p}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(1.58)

Substituting (
1.58
) into
(
1.57
) yields

\frac{p-1}{p}c_{0,\mathrm{HR}}\,\bm{V}_{0}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+O_{p}(n^{-1}).

Multiplying both sides by
\sqrt{n}
gives
(
1.50
).

Finally, since
\bm{U}_{1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{n}
are i.i.d. with mean
\bm{0}
and covariance
matrix
p^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
, the multivariate central limit theorem implies

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}\overset{d}{\to}N_{p}\!\left(\bm{0},\frac{1}{p}\bm{I}_{p}\right).

Combining this with (
1.50
) and Slutsky’s theorem yields
(
1.51
). Equation (
1.52
) follows
by projecting onto the fixed direction
\bm{a}
.
∎

Remark 1.9

(Why the shape estimation does not affect the first-order limit)
.

The proof shows that the effect of estimating
\bm{V}_{0}
is asymptotically of
smaller order in the location equation. The key reason is spherical symmetry in
the whitened coordinates: the first-order derivative of the sign score with
respect to the shape perturbation is an odd function of the direction
\bm{U}
, and
hence its empirical contribution is multiplied by the already root-
n
small
matrix
\widehat{\bm{H}}
. This orthogonality is what allows the HR location
estimator to have the same leading limit as if the true shape were known.

Remark 1.10

(Connection with the high-dimensional HR estimator)
.

In high dimensions the full affine-equivariant update
(
1.45
) is typically infeasible without structural
regularization, because the unregularized sign covariance may be unstable or
singular. Chapter 2 therefore develops diagonal and weighted analogues for
location inference, while Chapters 5 and 6 return to structured HR-type
estimators for discriminant analysis and latent-factor problems. A recent direct
high-dimensional extension of the classical HR system is given by

145
.

Our working philosophy is as follows. When robust geometry is the primary
concern and
p
is moderately large, Tyler-type and Hettmansperger–Randles-type
procedures are conceptually ideal. When
p
is very large relative to
n
,
computational constraints and singularity issues make direct affine-equivariant
estimation more delicate. In that regime one often relies on a combination of
scalar standardization, spatial signs, spatial ranks, multivariate Kendall’s
tau matrices, and structural regularization. Recent work in this direction
includes high-dimensional Hettmansperger–Randles-type estimation and robust
matrix estimation under elliptical factor models

(
145
;
35
)
.

1.8
High-dimensional implications

The high-dimensional regime introduces several structural complications that are
visible already at the level of Chapter 1.

Ill conditioning and noninvertibility.

When
p
is comparable to or larger than
n
, the sample covariance matrix is
poorly conditioned or singular. This is the most obvious reason why Hotelling’s

T^{2}
and many classical multivariate procedures fail in high dimensions.

Location estimation bias.

Many sign- and rank-based procedures require centering by an estimated location.
In high dimensions, the bias induced by this preliminary centering is often of
the same order as the stochastic fluctuation of the final statistic. The
leave-one-out and pairwise correction devices that appear later in this book are
designed precisely to eliminate such bias.

Sparse and dense alternatives.

A signal may be spread across many coordinates or concentrated in only a few.
These two regimes require different testing strategies. Sum-type statistics are
effective against diffuse signals, whereas max-type statistics are sensitive to
rare but strong coordinates. The tension is not specific to Gaussian mean
testing; it persists throughout the elliptical framework.

Moment-free or weak-moment analysis.

Heavy-tailed data may violate the assumptions under which classical covariance
estimators and Gaussian approximations are developed. Spatial signs, ranks,
Tyler-type shape functionals, and self-normalized constructions provide tools
that remain meaningful under substantially weaker conditions.

These themes recur throughout Chapters 2–6. Chapter 2 treats location
estimation and testing, Chapter 3 studies matrix estimation and testing under
shape-based formulations, Chapter 4 discusses additional high-dimensional test
problems, Chapter 5 develops robust discriminant analysis, and Chapter 6 turns
to principal component analysis and factor models.

1.9
Standing notation and conventions

The book will repeatedly use the following symbols. We state them once here and
adhere to them throughout unless an exception is explicitly announced.

Notation

Meaning

Convention

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}

observations

Independent unless temporal or panel dependence is explicitly introduced.

\bm{\mu}

location parameter

Population center under an elliptical model.

\bm{S}

scatter matrix

Defined only up to positive scale.

\bm{V}

normalized shape matrix

Always normalized by
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{V})=p
.

\bm{\Sigma}

covariance matrix

Used only when second moments exist.

\bm{\Omega}

precision or inverse shape

Interpreted according to context and defined only when invertibility is available.

U(\bm{x})

spatial sign

U(\bm{x})=\bm{x}/\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}
for
\bm{x}\neq\bm{0}
and
U(\bm{0})=\bm{0}
.

R(\bm{x};F)

spatial rank

Population spatial rank with respect to distribution
F
.

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}

Default robust location estimator unless another estimator is specified.

\widehat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{HR}}

Affine-equivariant location estimator solving (

) jointly with a shape equation.

.

; automatically translation invariant.

).

Bibliographic notes

For a systematic treatment of multivariate nonparametric methods based on
spatial signs and ranks, including location, scatter, and regression problems,
the natural starting point is
104
. Tyler’s affine-equivariant
shape estimator is foundational for robust scatter estimation under elliptical
models
(
127
)
. Semiparametric optimality theory for sign- and
rank-based multivariate inference, especially for location and shape, is
developed by
58
;
59
. Practical
affine-equivariant multivariate medians, their asymptotic distributions, and
their robustness properties are developed by

64
. A recent high-dimensional extension tailored
to elliptical models is given by
145
. Sign and rank covariance matrices were
systematically studied by
128
; their connections to
robust correlation and principal component analysis appear in

125
;
30
. Large-scale robust matrix
estimation under elliptical models is further developed in

35
. For closure properties of elliptical distributions
under linear transformations and conditioning, see
36
.
The multivariate Kendall’s tau matrix, its eigenspace structure, and its role in
robust high-dimensional matrix analysis are discussed in

60
;
35
.

Chapter 1 has deliberately separated
scatter
from
covariance
and

shape
from
scale
. This distinction is the conceptual thread that
holds together the rest of the book. Once these objects have been fixed, the
remaining chapters can be read as repeated answers to the same question: how
should classical high-dimensional procedures be reformulated when shape is more
fundamental than covariance and when directional information is more stable than
raw magnitude?

Chapter 2
High-Dimensional Location Estimation and Testing under Elliptical Symmetry

2.1
Introduction

Location inference is the oldest and most fundamental topic in multivariate
statistics. In low dimensions, the theory begins with Hotelling’s

T^{2}
statistic and the likelihood theory of the multivariate normal model.
A second classical line replaces moment-based procedures by sign- and rank-based
methods, leading to the spatial median, multivariate sign tests, signed-rank
tests, and affine-equivariant rank procedures; see

104
;
92
;
63
;
113
;
58
.
In modern applications, however, the dimension
p
is often comparable to or
much larger than the sample size
n
. In that regime the classical covariance
matrix becomes singular or poorly estimated, and the geometry of Hotelling’s
statistic breaks down.

The present chapter develops a unified account of location estimation and
testing under elliptical symmetry. Following your requested writing principle,
we proceed in three layers.

(i)

We first review the fixed-
p
theory in enough detail that a reader
encountering multivariate location inference for the first time can see the
classical statistics, the corresponding null distributions, and the basic
proof mechanisms.

(ii)

We then review the main high-dimensional benchmark procedures developed
under Gaussian or light-tail assumptions, such as the Bai–Saranadasa,
Srivastava–Du, Park–Ayyala, Chen–Qin, Cai–Liu–Xia, and adaptive
norm-combination tests.

(iii)

Finally, we turn to the book’s main theme: robust location inference
under elliptical symmetry. This includes the spatial median, diagonally
standardized location estimation, weighted spatial-sign tests, inverse norm
weighting, max-type statistics, max-sum combinations, asymptotic
independence, and the corresponding one-sample and two-sample procedures.

Two features distinguish the elliptical approach from the Gaussian benchmark
literature. The first is that the radial distribution is allowed to be heavy
tailed, so one should not rely on sample means and sample covariances more than
necessary. The second is that the correct notion of invariance is no longer full
orthogonal invariance but rather the geometry induced by location, diagonal
scale, and shape. This is why scalar-invariant diagonal standardization,
spatial signs, and spatial ranks recur throughout the chapter.

A second organizing principle is the distinction between
dense
and

sparse
alternatives. Sum-type statistics are usually effective under
dense alternatives, whereas coordinatewise maxima are more effective under
sparse alternatives. A major development in your recent work is that this
dense–sparse dichotomy can be handled within the same robust framework: first
by establishing a Bahadur representation and Gaussian approximation for the
scaled spatial median, and then by proving the asymptotic independence between
the resulting max-type statistic and the existing sum-type statistic; see

44
;
86
;
146
;
38
. This
chapter makes that development fully explicit.

2.2
Problem formulation, notation, and standing models

2.2.1
One-sample and two-sample hypotheses

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent observations from a population
with location parameter
\bm{\mu}
. The one-sample problem is

H_{0}:\ \bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}\qquad\text{versus}\qquad H_{1}:\ \bm{\mu}\neq\bm{\mu}_{0}.

(2.1)

For the two-sample problem, let

\{\bm{X}_{1i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\{\bm{X}_{2j}\}_{j=1}^{n_{2}}
be independent
samples with locations
\bm{\mu}_{1}
and
\bm{\mu}_{2}
. The null hypothesis is

H_{0}:\ \bm{\mu}_{1}=\bm{\mu}_{2}\qquad\text{versus}\qquad H_{1}:\ \bm{\mu}_{1}\neq\bm{\mu}_{2}.

(2.2)

When the underlying distribution is Gaussian, “location” simply means mean
vector. Under elliptical symmetry we deliberately use the broader term

location parameter
, because the center can be characterized either by the
population mean (when moments exist) or by a robust functional such as the
spatial median.

2.2.2
Elliptical models and scalar standardization

Throughout the chapter, unless a method explicitly assumes a lighter-tailed
model, we work under the following elliptical representation:

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u},\qquad\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1}),\qquad\xi\geq 0,

(2.3)

where
\xi
and
\bm{u}
are independent, and
\bm{S}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}
is the scatter
matrix. When the covariance exists, it is proportional to
\bm{S}
; see
Chapter 1. In high-dimensional location testing it is customary to separate the
diagonal scale and the correlation structure. We therefore write

\bm{D}=\operatorname{diag}(\bm{\Sigma}),\qquad\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1/2},

(2.4)

whenever
\bm{\Sigma}
exists. The matrix
\bm{D}
records marginal scales, whereas

\bm{R}
records correlations or, more generally, shape after marginal
standardization.

The basic building blocks are the spatial sign and the spatial rank:

U(\bm{x})=\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}}\,\mathbbm{1}(\bm{x}\neq\bm{0}),\qquad R(\bm{x};F)=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{x}-\bm{X})\},\ \bm{X}\sim F.

(2.5)

For later use we also introduce the diagonally standardized residuals

\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}),\qquad U_{i}=U(\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}),\qquad r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}.

(2.6)

When the weight function
w(\cdot)
is used, we will also write

w_{i}=w(r_{i})
.

2.2.3
Dense and sparse alternatives

As in the rest of the book, we distinguish between dense and sparse signals.
For the one-sample problem, dense alternatives have many modestly perturbed
coordinates in
\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}_{0}
, whereas sparse alternatives have only a few
nonzero coordinates, possibly of larger magnitude. The same distinction applies
to the two-sample difference
\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}
. The distinction is not cosmetic:
it determines which class of statistics is appropriate. Sum-type statistics are
naturally aligned with dense alternatives, while max-type statistics are tuned
to sparse alternatives. The later max-sum sections of this chapter are designed
precisely to handle both regimes at once.

2.3
Classical low-dimensional location inference

2.3.1
Gaussian likelihood theory and Hotelling’s
T^{2}

We begin with the fixed-
p
Gaussian model, not because the book is about
Gaussian methods, but because the entire subject of multivariate location
inference is historically anchored there.

2.3.1.1
One-sample Hotelling
T^{2}

Assume
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
with unknown positive
definite covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
, and consider testing

H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}
. Let

\bar{\bm{X}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i},\qquad\bm{S}_{n}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(2.7)

Hotelling’s statistic is

T^{2}_{H}=n(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{n}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0}).

(2.8)

Theorem 2.1

(Exact null law of one-sample Hotelling’s
T^{2}
)
.

If
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
and
n>p
, then under

H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}
,

\frac{n-p}{p(n-1)}T_{H}^{2}\sim F_{p,n-p}.

(2.9)

Equivalently, as
n\to\infty
with fixed
p
,

T_{H}^{2}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{p}.

(2.10)

Proof.

Under Gaussian sampling,

\sqrt{n}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma})
under
H_{0}
, while

(n-1)\bm{S}_{n}\sim W_{p}(n-1,\bm{\Sigma})
and is independent of
\bar{\bm{X}}
. Write

\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})=\bm{Z}
so that
\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
,
and

\bm{W}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(n-1)\bm{S}_{n}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\sim W_{p}(n-1,\bm{I}_{p})
. Then

T_{H}^{2}=(n-1)\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Z}.

The standard relation between the Wishart and
F
distributions yields
(
2.9
). The asymptotic chi-square limit in
(
2.10
) follows because, for fixed
p
, the sample
covariance is consistent and therefore
\bm{S}_{n}^{-1}
may be replaced by

\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
up to
o_{p}(1)
.
∎

Under local alternatives of the form

\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}+n^{-1/2}\bm{\delta}
, the limiting law becomes noncentral

\chi^{2}_{p}(\lambda)
with noncentrality parameter

\lambda=\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
. Thus, even in the classical
low-dimensional setting, local power is driven by Mahalanobis geometry.

2.3.1.2
Two-sample Hotelling
T^{2}

Now assume independent samples

\bm{X}_{11},\ldots,\bm{X}_{1n_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{1},\bm{\Sigma})
and

\bm{X}_{21},\ldots,\bm{X}_{2n_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{2},\bm{\Sigma})
with common covariance

\bm{\Sigma}
. Let
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
, let
\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}
be the sample
means, and let

\bm{S}_{p}=\frac{1}{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{1})(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}(\bm{X}_{2j}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})(\bm{X}_{2j}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigg].

(2.11)

The classical statistic is

T^{2}_{H,2}=\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}).

(2.12)

Theorem 2.2

(Exact null law of two-sample Hotelling’s
T^{2}
)
.

If the two Gaussian samples have common covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
and

n_{1}+n_{2}>p+1
, then under
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}_{1}=\bm{\mu}_{2}
,

\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}-p-1}{p(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)}T^{2}_{H,2}\sim F_{p,n_{1}+n_{2}-p-1}.

(2.13)

Hence, for fixed
p
,
T^{2}_{H,2}\Rightarrow\chi_{p}^{2}
under the null, and under
local alternatives

\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}=(n_{1}^{-1}+n_{2}^{-1})^{1/2}\bm{\delta}
the limit becomes a noncentral

\chi_{p}^{2}
law with noncentrality parameter

\lambda=\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
.

Proof.

The proof is the two-sample analogue of Theorem
2.1
.
The pooled covariance matrix is independent of

(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})
, and the standardized quadratic form reduces to a
ratio of a Gaussian quadratic form and an independent Wishart matrix.
∎

Hotelling’s statistic provides the correct fixed-
p
benchmark, but it already
suggests why high dimensions are difficult: once
p\geq n
or

p\geq n_{1}+n_{2}-2
, the inverse sample covariance matrix no longer exists.

2.3.2
Classical sign, signed-rank, and spatial-rank procedures

The Gaussian likelihood paradigm is not the only classical route. A second
classical route treats location through directional information. The central
objects are the spatial sign, the spatial rank, and the spatial median.

2.3.2.1
Spatial median

For a distribution
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the population spatial median is

\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2},

(2.14)

provided the minimizer is unique. The sample spatial median is

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}.

(2.15)

If no observation equals the minimizer, the estimating equation is

\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{0}.

(2.16)

Theorem 2.3

(Fixed-
p
asymptotic linearization of the spatial median)
.

Assume that
p
is fixed, that
\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}
is unique, and that

\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{2}}\{\bm{I}_{p}-U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})^{\mathsf{T}}\}\right]

(2.17)

is positive definite. Then

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})+o_{p}(1),

(2.18)

and therefore

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N_{p}\left(\bm{0},\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\right),

(2.19)

where

\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\operatorname{Var}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})\}
.

Proof.

The estimating equation (
2.16
) may be written as

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})=0
, where

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})
. A first-order Taylor
expansion around
\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}
yields

\bm{0}=\Psi_{n}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\dot{\Psi}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})+o_{p}(\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{2}),

where the derivative matrix is exactly
\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}
. Rearranging gives
(
2.18
). The central limit theorem for
the empirical average of signs then implies
(
2.19
).
∎

The theorem above is classical, but it is extremely important for later high-
dimensional theory: the high-dimensional Bahadur expansion is conceptually the
same, except that one must track the remainder term much more carefully.

2.3.2.2
One-sample sign and signed-rank tests

For the one-sample problem
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}
with fixed
p
, the most direct
sign-based statistic is built from

\bar{\bm{U}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0}),\qquad\hat{\bm{B}}_{U}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(2.20)

The classical one-sample spatial-sign statistic is

Q_{\mathrm{sign}}=n\bar{\bm{U}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{B}}_{U}^{-1}\bar{\bm{U}}.
