(2.21)

Under central symmetry about
\bm{\mu}_{0}
one has
\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\}=\bm{0}
,
and therefore a multivariate central limit theorem yields the chi-square limit.

A more efficient fixed-
p
procedure uses spatial signed ranks. Define

\bm{R}_{i}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U\{(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})+(\bm{X}_{j}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\},\qquad\bar{\bm{R}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{R}_{i},

(2.22)

and let

\hat{\bm{B}}_{R}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{R}_{i}\bm{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
. The one-sample
signed-rank statistic is

Q_{\mathrm{SR}}=n\bar{\bm{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{B}}_{R}^{-1}\bar{\bm{R}}.

(2.23)

Theorem 2.4

(Fixed-
p
sign and signed-rank null laws)
.

Assume
p
is fixed and the distribution is centrally symmetric about

\bm{\mu}_{0}
. Then, under standard nonsingularity conditions,

Q_{\mathrm{sign}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{p}^{2},\qquad Q_{\mathrm{SR}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{p}^{2}.

(2.24)

Proof.

The proof is again standard M-estimation plus multivariate CLT. Under
H_{0}
,

\sqrt{n}\bar{\bm{U}}
and
\sqrt{n}\bar{\bm{R}}
are asymptotically Gaussian with
covariance matrices consistently estimated by
\hat{\bm{B}}_{U}
and

\hat{\bm{B}}_{R}
. Studentization converts the limiting Gaussian quadratic forms
into chi-square limits.
∎

For semiparametric efficiency under ellipticity one may replace spatial signs by
pseudo-Mahalanobis signs and ranks, as in

58
. The message for the present book is that, even in
the fixed-
p
theory, the efficient geometry is already sign/rank based once one
moves beyond the Gaussian likelihood paradigm.

2.3.2.3
Two-sample spatial sign and spatial rank procedures

For the two-sample problem with fixed dimension, one may combine the two
samples after alignment or define ranks relative to the pooled sample. One
classical version is the affine-invariant multisample sign test of

63
, while a particularly transparent spatial-rank
formulation is given in
92
and in Oja’s monograph.
Let
\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n}
denote the pooled sample with labels indicating group
membership. For each observation define its spatial rank in the pooled sample,

\widehat{R}(\bm{Y}_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U(\bm{Y}_{i}-\bm{Y}_{j}).

(2.25)

Write
\bar{\bm{R}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bm{R}}_{2}
for the average pooled ranks in the two
groups, and let
\hat{\bm{C}}
be the pooled covariance matrix of the rank
vectors. Then the fixed-
p
spatial-rank statistic takes the form

Q_{2\mathrm{SR}}=\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\bar{\bm{R}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{R}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{C}}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{R}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{R}}_{2}),

(2.26)

and satisfies

Q_{2\mathrm{SR}}\Rightarrow\chi_{p}^{2}
under
H_{0}
.

The fixed-
p
chapter of the story is therefore clear. Under Gaussianity one has
Hotelling’s
T^{2}
and exact
F
distributions. Under broader elliptical or
symmetric models one has sign, signed-rank, and spatial-rank procedures, again
with asymptotic chi-square null laws. High-dimensional methods should be read as
extensions or replacements of these fixed-
p
constructions.

2.4
High-dimensional benchmark procedures under Gaussian or light-tailed models

We now turn to the large-
p
literature that developed before the robust
elliptical program became mature. These methods remain indispensable because
later robust procedures are most naturally understood relative to them.

2.4.1
One-sample quadratic-form and diagonal procedures

Srivastava–Du.

For
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}_{0}
,
121
proposed replacing the sample
covariance matrix by its diagonal part. Writing

\bm{D}_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(\bm{S}_{n})
, their statistic can be expressed as

T_{\mathrm{SD}}=n(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{S}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})-\frac{(n-1)p}{n-3},

(2.27)

followed by a variance normalization depending on

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})
and related quantities. The procedure is invariant under component-
wise scale transformations and is effective when the signal is dense and the
marginal scales vary substantially.

Park–Ayyala.

The leave-out improvement of
108
removes a non-negligible
bias in diagonal Hotelling-type procedures. Their statistic can be written in the
cross-validation form

T_{\mathrm{PA}}=\frac{n-5}{n(n-1)(n-3)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{X}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{D}}_{S(i,j)}^{-1}\bm{X}_{j},

(2.28)

where
\hat{\bm{D}}_{S(i,j)}
is the diagonal sample covariance computed from the
leave-two-out sample. After suitable variance normalization,

T_{\mathrm{PA}}
is asymptotically normal under the null. This procedure is the
most important scale-invariant Gaussian/light-tail benchmark for the one-sample
problem, and it is repeatedly used as a comparison target in the later robust
papers.

2.4.2
Two-sample quadratic-form procedures

Bai–Saranadasa.

For the two-sample problem,

8
proposed the bias-corrected Euclidean statistic

T_{\mathrm{BS}}=\left\lVert\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1})}{n_{1}}-\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2})}{n_{2}},

(2.29)

where
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}
and
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}
are the two sample covariance
matrices. After normalization by a consistent estimator of its variance,

T_{\mathrm{BS}}
is asymptotically Gaussian under the null. Its strength is that
it avoids matrix inversion entirely, but it is not scalar invariant under
coordinatewise rescaling.

Chen–Qin.

22
refined the Bai–Saranadasa construction into the now
standard
U
-statistic

T_{\mathrm{CQ}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{X}_{1i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}_{1j}+\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{X}_{2i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}_{2j}-\frac{2}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{X}_{1i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}_{2j}.

(2.30)

Because (
2.30
) is an unbiased estimator of

\left\lVert\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, it has become the standard dense-alternative
benchmark in the high-dimensional two-sample literature.

Assumption 2.1

(Chen–Qin balance and trace conditions)
.

Let
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Assume:

(CQ1)

n_{1}/n\to\kappa\in(0,1)
as
n\to\infty
.

(CQ2)

For every
i,j,\ell,h\in\{1,2\}
,

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\bm{\Sigma}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}_{\ell}\bm{\Sigma}_{h})=o\!\left[\operatorname{tr}^{2}\{(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}+\bm{\Sigma}_{2})^{2}\}\right].

(2.31)

(CQ3)

When local alternatives are studied, the signal is not so large that it changes
the variance order, namely

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})=o\!\left[n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\{(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}+\bm{\Sigma}_{2})^{2}\}\right],\qquad k=1,2.

(2.32)

Theorem 2.5

(Null law of the Chen–Qin statistic)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds. Then, under

H_{0}:\bm{\mu}_{1}=\bm{\mu}_{2}
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{CQ}}}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{CQ}})}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.33)

Moreover,

\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{CQ}})=\frac{2}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{2})+\frac{2}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{2})+\frac{4}{n_{1}n_{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2})+o(1)

(2.34)

after normalization by the leading trace order. Under the local alternatives in
(
2.32
),

\mathbb{E}(T_{\mathrm{CQ}})=\left\lVert\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, so the power is driven by the
ratio between the squared Euclidean signal and the square root of the trace-type
variance in (
2.34
).

Srivastava–Katayama–Kano.

A different benchmark, due to
122
, starts from
the diagonal Hotelling-type quantity

Q_{2}=(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\frac{\bm{D}_{1}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\bm{D}_{2}}{n_{2}}\right)^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}),

(2.35)

where
\bm{D}_{i}=\operatorname{diag}(\bm{\Sigma}_{i})
and the sample version uses

\hat{\bm{D}}_{i}=\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{i})
. Define

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}}=(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})-p,\qquad\hat{\bm{D}}=\frac{\hat{\bm{D}}_{1}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\hat{\bm{D}}_{2}}{n_{2}}.

(2.36)

Let

\bm{D}=\frac{\bm{D}_{1}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\bm{D}_{2}}{n_{2}},\qquad\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\left(\frac{\bm{\Sigma}_{1}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\bm{\Sigma}_{2}}{n_{2}}\right)\bm{D}^{-1/2}.

(2.37)

The leading variance is

\sigma_{\mathrm{SKK}}^{2}=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}).

(2.38)

To estimate it,
122
proposed

\hat{F}_{i}=p^{-1}\left[\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{i})^{2}-n_{i}^{-1}\{\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{i})\}^{2}\right],\qquad\hat{G}=p^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}),

(2.39)

with

\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}})=\frac{2\hat{F}_{1}}{n_{1}^{2}}+\frac{2\hat{F}_{2}}{n_{2}^{2}}+\frac{4\hat{G}}{n_{1}n_{2}}.

(2.40)

The standardized statistic is

T_{\mathrm{SKK}}=\frac{\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}})\,c_{p,n}}},\qquad c_{p,n}=1+\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}{p^{3/2}}.

(2.41)

A decisive point for our book is that
(
2.41
) is invariant under componentwise nonzero diagonal
transformations
\bm{X}\mapsto\bm{D}_{0}\bm{X}
, because both the numerator and the
diagonal standardizer transform equivariantly.

Assumption 2.2

(Srivastava–Katayama–Kano conditions)
.

Assume:

(SKK1)

There exist constants
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<\infty
such that

c_{1}\leq\,\min_{i,k}\sigma_{ikk}\leq\,\max_{i,k}\sigma_{ikk}\leq c_{2}.

(2.42)

(SKK2)

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\to 0.

(2.43)

(SKK3)

n_{1}/(n_{1}+n_{2})\to\kappa\in(0,1)
.

(SKK4)

If
N_{*}=\min(n_{1},n_{2})
, then

N_{*}=O(p^{\delta})\qquad\text{for some }\delta>1/2.

(2.44)

(SKK5)

Under local alternatives,

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{R}\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})=o\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}.

(2.45)

Theorem 2.6

(Srivastava–Katayama–Kano null and local-alternative laws)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.2
holds and the samples are Gaussian. Then,
under
H_{0}
,

T_{\mathrm{SKK}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.46)

Under the local alternatives in (
2.45
),

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\mathrm{SKK}}>z_{1-\alpha}\}\to\Phi\left(-z_{1-\alpha}+\frac{(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})}{\sqrt{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}}\right).

(2.47)

The scale-invariant Behrens–Fisher test of Feng, Zou, Wang, and Zhu.

The scale-invariant Behrens–Fisher test of

48
is particularly important for the present book,
because it shows exactly where a diagonal standardization scheme can fail if the
plug-in bias is not corrected. Write
\gamma=n_{1}/n_{2}
and define

\bm{\Lambda}=\operatorname{diag}\left\{(\sigma_{11}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{21}^{2})^{-1/2},\ldots,(\sigma_{1p}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2p}^{2})^{-1/2}\}\right.

(2.48)

with sample counterpart
\hat{\bm{\Lambda}}
. For each coordinate
k
, let

A_{k}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{1ik}X_{1jk}+\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{2ik}X_{2jk}-\frac{2}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}X_{1ik}X_{2jk},

(2.49)

so that
\sum_{k=1}^{p}A_{k}=T_{\mathrm{CQ}}
. The Behrens–Fisher statistic is

T_{\mathrm{BF}}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{A_{k}}{\hat{\sigma}_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\hat{\sigma}_{2k}^{2}}.

(2.50)

Equivalently,

T_{\mathrm{BF}}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{2}A_{k}.

(2.51)

This statistic is shift invariant and scalar invariant, but not orthogonally
invariant. The main contribution of
48
is the careful
analysis of the non-negligible bias generated by plugging the marginal variance
estimators into the denominator.

Define

\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}^{2}=\frac{2}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\operatorname{tr}\{(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Lambda})^{2}\}+\frac{2}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\operatorname{tr}\{(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\Lambda})^{2}\}+\frac{4}{n_{1}n_{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\Lambda}).

(2.52)

The exact mean contains a signal term

\left\lVert\bm{\Lambda}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
and a bias correction involving the
marginal variances, marginal skewnesses, and marginal kurtoses. Writing

\kappa_{ik}=\mathbb{E}(X_{ijk}-\mu_{ik})^{3}
and

\nu_{ik}=\mathbb{E}(X_{ijk}-\mu_{ik})^{4}
, one may decompose it as

\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}=\left\lVert\bm{\Lambda}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+b_{n,1}+b_{n,2}+b_{n,3},

(2.53)

where

\displaystyle b_{n,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left[\frac{2\sigma_{1k}^{4}}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{2}}+\frac{2\gamma^{2}\sigma_{2k}^{4}}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{2}}\right],

(2.54)

\displaystyle b_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left[\frac{2\kappa_{1k}^{2}}{n_{1}^{2}(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{3}}+\frac{2\gamma^{2}\kappa_{2k}^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{3}}-\frac{4\gamma\kappa_{1k}\kappa_{2k}}{n_{1}n_{2}(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{3}}\right],

(2.55)

\displaystyle b_{n,3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}(\mu_{1k}-\mu_{2k})^{2}\frac{n_{1}^{-1}\nu_{1k}+4\{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)\}^{-1}\sigma_{1k}^{4}+\gamma n_{2}^{-1}\nu_{2k}+4\gamma^{2}\{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)\}^{-1}\sigma_{2k}^{4}}{(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{3}}

(2.56)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{2n_{1}^{-1}\kappa_{1k}(\mu_{2k}-\mu_{1k})+2\gamma n_{2}^{-1}\kappa_{2k}(\mu_{1k}-\mu_{2k})}{(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{2}}.

(2.57)

The terms
b_{n,1}
and
b_{n,2}
remain under
H_{0}
, which is exactly why a
bias correction is essential when
p
is as large as order
n^{2}
or larger.

Assumption 2.3

(Scale-invariant Behrens–Fisher conditions)
.

Assume:

(BF1)

n_{1}/(n_{1}+n_{2})\to\lambda\in(0,1)
.

(BF2)

For all
i,j,\ell,h\in\{1,2\}
,

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{j}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\ell}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{h}\bm{\Lambda})=o\!\left[\operatorname{tr}^{2}\{(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Lambda}+\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\Lambda})^{2}\}\right].

(2.58)

(BF3)

\frac{p^{2}}{n^{5}\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}^{2}}\to 0.

(2.59)

(BF4)

With

\bm{\Pi}_{1i}=\mathbb{E}\Big(\big[\{\bm{\Lambda}(\bm{X}_{ij}-\bm{\mu}_{i})\}^{\circ 3}\big]\big[\bm{\Lambda}(\bm{X}_{ij}-\bm{\mu}_{i})\big]^{\mathsf{T}}\Big),\qquad\bm{\Pi}_{2i}=\mathbb{E}\Big(\big[\bm{\Lambda}(\bm{X}_{ij}-\bm{\mu}_{i})(\bm{X}_{ij}-\bm{\mu}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}\big]^{\circ 3}\Big).

(2.60)

one has

n_{i}^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Pi}_{1i}^{2})=o(\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}^{2}),\qquad n_{i}^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Pi}_{2i})=o(\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}^{2}),\qquad i=1,2.

(2.61)

(BF5)

Under local alternatives,

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})=o\!\left[n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\{(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Lambda}+\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\Lambda})^{2}\}\right],\quad i=1,2,

(2.62)

and

\big[(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\big]^{2}=o\!\left[n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\{(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Lambda}+\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\Lambda})^{2}\}\right].

(2.63)

Theorem 2.7

(Scale-invariant Behrens–Fisher test)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.3
holds. Then

\frac{T_{\mathrm{BF}}-\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.64)

Under
H_{0}
, the non-negligible quantity
\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}
must be estimated
and subtracted. If
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{BF},n}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{BF},n}
are the
plug-in estimators proposed in
48
, then

\frac{T_{\mathrm{BF}}-\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{BF},n}}{\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{BF},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.65)

The resulting procedure is scalar invariant and remains valid in regimes where
uncorrected diagonal Hotelling-type tests accumulate a bias of the same order as
their standard deviation.

Composite and Behrens–Fisher type combinations.

When no single benchmark is uniformly best, one may also combine different
quadratic-form statistics. This idea appears in the composite
T^{2}
test of

49
, which mixes the information carried by different
quadratic forms. This philosophy will reappear in robust max-sum combinations
later in the chapter.

2.4.3
Max-type and adaptive procedures

Cai–Liu–Xia.

Sum-type tests are often suboptimal under sparse alternatives. A seminal response
is the precision-adjusted max statistic of
15
. To write the
method in a form that matches later chapters, suppose first that the two
samples are Gaussian with a common covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
and precision
matrix
\bm{\Omega}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}=(\omega_{ij})
. Define

\bar{\bm{Z}}=\bm{\Omega}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})

(2.66)

and the oracle statistic

M_{\bm{\Omega}}=\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\frac{\bar{Z}_{j}^{2}}{\omega_{jj}}.

(2.67)

The intuition is simple but powerful: under sparse alternatives the transformed
coordinates
\bar{Z}_{j}
amplify weak mean signals by removing the correlation
masking effect induced by
\bm{\Sigma}
.

Assumption 2.4

(Oracle CLX conditions)
.

Assume:

(CLX1)

The common covariance matrix is positive definite and its eigenvalues are
uniformly bounded away from zero and infinity:

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq C_{0}

(2.68)

for some fixed
C_{0}>0
.

(CLX2)

The transformed coordinates are weakly dependent:

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq p}\left|\frac{\omega_{ij}}{\sqrt{\omega_{ii}\omega_{jj}}}\right|\leq r_{0}<1.

(2.69)

(CLX3)

The maximum degree of strong transformed correlations is negligible in the
sense required by the Gaussian extreme-value theory of

15
; in particular, no positive fraction of coordinates may be
highly correlated with polynomially many others.

Theorem 2.8

(Extreme-value limit of the oracle CLX statistic)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.4
holds and
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}_{1}=\bm{\mu}_{2}
is true.
Then

\mathbb{P}\{M_{\bm{\Omega}}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\}\longrightarrow\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}.

(2.70)

Hence the critical region of the oracle max test is of Gumbel type rather than
Gaussian. In the feasible CLX procedure,
\bm{\Omega}
is replaced by a sparse
precision matrix estimator, and the same limit continues to hold under the extra
estimation conditions given in
15
.

The appeal of (
2.67
) is that it targets the sparse regime in
which only a small number of coordinates depart from the null. Later robust
max-type procedures in this book should be read as elliptical analogues of the
CLX paradigm.

Adaptive norm-combination tests.

143
proposed combining statistics based on different norms
of the coordinatewise standardized mean contrasts. Let

W_{j}=\frac{\bar{X}_{1j}-\bar{X}_{2j}}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{1,jj}/n_{1}+\hat{\sigma}_{2,jj}/n_{2}}},\qquad j=1,\ldots,p.

(2.71)

For an even integer
\gamma\geq 1
, define the sum-of-powered-score statistic

T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}W_{j}^{\gamma},

(2.72)

and for the max-type member of the family,

T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\infty)=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|W_{j}|.

(2.73)

The notation “SPU” follows the paper: for small
\gamma
the statistic behaves
like a dense-alternative
L_{\gamma}
-type aggregate, whereas
\gamma=\infty

behaves like a sparse max test.

Assumption 2.5

(Conditions for adaptive norm-combination tests)
.

Assume:

(XU1)

The covariance matrices have eigenvalues uniformly bounded away from zero and
infinity, and the marginal variances are bounded away from zero and infinity.

(XU2)

The dependence among coordinates is weak enough that finite collections of the
centered statistics
T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)
satisfy a multivariate central
limit theorem; in the framework of
143
, this is ensured by
their mixing and moment conditions.

(XU3)

The collection of candidate norms
\Gamma
is finite; in practice the choice

\Gamma=\{1,2,3,4,5,6,\infty\}

(2.74)

is recommended and widely used.

Theorem 2.9

(Joint asymptotic law of the SPU family)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.5
holds. For any finite set

\Gamma_{0}\subset\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
,

\Bigg(\frac{T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)-\mu_{\gamma}}{\sigma_{\gamma}}\Bigg)_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N_{|\Gamma_{0}|}(\bm{0},\bm{R}_{\Gamma_{0}}),

(2.75)

where
\mu_{\gamma}=\mathbb{E}\{T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)\}
,

\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}=\operatorname{Var}\{T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)\}
, and

\bm{R}_{\Gamma_{0}}
is the limiting correlation matrix of the finite collection.
Therefore an adaptive statistic can be defined by

T_{\mathrm{aSPU}}=\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\hat{P}_{\gamma},

(2.76)

where
\hat{P}_{\gamma}
is the bootstrap or simulation-based
p
-value of

T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)
. The resulting procedure adapts to both dense and
sparse alternatives by selecting, in a data-dependent way, the most favorable
norm index.

The practical message is worth stating explicitly. If the signal is dense, small
values of
\gamma
behave like quadratic-form tests and often dominate. If the
signal is sparse, large values of
\gamma
, especially
\gamma=\infty
, are more
effective. The adaptive minimum-
p
combination in
(
2.76
) is designed precisely to bridge these regimes.

2.5
Spatial median and diagonal standardization in high dimensions

The real turning point is that, in high dimensions, robust location estimation is
not merely a preliminary step. It enters directly into the construction and
validity of the test statistics. We therefore treat it in detail.

2.5.1
The ordinary spatial median and its high-dimensional Bahadur expansion

We first recall the ordinary spatial median

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}
defined by
(
2.15
). When
p
grows with
n
, the key question
is no longer mere consistency but the existence of an explicit asymptotic
expansion with a remainder term small enough for subsequent testing problems.
Recent results of
79
and the subsequent analysis in

157
show that such an expansion is available under
elliptical models.

To state the result in a simple form, consider the model

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{S}^{1/2}\bm{Z}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.77)

where
\bm{Z}_{i}
is spherically symmetric with center
\bm{0}
. Write

r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\bm{U}_{i}=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}).

(2.78)

The relevant high-dimensional question is whether the estimating equation for the
spatial median can be linearized with a remainder small enough for later
coordinatewise testing and Gaussian approximation. The answer is yes: under the
conditions stated below, the sample spatial median admits a Bahadur expansion in
which the leading term is the empirical average of the spatial signs and the
remainder is asymptotically negligible. This is the aspect of the ordinary
spatial median that will later be inherited by the scalar-invariant and weighted
estimators used in the testing procedures.

Assumption 2.6

(Ordinary spatial median in increasing dimension)
.

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
be independent observations from the elliptical model
(
2.77
). Write

\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{r_{i}}\{\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}\right],\qquad\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}),

(2.79)

where
r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}
and
\bm{U}_{i}=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})
. Assume:

(SM1)

The population spatial median exists, is unique, and equals
\bm{\mu}
.

(SM2)

There are constants
0<\underline{a}\leq\bar{a}<\infty
such that

\underline{a}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}})\leq\bar{a}.

(2.80)

(SM3)

The sign covariance is nondegenerate and not too concentrated in a single
direction:

\left\lVert\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1-\psi

(2.81)

for some fixed
\psi\in(0,1)
.

(SM4)

The stochastic equicontinuity remainder in the Taylor expansion of the score
process

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})

is
o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
uniformly on an
n^{-1/2}
-neighborhood of
\bm{\mu}
.

Theorem 2.10

(High-dimensional Bahadur representation of the spatial median)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.6
holds. Then

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})=\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{r}_{n,\mathrm{SM}},

(2.82)

where
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{n,\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{p}(1)
. Consequently, for every fixed
vector
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert_{2}=1
,

\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N\!\left(0,\,\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{a}\right).

(2.83)

The detailed proof is given in Appendix A below.

Remark 2.1
.

Theorem
2.10
is the cleanest way to formulate the
self-contained role of the ordinary spatial median in this chapter. The
published high-dimensional papers often work directly with the diagonally scaled
version because scalar invariance is needed in the subsequent testing problems,
but the proof mechanism is already visible here: one linearizes the estimating
equation, identifies the deterministic Jacobian, and shows that the empirical
remainder is of smaller order.

2.5.2
Scalar-invariant diagonal standardization

The ordinary spatial median is robust but not scalar invariant. High-dimensional
omics and finance data often contain coordinates with widely different marginal
scales, and a practical location procedure should treat these coordinates fairly.
This motivates the diagonal standardization used in

50
,
44
,

41
, and
86
.

The basic idea is to estimate location and diagonal scale jointly. For the
one-sample problem under
H_{0}:\bm{\theta}=\bm{0}
we seek a pair
(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})
satisfying

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\}=\bm{0},

(2.84)

and

\frac{p}{n}\operatorname{diag}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\}^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigg\}=\bm{I}_{p}.

(2.85)

This is a diagonal analogue of the Hettmansperger–Randles estimating system.
We shall call
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
the
scaled spatial median
or

scalar-invariant spatial median
.

A practical algorithm updates location and scale iteratively. Starting from an
initial pair
(\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{(0)},\hat{\bm{D}}^{(0)})
, define

\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}=(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{(m)})
.
Then set

\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{(m+1)}=\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{(m)}+(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{1/2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}},

(2.86)

and

\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m+1)}=p(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{1/2}\operatorname{diag}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\}(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{1/2}.

(2.87)

This is exactly the scalar-invariant normalization that later enters the one-
sample sign test, the two-sample sign test, and the max-type procedures.

2.5.3
Bahadur expansion of the scaled spatial median

The scaled spatial median is the actual location estimator used in the high-
dimensional sign-based testing literature. For this reason we record its
first-order expansion in a detailed and self-contained form.

Let
\bm{\theta}
denote the true location and
\bm{D}
the true diagonal scale. Write

\bm{U}_{i}=U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\},\qquad r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\right\rVert_{2},\qquad c_{0}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-1}).

(2.88)

The following result is the precise form used repeatedly in your location,
sphericity, and max-type papers.

Assumption 2.7

(Diagonal sign model for the scaled spatial median)
.

Suppose that

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\theta}+\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{i},\qquad\operatorname{diag}(\bm{D})=\bm{D},\qquad\operatorname{diag}\{\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{i})\}=\bm{I}_{p},

(2.89)

and write

r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\bm{U}_{i}=U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\},\qquad c_{0}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-1}).

(2.90)

Assume:

(SD1)

The inverse radial moments are finite and bounded away from zero and infinity:
there exist constants
0<\underline{b}\leq\bar{B}<\infty
such that

\underline{b}\leq\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-k})\leq\bar{B},\qquad k=1,2,3,4.

(2.91)

(SD2)

Let
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
denote the standardized shape matrix.
Then

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})=o\{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})\}.

(2.92)

(SD3)

The dimensional growth satisfies

\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}=O(1),\qquad\log p=o(n^{1/3}).

(2.93)

(SD4)

The row sums of
\bm{R}
are controlled: for some
\delta\in(0,1/2]
and a
positive sequence
a_{0}(p)\asymp p^{1-\delta}
,

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}|\rho_{j\ell}|\leq a_{0}(p),\qquad\log n=o\!\big(p^{1/3\wedge\delta}\big).

(2.94)

Lemma 2.1

(Uniform consistency of the diagonal scale estimator)
.

Under Assumption
2.7
, the diagonal solution

\hat{\bm{D}}=\operatorname{diag}(\hat{d}_{1},\ldots,\hat{d}_{p})
of
(
2.85
) satisfies

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{\hat{d}_{j}}{d_{j}}-1\right|=o_{p}(1).

(2.95)

More precisely, under the growth regime in
(
2.93
)–(
2.94
),

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{\hat{d}_{j}}{d_{j}}-1\right|=O_{p}\Bigg[\left\{\frac{\log p}{n}\right\}^{1/2}+\frac{a_{0}(p)}{p}+\frac{\log p}{n^{3/4}}\Bigg].

(2.96)

Theorem 2.11

(Bahadur expansion of the scaled spatial median)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.7
holds. Then the solution

\hat{\bm{\theta}}
to
(
2.84
)–(
2.85
) satisfies

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta})=c_{0}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{C}_{n},

(2.97)

with remainder

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{C}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\Big\{

\displaystyle n^{-1/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle+n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big\}.

(2.98)

In particular, every fixed coordinate and every finite collection of coordinates
of
\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta})
are asymptotically Gaussian with
leading covariance matrix
c_{0}^{-2}p^{-1}\bm{R}
.

The detailed proof is given in Appendix C below.

Remark 2.2
.

Theorem
2.11
is the main location-estimation result
behind the robust one-sample sign tests, the max-type procedures, and several
later chapters of the book. The first-order term is the simple empirical mean of
the standardized signs, while all of the technical work is buried in the proof
that the location-scale coupling produces only the remainder
(
2.11
).

2.5.4
Weighted location equation and weighted Bahadur expansion

The next step, developed in
41
for sum-type tests and in

146
for max-type tests, is to weight the
sign equation by a radial function. This is the point at which the book must be
more detailed than a short survey, because the weighted estimator is the common
starting point of the INST, the weighted max statistic, and the weighted max-sum
combination.

Let
K:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable weight function. The weighted location
estimating equation is

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\!\left(\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K})\right\rVert_{2}\right)U\{\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K})\}=\bm{0},

(2.99)

while the diagonal scale still satisfies the unweighted normalizing equation
(
2.85
). The iterative update becomes

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}^{(m+1)}=\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}^{(m)}+(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{1/2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\hat{r}_{i}^{(m)})U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\hat{r}_{i}^{(m)})\{\hat{r}_{i}^{(m)}\}^{-1}},

(2.100)

where

\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}=(\hat{\bm{D}}^{(m)})^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}^{(m)})

and
\hat{r}_{i}^{(m)}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}
.

The most important special case is the power family
K(t)=t^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
It includes the spatial-sign estimator (
m=0
), the inverse norm weighting
(
m=-1
), and the radially amplified version (
m=1
). To unify notation across
all weighted procedures in this chapter, write

\nu_{\ell,K}=\mathbb{E}\{K^{\ell}(r_{i})\},\qquad c_{0,K}=\mathbb{E}\{K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}\},\qquad\bm{\Sigma}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}.

(2.101)

When
K(t)=t^{m}
, the shorthand becomes

\nu_{\ell,m}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{m\ell})
and
c_{0,m}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{m-1})
.

Assumption 2.8

(Weighted sign moments and local alternatives)
.

In addition to Assumption
2.7
, assume:

(WS1)

The weight function satisfies

0<\nu_{2,K}<\infty,\qquad\nu_{4,K}=O(\nu_{2,K}^{2}),\qquad 0<c_{0,K}<\infty.

(2.102)

(WS2)

Under local alternatives,

\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}=O(c_{0,K}^{-2}\sigma_{n,K}),

(2.103)

and

\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}=o\!\left(np\,c_{0,K}^{-2}\sigma_{n,K}\right),

(2.104)

where

\sigma_{n,K}^{2}=\frac{2\nu_{2,K}^{2}}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}).

(2.105)

Theorem 2.12

(Weighted Bahadur expansion and Gaussian approximation)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7

and
2.8
hold. Then

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})=c_{0,K}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{C}_{n,K},

(2.106)

where the remainder
\bm{C}_{n,K}
satisfies the same order as in
(
2.11
). Moreover,

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{re}}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})\in A\}-\mathbb{P}\{\bm{Z}_{K}\in A\}\right|\to 0,

(2.107)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{re}}
is the class of hyperrectangles in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and

\bm{Z}_{K}\sim N_{p}\!\left(\bm{0},\,c_{0,K}^{-2}\bm{\Sigma}_{K}\right).

(2.108)

The detailed proof is given in Appendix C below.

Remark 2.3
.

Theorem
2.12
is the weighted analogue of the SSPCA-type
linearization for the unweighted estimator. In later sections, the difference
between the unweighted and weighted max statistics is not in the proof
architecture but only in the score vector

K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}
and in the normalization constants
c_{0,K}
and
\nu_{2,K}
.

2.6
One-sample spatial-sign and weighted-sign tests

We now turn from estimation to testing. To make the logic transparent, we begin
with the
oracle
statistics that assume the true
(\bm{\theta},\bm{D})
are known.
After that, we discuss the practical leave-out versions used in the published
procedures.

2.6.1
A generic weighted-sign sum-type statistic

Consider the one-sample problem
H_{0}:\bm{\theta}=\bm{0}
. For a weight function

K(\cdot)
define

\bm{V}_{i}(K)=K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i},\qquad r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\bm{U}_{i}=U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\}.

(2.109)

The corresponding quadratic-form
U
-statistic is

T_{n}(K)=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j}(K).

(2.110)

The choice
K(t)\equiv 1
yields the spatial-sign statistic; the choice

K(t)=t^{-1}
yields the inverse norm sign statistic.

Proposition 2.1

(Moments of the weighted-sign statistic)
.

Let

\bm{A}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)\bm{V}_{1}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\}
and

\bm{\eta}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)\}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)\}=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{K}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(2.111)

Under
H_{0}
and central symmetry,

\bm{\eta}_{K}=\bm{0}
and

\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{2}).

(2.112)

Proof.

The expectation identity follows from independence:

\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{2}\}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{V}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbb{E}(\bm{V}_{2})
. Under central symmetry,

\bm{V}_{1}(K)\overset{d}{=}-\bm{V}_{1}(K)
, so the mean is zero. The variance formula is
the standard variance expression for a degenerate quadratic-form
U
-statistic.
∎

Theorem 2.13

(Oracle weighted-sign statistic under the null)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7

and
2.8
hold and consider the one-sample problem

H_{0}:\bm{\theta}=\bm{0}
. Then

\frac{T_{n}(K)}{\sigma_{n,K}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(2.113)

where

\sigma_{n,K}^{2}=\frac{2\nu_{2,K}^{2}}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}).

(2.114)

Theorem 2.14

(Oracle weighted-sign statistic under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7

and
2.8
hold. Then, under the local alternatives described in
(
2.103
)–(
2.104
),

\frac{T_{n}(K)-c_{0,K}^{2}\,\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}{\sqrt{\sigma_{n,K}^{2}+\dfrac{4c_{0,K}^{2}}{np}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.115)

In particular, the local asymptotic power is governed by the signal-to-noise
ratio

\frac{c_{0,K}^{2}}{\nu_{2,K}}=\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}}.

(2.116)

These two theorems are the oracle core of the entire weighted-sign program.
Published procedures differ in two further steps: they replace the unknown

(\bm{\theta},\bm{D})
by leave-out versions of the weighted estimator, and they prove
that the feasible statistic has the same first-order expansion as its oracle
counterpart.

2.6.2
The Wang–Peng–Li nonparametric spatial-sign test

Before the scalar-invariant sign program matured,
136

proposed a one-sample high-dimensional nonparametric mean test based directly on
raw spatial signs. Their model may be written as

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{i},\qquad\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{Z}_{i},

(2.117)

where
\mathbb{E}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{i})=\bm{0}
and the radial and directional parts of

\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}
are separated under elliptical symmetry. For the null problem

H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
, define the raw spatial signs

\bm{Z}_{i}^{\mathrm{sgn}}=U(\bm{X}_{i}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.118)

and the quadratic-form
U
-statistic

T_{\mathrm{WPL}}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(\bm{Z}_{i}^{\mathrm{sgn}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{sgn}}.

(2.119)

Let

\bm{B}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right\},\qquad\bm{A}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\left(\bm{I}_{p}-\frac{\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)\right].

(2.120)

Under
H_{0}
, one has

\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{WPL}})=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2}).

(2.121)

Assumption 2.9

(Wang–Peng–Li conditions)
.

Assume:

(WPL1)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{4})=o\big[\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\big].

(2.122)

(WPL2)

\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{4}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})}\exp\left\{-\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{128p\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}\right\}=o(1).

(2.123)

(WPL3)

For local alternatives,

\exp\left\{-\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{256p\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}\right\}=o\!\left\{\min\left(\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})},\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})}\right)\right\}.

(2.124)

(WPL4)

\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})=o\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})\}.

(2.125)

(WPL5)

\left\lVert\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big(\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{-2}\big)=o\!\left\{\min\left(\frac{n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})}{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})},\frac{n^{-1/2}\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})}\right)\right\}.

(2.126)

(WPL6)

For some
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\left\lVert\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2\delta}\mathbb{E}\big(\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{-2-2\delta}\big)=o\big[\mathbb{E}^{2}\{\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\}\big].

(2.127)

Theorem 2.15

(Wang–Peng–Li spatial-sign test under the null)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.9
(WPL1)
–
(WPL2)
hold.
Then, under
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{WPL}}}{\sqrt{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.128)

A ratio-consistent estimator of
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})
can be constructed by the
cross-validation scheme of
136
, so the feasible statistic is
obtained by replacing
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})
with its estimator.

Theorem 2.16

(Wang–Peng–Li local alternative theory)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.9
holds. Then, under the local alternatives
specified by (
2.124
)–(
2.127
),

\frac{T_{\mathrm{WPL}}-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{2}\bm{\mu}\{1+o(1)\}}{\sqrt{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.129)

Consequently, the leading local power is controlled by the ratio

\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{2}\bm{\mu}/\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{B}^{2})
.

Remark 2.4

(Why the WPL statistic is not scalar invariant)
.

The key limitation of (
2.119
) is that it is built from raw signs

U(\bm{X}_{i})
rather than signs of diagonally standardized observations. If the data
are rescaled coordinatewise by a nonsingular diagonal matrix
\bm{D}_{0}
, then in
general

U(\bm{D}_{0}\bm{X}_{i})\neq\bm{D}_{0}U(\bm{X}_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad U(\bm{D}_{0}\bm{X}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}U(\bm{D}_{0}\bm{X}_{j})\neq U(\bm{X}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}U(\bm{X}_{j}).

(2.130)

Therefore the rejection decision can change under simple marginal rescalings.
This lack of scalar invariance is exactly what motivated the diagonally
standardized sign procedures developed later by
44
and in the
subsequent elliptical literature.

2.6.3
The high-dimensional spatial-sign test

44
proposed the first systematic one-sample spatial-sign test
for high-dimensional elliptical data. Their practical statistic uses diagonal
standardization and leave-two-out scale estimation. Let
\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}
denote
the diagonal estimator computed from the sample with observations
i
and
j

removed, and define

T_{\mathrm{SS}}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}^{-1/2}\bm{X}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}^{-1/2}\bm{X}_{j}).

(2.131)

Under the null the statistic behaves like an oracle sign
U
-statistic, but the
leave-two-out device eliminates the bias caused by estimating the diagonal scale.

Let

\sigma_{n}^{2}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}),

(2.132)

with
\bm{R}
as in (
2.4
).

Assumption 2.10

(One-sample spatial-sign test conditions)
.

Let
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
and

c_{0}=\mathbb{E}\{\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\}
. Assume:

(SS1)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})=o\{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})\}.

(2.133)

(SS2)

\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}=O(1),\qquad\log p=o(n).

(2.134)

(SS3)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})-p=o(n^{-1}p^{2}).

(2.135)

(SS4)

Under local alternatives,

\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}=O(c_{0}^{-2}\sigma_{n}).

(2.136)

Theorem 2.17

(One-sample spatial-sign test under the null)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.10
holds. Then, under
H_{0}
, as
n,p\to\infty
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS}}}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.137)

Theorem 2.18

(One-sample spatial-sign test under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.10
holds. Then

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS}}-c_{0}^{2}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}}{\sqrt{\sigma_{n}^{2}+\frac{4c_{0}^{2}}{np}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.138)

Hence the sign test detects dense alternatives of size roughly

\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}\asymp n^{-1/2}p^{-1/2}\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}.

Remark 2.5

(ARE relative to diagonal light-tail tests)
.

For spherical
t_{\nu}
distributions with
\nu>2
, the asymptotic relative
efficiency of the spatial-sign test relative to the Park–Ayyala diagonal test
is

\mathrm{ARE}(T_{\mathrm{SS}},T_{\mathrm{PA}})=\mathbb{E}^{2}(\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1})\mathbb{E}(\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})=\frac{2}{\nu-2}\left\{\frac{\Gamma((\nu+1)/2)}{\Gamma(\nu/2)}\right\}^{2}.

(2.139)

This exceeds one for heavy tails, illustrating why robust sign methods are not
merely safer but often more efficient.

2.6.4
Weighted spatial-sign tests and local optimality

The limitation of pure signs is that they discard all radial information.

41
therefore introduced a weighted class that keeps the
robust directional structure while reintroducing radial information in a
controlled way. The point is not to abandon robustness; the point is to retain
the stable directional geometry of signs while using the radius only through a
carefully chosen one-dimensional weight.

To explain the idea cleanly, consider the oracle weighted statistic
(
2.110
). Under the local alternative

H_{1n}:\ \bm{\theta}=n^{-1/2}\bm{\delta},

(2.140)

Theorem
2.14
shows that its local asymptotic power
is governed by

\frac{c_{0,K}^{2}}{\nu_{2,K}}=\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}}.

(2.141)

This criterion is the exact quantity one obtains after writing out the mean
shift under
H_{1n}
and dividing by the square root of the leading null
variance.

Proposition 2.2

(Why the inverse norm weight is optimal)
.

Over the class of square-integrable weight functions
K
, the criterion
(
2.141
) is maximized by

K(t)\propto t^{-1}.

(2.142)

Equivalently, the inverse norm weight is locally most powerful in the weighted
spatial-sign class.

Proof.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\{\mathbb{E}(K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1})\}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}),

(2.143)

with equality if and only if
K(r_{i})
is proportional to
r_{i}^{-1}
almost
surely. Dividing both sides by
\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}
yields

\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}}\leq\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}),

and equality is attained precisely when
K(t)\propto t^{-1}
.
∎

The proof is elementary, but it is also one of the conceptual centers of this
chapter: once the signal and noise are written in the unified sign-based
parametrization, the inverse norm weight emerges as the optimal way to recover
radial information under local elliptical alternatives.

2.6.5
The inverse norm sign test (INST)

The INST of
41
uses the choice
K(t)=t^{-1}
. In oracle
form the score vector becomes

\bm{V}_{i}^{\mathrm{IN}}=r_{i}^{-1}\bm{U}_{i}=\frac{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})}{r_{i}^{2}}.

(2.144)

The feasible statistic again uses the diagonally standardized leave-two-out
estimator. Let
\hat{r}_{ij,k}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}^{-1/2}\bm{X}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}
. Then

T_{\mathrm{INST}}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\hat{r}_{ij,i}^{-1}\hat{r}_{ij,j}^{-1}U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}^{-1/2}\bm{X}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{ij}^{-1/2}\bm{X}_{j}).

(2.145)

To unify notation with the previous subsection, note that for the inverse norm
weight,

\nu_{2,\mathrm{IN}}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}),\qquad c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}).

(2.146)

Theorem 2.19

(One-sample inverse norm sign test)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7

and
2.8
hold with
K(t)=t^{-1}
. Then, under
H_{0}
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{INST}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(2.147)

where

\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}^{2}=\frac{2\nu_{2,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}).

(2.148)

Under the local alternatives in Assumption
2.8
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{INST}}-c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}\,\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}^{2}+\dfrac{4c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}}{np}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.149)

Remark 2.6

(Efficiency of INST)
.

Because
K(t)=t^{-1}
maximizes the local asymptotic signal-to-noise ratio in
(
2.141
), the INST is asymptotically at least as efficient as
every other member of the weighted spatial-sign family under the local
alternatives considered above. In particular,

\mathrm{ARE}(T_{\mathrm{INST}},T_{\mathrm{SS}})=\frac{\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2})}{\mathbb{E}^{2}(r_{i}^{-1})}\geq 1,

(2.150)

and, relative to diagonal light-tail procedures such as Park–Ayyala,

\mathrm{ARE}(T_{\mathrm{INST}},T_{\mathrm{PA}})=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2})\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{2})\geq 1,

(2.151)

with strict inequality for genuinely heavy-tailed radial laws.

2.7
One-sample max-type and max-sum procedures

The previous section deals with dense alternatives. We now move to sparse
alternatives and the combination of sparse and dense regimes.

2.7.1
A spatial-sign max-type statistic

The max-type procedure of
86
is built
directly from the scaled spatial median. Let
(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})
solve
(
2.84
)–(
2.85
), let

\hat{r}_{i}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\right\rVert_{2}
, and define

\hat{c}_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{r}_{i}^{-1}.

(2.152)

The max statistic is

T_{\max}=n\,\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\hat{d}_{j}^{-1}\hat{\theta}_{j}^{2}\,\hat{c}_{0}^{2}\,p\,(1-n^{-1/2}),

(2.153)

or, equivalently,

T_{\max}=n\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\right\rVert_{\max}^{2}\,\hat{c}_{0}^{2}\,p\,(1-n^{-1/2}).

(2.154)

The normalization is chosen so that the oracle version of
T_{\max}
becomes the
maximum of approximately standard Gaussian coordinates after the Bahadur
expansion in Theorem
2.11
.

Assumption 2.11

(Explicit growth conditions for max-type statistics)
.

In addition to Assumption
2.7
, assume:

(MX1)

The Bahadur remainder in
(
2.11
) is negligible on the extreme-value
scale, namely

\sqrt{p}\Big[n^{-1/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big]=o\{(\log p)^{-1/2}\}.

(2.155)

(MX2)

The dimensional growth is strong enough for maxima:

\log p=o(n^{1/5}),\qquad\log n=o\!\big(p^{1/3\wedge\delta}\big),

(2.156)

where
\delta
is the row-sum exponent in
Assumption
2.7
(SD4)
.

Assumption 2.12

(Weak dependence for the Gumbel law)
.

There exists a constant
\rho\in(0,1)
such that

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq p}|\rho_{ij}|\leq\rho.

(2.157)

Moreover, there are sequences
\delta_{p}=o((\log p)^{-1})
and
\kappa_{p}\to 0

such that, with

B_{p,i}=\{j:\ |\rho_{ij}|\geq\delta_{p}\},\qquad C_{p}=\{i:\ |B_{p,i}|\geq p^{\kappa_{p}}\},

(2.158)

one has

\frac{|C_{p}|}{p}\to 0.

(2.159)

Assumption 2.13

(Additional conditions for asymptotic independence)
.

For some fixed constant
C>0
and some
\tau\in(0,1/4)
,

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\rho_{ij}^{2}\leq(\log p)^{C},

(2.160)

and

p^{-1/2}(\log p)^{C}\ll\lambda_{\min}(\bm{R})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\bm{R})\ll p(\log p)^{-1},\qquad\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{R})}{\lambda_{\min}(\bm{R})}=O(p^{\tau}).

(2.161)

Theorem 2.20

(Null law of the spatial-sign max statistic)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7
,

2.11
, and
2.12
hold. Then

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\}\longrightarrow\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}.

(2.162)

Therefore a level-
\alpha
test rejects whenever

T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p>q_{1-\alpha}
, where
q_{1-\alpha}
is the

(1-\alpha)
quantile of the limiting Gumbel law.

Theorem 2.21

(Consistency under sparse alternatives)
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
2.20
hold. If for some
sufficiently large constant
C_{e}
,

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\frac{|\theta_{j}|}{\sqrt{d_{j}}}\geq C_{e}n^{-1/2}\left\{\log p-2\log\log(1-\alpha)^{-1}\right\}^{1/2},

(2.163)

then

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p>q_{1-\alpha}\mid H_{1}\}\to 1.

(2.164)

Thus the sparse detection boundary is of order
\sqrt{\log p/n}
after marginal
standardization.

2.7.2
Inverse norm weighted max-type statistics

The same max construction can be carried out with the weighted estimator

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
. For
K(t)=t^{m}
, define

\hat{c}_{0,m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{r}_{i}^{m-1},\qquad\hat{\nu}_{2,m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{r}_{i}^{2m},

(2.165)

and

T_{\max}^{(m)}=n\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}\right\rVert_{\max}^{2}\,\hat{c}_{0,m}^{2}\hat{\nu}_{2,m}^{-1}p(1-n^{-1/2}).

(2.166)

Theorem 2.22

(Null law of the weighted max statistic)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7
,

2.8
,
2.11
, and
2.12

hold. Then

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}^{(m)}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\}\longrightarrow\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}.

(2.167)

The corresponding sparse detection boundary remains of order

\sqrt{\log p/n}
.

A key refinement is that under one-coordinate sparse alternatives, the inverse
norm choice
m=-1
is again locally optimal. More precisely,

\mathrm{ARE}_{\mathrm{IN}-\max,\max}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2})\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{2})\geq 1,

(2.168)

with strict inequality for genuinely heavy-tailed radial laws.

2.7.3
Asymptotic independence and max-sum combination

The real methodological breakthrough in the recent literature is that the robust
sum-type and robust max-type statistics are asymptotically independent, even
though they are computed from the same observations. This is exactly what makes
a valid and powerful max-sum combination possible.

For the spatial-sign case, let
T_{\mathrm{SUM}}
denote the one-sample sign test
of
44
and
T_{\max}
the max statistic in
(
2.154
).

Theorem 2.23

(Asymptotic independence: unweighted case)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.7
,

2.10
,
2.11
,
2.12
, and

2.13
hold. Then, under
H_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq x,\,T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq y\right\}\to\Phi(x)F(y),

(2.169)

where
F(y)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}\}
. The same factorization remains valid
under the local alternatives covered by
Assumption
2.10
(SS4)
.

Theorem 2.24

(Asymptotic independence: weighted case)
.

Let
T_{\mathrm{SUM}}^{(m)}
be the weighted sum-type statistic and

T_{\max}^{(m)}
the weighted max-type statistic. Suppose
Assumptions
2.7
,

2.8
,
2.11
,

2.12
, and
2.13
hold. Then

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}^{(m)}}{\sigma_{n,K}}\leq x,\,T_{\max}^{(m)}\leq y\right\}\to\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}^{(m)}}{\sigma_{n,K}}\leq x\right\}\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{\max}^{(m)}\leq y\right\}.

(2.170)

Under
H_{0}
this becomes

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}^{(m)}}{\sigma_{n,K}}\leq x,\,T_{\max}^{(m)}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq y\right\}\to\Phi(x)F(y).

(2.171)

The practical implication is immediate. Let

p_{\max}=1-F\{T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p\},\qquad p_{\mathrm{sum}}=1-\Phi\left(\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}}{\hat{\sigma}_{n}}\right).

(2.172)

Then one may combine them by the Cauchy combination rule:

p_{\mathrm{CC}}=1-G\Big[0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\max})\}+0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\mathrm{sum}})\}\Big],

(2.173)

where
G
is the standard Cauchy cdf. Reject when
p_{\mathrm{CC}}<\alpha
.
This yields a robust test that is competitive across the full spectrum from
dense to sparse alternatives.

2.8
Two-sample procedures under elliptical symmetry

We now revisit the two-sample problem in the same level of detail. The main
published procedures are the multivariate-sign test, the spatial-rank test, and
the inverse norm weighted sign test.

2.8.1
Two-sample multivariate-sign test

The JASA paper
50
is the starting point of the
high-dimensional two-sample elliptical program. For each sample
k\in\{1,2\}
,
let
(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\bm{D}}_{k})
solve the diagonal estimating equations

\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{k}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{kj}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\}=\bm{0},

(2.174)

and

\frac{p}{n_{k}}\operatorname{diag}\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{k}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{kj}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{kj}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\}^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigg\}=\bm{I}_{p}.

(2.175)

The iterative algorithm is exactly the one-sample algorithm applied to each
sample separately.

A naive pooled plug-in version would produce a non-negligible bias because the
locations are estimated from the same data used in the test statistic. The JASA
paper removes this bias by a leave-one-out construction. Let

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k,\ell}
and
\hat{\bm{D}}_{k,\ell}
denote the estimators obtained
from sample
k
with the
\ell
th observation removed. The statistic is

R_{n}=-\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{1,i}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2,j})\}^{\mathsf{T}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{2,j}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{2j}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1,i})\}.

(2.176)

To describe the limit theory, let

\bm{R}_{k}=\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2},\qquad c_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left\lVert\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{kj}-\bm{\theta}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\right\},

(2.177)

\bm{A}_{1}=c_{2}^{2}c_{1}^{-2}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2},\qquad\bm{A}_{2}=c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{-2}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2},

(2.178)

and

\bm{A}_{3}=\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2}.

(2.179)

Set

\sigma_{n}^{2}=\frac{2}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{1}^{2})+\frac{2}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{2}^{2})+\frac{4}{n_{1}n_{2}p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{3}).

(2.180)

Assumption 2.14

(Two-sample multivariate-sign conditions)
.

Let
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Assume:

(TS1)

n_{1}/n\to\kappa\in(0,1)
.

(TS2)

For all
i,j,\ell,h\in\{1,2,3\}
,

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{j}\bm{A}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{h})=o\!\left[\operatorname{tr}^{2}\!\left\{(\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{A}_{3})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{A}_{3})\right\}\right].

(2.181)

(TS3)

\frac{n^{-2}}{\sigma_{n}}=O(1),\qquad\log p=o(n).

(2.182)

(TS4)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{k}^{2})-p=o(n^{-1}p^{2}),\qquad k=1,2.

(2.183)

(TS5)

Under local alternatives with
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\theta}_{1}-\bm{\theta}_{2}
,

\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}=O(c_{1}^{-1}c_{2}^{-1}\sigma_{n}).

(2.184)

Theorem 2.25

(Two-sample sign test under the null)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.14
holds. Then, under

H_{0}:\bm{\theta}_{1}=\bm{\theta}_{2}
,

\frac{R_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.185)

Under local alternatives the mean is no longer zero. Let

\delta_{n}=c_{1}c_{2}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}.

(2.186)

The corresponding asymptotic variance becomes

\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{2}=\sigma_{n}^{2}+\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{n_{1}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{R}_{1}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}+\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{n_{2}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{R}_{2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}.

(2.187)

Theorem 2.26

(Two-sample sign test under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.14
holds. Then

\frac{R_{n}-\delta_{n}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.188)

The leading signal is therefore the scalar-invariant quadratic form

\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}
.

2.8.2
A simpler bias-corrected spatial-sign test

The leave-one-out construction in
50
is theoretically
clean but computationally expensive, because every term in the statistic uses
sample-specific leave-out location and scale estimators. The note of

81
replaces the full leave-one-out centering
by a simpler plug-in correction while keeping the same first-order efficiency.
Let
(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\bm{D}}_{k})
,
k=1,2
, be the full-sample diagonal
estimators obtained from
(
2.174
)–(
2.175
). The simplified statistic
is

T_{\mathrm{SST}}=-\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{1}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2})\}^{\mathsf{T}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{2}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{2j}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})\}.

(2.189)

With

\bm{R}_{k}=\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2},\qquad c_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left\lVert\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{kj}-\bm{\theta}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\right\},

(2.190)

let

\bm{D}_{n}=\bm{D}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{1/2},

(2.191)

\bm{A}_{1}=c_{2}c_{1}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2},\qquad\bm{A}_{2}=c_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2},

(2.192)

\bm{A}_{3}=\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2},

(2.193)

and

\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}^{2}=\frac{2}{n_{1}^{2}p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{1}^{2})+\frac{2}{n_{2}^{2}p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{2}^{2})+\frac{4}{n_{1}n_{2}p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{3}).

(2.194)

Because the full-sample estimators create a non-negligible mean shift, the null
center is

\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}=\frac{1}{n_{1}p}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{1})+\frac{1}{n_{2}p}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{2}).

(2.195)

Assumption 2.15

(Conditions for the simpler two-sample sign statistic)
.

Assume:

(SST1)

n_{1}/(n_{1}+n_{2})\to\kappa\in(0,1)
.

(SST2)

n^{-2}=O(\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}).

(2.196)

(SST3)

For all
i,j,\ell,h\in\{1,2,3\}
,

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{j}\bm{A}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{h})=o\!\left[\operatorname{tr}^{2}\!\left\{(\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{A}_{3})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{A}_{3})\right\}\right].

(2.197)

(SST4)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{k}^{2})-p=o(n^{-1}p^{2}),\qquad k=1,2.

(2.198)

Theorem 2.27

(Simpler two-sample sign statistic under the null)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.15
holds. Then, under

H_{0}:\bm{\theta}_{1}=\bm{\theta}_{2}
,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SST}}-\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.199)

Moreover, the plug-in estimators proposed in

81
are ratio consistent, so the feasible
studentized statistic is asymptotically standard normal.

Under local alternatives with
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\theta}_{1}-\bm{\theta}_{2}
, define

\delta_{\mathrm{SST},n}=c_{1}c_{2}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}

(2.200)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SST},n}^{2}={}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}^{2}+\frac{4c_{2}^{2}}{n_{1}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}

(2.201)

\displaystyle+\frac{4c_{1}^{2}}{n_{2}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}.

(2.202)

Theorem 2.28

(Simpler two-sample sign statistic under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.15
holds. Then

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SST}}-\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}-\delta_{\mathrm{SST},n}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SST},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.203)

Hence the simplified statistic achieves the same first-order local power as the
original leave-one-out test while reducing the computational burden from order

O(n^{3}p)
to order
O(n^{2}p)
.

2.8.3
Two-sample spatial-rank test

The sign test above requires estimating the two locations. A different strategy,
proposed in
45
, is to construct the test from
pairwise cross-sample differences so that explicit location estimation is
avoided.

Assume first that the two populations share a common scatter matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
.
Let
\hat{\bm{D}}_{1,n_{1}}
and
\hat{\bm{D}}_{2,n_{2}}
be diagonal scale estimators and
define the pooled diagonal scale

\hat{\bm{D}}_{n}=\frac{n_{1}}{n}\hat{\bm{D}}_{1,n_{1}}+\frac{n_{2}}{n}\hat{\bm{D}}_{2,n_{2}},\qquad n=n_{1}+n_{2}.

(2.204)

The leave-two-out statistic is

T_{n}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{s\neq l}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{n(i,j,s,l)}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bm{X}_{2s})\}^{\mathsf{T}}U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{n(i,j,s,l)}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1j}-\bm{X}_{2l})\},

(2.205)

where

\hat{\bm{D}}_{n(i,j,s,l)}=\frac{n_{1}}{n}\hat{\bm{D}}_{1,n_{1}(i,j)}+\frac{n_{2}}{n}\hat{\bm{D}}_{2,n_{2}(s,l)}.

(2.206)

Let
\bm{D}_{n}
be the population counterpart and define

\bm{R}_{n}=\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}.

(2.207)

The variance scale is

\sigma_{n}^{2}=\left\{\frac{1}{2n_{1}(n_{1}-1)p^{2}}+\frac{1}{2n_{2}(n_{2}-1)p^{2}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}p^{2}}\right\}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{n}^{2}).

(2.208)

Assumption 2.16

(Two-sample spatial-rank conditions)
.

Assume:

(TR1)

The two samples are elliptical with a common scatter matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
and

n_{1}/n\to\kappa\in(0,1)
.

(TR2)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{n}^{4})=o\{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}_{n}^{2})\}.

(2.209)

(TR3)

\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{n}^{2})}=O(1),\qquad\log p=o(n),\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{n}^{2})-p=o(n^{-1}p^{2}).

(2.210)

(TR4)

Let

c_{0,n}=\mathbb{E}\big\{\left\lVert\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bm{X}_{2j})\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\big\}.

(2.211)

Under local alternatives,

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})=O(c_{0,n}^{-2}\sigma_{n}),

(2.212)

and

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}\bm{R}_{n}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})=o(np\,c_{0,n}^{-2}\sigma_{n}).

(2.213)

Theorem 2.29

(Two-sample spatial-rank test under the null)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.16
holds. Then

\frac{T_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)

(2.214)

under
H_{0}:\bm{\mu}_{1}=\bm{\mu}_{2}
.

Theorem 2.30

(Two-sample spatial-rank test under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.16
holds. Then

\frac{T_{n}-c_{0,n}^{2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.215)

Therefore the spatial-rank test is a dense-alternative procedure whose leading
signal is again the scalar-invariant quadratic form in the diagonally
standardized coordinates.

Because spatial ranks automatically difference out the common location, this
procedure remains valid in regimes where direct location estimation would be
awkward. Under heavy tails it also enjoys a clear efficiency advantage over
purely moment-based quadratic-form tests.

2.8.4
Two-sample inverse norm weighted signs and tINST

The two-sample weighted-sign program parallels the one-sample theory.

68
first define a general weighted class
for the two-sample problem and then show that the inverse norm weight is again
locally optimal.

Let
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\theta}_{1}-\bm{\theta}_{2}
and consider the oracle weighted vectors

\bm{V}_{1i}(K)=K(r_{1i})U\{\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bm{\theta}_{1})\},\qquad\bm{V}_{2j}(K)=K(r_{2j})U\{\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{2j}-\bm{\theta}_{2})\},

(2.216)

where

r_{ki}=\left\lVert\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{ki}-\bm{\theta}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}
.
The oracle weighted quadratic form is

T_{n_{1},n_{2}}(K)=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{V}_{1i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{1j}(K)+\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{V}_{2i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{2j}(K)-\frac{2}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{V}_{1i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{2j}(K).

(2.217)

The feasible statistic replaces
(\bm{\theta}_{k},\bm{D}_{k})
by the leave-out
scalar-invariant estimators described in the JASA paper.

Under local alternatives, the leading signal again depends on

\frac{\{\mathbb{E}(K(r)r^{-1})\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}},

(2.218)

so the same Cauchy–Schwarz argument shows that
K(t)=t^{-1}
is optimal.

Assumption 2.17

(Two-sample weighted-sign conditions)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.14
holds and, in addition, the weight
function
K
satisfies

0<\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{ki})\}<\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}\{K^{4}(r_{ki})\}=O\!\left(\mathbb{E}^{2}\{K^{2}(r_{ki})\}\right),\qquad k=1,2.

(2.219)

Theorem 2.31

(Local optimality of tINST)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.17
holds. Within the weighted
spatial-sign class, the locally most powerful choice is

K(t)=t^{-1}.

(2.220)

The resulting two-sample inverse norm sign test (tINST) is therefore
asymptotically at least as powerful as any other weighted member of the class
under the local elliptical alternatives governed by
(
2.218
).

The one-sample and two-sample theories therefore fit into the same template:
first diagonal standardization, then weighted signs, and finally the inverse
norm choice as the optimal weight.

2.9
Adaptive rank-based location tests and structured dependence

The weighted-sign theory above addresses robustness and radial efficiency. A
separate but complementary question is adaptivity over dense and sparse signal
regimes. This is the topic of
151
and

88
.

2.9.1
Adaptive rank-based tests

The starting point of
151
is to construct marginal
rank-based scores
W_{j}
and then aggregate them over a range of
L_{q}
norms. In
a stylized form one considers

T_{q}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}|W_{j}|^{q},\qquad q\in\mathcal{Q},

(2.221)

where
\mathcal{Q}
contains both small and large values of
q
together with the
extreme case

T_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|W_{j}|.

(2.222)

Small
q
’s favor dense alternatives; large
q
’s favor sparse alternatives. The
adaptive test combines the corresponding
p
-values, either by a minimum

p
-value rule or by a Cauchy-type combination. The resulting procedure is fully
rank based and therefore robust under heavy tails.

2.9.2
Structured correlations

When the dependence structure has additional information, such as a linear or
banded structure in the precision matrix, one can sharpen the coordinatewise
statistics by incorporating that structure. This is the idea behind

88
. The general strategy is the same as in
the Gaussian adaptive literature: construct a dense-alternative statistic, a
sparse-alternative statistic, establish their null laws, and then combine them.
The difference is that here the entire construction is embedded in the robust
elliptical setting.

2.10
Strong correlation, normal-reference calibration, and spatial-sign testing

The preceding sections mostly follow the now standard high-dimensional route: after a
proper centering and bias correction, one proves that a quadratic-form or
U
-statistic
converges to a Gaussian law, an extreme-value law, or a combination of the two. That
route is appropriate only in a
weak-correlation
regime. In many problems this regime
can be summarized spectrally by the requirement

\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\longrightarrow 0,

(2.223)

or by an equivalent delocalization condition on the leading eigenvalues. Once
(
2.223
) fails, a purely Gaussian calibration can be wrong,
even under the null. This issue has become increasingly important in modern applications,
where strong factor-like dependence, compound symmetry, and spiked structures are common.
The strongest recent lines of work in this direction are the normal-reference procedures of

149
;
150
, the approximate-randomization procedure of

138
, and the spatial-sign correction developed in

155
.

2.10.1
Why Gaussian calibration fails under strong correlation

To fix ideas, consider first the one-sample mean problem under a Gaussian model,

\bm{X}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.224)

and the quadratic statistic

Q_{n}=n\left\lVert\bar{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\bar{\bm{X}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}.

(2.225)

If

\bm{\Sigma}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0,

(2.226)

then under
H_{0}
we have the exact representation

Q_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2},\qquad Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N(0,1).

(2.227)

Thus the centered statistic is a weighted chi-square mixture, not a sum of approximately
independent coordinates in the original basis. The usual Gaussian limit appears only when
no single eigen-direction contributes nonnegligibly after standardization.

Assumption 2.18

(Leading-eigenvalue regime)
.

Assume that for some fixed integer
r\geq 1
,

\omega_{j}=\lim_{n,p\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\in[0,1],\qquad j=1,\ldots,r,

(2.228)

exist, and that the remaining spectrum satisfies

\frac{\max_{j>r}\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{k>r}\lambda_{k}^{2}}\longrightarrow 0.

(2.229)

In addition,

\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{j}^{2}\leq 1.

(2.230)

Theorem 2.32

(Mixed Gaussian–chi-square limit)
.

Under (
2.227
) and Assumption
2.18
,

\frac{Q_{n}-\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{j}\,\frac{Z_{j}^{2}-1}{\sqrt{2}}+\Bigl(1-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{j}^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}Z_{0},

(2.231)

where
Z_{0}\sim N(0,1)
is independent of
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
.

Proof.

From (
2.227
),

Q_{n}-\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}(Z_{j}^{2}-1).

(2.232)

Split the right-hand side into the leading
r
terms and the remainder:

\frac{Q_{n}-\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}=A_{n,p}+B_{n,p},

(2.233)

where

\displaystyle A_{n,p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}(Z_{j}^{2}-1),

(2.234)

\displaystyle B_{n,p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}(Z_{j}^{2}-1).

(2.235)

By (
2.228
),

A_{n,p}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{j}\,\frac{Z_{j}^{2}-1}{\sqrt{2}}.

(2.236)

For the remainder, define

s_{n,p}^{2}=2\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j}^{2},\qquad\widetilde{B}_{n,p}=\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{s_{n,p}}(Z_{j}^{2}-1).

(2.237)

Then
\operatorname{Var}(\widetilde{B}_{n,p})=1
, and

\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left|\frac{\lambda_{j}}{s_{n,p}}(Z_{j}^{2}-1)\right|^{4}=O\!\left(\frac{\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j}^{4}}{(\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j}^{2})^{2}}\right)\leq O\!\left(\frac{\max_{j>r}\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{k>r}\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)\longrightarrow 0

(2.238)

by (
2.229
). Hence the Lyapunov central limit theorem gives

\widetilde{B}_{n,p}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)
. Since

B_{n,p}=\frac{s_{n,p}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\,\widetilde{B}_{n,p}=\left(1-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})}\right)^{1/2}\widetilde{B}_{n,p},

(2.239)

we obtain

B_{n,p}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\Bigl(1-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{j}^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}Z_{0}.

(2.240)

The leading and remainder parts involve disjoint Gaussian coordinates and are therefore
independent. Combining (
2.236
) and
(
2.240
) proves
(
2.231
).
∎

Theorem
2.32
explains the basic obstruction: once a few spiked
eigenvalues persist after normalization, a fixed Gaussian calibration is no longer exact.
The same phenomenon lies behind a large family of recent
normal-reference
and

randomization-based
procedures.

2.10.2
The normal-reference line of Jin-Ting Zhang and collaborators

The papers of
149
and
150
start from the
observation that the null law of a centered
L_{2}
-type statistic is usually much closer to a
chi-square-type mixture than to a standard normal variable when the covariance structure is
strongly correlated. For the one-sample problem, the starting statistic is again
(
2.225
). Writing

a_{1}=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}),\qquad a_{2}=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}),\qquad a_{3}=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}^{3}),

(2.241)

we see from (
2.227
) that

\mathbb{E}(Q_{n})=a_{1},\qquad\operatorname{Var}(Q_{n})=2a_{2},\qquad\operatorname{cum}_{3}(Q_{n})=8a_{3}.

(2.242)

where
\kappa_{3}(Q_{n})
denotes the third cumulant. The normal-reference idea is to replace the
standard normal approximation by a centered chi-square-type reference

R_{n}=a\chi_{\nu}^{2}+b,

(2.243)

where
(a,\nu,b)
are chosen so that the first few cumulants of
R_{n}
match those of the target
quadratic-form statistic. In
149
, this matching is carried out at the level
of a three-cumulant chi-square approximation. The resulting procedure is especially useful in
regimes where the limiting law is non-Gaussian but still dominated by a moderate number of
leading eigen-directions.

For the two-sample Behrens–Fisher problem,
150
pursue the same
principle in a scale-invariant setting. Let

\bm{\Psi}=\frac{\bm{\Sigma}_{1}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\bm{\Sigma}_{2}}{n_{2}},

(2.244)

and consider the centered
L_{2}
-type two-sample statistic

Q_{BF}=\left\lVert\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}(Q_{BF})=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Psi}).

(2.245)

The null fluctuation is again determined by the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Psi}
, and the weak-correlation
normal approximation can fail for exactly the same reason as in the one-sample case.

150
therefore calibrate the test by an
F
-type normal-reference law,
constructed from a ratio of chi-square-type mixtures with consistently estimated degrees of
freedom. From the methodological standpoint of this book, the main message is that these
normal-reference procedures retain the classical
L_{2}
statistic but replace the fragile Gaussian
reference by a reference family that still behaves correctly under strong dependence.

2.10.3
Approximate randomization under arbitrary covariances

The Biometrika paper of
138
takes a different route. Instead of approximating
the non-Gaussian limit analytically, it builds a conditional reference distribution by
randomization. The starting point is the Chen–Qin two-sample statistic

T_{CQ}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n_{1}}\bm{X}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}_{j}+\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n_{2}}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Y}_{j}-\frac{2}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{X}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Y}_{j}.

(2.246)

Under weak conditions, the null law of (
2.246
) may have many possible
subsequential limits, depending on the covariance eigenstructure. This is precisely the situation
in which a single closed-form approximation is hard to justify uniformly.

To overcome this difficulty,
138
construct an approximate-randomization
reference by reassigning the centered observations to pseudo-groups of sizes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
and
recomputing the same Chen–Qin statistic. Denote the randomized version by

T_{CQ}^{\pi}
, where
\pi
indexes the random reassignment. The test then rejects for large values
of
T_{CQ}
relative to the conditional distribution of
T_{CQ}^{\pi}
. The core theoretical result
shows that, under the null, the randomization distribution automatically adapts to all possible
subsequential limits of the original statistic. Therefore the procedure remains asymptotically exact
without imposing any weak-correlation condition such as (
2.223
).
This is the main reason the Wang–Xu paper has become a central reference in the strong-correlation
literature.

2.10.4
The Zhao–Feng note on the one-sample spatial-sign test

The strong-correlation issue also arises for robust procedures. Earlier in this chapter, in the discussion surrounding Theorem
2.15

and Remark
2.4
, we reviewed the
one-sample spatial-sign test of
136
. That paper established asymptotic
normality under structural conditions on the scatter matrix. The note of

155
revisits the same statistic and shows that under strong
dependence the null law is generally not Gaussian.

Let
\bm{U}_{i}=U(\bm{X}_{i})
and define

S_{n}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{j}.

(2.247)

Using
\left\lVert\bm{U}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=1
, one has the identity

S_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}\right\|^{2}-\frac{n}{2}.

(2.248)

Let

\bm{\Sigma}_{U}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}),\qquad\tau=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U}^{2}),\qquad\sigma_{n}^{2}=\binom{n}{2}\tau,\qquad T_{n}=\frac{S_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}.

(2.249)

The first key theorem in
155
compares
T_{n}
with a Gaussian
counterpart. Let
\bm{G}_{1},\ldots,\bm{G}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{U})
and define

S_{n}^{(G)}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\bm{G}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{G}_{j},\qquad T_{n}^{(G)}=\frac{S_{n}^{(G)}}{\sigma_{n}}.

(2.250)

Also let

\kappa_{4}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{2})^{4}}{\mathbb{E}^{2}(\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{2})^{2}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{2})^{4}}{\tau^{2}}.

(2.251)

Then the paper proves the following comparison bound.

Theorem 2.33

(Gaussian comparison for the spatial-sign statistic)
.

Assume
\lx@paragraphsign(\bm{X}_{1}=\bm{0})=0
and
\tau=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U}^{2})>0
. Then

\bigl\|\mathcal{L}(T_{n})-\mathcal{L}(T_{n}^{(G)})\bigr\|_{3}\leq C\kappa_{4}^{3/4}n^{-1/2},

(2.252)

for a universal constant
C>0
. In particular, if
