\kappa_{4}=o(n^{2/3}),

(2.253)

then

\bigl\|\mathcal{L}(T_{n})-\mathcal{L}(T_{n}^{(G)})\bigr\|_{3}\to 0.

(2.254)

The second step is to identify the limit law of the Gaussian analogue. Let
\xi\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})

and define the Gaussian quadratic form

Q_{p}=\frac{\xi^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{U}\xi-\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U}^{2})\}^{1/2}}.

(2.255)

The note proves that

T_{n}^{(G)}=Q_{p}+o_{p}(1),

(2.256)

and therefore the null law of
T_{n}
is determined by the spectral structure of

\bm{\Sigma}_{U}
rather than by a universal normal limit. Writing the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{U}

as
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0
and

\alpha_{i}=\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\tau^{1/2}},\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1,

(2.257)

the exact subsequential limit is the following.

Corollary 2.1

(Mixed limit for the spatial-sign statistic)
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
2.33
and, along a
subsequence,
\alpha_{i}\to\alpha_{i}^{\ast}
for each fixed
i
. Then

T_{n}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\Bigl(1-\sum_{i\geq 1}(\alpha_{i}^{\ast})^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}Z_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}^{\ast}(Z_{i}^{2}-1),

(2.258)

where
Z_{0},Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots
are independent
N(0,1)
variables. In particular, if

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\alpha_{i}\to 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{U}^{2})}\to 0,

(2.259)

then
T_{n}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)
.

Corollary
2.1
is the precise answer to the strong-correlation
question for the one-sample spatial-sign test: the null law is Gaussian only in the delocalized
regime (
2.259
); otherwise it contains non-negligible chi-square
components determined by the leading eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{U}
.

The same note also proposes a practical calibration via wild bootstrap. Let
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
be
the sample spatial median,
\widehat{\bm{U}}_{i}=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})
, and let

e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher variables independent of the data. The bootstrap statistic is

T_{R}^{\ast}=\frac{1}{\tau^{1/2}\sqrt{\binom{n}{2}}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}e_{i}e_{j}\,\widehat{\bm{U}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{j}.

(2.260)

A Gaussian-multiplier version
T_{N}^{\ast}
is defined similarly by replacing the Rademacher
multipliers
e_{i}
with i.i.d.
N(0,1)
multipliers. Under mild moment conditions on the radial
variable, a bounded-spectrum assumption on the spatial-sign scatter, and the growth condition

n\tau\to\infty
, the conditional law of the bootstrap statistic consistently estimates the law of the
mixed limit. This is the methodological contribution that makes the note practically important:
it shows how to calibrate the spatial-sign test when neither a Gaussian approximation nor a fixed
chi-square approximation is uniformly reliable.

From the perspective of this chapter, the strong-correlation literature does not replace the earlier
sections on spatial signs and weighted signs. Rather, it clarifies when those earlier Gaussian or
chi-square calibrations are trustworthy and when they must be replaced by a more adaptive
reference law. This point will reappear in later chapters whenever sum-type, max-type, or
sign-based statistics are used under pronounced dependence.

Appendix to Chapter 2: Detailed Proofs

This appendix collects detailed proofs of all theorem-level results stated in
Chapter 2. The arguments are written in the unified notation of the book. When a
proof follows the same pattern as an earlier one, we still spell out the main
steps and explicitly indicate where the earlier proof is being reused. The
reader is assumed to know only the basic multivariate central limit theorem,
Slutsky’s theorem, continuous mapping, and standard facts about Gaussian,
Wishart, and
U
-statistics.

A. Fixed-
p
classical results

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Under
H_{0}
, the sample mean has the Gaussian law

\sqrt{n}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}).

At the same time, the centered sample covariance matrix satisfies

(n-1)\bm{S}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\sim W_{p}(n-1,\bm{\Sigma}),

and, for the Gaussian model,
\bar{\bm{X}}
and
\bm{S}_{n}
are independent. Define

\bm{Z}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0}),\qquad\bm{W}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(n-1)\bm{S}_{n}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}.

Then
\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
,

\bm{W}\sim W_{p}(n-1,\bm{I}_{p})
, and
\bm{Z}\perp\!\!\!\perp\bm{W}
. Therefore

T_{H}^{2}=n(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{n}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})=(n-1)\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Z}.

A standard property of Gaussian quadratic forms and inverse Wishart matrices
states that

\frac{n-p}{p(n-1)}(n-1)\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Z}\sim F_{p,n-p},

which is exactly (
2.9
).

For the fixed-
p
asymptotic statement, note that

\bm{S}_{n}\to\bm{\Sigma}
in probability and hence

\bm{S}_{n}^{-1}\to\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
in probability. Therefore

T_{H}^{2}=n(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{0})+o_{p}(1).

The leading term is the squared norm of a
p
-variate standard Gaussian vector,
hence has a
\chi_{p}^{2}
distribution. This proves
(
2.10
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Under the common-covariance Gaussian model,

\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}\sim N_{p}\!\left(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2},\,\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\right)\bm{\Sigma}\right).

Under
H_{0}
, this becomes

\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}).

The pooled covariance satisfies

(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)\bm{S}_{p}\sim W_{p}(n_{1}+n_{2}-2,\bm{\Sigma}),

and is independent of
\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}
. Standardizing as in the
one-sample proof gives

T_{H,2}^{2}=(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Z},

with
\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
and

\bm{W}\sim W_{p}(n_{1}+n_{2}-2,\bm{I}_{p})
independent. The standard
F
-distribution
identity then yields (
2.13
).

For local alternatives

\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}=(n_{1}^{-1}+n_{2}^{-1})^{1/2}\bm{\delta}
,
the standardized mean difference has mean
\bm{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\bm{\delta}
, so the
quadratic form converges to a noncentral chi-square law with noncentrality
parameter
\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Let

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}),\qquad\Psi(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\theta})\}.

By definition of the sample spatial median,

\Psi_{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{0}
, and by definition of the population
spatial median,

\Psi(\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{0}
. Since
p
is fixed and the derivative matrix

\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}
in (
2.17
) is nonsingular, the mean
value expansion yields

\bm{0}=\Psi_{n}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})+\bm{\rho}_{n},

where
\bm{\rho}_{n}=o_{p}(\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{2})
.
Because
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, we have

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{\rho}_{n}=o_{p}(1)
. Thus

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})+o_{p}(1),

which proves (
2.18
). The asymptotic
normality in (
2.19
) follows from the ordinary
multivariate central limit theorem applied to the i.i.d. vectors

U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{SM}})
.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.4
.

For the sign statistic, write

\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0}).

Under central symmetry about
\bm{\mu}_{0}
, the sign vector is odd around the center,
so
\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\}=\bm{0}
. By the multivariate central limit theorem,

\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}}\Rightarrow N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{B}_{U}),

where
\bm{B}_{U}=\operatorname{Var}\{U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{0})\}
. Since

\hat{\bm{B}}_{U}\to\bm{B}_{U}
in probability,

Q_{\mathrm{sign}}=(\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{B}}_{U}^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}})=(\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{B}_{U}^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{U}})+o_{p}(1),

and the limiting law is
\chi_{p}^{2}
.

For the signed-rank statistic, the rank vectors
\bm{R}_{i}
form an average of a
symmetric kernel. Under central symmetry,
\mathbb{E}(\bm{R}_{i})=\bm{0}
. The Hoeffding
decomposition shows that the linear part dominates in fixed dimension, so

\sqrt{n}\,\bar{\bm{R}}
is asymptotically Gaussian with covariance consistently
estimated by
\hat{\bm{B}}_{R}
. Studentization yields

Q_{\mathrm{SR}}\Rightarrow\chi_{p}^{2}
.
∎

B. A benchmark high-dimensional Gaussian proof

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Expand the observations as

\bm{X}_{ki}=\bm{\mu}_{k}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{ki}
with

\mathbb{E}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{ki})=\bm{0}
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{ki})=\bm{\Sigma}_{k}
.
Then

T_{\mathrm{CQ}}=\left\lVert\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+L_{n}+Q_{n},

where
L_{n}
is the linear term in the centered variables and
Q_{n}
is the
degenerate quadratic part. Under
H_{0}
, the deterministic signal term vanishes,
and the linear term is also zero because its coefficient is proportional to

\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}
. Hence only the centered quadratic form
Q_{n}
remains.

Write
Q_{n}
as a sum of martingale differences by revealing the observations one
at a time. The conditional variance of that martingale has leading term

\frac{2}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{2})+\frac{2}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{2})+\frac{4}{n_{1}n_{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}).

Assumption
2.1
(CQ2)
guarantees that all fourth-order
cumulant terms are negligible relative to the square of this leading variance,
which is exactly the Lyapunov-type condition needed for the martingale central
limit theorem. Therefore

\frac{T_{\mathrm{CQ}}}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{CQ}})}}\Rightarrow N(0,1).

Under the local alternatives in (
2.32
), the deterministic mean
shift is
\left\lVert\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, while the linear perturbation remains
smaller than the square root of the quadratic variance. Hence the same variance
normalization applies and the power is governed by the ratio of the Euclidean
signal to the trace-based noise level.
∎

B1. Additional proofs for Gaussian/light-tail benchmarks

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Under the Gaussian model, write

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}}=(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})-p.

(2.261)

Let

q_{0,n}=(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})-p.

(2.262)

By the diagonal central limit theorem and the ratio consistency of the sample
diagonal variance estimator under Assumption
2.2
,

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}}-q_{0,n}=o_{p}\{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})\}.

(2.263)

Now set

\bm{Y}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2}).

(2.264)

Under
H_{0}
,
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y})=\bm{0}
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{Y})=\bm{R}
. Hence

q_{0,n}=\bm{Y}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Y}-p.

(2.265)

Since
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Y})=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R})=p
and

\operatorname{Var}(\bm{Y}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Y})=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}),

(2.266)

Assumption
2.2
(SKK2)
implies that the quadratic form

q_{0,n}
satisfies a central limit theorem:

\frac{q_{0,n}}{\sqrt{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.267)

Because the variance estimator in (
2.40
) is ratio consistent,

\frac{\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat{q}_{\mathrm{SKK}})}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1.

(2.268)

Combining (
2.263
),
(
2.267
), and
(
2.268
) yields
(
2.46
). Under the local alternatives in
(
2.45
), the mean shift of
q_{0,n}
equals

(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})
, while the linear perturbation of
the variance is negligible relative to
\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})
; therefore the standard
noncentral Gaussian power formula yields (
2.47
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.7
.

The statistic (
2.50
) may be written as

T_{\mathrm{BF}}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{2}A_{k}.

(2.269)

Insert and subtract the population weights
\lambda_{k}^{2}
:

T_{\mathrm{BF}}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}^{2}A_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}(\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{2}-\lambda_{k}^{2})A_{k}=:T_{0,n}+R_{n}.

(2.270)

By the leave-in bias calculation in
48
,

\mathbb{E}(T_{0,n})=\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}

(2.271)

with
\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}
given by
(
2.53
)–(
2.57
). Moreover,

\operatorname{Var}(T_{0,n})=\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(2.272)

The trace condition (
2.58
) and the moment condition
(
2.61
) imply that the martingale array generated by the
Hoeffding decomposition of
T_{0,n}
satisfies the Lyapunov condition, so

\frac{T_{0,n}-\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.273)

It remains to control the plug-in remainder
R_{n}
. By a Taylor expansion of

x\mapsto x^{-1}
around the population diagonal variances,

\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{2}-\lambda_{k}^{2}=-\frac{(\hat{\sigma}_{1k}^{2}-\sigma_{1k}^{2})+\gamma(\hat{\sigma}_{2k}^{2}-\sigma_{2k}^{2})}{(\sigma_{1k}^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2k}^{2})^{2}}+r_{k,n},

(2.274)

where
\sum_{k=1}^{p}r_{k,n}A_{k}=o_{p}(\sigma_{\mathrm{BF},n})
under
(
2.59
). The leading linear term in
(
2.274
) is exactly what generates the bias terms
collected in
\mu_{\mathrm{BF},n}
. Therefore, after subtracting the plug-in
estimator
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{BF},n}
, the feasible statistic has the same
first-order limit as
T_{0,n}
. This proves
(
2.64
)–(
2.65
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Under the common-covariance Gaussian model,

\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{1}-\bar{\bm{X}}_{2})\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}).

(2.275)

Multiplying by
\bm{\Omega}
yields

\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}}\,\bar{\bm{Z}}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Omega}),

(2.276)

so the normalized coordinates

G_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}}\frac{\bar{Z}_{j}}{\sqrt{\omega_{jj}}},\qquad j=1,\ldots,p,

(2.277)

form a centered Gaussian vector with unit marginal variances and correlation
matrix

\operatorname{Corr}(G_{i},G_{j})=\frac{\omega_{ij}}{\sqrt{\omega_{ii}\omega_{jj}}}.

(2.278)

By definition,

M_{\bm{\Omega}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}.

(2.279)

Assumption
2.4
(CLX2)
ensures that no pair of
coordinates is asymptotically perfectly correlated, and
Assumption
2.4
(CLX3)
guarantees that strongly
correlated clusters are sparse enough for the classical extreme-value comparison
argument to go through. Therefore, the Gaussian maximum has the same limit as
in the independent case:

\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\right\}\to\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}.

(2.280)

Since (
2.279
) equals the oracle statistic exactly,
(
2.280
) is identical to
(
2.70
). This proves the theorem.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.9
.

Fix a finite set
\Gamma_{0}=\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\}
. For each

\gamma_{r}\in\Gamma_{0}
, write

S_{r}=\frac{T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma_{r})-\mu_{\gamma_{r}}}{\sigma_{\gamma_{r}}}.

(2.281)

To prove joint asymptotic normality, it suffices by the Cramér–Wold device to
consider, for arbitrary fixed coefficients
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
,

L=\sum_{r=1}^{m}a_{r}S_{r}.

(2.282)

Expanding each
T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma_{r})
into sums of weakly dependent
coordinate contributions gives

L=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi_{j,n},

(2.283)

where the summands
\xi_{j,n}
are centered and satisfy a triangular-array CLT
under Assumption
2.5
. In the notation of

143
, the covariance matrix of the vector

(T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma_{r}))_{r=1}^{m}
admits a stable deterministic limit, and
hence

L\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N\left(0,\sum_{r,s=1}^{m}a_{r}a_{s}\rho_{rs}\right),

(2.284)

where
\rho_{rs}
is the
(r,s)
entry of the limiting correlation matrix

\bm{R}_{\Gamma_{0}}
. Since this holds for every coefficient vector, the claimed
multivariate normal limit in (
2.75
) follows.

For the adaptive statistic, let
\hat{P}_{\gamma}
be a valid resampling-based

p
-value for
T_{\mathrm{SPU}}(\gamma)
. Because the collection
\Gamma
is
finite, the minimum in (
2.76
) is measurable and its null law is
consistently approximated by the same resampling device applied jointly to the
whole SPU family. This proves the validity of the adaptive norm-combination
test.
∎

C. Proofs for spatial median and weighted location estimators

We begin with two deterministic expansions that are repeatedly used below.
The first one is the usual Tyler expansion for the spatial-sign map; the second
one is the corresponding expansion for the power-weighted sign map that contains
as special cases the spatial-sign choice
m=0
and the inverse-norm choice

m=-1
.

Lemma 2.2

(Tyler expansion of the sign map)
.

Let
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, let
r=\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}
, and put

\bm{u}=U(\bm{z})=\bm{z}/r
. If
\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfies

\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}\leq r/2
, then

U(\bm{z}+\bm{h})=\bm{u}+r^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{h}+\bm{R}(\bm{z},\bm{h}),

(2.285)

where

\left\lVert\bm{R}(\bm{z},\bm{h})\right\rVert_{2}\leq 8r^{-2}\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(2.286)

Consequently,

\left\lVert\bm{R}(\bm{z},\bm{h})\right\rVert_{\max}\leq 8r^{-2}\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(2.287)

Proof.

For
\bm{w}\neq\bm{0}
define
F(\bm{w})=U(\bm{w})=\bm{w}/\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}
. Its Fréchet
derivative is

DF(\bm{w})[\bm{h}]=\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\{\bm{I}_{p}-U(\bm{w})U(\bm{w})^{\mathsf{T}}\}\bm{h}.

(2.288)

Hence

U(\bm{z}+\bm{h})-U(\bm{z})=\int_{0}^{1}DF(\bm{z}+t\bm{h})[\bm{h}]\,dt.

(2.289)

Subtracting the value of the integrand at
t=0
gives

\bm{R}(\bm{z},\bm{h})=\int_{0}^{1}\{DF(\bm{z}+t\bm{h})-DF(\bm{z})\}[\bm{h}]dt.

(2.290)

If
\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}\leq r/2
, then
\left\lVert\bm{z}+t\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}\geq r/2
for all

t\in[0,1]
. On the set
\{\bm{w}:\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}\geq r/2\}
the operator

DF(\bm{w})
is Lipschitz with constant bounded by
8r^{-2}
, because both the
map
\bm{w}\mapsto\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}
and the map

\bm{w}\mapsto U(\bm{w})U(\bm{w})^{\mathsf{T}}
have derivatives of order
r^{-2}
there.
Using (
2.290
) yields

\left\lVert\bm{R}(\bm{z},\bm{h})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{1}8r^{-2}t\,dt\;\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 8r^{-2}\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

This proves (
2.286
);
(
2.287
) follows from

\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 2.3

(Power-weighted Tyler expansion)
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{R}
and define
F_{m}(\bm{z})=\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{m}U(\bm{z})
for

\bm{z}\neq\bm{0}
. Let
r=\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}
and
\bm{u}=U(\bm{z})
. If

\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}\leq r/2
, then

F_{m}(\bm{z}+\bm{h})=F_{m}(\bm{z})+r^{m-1}\{\bm{I}_{p}+(m-1)\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\}\bm{h}+\bm{R}_{m}(\bm{z},\bm{h}),

(2.291)

where

\left\lVert\bm{R}_{m}(\bm{z},\bm{h})\right\rVert_{2}\leq C_{m}r^{m-2}\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

(2.292)

for a constant
C_{m}
depending only on
m
.

Proof.

Since

F_{m}(\bm{z})=\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{m-1}\bm{z},

a direct differentiation gives

DF_{m}(\bm{z})[\bm{h}]=r^{m-1}\bm{h}+(m-1)r^{m-3}(\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{h})\bm{z}=r^{m-1}\{\bm{I}_{p}+(m-1)\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\}\bm{h}.

(2.293)

Applying the mean-value theorem exactly as in the proof of
Lemma
2.2
, and using that the derivative of
F_{m}
is
Lipschitz on
\{\bm{w}:\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}\geq r/2\}
with modulus of order

r^{m-2}
, proves (
2.291
) and
(
2.292
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.10
.

Define the empirical score

\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}),\qquad\Psi(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\{\Psi_{n}(\bm{\theta})\}.

(2.294)

Since
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}
solves
\Psi_{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})=\bm{0}
,
for each
i
the mean-value theorem and
Lemma
2.2
yield

U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})-\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}(t)\bm{U}_{i}(t)^{\mathsf{T}}}{r_{i}(t)}dt\right\}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}),

(2.295)

where

\bm{U}_{i}(t)=U\{\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-t(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})\},\qquad r_{i}(t)=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-t(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}.

Summing (
2.295
) over
i
gives the exact identity

\bm{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}-\bm{A}_{n}^{\star}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}),

(2.296)

with

\bm{A}_{n}^{\star}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}(t)\bm{U}_{i}(t)^{\mathsf{T}}}{r_{i}(t)}dt.

(2.297)

Hence

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})=(\bm{A}_{n}^{\star})^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}.

(2.298)

Subtract and add
\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\Big\{(\bm{A}_{n}^{\star})^{-1}-\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\Big\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}(\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{A}_{n}^{\star})(\bm{A}_{n}^{\star})^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}.

(2.299)

Write the second term in (
2.299
) as

\bm{r}_{n,\mathrm{SM}}
. By
Assumption
2.6
(SM2)
,

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\underline{a}^{-1}
. By the law of large
numbers and the scalar CLT,

\left\lVert n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{p}(1)

(2.300)

for every fixed-dimensional projection. Finally,
Assumption
2.6
(SM4)
gives

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{n}^{\star}-\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1),\qquad\left\lVert(\bm{A}_{n}^{\star})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(1).

(2.301)

Combining (
2.299
)–
(
2.301
) proves
(
2.82
). More precisely, under the stronger
ultrahigh-dimensional assumptions used in

79
;
157
, one has the max-norm rate

\left\lVert\bm{r}_{n,\mathrm{SM}}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\Big\{n^{-1/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big\}.

(2.302)

To prove (
2.83
), fix a unit vector
\bm{a}
and
project (
2.82
) onto
\bm{a}
:

\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{U}_{i}+o_{p}(1).

The summands are independent and mean zero; their variance is

\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{B}_{\mathrm{SM}}\bm{A}_{\mathrm{SM}}^{-1}\bm{a}
.
The scalar Lindeberg–Feller theorem therefore yields
(
2.83
).
∎

Detailed proof of Lemma
2.1
.

Let

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}=\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}),\qquad\bm{z}_{i}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})=r_{i}\bm{U}_{i}.

(2.303)

The diagonal estimating equation (
2.85
) states that
for each coordinate
j
,

\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{j}^{2}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})=1.

(2.304)

Because
\mathbb{E}\{pU_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})\}=1
, subtracting the population version gives

\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{U_{j}^{2}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})-U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})\}=-\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})\}.

(2.305)

By Bernstein’s inequality and the row-sum control in
(
2.94
),

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})\}\right|=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right).

(2.306)

Next, expand
U_{j}^{2}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})
around
U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})
. Since

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}
depends linearly on
\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}
and

\hat{d}_{j}-d_{j}
, the derivative of
U_{j}^{2}(\cdot)
and the Taylor expansion in
Lemma
2.2
give

\frac{p}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{U_{j}^{2}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})-U_{j}^{2}(\bm{z}_{i})\}=-\frac{\hat{d}_{j}-d_{j}}{d_{j}}+R_{j,n},

(2.307)

where

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|R_{j,n}|=O_{p}\left(\frac{a_{0}(p)}{p}+\frac{\log p}{n^{3/4}}\right).

(2.308)

Combining (
2.305
),
(
2.306
), and (
2.307
) yields

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{\hat{d}_{j}-d_{j}}{d_{j}}\right|=O_{p}\Bigg[\left\{\frac{\log p}{n}\right\}^{1/2}+\frac{a_{0}(p)}{p}+\frac{\log p}{n^{3/4}}\Bigg].

(2.309)

This is exactly (
2.96
) and implies
(
2.95
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.11
.

Write

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}=\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}),\qquad\bm{z}_{i}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})=r_{i}\bm{U}_{i}.

(2.310)

The estimating equation is

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})=\bm{0}.

(2.311)

Set
\bm{\Delta}_{\theta}=\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}
and

\bm{\Delta}_{D}=\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}-\bm{D}^{-1/2}
. Then

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}=-\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}_{\theta}+\bm{\Delta}_{D}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})+\bm{\rho}_{i,D},

(2.312)

where the diagonal Taylor expansion of
x\mapsto x^{-1/2}
implies

\left\lVert\bm{\rho}_{i,D}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{\hat{d}_{j}}{d_{j}}-1\right|^{2}r_{i}.

(2.313)

Apply Lemma
2.2
with

\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{i}
and
\bm{h}=\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}
:

U(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})=\bm{U}_{i}+r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i})+\bm{R}_{i,1},

(2.314)

with

\left\lVert\bm{R}_{i,1}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq 8r_{i}^{-2}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(2.315)

Substituting (
2.312
) into
(
2.314
) and averaging over
i
gives

\displaystyle\bm{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}-\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\right\}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}_{\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{\Delta}_{D}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})+\bm{R}_{n,1}+\bm{R}_{n,2},

(2.316)

where

\bm{R}_{n,1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{R}_{i,1},\qquad\bm{R}_{n,2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{\rho}_{i,D}.

(2.317)

By spherical symmetry of the standardized errors,

\mathbb{E}\left[r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\right]=c_{0}\bm{I}_{p}.

(2.318)

Therefore

\bm{A}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})=c_{0}\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{E}_{n},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{E}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\{n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\}.

(2.319)

The term involving
\bm{\Delta}_{D}
is controlled by the scale equation.
Indeed, the diagonal normalization
(
2.85
) and the same Taylor expansion imply

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{\Delta}_{D}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\Big\{p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big\}n^{-1/2}.

(2.320)

Similarly,

\left\lVert\bm{R}_{n,1}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\{n^{-3/4}(\log(np))^{1/2}\},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{R}_{n,2}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\{n^{-1}(\log(np))^{1/2}\}.

(2.321)

Multiply (
2.316
) by
\sqrt{n}
and rearrange:

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}_{\theta}=(c_{0}\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{E}_{n})^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\sqrt{n}\,\bm{R}_{n,3}\right],

(2.322)

where
\bm{R}_{n,3}
collects the right-hand side of
(
2.320
) and
(
2.321
). Since

\left\lVert\bm{E}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=o_{p}(1)
, expand the inverse once more:

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta})=c_{0}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{C}_{n},

(2.323)

with

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{C}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\Big\{

\displaystyle n^{-1/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle+n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big\}.

This is exactly (
2.97
)–
(
2.11
). The final Gaussian assertion follows
from the leading linear term and the Cramér–Wold device.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.12
.

To make the differentiation transparent, first treat the power family

K(t)=t^{m}
; the inverse-norm case corresponds to
m=-1
, and the same argument
applies to a differentiable weight
K
with the moment bounds in
Assumption
2.8
. Define

F_{m}(\bm{z})=\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{m}U(\bm{z}),\qquad\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}=\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}),\qquad\bm{z}_{i}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}).

The weighted estimating equation can be written as

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{m}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})=\bm{0}.

(2.324)

By Lemma
2.3
,

F_{m}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i})=F_{m}(\bm{z}_{i})+r_{i}^{m-1}\{\bm{I}_{p}+(m-1)\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}(\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i})+\bm{R}_{i,m}.

(2.325)

Using (
2.312
) to expand

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}
, averaging over
i
, and collecting the location terms, we
obtain

\bm{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}-\bm{J}_{n,K}\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})+\bm{R}_{n,K},

(2.326)

where

\bm{J}_{n,K}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{m-1}\{\bm{I}_{p}+(m-1)\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}

(2.327)

and
\bm{R}_{n,K}
contains the diagonal-scale contribution and the second-order
Taylor remainder.
Under elliptical symmetry,

\mathbb{E}(\bm{J}_{n,K})=c_{0,K}\bm{I}_{p},\qquad c_{0,K}=\mathbb{E}\{K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}\},

(2.328)

so that

\left\lVert\bm{J}_{n,K}-c_{0,K}\bm{I}_{p}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\{n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\}.

(2.329)

Exactly the same diagonal-scale calculations as in the proof of
Theorem
2.11
, together with the extra moment condition

\nu_{4,K}=O(\nu_{2,K}^{2})
, imply

\left\lVert\bm{R}_{n,K}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\Big\{n^{-3/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+n^{-1/2}p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+n^{-1}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}\Big\}.

(2.330)

Multiplying (
2.326
) by
\sqrt{n}
and solving
for
\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})
yields

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})=\bm{J}_{n,K}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}+\sqrt{n}\,\bm{J}_{n,K}^{-1}\bm{R}_{n,K}.

(2.331)

Expanding
\bm{J}_{n,K}^{-1}
around
c_{0,K}^{-1}\bm{I}_{p}
gives

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta})=c_{0,K}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{C}_{n,K},

(2.332)

with the same rate as in
(
2.11
). This proves
(
2.106
). For the Gaussian approximation over
hyperrectangles, write the leading term as a sum of independent centered random
vectors and apply the high-dimensional Gaussian approximation theorem of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and Kato to the class of rectangles. The additional
remainder
\bm{C}_{n,K}
is negligible in sup norm because of
(
2.330
), and therefore
(
2.107
) follows.
∎

D. Proofs for one-sample weighted-sign statistics

Detailed proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Use the identity

T_{n}(K)=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\left\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{V}_{i}(K)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{V}_{i}(K)\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{V}_{i}(K)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(2.333)

Taking expectations and using independence gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j}(K)\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{i}(K)\}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{j}(K)\}=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{K}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},

(2.334)

which proves (
2.111
).

Under
H_{0}
and central symmetry,
\bm{\eta}_{K}=\bm{0}
, and therefore

T_{n}(K)=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}h_{K}(\bm{V}_{i},\bm{V}_{j}),\qquad h_{K}(\bm{a},\bm{b})=\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b},

(2.335)

with degenerate kernel
\mathbb{E}\{h_{K}(\bm{V}_{1},\bm{V}_{2})\mid\bm{V}_{1}\}=0
.
Consequently, all cross-products with four distinct indices vanish. Expanding

T_{n}(K)^{2}
and retaining only the nonzero contractions yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)^{2}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\big[(\bm{V}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j})^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\binom{n}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\bm{V}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{2}\bm{V}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\bm{V}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{1}\big]=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{2}),

(2.336)

which is (
2.112
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.13
.

Under
H_{0}
the kernel

h_{K}(\bm{V}_{i},\bm{V}_{j})=\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j}(K)
is degenerate. Define the
filtration
\mathcal{F}_{n,j}=\sigma\{\bm{V}_{1},\ldots,\bm{V}_{j}\}
and the martingale
differences

Z_{n,j}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j}(K),\qquad j=2,\ldots,n.

(2.337)

Then
T_{n}(K)=\sum_{j=2}^{n}Z_{n,j}
and

\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})=0
.

The conditional variance process is

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{j-1}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{j-1}\bm{V}_{i_{1}}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{i_{2}}(K)

\displaystyle=C_{n,1}+C_{n,2},

(2.338)

where

\displaystyle C_{n,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{i}(K),

(2.339)

\displaystyle C_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq j-1}\bm{V}_{i_{1}}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{i_{2}}(K).

(2.340)

Using independence and stationarity,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(C_{n,1})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{1}(K)\}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{2})=\sigma_{n,K}^{2}.

(2.341)

Next,

\operatorname{Var}(C_{n,1})\leq Cn^{-5}\mathbb{E}\big\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{V}_{1}(K)\big\}^{2}\leq Cn^{-5}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{4})=o(\sigma_{n,K}^{4}),

(2.342)

where the last step uses

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{4})=\nu_{2,K}^{4}p^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})
and
Assumption
2.7
(SD2)
.
Similarly,
\mathbb{E}(C_{n,2})=0
and

\operatorname{Var}(C_{n,2})\leq Cn^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{4})=o(\sigma_{n,K}^{4}).

(2.343)

Therefore,

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})=\sigma_{n,K}^{2}\{1+o_{p}(1)\}.

(2.344)

To verify Lyapunov’s condition, note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{4})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{8}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bm{V}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}_{j}(K)\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{8}}\Big\{n^{3}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{A}_{K}^{2})+n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{4})\Big\}=o(\sigma_{n,K}^{4}),

(2.345)

where the bound uses the fourth-moment condition

\nu_{4,K}=O(\nu_{2,K}^{2})
from
Assumption
2.8
(WS1)
. Hall and Heyde’s
martingale CLT therefore gives

\frac{T_{n}(K)}{\sigma_{n,K}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

Finally, under ellipticity,

\bm{A}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}=\nu_{2,K}p^{-1}\bm{R}
, and hence

\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}=\frac{2\nu_{2,K}^{2}}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}),

which is exactly (
2.114
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.14
.

Let

\bm{\eta}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)\}
and define centered scores

\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K)=\bm{V}_{i}(K)-\bm{\eta}_{K}
. Then the quadratic-form statistic has
the exact Hoeffding decomposition

T_{n}(K)=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{K}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\eta}_{K}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K)+\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{j}(K).

(2.346)

By a first-order expansion of the weighted sign expectation at the origin,

\bm{\eta}_{K}=c_{0,K}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\theta}+\bm{r}_{K,1},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{K,1}\right\rVert_{2}=O(\left\lVert\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}).

(2.347)

Hence

\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)\}=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{K}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=c_{0,K}^{2}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}+O(\left\lVert\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}).

(2.348)

Under Assumption
2.8
(WS2)
, the cubic remainder in
(
2.348
) is
o(\sigma_{n,K})
.

Set

L_{n}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\eta}_{K}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K),\qquad Q_{n}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i<j}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{j}(K).

(2.349)

Then
T_{n}(K)-\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)\}=L_{n}+Q_{n}+o(\sigma_{n,K})
.
Because
Q_{n}
is degenerate and
L_{n}
is the first Hoeffding projection,

\operatorname{Cov}(L_{n},Q_{n})=0.

(2.350)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(L_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n}\bm{\eta}_{K}^{\mathsf{T}}\operatorname{Cov}\{\bm{V}_{1}(K)\}\bm{\eta}_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n}\bm{\eta}_{K}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{K}\bm{\eta}_{K}-\frac{4}{n}\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{K}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{4c_{0,K}^{2}}{np}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}\{1+o(1)\},

(2.351)

while the degenerate component satisfies

\operatorname{Var}(Q_{n})=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{K}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}=\sigma_{n,K}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(2.352)

Therefore

\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}=\sigma_{n,K}^{2}+\frac{4c_{0,K}^{2}}{np}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}+o(\sigma_{n,K}^{2}).

(2.353)

Let

D_{n,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{i}(K)^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{j}(K)+\frac{2}{n}\bm{\eta}_{K}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}_{j}(K),\qquad 2\leq j\leq n.

Then
L_{n}+Q_{n}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}D_{n,j}
is a martingale difference sum with respect
to
\mathcal{F}_{j}=\sigma\{\bm{V}_{1}(K),\ldots,\bm{V}_{j}(K)\}
. Using
(
2.350
),
(
2.351
), and
(
2.352
),

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(D_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1})=\operatorname{Var}(L_{n})+\operatorname{Var}(Q_{n})+o_{p}(\sigma_{n,K}^{2})=\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}+o_{p}(\sigma_{n,K}^{2}).

The fourth-moment bound in Assumption
2.8
(WS1)

gives

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{D_{n,j}^{4}\}=o\big(\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}^{2}\big),

so the martingale Lindeberg condition holds. Hall and Heyde’s theorem therefore
implies

\frac{T_{n}(K)-\mathbb{E}\{T_{n}(K)\}}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}\{T_{n}(K)\}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

which is (
2.115
).
Finally, dividing the squared local signal in
(
2.348
) by the leading null standard deviation in
(
2.114
) gives the local asymptotic
signal-to-noise ratio

\frac{c_{0,K}^{2}}{\nu_{2,K}}=\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r_{i})r_{i}^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\}},

which is exactly (
2.116
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.17
.

Let

T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{j}

(2.354)

be the oracle statistic built from the true diagonal standardization.
The leave-two-out construction and the Tyler expansion in
Lemma
2.2
imply

T_{\mathrm{SS}}-T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}=O_{p}\{n^{-1}\sigma_{n}+n^{-2}p^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})^{1/2}\}=o_{p}(\sigma_{n}),

(2.355)

so it suffices to study
T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}
.

Define

Z_{n,j}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{U}_{j},\qquad W_{n,m}=\sum_{j=2}^{m}Z_{n,j},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n,m}=\sigma\{\bm{U}_{1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{m}\}.

(2.356)

Then
T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}=W_{n,n}
and

\{W_{n,m},\mathcal{F}_{n,m}\}
is a square-integrable martingale. Since

\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{j}\bm{U}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}})=p^{-1}\bm{R}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}p}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{j-1}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{j-1}\bm{U}_{i_{1}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{R}\bm{U}_{i_{2}}

\displaystyle=C_{n,1}+C_{n,2},

(2.357)

where

\displaystyle C_{n,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}p}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{R}\bm{U}_{i},

(2.358)

\displaystyle C_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}p}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq j-1}\bm{U}_{i_{1}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{R}\bm{U}_{i_{2}}.

(2.359)

Now

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(C_{n,1})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}p}\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\operatorname{tr}\{\bm{R}\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{1}\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}})\}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}p^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})=\frac{2}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})=\sigma_{n}^{2}.

(2.360)

Further,

\operatorname{Var}(C_{n,1})\leq Cn^{-5}p^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big(\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{R}\bm{U}_{1}\big)^{2}=O\big(n^{-5}p^{-4}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})\big)=o(\sigma_{n}^{4}),

(2.361)

where we used

\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{R}\bm{U}_{1})^{2}=O\{p^{-2}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})\}
.
Similarly,

\mathbb{E}(C_{n,2})=0,\qquad\operatorname{Var}(C_{n,2})=O\big(n^{-4}p^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})\big)=o(\sigma_{n}^{4})

(2.362)

by Assumption
2.10
(SS1)
. Hence

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})=\sigma_{n}^{2}\{1+o_{p}(1)\}.

(2.363)

For Lyapunov’s condition,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{4})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{8}}\Big\{n^{3}p^{-4}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})+n^{2}p^{-4}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})\Big\}=o(\sigma_{n}^{4}).

(2.364)

The martingale CLT therefore gives

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

Combining this with (
2.355
) proves
(
2.137
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.18
.

As in the null proof,

T_{\mathrm{SS}}=T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}+o_{p}(\sigma_{n})
, so it is enough to
work with the oracle statistic

T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}
in (
2.354
). Let

\bm{\eta}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i})
. By the Tyler expansion at the true center,

\bm{\eta}=c_{0}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\mu}+\bm{r}_{\mu},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}=O(\left\lVert\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}).

(2.365)

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\{T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}\}=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=c_{0}^{2}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}+O(\left\lVert\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}).

(2.366)

Write
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}=\bm{U}_{i}-\bm{\eta}
. Then

T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}-\mathbb{E}\{T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}\}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\eta}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}+\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i<j}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{j}.

(2.367)

The two terms on the right-hand side are uncorrelated, because the second one is
degenerate. Their variances are

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\eta}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n}\bm{\eta}^{\mathsf{T}}\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{U}_{1})\bm{\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{4c_{0}^{2}}{np}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}\{1+o(1)\},

(2.368)

and

\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i<j}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{j}\right)=\sigma_{n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(2.369)

Hence

\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})=\sigma_{n}^{2}+\frac{4c_{0}^{2}}{np}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\mu}+o(\sigma_{n}^{2}).

(2.370)

Under Assumption
2.10
(SS4)
, the cubic remainder in
(
2.366
) is negligible relative to the standard
deviation. Write

D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{FS}}=\frac{2}{n}\bm{\eta}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{j},\qquad 2\leq j\leq n.

Then
T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}-\mathbb{E}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})=\sum_{j=2}^{n}D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{FS}}
. Since the two Hoeffding components are
uncorrelated,

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{(D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{FS}})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\}=\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})+o_{p}\big(\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})\big),

where
\mathcal{F}_{j}=\sigma(\bm{U}_{1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{j})
. The fourth-order contraction
bounds used in the proof of Theorem
2.17
imply

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{(D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{FS}})^{4}\}=o\big(\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})^{2}\big).

Hence the martingale Lindeberg condition holds, and Hall and Heyde’s theorem
shows that

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}}-\mathbb{E}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathrm{or}})}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

Together with (
2.366
) and
(
2.370
), this proves (
2.138
).
∎

Detailed proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Consider the Hilbert space
L_{2}(F_{r})
associated with the radial law of
r
and
inner product

\langle f,g\rangle=\mathbb{E}\{f(r)g(r)\}.

Take
f(r)=K(r)
and
g(r)=r^{-1}
. Then

\big[\mathbb{E}\{K(r)r^{-1}\}\big]^{2}=\langle f,g\rangle^{2}\leq\langle f,f\rangle\langle g,g\rangle=\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}\mathbb{E}(r^{-2}).

(2.371)

Dividing both sides by
\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}
gives

\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r)r^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}}\leq\mathbb{E}(r^{-2}).

(2.372)

Equality in (
2.371
) holds if and only if

f
and
g
are linearly dependent in
L_{2}(F_{r})
, namely,

K(r)=c\,r^{-1}
almost surely for some constant
c\neq 0
. This is precisely
(
2.142
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.19
.

Set
K(t)=t^{-1}
. Then

c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}),\qquad\nu_{2,\mathrm{IN}}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}),\qquad\bm{A}_{\mathrm{IN}}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-2}\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})=\nu_{2,\mathrm{IN}}p^{-1}\bm{R}.

(2.373)

Hence the oracle statistic satisfies the null limit

\frac{T_{n}(t^{-1})}{\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),\qquad\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}^{2}=\frac{2\nu_{2,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}}{n(n-1)p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}),

(2.374)

by Theorem
2.13
. Under local alternatives,
Theorem
2.14
yields

\frac{T_{n}(t^{-1})-c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}^{2}+\dfrac{4c_{0,\mathrm{IN}}^{2}}{np}\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\theta}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.375)

It remains to pass from the oracle statistic to the feasible leave-two-out
statistic. By the weighted Bahadur expansion with
K(t)=t^{-1}
and the same
leave-two-out argument as in
(
2.355
),

T_{\mathrm{INST}}-T_{n}(t^{-1})=o_{p}(\sigma_{\mathrm{IN},n}).

(2.376)

Combining (
2.374
),
(
2.375
), and
(
2.376
) proves
(
2.147
) and (
2.149
).
∎

D1. Proofs for additional sign-based location tests

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.15
.

Under
H_{0}
,
\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
and hence

T_{\mathrm{WPL}}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(\bm{Z}_{i}^{\mathrm{sgn}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{sgn}}

(2.377)

is a degenerate
U
-statistic with kernel

h(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j})=U(\bm{x}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}U(\bm{x}_{j})
. Since

\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}_{i})\}=\bm{0}
under central symmetry,

\mathbb{E}(T_{\mathrm{WPL}})=0.

(2.378)

Let
\bm{B}=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}_{i})U(\bm{X}_{i})^{\mathsf{T}}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(T_{\mathrm{WPL}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sum_{1\leq k<\ell\leq n}\mathbb{E}\Big[(\bm{Z}_{i}^{\mathrm{sgn}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{sgn}}(\bm{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{sgn}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{Z}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{sgn}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2}),

(2.379)

which is (
2.121
). To prove asymptotic normality, write

T_{\mathrm{WPL}}
as a martingale array with respect to the filtration generated
by
(\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{k})
. The conditional variance of the martingale equals

(n(n-1)/2)\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}
under
(
2.122
)–(
2.123
), while the fourth conditional
moments are negligible relative to the square of that variance. The martingale
central limit theorem therefore yields (
2.128
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.16
.

Under the local alternative, expand

U(\bm{\mu}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{i})=U(\bm{\varepsilon}_{i})+\left\{\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}\left(\bm{I}_{p}-\frac{\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}}{\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)\right\}\bm{\mu}+\bm{r}_{i,n}=U(\bm{\varepsilon}_{i})+\bm{A}_{i}\bm{\mu}+\bm{r}_{i,n},

(2.380)

where
\mathbb{E}(\bm{A}_{i})=\bm{A}
and the remainder satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bm{r}_{i,n}\right\rVert_{2}=o\{\left\lVert\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\}
by
(
2.126
)–(
2.127
). Substituting
(
2.380
) into the definition of

T_{\mathrm{WPL}}
gives

T_{\mathrm{WPL}}=T_{0,n}+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\bm{\mu}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{2}\bm{\mu}+R_{n},

(2.381)

where
T_{0,n}
is the null-centered statistic and

R_{n}=o_{p}\{n\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{B}^{2})\}
. Since

T_{0,n}/\sqrt{n(n-1)\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{2})/2}\Rightarrow N(0,1)
by the previous proof,
(
2.129
) follows.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.27
.

Write the full-sample statistic as

T_{\mathrm{SST}}=Z_{n}+B_{n},

(2.382)

where
Z_{n}
is the oracle statistic obtained by replacing

(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2},\hat{\bm{D}}_{1},\hat{\bm{D}}_{2})
by their population
counterparts, and
B_{n}
is the bias induced by full-sample estimation. Under
Assumption
2.15
, the same linearization used in the proof of
Theorem
2.25
gives

B_{n}=\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}+o_{p}(\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}).

(2.383)

The oracle part
Z_{n}
is a degenerate two-sample
U
-statistic with variance

\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}
. The trace condition
(
2.197
) implies the Lyapunov condition for the associated
martingale array, hence

\frac{Z_{n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{SST},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(2.384)

Combining (
2.383
) and
(
2.384
) proves
(
2.199
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.28
.

Under local alternatives with
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\theta}_{1}-\bm{\theta}_{2}
, the same expansion
used for Theorem
2.26
yields

\mathbb{E}(T_{\mathrm{SST}})=\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}+\delta_{\mathrm{SST},n}+o(\tilde{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SST},n}).

(2.385)

The additional variance contribution under
H_{1}
comes from the linear terms in

\bm{\Delta}
and equals the two quadratic forms in
(
2.201
). Therefore

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SST}}-\mu_{\mathrm{SST},n}-\delta_{\mathrm{SST},n}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SST},n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(2.386)

which is exactly (
2.203
).
∎

E. Proofs for one-sample max, weighted max, and max-sum procedures

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.20
.

Define the normalized location vector

\bm{Y}_{n}=\sqrt{n}\,\hat{c}_{0}\,p^{1/2}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}.

(2.387)

By Theorem
2.11
,

\bm{Y}_{n}=p^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{\Delta}_{n},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=O_{p}\{L_{n,p}\},

(2.388)

where

L_{n,p}=n^{-1/4}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+p^{-(1/6\wedge\delta/2)}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}+n^{-1/2}(\log p)^{1/2}\{\log(np)\}^{1/2}.

(2.389)

Assumptions
2.11
and
2.12
, together with the hyperrectangle Gaussian approximation implied by Theorem
2.12
with
K\equiv 1
, imply that there exists

\bm{G}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{R})
such that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{\left\lVert\bm{Y}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq t\}-\mathbb{P}\{\left\lVert\bm{G}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq t\}\right|\to 0.

(2.390)

Since

T_{\max}=(1-n^{-1/2})\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Y_{n,j}^{2},

(2.391)

it follows from (
2.390
) that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}\leq x\}-\mathbb{P}\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}\leq x\}\right|\to 0.

(2.392)

Finally, Assumption
2.12
is precisely the weak-dependence
condition under which the dependent Gaussian maximum obeys the same Gumbel limit
as in the independent case:

\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\right\}\to\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}.

(2.393)

Combining (
2.392
) and
(
2.393
) proves
(
2.162
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.21
.

Under
H_{1}
write

\bm{Y}_{n}=\sqrt{n}\,c_{0}p^{1/2}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\theta}+\bm{G}_{n}+\bm{\Delta}_{n},

(2.394)

where
\bm{G}_{n}
has the same weak limit as
\bm{G}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{R})
and

\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{n}\right\rVert_{\max}=o_{p}(1)
by
(
2.388
). Let
j_{\star}
satisfy

|\theta_{j_{\star}}|/\sqrt{d_{j_{\star}}}=\max_{j}|\theta_{j}|/\sqrt{d_{j}}
. Then

\displaystyle T_{\max}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{1-n^{-1/2}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\,c_{0}p^{1/2}d_{j_{\star}}^{-1/2}|\theta_{j_{\star}}|-|G_{n,j_{\star}}|-|\Delta_{n,j_{\star}}|\Big).

(2.395)

Because
|G_{n,j_{\star}}|=O_{p}(1)
whereas

\sqrt{n}\,c_{0}p^{1/2}d_{j_{\star}}^{-1/2}|\theta_{j_{\star}}|

is of order
\sqrt{\log p}
under
(
2.163
), the deterministic shift dominates the Gumbel
critical value once
C_{e}
is chosen sufficiently large. Therefore

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}>2\log p-\log\log p+q_{1-\alpha}\mid H_{1}\}\to 1,

which is exactly (
2.164
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.22
.

Define

\bm{Y}_{n,K}=\sqrt{n}\,\hat{c}_{0,m}\hat{\nu}_{2,m}^{-1/2}p^{1/2}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}.

(2.396)

By Theorem
2.12
,

\bm{Y}_{n,K}=\hat{\nu}_{2,m}^{-1/2}p^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{\Delta}_{n,K},

(2.397)

with
\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{n,K}\right\rVert_{\max}=o_{p}(1)
. Put

\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K}=\hat{\nu}_{2,m}^{-1/2}p^{1/2}n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}.

Because
\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K})=\bm{0}
and

\operatorname{Cov}(\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K})=\nu_{2,K}^{-1}p\,\operatorname{Cov}\{K(r_{1})\bm{U}_{1}\}=\bm{R},

Assumptions
2.11
and
2.12
imply, via the same Gaussian comparison argument, that for
\bm{G}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{R})
,

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq t\}-\mathbb{P}\{\left\lVert\bm{G}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq t\}\right|\to 0.

(2.398)

Since
\left\lVert\bm{Y}_{n,K}-\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K}\right\rVert_{\max}=o_{p}(1)
, the same relation
holds with
\widetilde{\bm{Y}}_{n,K}
replaced by
\bm{Y}_{n,K}
, and therefore

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{T_{\max}^{(m)}\leq x\}-\mathbb{P}\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}\leq x\}\right|\to 0.

Assumption
2.12
then yields the Gaussian extreme-value limit

\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq x\right\}\to\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\},

which proves (
2.167
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.23
.

Write

S_{n}=\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}}{\sigma_{n}},\qquad M_{n}=T_{\max}-2\log p+\log\log p.

(2.399)

By the Bahadur expansions in the proofs of
Theorems
2.17
and
2.20
, there exists a
vector array
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i})=0
and

\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}\xi_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})=p^{-1}\bm{R}
such that

S_{n}=W_{n}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})+o_{p}(1),\qquad M_{n}=V_{n}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})+o_{p}(1),

(2.400)

where

\displaystyle W_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{p\sum_{i\neq j}x_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}x_{j}}{\sqrt{2n(n-1)\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}},

(2.401)

\displaystyle V_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i,j}\right)^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p.

(2.402)

Let
\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. Gaussian with the same covariance as
\xi_{i}
.
The smooth-max argument in the proof of the max-sum theorem works as follows.
Let

F_{\beta}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{p})=\beta^{-1}\log\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{\beta z_{j}}\right),

(2.403)

so that
0\leq F_{\beta}(z)-\max_{j}z_{j}\leq\beta^{-1}\log p
. Choose

\beta\asymp(\log p)^{1/2}
. For any smooth bounded function
\varphi
on

\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a Lindeberg interpolation between
\xi_{i}
and
g_{i}
gives

\left|\mathbb{E}\,\varphi\big(W_{n}(\xi),F_{\beta}(V_{n}(\xi))\big)-\mathbb{E}\,\varphi\big(W_{n}(g),F_{\beta}(V_{n}(g))\big)\right|\to 0.

(2.404)

The proof uses Assumption
2.13
to bound the derivatives of the
quadratic part and the smooth maximum and to control the contribution of the few
strongly correlated coordinates. Since
\beta^{-1}\log p=o(1)
, the same limit
holds with
F_{\beta}
replaced by the maximum.

For the Gaussian array
g_{i}
, the statistic
W_{n}(g)
is a centered quadratic form
and
V_{n}(g)
is the centered maximum of a Gaussian vector. By the asymptotic
independence theorem for the sum and maximum of dependent Gaussian coordinates,

\mathbb{P}\{W_{n}(g)\leq x,\,V_{n}(g)\leq y\}\to\Phi(x)F(y),

(2.405)

where
F(y)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}\}
. Combining
(
2.400
),
(
2.404
), and
(
2.405
) yields
(
2.169
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.24
.

Normalize the weighted sum and max statistics by

S_{n,K}=\frac{T_{\mathrm{SUM}}^{(m)}}{\sigma_{n,K}},\qquad M_{n,K}=T_{\max}^{(m)}-2\log p+\log\log p.

(2.406)

The weighted Bahadur expansion implies that both are functions of the same
normalized weighted score array

\xi_{i,K}=\nu_{2,K}^{-1/2}p^{1/2}K(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}.

More precisely,

S_{n,K}=W_{n}(\xi_{1,K},\ldots,\xi_{n,K})+o_{p}(1),\qquad M_{n,K}=V_{n}(\xi_{1,K},\ldots,\xi_{n,K})+o_{p}(1),

(2.407)

with the same functionals
W_{n}
and
V_{n}
as in
(
2.401
)–(
2.402
). Since

\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i,K})=\bm{0},\qquad\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i,K}\xi_{i,K}^{\mathsf{T}})=\nu_{2,K}^{-1}p\,\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{i})\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\}=\bm{R},

we may introduce i.i.d. Gaussian vectors
g_{i,K}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},p^{-1}\bm{R})
.
Let
F_{\beta}
be the smooth maximum in
(
2.403
). By the same Lindeberg interpolation used
in the proof of Theorem
2.23
, for every bounded
smooth function
\varphi
,

\left|\mathbb{E}\,\varphi\big(W_{n}(\xi_{K}),F_{\beta}(V_{n}(\xi_{K}))\big)-\mathbb{E}\,\varphi\big(W_{n}(g_{K}),F_{\beta}(V_{n}(g_{K}))\big)\right|\to 0,

(2.408)

where
\xi_{K}=(\xi_{1,K},\ldots,\xi_{n,K})
and

g_{K}=(g_{1,K},\ldots,g_{n,K})
. Because
\beta^{-1}\log p=o(1)
,

\mathbb{P}\{S_{n,K}\leq x,\,M_{n,K}\leq y\}-\mathbb{P}\{W_{n}(g_{K})\leq x,\,V_{n}(g_{K})\leq y\}\to 0.

(2.409)

The Gaussian pair
(W_{n}(g_{K}),V_{n}(g_{K}))
has exactly the same joint law as the
pair in the proof of Theorem
2.23
, because its
covariance is again
\bm{R}
. Hence

\mathbb{P}\{W_{n}(g_{K})\leq x,\,V_{n}(g_{K})\leq y\}\to\Phi(x)\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}\}.

(2.410)

Combining (
2.409
) and
(
2.410
) yields
(
2.170
); the null version
(
2.171
) follows from the marginal limits.
∎

F. Proofs for the two-sample procedures

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.25
.

Let
R_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}
denote the statistic obtained from
(
2.176
) after replacing all leave-out estimators by the true
parameters. Lemma 3 and Lemma 4 of the JASA paper give the expansions

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{k}=\frac{1}{n_{k}-1}c_{k}^{-1}\bm{D}_{k}^{1/2}\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{U}_{kj}+o_{p}(n_{k}^{-1/2}),\qquad k=1,2,

(2.411)

and

\displaystyle U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{k,i}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{ki}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{3-k,j})\}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{ki}-r_{ki}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{ki}\bm{U}_{ki}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{3-k,j}-\bm{\theta}_{3-k})

\displaystyle\quad+(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{ki}\bm{U}_{ki}^{\mathsf{T}})(\hat{\bm{D}}_{k,i}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{k}^{1/2}-\bm{I}_{p})\bm{U}_{ki}+o_{p}(n^{-1}).

(2.412)

Substituting (
2.411
) and
(
2.412
) into (
2.176
) and collecting
the leading terms yields

R_{n}=Z_{n}+o_{p}(\sigma_{n}),

(2.413)

where

Z_{n}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{U}_{1i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{1}\bm{U}_{1j}+\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{U}_{2i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{2}\bm{U}_{2j}-\frac{2}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{U}_{1i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{3}\bm{U}_{2j}.

(2.414)

Introduce a pooled sequence

\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n}
with
\bm{Y}_{i}=\bm{U}_{1i}
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
and

\bm{Y}_{n_{1}+j}=\bm{U}_{2j}
for
1\leq j\leq n_{2}
. Define

\phi_{ij}=\begin{cases}\{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)\}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{1}\bm{Y}_{j},&1\leq i\neq j\leq n_{1},\\
-\{n_{1}n_{2}\}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{3}\bm{Y}_{j},&1\leq i\leq n_{1}<j\leq n,\\
\{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)\}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}_{2}\bm{Y}_{j},&n_{1}<i\neq j\leq n.\end{cases}

(2.415)

Then

Z_{n}=2\sum_{j=2}^{n}Z_{n,j},\qquad Z_{n,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\phi_{ij},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n,j}=\sigma(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{j}).

(2.416)

Thus
\{\sum_{m=2}^{j}Z_{n,m},\mathcal{F}_{n,j}\}
is a martingale.
A direct contraction calculation shows

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}(Z_{n,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1})=\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}}{4}\{1+o_{p}(1)\},

(2.417)

while Assumption
2.14
(TS2)
implies the Lindeberg
condition

\sigma_{n}^{-2}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{Z_{n,j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}(|Z_{n,j}|>\epsilon\sigma_{n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1}\}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.418)

for every
\epsilon>0
. Hall and Heyde’s martingale CLT therefore yields

Z_{n}/\sigma_{n}\Rightarrow N(0,1)
. Combining this with
(
2.413
) proves
(
2.185
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.26
.

Under the local alternative
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\theta}_{1}-\bm{\theta}_{2}
, the expansion
(
2.412
) acquires an additional deterministic shift:
for
k=1,2
,

\displaystyle U\{\hat{\bm{D}}_{k,i}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{ki}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{3-k,j})\}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{ki}-r_{ki}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{ki}\bm{U}_{ki}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{3-k,j}-\bm{\theta}_{3-k})

\displaystyle\quad+r_{ki}^{-1}(\bm{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{ki}\bm{U}_{ki}^{\mathsf{T}})\bm{D}_{k}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta}_{k}-\bm{\theta}_{3-k})+o_{p}(n^{-1}).

(2.419)

Substituting (
2.419
) into
(
2.176
) yields the decomposition

R_{n}=\delta_{n}+L_{n}+Z_{n}+o_{p}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n}),

(2.420)

where
Z_{n}
is the same degenerate quadratic form as in the null proof and

L_{n}=\frac{c_{2}}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\bm{U}_{1i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}+\frac{c_{1}}{n_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{U}_{2j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}.

(2.421)

The deterministic term is exactly

\delta_{n}=c_{1}c_{2}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}.

(2.422)

Since
L_{n}
is the first Hoeffding projection and
Z_{n}
is degenerate,

\operatorname{Cov}(L_{n},Z_{n})=0
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(L_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{n_{1}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{R}_{1}\bm{D}_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta}+\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{n_{2}p}\bm{\Delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{R}_{2}\bm{D}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{\Delta},

(2.423)

while
\operatorname{Var}(Z_{n})=\sigma_{n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}
. Therefore

\operatorname{Var}(R_{n})=\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(2.424)

Define

D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{J}}=Z_{n,j}+\ell_{n,j},

where
Z_{n,j}
is given by (
2.416
) and

L_{n}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}\ell_{n,j}
is the decomposition of the linear term in
(
2.421
) into independent summands. Then

R_{n}-\delta_{n}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{J}}+o_{p}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n})
is a
martingale array with respect to the pooled filtration.
Using (
2.424
) and the degeneracy of
Z_{n}
,

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{(D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{J}})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,j-1}\}=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{2}}{4}\{1+o_{p}(1)\}.

Assumption
2.14
(TS2)
also implies

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\{(D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{J}})^{4}\}=o(\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{4}),

so the martingale Lindeberg condition holds. Hall and Heyde’s theorem therefore
shows

\frac{R_{n}-\delta_{n}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

which is (
2.188
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.29
.

Let

\xi_{is}=U\{\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{1i}-\bm{X}_{2s})\},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n_{1},\ 1\leq s\leq n_{2},

(2.425)

and let
T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}
be the oracle version of (
2.205
). The
leave-two-out diagonal estimator admits the same expansion as in the one-sample
problem, so

T_{n}-T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}=o_{p}(\sigma_{n}).

(2.426)

To analyze
T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}
, write the fourth-order kernel as

h\big((i,s),(j,l)\big)=\xi_{is}^{\mathsf{T}}\xi_{jl}.

(2.427)

Under
H_{0}
and the common-scatter assumption, the first Hoeffding projections
vanish:

\mathbb{E}\{h((i,s),(j,l))\mid\bm{X}_{1i}\}=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}\{h((i,s),(j,l))\mid\bm{X}_{2s}\}=0.

(2.428)

Hence the leading term is the degenerate second-order component

T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{s\neq l}\xi_{is}^{\mathsf{T}}\xi_{jl}.

(2.429)

Because
\mathbb{E}(\xi_{is}\xi_{is}^{\mathsf{T}})=p^{-1}\bm{R}_{n}
, expanding the square of
(
2.429
) shows that only index pairings that share
both a first-sample index and a second-sample index contribute to the leading
variance. Consequently,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)\}^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{s\neq l}\mathbb{E}\big[(\xi_{is}^{\mathsf{T}}\xi_{jl})^{2}\big]+o(\sigma_{n}^{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{1}{2n_{1}(n_{1}-1)p^{2}}+\frac{1}{2n_{2}(n_{2}-1)p^{2}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}p^{2}}\right\}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}_{n}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}=\sigma_{n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(2.430)

Let

D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{SR}}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\sum_{s\neq l}\frac{\xi_{is}^{\mathsf{T}}\xi_{jl}}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}

with the obvious pooled ordering of the index pairs. Then

T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}=\sum_{j}D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{SR}}
is a martingale array.
Assumption
2.16
(TR2)
yields

\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\{(D_{n,j}^{\mathrm{SR}})^{4}\}=o(\sigma_{n}^{4}),

so the martingale Lindeberg condition holds. Hence

T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}/\sigma_{n}\Rightarrow N(0,1)
, and
(
2.426
) proves
(
2.214
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.30
.

Under the local alternative
\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\neq\bm{0}
, the cross-sample sign
vector has nonzero mean. Let

\bm{\eta}_{n}=\mathbb{E}(\xi_{12})=c_{0,n}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1/2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})+\bm{r}_{n},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(\left\lVert\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}).

(2.431)

Using
\widetilde{\xi}_{is}=\xi_{is}-\bm{\eta}_{n}
, the oracle statistic decomposes as

T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}=\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+L_{n}^{\mathrm{SR}}+Q_{n}^{\mathrm{SR}},

(2.432)

where
L_{n}^{\mathrm{SR}}
is the linear Hoeffding projection and

Q_{n}^{\mathrm{SR}}
is the degenerate quadratic part. The centering term is

\left\lVert\bm{\eta}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=c_{0,n}^{2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})+o(\sigma_{n})

(2.433)

by Assumption
2.16
(TR4)
. The variance of the linear
term is of smaller order because of
(
2.213
), whereas the degenerate part still has variance

\sigma_{n}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}
. Therefore the same martingale CLT as in the null proof
shows that

\frac{T_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}-c_{0,n}^{2}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})}{\sigma_{n}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

Passing from the oracle statistic to the feasible leave-two-out version proves
(
2.215
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
2.31
.

For a general weight
K
, the two-sample weighted sign statistic has the local
mean shift proportional to
\mathbb{E}\{K(r)r^{-1}\}
and the null standard deviation
proportional to
\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}^{1/2}
. Consequently, the local asymptotic power is
governed by the ratio

\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r)r^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}}.

(2.434)

Exactly as in the one-sample case, apply Cauchy–Schwarz in
L_{2}(F_{r})
:

\{\mathbb{E}[K(r)r^{-1}]\}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}\mathbb{E}(r^{-2}).

(2.435)

Hence

\frac{\{\mathbb{E}[K(r)r^{-1}]\}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r)\}}\leq\mathbb{E}(r^{-2}),

(2.436)

with equality if and only if
K(r)=c\,r^{-1}
almost surely. Therefore

K(t)=t^{-1}
maximizes the local asymptotic signal-to-noise ratio within the
weighted two-sample spatial-sign class, proving
(
2.220
).
∎

Chapter 3
High-Dimensional Matrix Estimation and Testing under Elliptical Symmetry

3.1
Introduction

Matrix-valued inference is the second major pillar of high-dimensional data analysis.
Once the location parameter has been handled, the next questions concern the global and
local structure of variability: Is the population spherical? Are two dependence structures
proportional? Can one estimate a large precision matrix under heavy tails? Can a
factor structure be used to regularize a large scatter matrix without imposing Gaussianity?
These questions are naturally grouped together because they all concern the estimation or
testing of covariance, scatter, shape, or precision matrices.

In the present book, the word
matrix
is used in a layered sense.
When second moments exist, the covariance matrix

\bm{\Sigma}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})

is the most familiar object. Under elliptical symmetry, however, the more primitive object
is the scatter matrix
\bm{\Theta}
or its normalized version

\bm{\Lambda}=\frac{p\bm{\Theta}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Theta})},

which we call the
shape matrix
. The shape matrix is well-defined even when the
covariance matrix is absent or unstable, and it is the correct invariant object in
sphericity and proportionality problems. When conditional linear dependence is the target,
the relevant object is the inverse shape matrix

\bm{V}_{0}=\bm{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1},

or, when
\bm{\Sigma}
exists and its scale is identifiable, the ordinary precision matrix

\bm{\Omega}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
.

As in Chapter 2, the chapter is organized in three layers.

(i)

We first review the classical fixed-
p
theory. This includes the Wishart law
for the sample covariance matrix, likelihood ratio and trace-type tests for covariance
structure, fixed-dimensional sphericity tests, and the classical sign/rank shape tests.
In every case we write down the statistic, the corresponding null law, and the local
asymptotic interpretation.

(ii)

We then review high-dimensional benchmark procedures developed under Gaussian
or light-tail models. These include regularized covariance and precision estimation,
corrected likelihood and trace tests for sphericity, and two-sample covariance tests.
These methods are indispensable benchmarks, and the reader should understand them before
turning to the robust elliptical theory.

(iii)

Finally, we develop the robust elliptical route that forms the main theme of the
book. This includes multivariate-sign and rank-based sphericity tests, adaptive
dense–sparse combinations, two-sample proportionality testing, sparse precision matrix
estimation through the spatial-sign covariance matrix, and matrix estimation through
elliptical factor models.

This chapter differs from many survey chapters in two respects.
First, sphericity testing is absorbed into the matrix chapter rather than treated as a
separate chapter, because under elliptical symmetry it is fundamentally a shape hypothesis.
Second, the chapter does not stop at testing. Robust high-dimensional inference also
requires estimation of large scatter and precision matrices, and the recent literature on
elliptical factor models shows that the robust and the latent-factor viewpoints can be
integrated within one unified framework.

3.2
Problem formulation, notation, and standing models

3.2.1
Scatter, shape, covariance, and precision

Let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
follow the elliptical model

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u},\qquad\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1}),\qquad\xi\geq 0,

(3.1)

where
\xi
and
\bm{u}
are independent, and
\bm{\Theta}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
As emphasized in Chapter 1, the scatter matrix
\bm{\Theta}
is defined only up to a
positive scalar multiplier. The identifiable structural object is therefore the normalized
shape matrix

\bm{\Lambda}=\frac{p\bm{\Theta}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Theta})},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda})=p.

(3.2)

If
\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})<\infty
, then

\bm{\Sigma}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})}{p}\bm{\Theta},

(3.3)

so
\bm{\Sigma}
is proportional to
\bm{\Theta}
and yields the same shape matrix

\bm{\Lambda}=p\bm{\Sigma}/\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})
.

The spatial-sign covariance matrix is

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sgn}}=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\},

(3.4)

with sample version

\hat{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{sgn}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})^{\mathsf{T}},

(3.5)

where
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
is typically the spatial median. Under elliptical symmetry,

\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sgn}}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
share the same eigenvectors. In high dimension,
the difference between
p\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sgn}}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
is often of order
p^{-1/2}

entrywise, which explains why spatial-sign based procedures can estimate large shape and
precision matrices with nearly the same rates as covariance-based procedures while enjoying
much stronger robustness.

When sparsity of conditional linear dependence is of interest, we define

\bm{V}_{0}=\bm{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1}.

(3.6)

Since
\bm{\Lambda}_{0}
is scale normalized,
\bm{V}_{0}
should be interpreted as an inverse shape
matrix. If
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
exists, then
\bm{V}_{0}
differs from
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}
only by a global
scalar factor.

3.2.2
Structural hypotheses

The matrix problems studied in this chapter can all be written as hypotheses on the shape
or covariance structure.

(H1)

Identity of covariance or shape

H_{0}^{\mathrm{id}}:\ \bm{\Sigma}=\bm{I}_{p}\qquad\text{or}\qquad H_{0}^{\mathrm{id,shape}}:\ \bm{\Lambda}=\bm{I}_{p}.

(3.7)

(H2)

Sphericity

H_{0}^{\mathrm{sph}}:\ \bm{\Sigma}=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p}\ \text{for some }\sigma^{2}>0,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad\bm{\Lambda}=\bm{I}_{p}.

(3.8)

(H3)

Equality of two covariance matrices

H_{0}^{\mathrm{eq}}:\ \bm{\Sigma}_{1}=\bm{\Sigma}_{2}.

(3.9)

(H4)

Proportionality of two scatter matrices

H_{0}^{\mathrm{prop}}:\ \bm{\Theta}_{1}=c\,\bm{\Theta}_{2}\ \text{for some }c>0.

(3.10)

The proportionality null is the natural two-sample shape hypothesis under elliptical
symmetry. Indeed, by (
3.2
),
H_{0}^{\mathrm{prop}}
holds if and only if the
two normalized shape matrices are equal:

H_{0}^{\mathrm{prop}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\bm{\Lambda}_{1}=\bm{\Lambda}_{2}.

(3.11)

3.2.3
Standing assumptions for the chapter

The chapter uses several recurring assumptions. To avoid repeating the same hypotheses
word-for-word in every section, we collect the most common ones here and then refer to
them in the theorem statements.

Assumption 3.1

(Fixed-dimensional Gaussian model)
.

Whenever a fixed-
p
likelihood-ratio or Wishart-based statement is invoked, we assume
that
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
with
p
fixed and
\bm{\Sigma}
positive
definite.

Assumption 3.2

(Trace regularity for high-dimensional shape testing)
.

For a sequence of
p\times p
symmetric nonnegative definite matrices
\bm{A}_{p}
, assume

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{A}_{p}^{4})=o\{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{A}_{p}^{2})\}

(3.12)

as
n,p\to\infty
. In most testing problems
\bm{A}_{p}
will be the true shape matrix

\bm{\Lambda}_{p}
or a local perturbation thereof.

Assumption 3.3

(Inverse radial moments)
.

Let
R=\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}
. For sufficiently large
p
, the inverse moments

\mathbb{E}(R^{-k})
exist for
k=1,2,3,4
, and there exist finite constants
d_{k}\in[1,\infty)

such that

\frac{\mathbb{E}(R^{-k})}{\mathbb{E}(R^{-1})^{k}}\longrightarrow d_{k},\qquad k=2,3,4.

(3.13)

Assumption 3.4

(Balanced two-sample growth)
.

In two-sample problems,

\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\longrightarrow\kappa\in(0,1).

(3.14)

Assumption 3.5

(Bounded eigenvalues and diagonal scales)
.

There exist constants
0<\eta<c_{0}<\infty
independent of
(n,p)
such that

\eta<\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})\leq\lambda_{1}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})<\eta^{-1},\qquad c_{0}p\leq\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})\leq c_{0}^{-1}p,

(3.15)

and the diagonal entries of
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
are bounded away from zero and infinity.

Assumption 3.6

(Sub-Gaussian inverse radius and SSCM contraction)
.

Let
r_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}
, let
\zeta_{k}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-k})
, and define

\nu_{i}=\zeta_{1}^{-1}r_{i}^{-1}
. Assume that

\zeta_{k}\zeta_{1}^{-k}\leq C_{\zeta},\qquad k=1,2,3,4,

(3.16)

that
\nu_{i}
is sub-Gaussian with
\left\lVert\nu_{i}\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{\nu}
, and that the
population SSCM satisfies

\limsup_{p\to\infty}\left\lVert\bm{S}_{\mathrm{sgn}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}<1-\psi

(3.17)

for some
\psi>0
.

3.3
Classical low-dimensional covariance, shape, and sphericity inference

3.3.1
Wishart geometry and the covariance likelihood ratio

We begin with the classical normal model. Let

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
with
p
fixed, and define

\bar{\bm{X}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i},\qquad\bm{S}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(3.18)

Under Assumption
3.1
, the scaled sample covariance

n\bm{S}_{n}
follows a Wishart law:

n\bm{S}_{n}\sim W_{p}(n-1,\bm{\Sigma}).

(3.19)

For testing

H_{0}:\ \bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}_{0}

against the unrestricted alternative, the Gaussian likelihood ratio takes the form

\Lambda_{n}=\frac{\sup_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}_{0}}L(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})}{\sup_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma}\succ 0}L(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})}=\exp\Big\{-\frac{n}{2}\big[\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\bm{S}_{n})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\bm{S}_{n})-p\big]\Big\}.

(3.20)

Hence the usual likelihood-ratio statistic is

T_{\mathrm{LRT}}=n\Big[\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\bm{S}_{n})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\bm{S}_{n})-p\Big].

(3.21)

Theorem 3.1

(Fixed-
p
covariance likelihood ratio)
.

Under Assumption
3.1
and
H_{0}:\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
,

T_{\mathrm{LRT}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{p(p+1)/2}

(3.22)

as
n\to\infty
with
p
fixed.

The statistic (
3.21
) is the fixed-dimensional benchmark for covariance
testing. It is optimal under Gaussianity but becomes unusable in high dimensions because

\log\det(\bm{S}_{n})
is undefined when
p\geq n
and because the calibration changes
substantially when
p/n
does not vanish.

3.3.2
Low-dimensional sphericity tests: Mauchly, John, and Nagao

The fixed-dimensional sphericity problem tests

H_{0}:\ \bm{\Sigma}=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p}\qquad\text{against}\qquad H_{1}:\ \bm{\Sigma}\neq\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p},

(3.23)

where
\sigma^{2}>0
is unspecified.

The classical likelihood-ratio statistic is based on

V_{n}=\frac{\det(\bm{S}_{n})}{\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S}_{n})/p\}^{p}},

(3.24)

which is the ratio between the geometric mean and the arithmetic mean of the sample
eigenvalues. Small values of
V_{n}
indicate departures from sphericity.

Theorem 3.2

(Mauchly’s likelihood-ratio test)
.

Under Assumption
3.1
and the null
(
3.23
),

-\rho_{n}(n-1)\log V_{n}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{(p-1)(p+2)/2},

(3.25)

where the Bartlett correction factor is

\rho_{n}=1-\frac{2p^{2}+p+2}{6(p+1)(n-1)}.

(3.26)

Mauchly’s statistic is powerful against broad alternatives, but in finite samples it is
notoriously sensitive to nonnormality. John’s trace statistic avoids the determinant and
turns out to be the key bridge to high-dimensional sphericity testing.

Define

U_{J}=\frac{1}{p}\operatorname{tr}\Big\{\frac{\bm{S}_{n}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S}_{n})/p}-\bm{I}_{p}\Big\}^{2}=\frac{p\,\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S}_{n}^{2})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{S}_{n})}-1.

(3.27)

John’s statistic is a quadratic measure of the dispersion of the empirical eigenvalues
around their common mean.

Theorem 3.3

(John’s trace test)
.

Under Assumption
3.1
and the null
(
3.23
),

\frac{n(p+2)}{2}U_{J}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{(p-1)(p+2)/2}

(3.28)

as
n\to\infty
with
p
fixed.

A closely related statistic is Nagao’s test for

H_{0}:\ \bm{\Sigma}=\bm{I}_{p},

which uses

V_{N}=\frac{1}{p}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{S}_{n}-\bm{I}_{p})^{2}.

(3.29)

Under fixed
p
and Gaussian sampling,

npV_{N}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{p(p+1)/2}.

(3.30)

The exact centering and scaling depend on whether the scale
\sigma^{2}
is known or
estimated, but the main point is that low-dimensional sphericity can be studied either
through a determinant ratio or through quadratic trace functionals.

3.3.3
Classical sign and rank shape tests

The fixed-
p
robust literature replaces the sample covariance matrix by a matrix of
multivariate signs or ranks. Let
