\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Sigma}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}(r_{n}+h^{-\alpha}).

(3.207)

Using the identity

\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathrm{HR}})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})=\log\det\!\left(\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Sigma}_{0})\right)

(3.208)

and the bound
|\log\det(\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{A})|\leq 2p\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
for sufficiently small

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
, we obtain

\left\lvert\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathrm{HR}})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})\right\rvert=O_{P}\!\left(p(r_{n}+h^{-\alpha})\right),

(3.209)

which proves (
3.155
). The small-
o_{P}(1)
statement
(
3.156
) is immediate.

Chapter 4
Other High-Dimensional Testing Problems

4.1
Introduction

The first three chapters of this book dealt with location parameters and matrix
structure. A substantial part of the recent literature on high-dimensional
inference, however, lies outside these two categories. The purpose of the
present chapter is to treat a number of important testing problems that are
methodologically linked but arise from rather different applications. The five
main topics are

1.

testing zero pricing errors in linear factor pricing models;

2.

selecting skilled funds by multiple testing with false discovery rate control;

3.

testing structural breaks and change points in high-dimensional sequences;

4.

testing high-dimensional white noise and serial uncorrelatedness;

5.

testing various forms of high-dimensional independence.

The common theme is that each problem admits three layers of analysis.
First, there is a low-dimensional classical theory in which exact or standard
large-sample distributions can be derived from Wishart theory, multivariate
central limit theorems, or Brownian bridge approximations. Second, there is a
high-dimensional Gaussian or light-tailed benchmark theory in which one chooses
between dense-signal statistics and sparse-signal statistics. Third, there is
an elliptical or robust extension in which sample means and sample covariance
matrices are replaced by sign, rank, spatial-median, or self-normalized
quantities. The detailed writing principle adopted in Chapter 2 will also be
used here: for each problem we state the statistic explicitly, record the
assumptions inside the book, formulate the limiting distributions carefully, and
place detailed proofs in the appendix to the chapter.

Throughout this chapter, all theorem-like environments are numbered separately,
as required by the book-wide style. In particular, assumptions, lemmas,
propositions and theorems each have their own chapter-based numbering scheme.

4.2
A reusable max–sum paradigm

Before entering the four applications, it is useful to record a generic
construction that will be reused repeatedly. Let
\{\mathcal{T}_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\}
be a family of local statistics extracted from a high-dimensional
problem. Two aggregations are particularly common:

T_{\max}=\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\phi_{a}(\mathcal{T}_{a}),\qquad T_{\mathrm{sum}}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\psi_{a}(\mathcal{T}_{a}),

(4.1)

where the transformations
\phi_{a}
and
\psi_{a}
are chosen to stabilize scale
and centering. The statistic
T_{\max}
is typically powerful against sparse
alternatives, whereas
T_{\mathrm{sum}}
is typically powerful against dense
alternatives. Once the corresponding null
p
-values

p_{\max}=1-F_{\max}(T_{\max}),\qquad p_{\mathrm{sum}}=1-F_{\mathrm{sum}}(T_{\mathrm{sum}}).

(4.2)

are available, an adaptive combination can be built from either the Fisher rule

T_{\mathrm{F}}=-2\log p_{\max}-2\log p_{\mathrm{sum}},

(4.3)

or the truncated Cauchy rule

T_{\mathrm{C}}=\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\max})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\max}<1/2)+\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\mathrm{sum}})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\mathrm{sum}}<1/2).

(4.4)

The mathematical reason for the success of (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
) is that, after proper studentization and centering,

T_{\max}
and
T_{\mathrm{sum}}
are frequently asymptotically independent.
The results of
38
formalize this idea
for a broad class of dependent random variables, and that theory now serves as a
common backbone for a series of tests in finance, time series, and panel data.

Assumption 4.1
.

For a sequence of problems indexed by
(n,p)
, suppose that there exist
non-random normalizing constants
a_{n,p},b_{n,p},\mu_{n,p},\sigma_{n,p}>0
such
that under the null hypothesis,

a_{n,p}(T_{\max}-b_{n,p})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}G,\qquad\frac{T_{\mathrm{sum}}-\mu_{n,p}}{\sigma_{n,p}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.5)

where
G
has a continuous distribution function
F_{G}
. In addition, assume
that for every fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{a_{n,p}(T_{\max}-b_{n,p})\leq x,\frac{T_{\mathrm{sum}}-\mu_{n,p}}{\sigma_{n,p}}\leq y\right\}-F_{G}(x)\Phi(y)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.6)

Theorem 4.1
.

Under Assumption
4.1
,

T_{\mathrm{F}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{4}

(4.7)

whenever
p_{\max}
and
p_{\mathrm{sum}}
are continuous null
p
-values. If
instead the Cauchy combination statistic (
4.4
) is used,
then for every fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathrm{C}}\leq t)-F_{\mathrm{C}}(t)\longrightarrow 0,

(4.8)

where
F_{\mathrm{C}}
is the distribution function of a standard Cauchy random
variable truncated at level
1/2
in the same way as
(
4.4
).

The proof is elementary once the asymptotic independence is available, but we
still record it in the appendix because exactly the same argument will be reused
several times. The point of including this generic theorem here is not to avoid
the problem-specific arguments, but to make explicit that most adaptive
procedures in this chapter are concrete realizations of the same template.

4.3
Testing alpha in linear factor pricing models

4.3.1
Model and hypothesis

Let
Y_{it}
denote the excess return of security
i
at time
t
, and let

f_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
denote the vector of observed factors. The linear factor pricing
model is written as

Y_{it}=\alpha_{i}+\bm{\beta}_{i}^{\top}f_{t}+\varepsilon_{it},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,\quad t=1,\ldots,T.

(4.9)

Define the vector form

\bm{Y}_{t}=\bm{\alpha}+\bm{B}f_{t}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,T,

(4.10)

where
\bm{Y}_{t}=(Y_{1t},\ldots,Y_{Nt})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N})^{\top}
, and
\bm{B}=(\bm{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\beta}_{N})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K}
.
The null hypothesis of no pricing error is

H_{0,\alpha}:\ \bm{\alpha}=\bm{0},\qquad H_{1,\alpha}:\ \bm{\alpha}\neq\bm{0}.

(4.11)

In the CAPM this is the mean–variance efficiency problem of

56
.

For matrix notation, let

\bm{Y}=(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times T},\qquad\bm{F}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{T})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K},

(4.12)

and define the projection and residual-maker matrices

\bm{P}_{F}=\bm{F}(\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{F})^{-1}\bm{F}^{\top},\qquad\bm{M}_{F}=\bm{I}_{T}-\bm{P}_{F},\qquad\bm{h}=\bm{M}_{F}\bm{1}_{T}.

(4.13)

Then
\bm{h}
is the residual from regressing the intercept vector
\bm{1}_{T}
on the
factor matrix. The ordinary least-squares estimator of
\bm{\alpha}
is

\hat{\bm{\alpha}}=(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{-1}\bm{Y}\bm{h}.

(4.14)

4.3.2
Classical low-dimensional tests

When
N
and
K
are fixed and
T\to\infty
, the classical reference point is
the Gibbons–Ross–Shanken statistic. Let

\bar{f}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}f_{t},\qquad\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{f}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f_{t}-\bar{f})(f_{t}-\bar{f})^{\top},

(4.15)

and let the residual covariance matrix be

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{T-K-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t})(\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t})^{\top},

(4.16)

where
\hat{\bm{B}}=\bm{Y}\bm{F}(\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{F})^{-1}
. The GRS statistic is

T_{\mathrm{GRS}}=\frac{T-N-K}{N}\frac{\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}}{1+\bar{f}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{f}^{-1}\bar{f}}.

(4.17)

Assumption 4.2
.

The vectors
(\bm{Y}_{t}^{\top},f_{t}^{\top})^{\top}
are independently and identically
distributed as multivariate normal random vectors with finite positive-definite
covariance matrix. In addition,
T>N+K
and
\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{F}
is invertible almost
surely.

Theorem 4.2
.

Under Assumption
4.2
and the null hypothesis
(
4.11
),

T_{\mathrm{GRS}}\sim F_{N,\,T-N-K}.

(4.18)

Consequently, the size-
\gamma
GRS procedure rejects
H_{0,\alpha}
whenever

T_{\mathrm{GRS}}>F_{N,T-N-K;1-\gamma}
, where
F_{a,b;1-\gamma}
is the

(1-\gamma)
-quantile of the
F_{a,b}
distribution.

The next classical benchmark is the likelihood-ratio or Wald-type statistic under
elliptical fixed-dimensional models. When
(\bm{Y}_{t}^{\top},f_{t}^{\top})^{\top}
is
elliptically distributed, one can still base inference on
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}
and a
shape estimator of the error distribution. The exact form depends on the
parametric or semiparametric model; see
65
. Since the purpose of the book is high-dimensional
inference, we do not reproduce those fixed-
N
exact tests here. Instead, we
use the GRS statistic as the classical reference point and then explain why it
fails when
N
is comparable to or much larger than
T
.

4.3.3
High-dimensional Gaussian and light-tailed benchmarks

When
N
diverges with
T
, the matrix
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
becomes ill
conditioned or singular, so the exact GRS statistic is no longer usable. There
are two broad directions in the literature. One uses global quadratic forms or
random projections and is most effective under dense alternatives; the other
uses a max-of-squares or Studentized maximum statistic and is most effective
under sparse alternatives. See
74
,

40
, and
110

for representative approaches.

A canonical sparse benchmark starts from the Studentized coordinates

\hat{t}_{i}=\frac{\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\alpha}_{i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}\sqrt{\hat{\omega}}},\qquad\hat{\omega}=T(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{-1},\qquad\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{T-K-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}^{2},

(4.19)

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}=Y_{it}-\hat{\alpha}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\top}f_{t}
. The
maximum statistic is

T_{\max,\alpha}=\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\hat{t}_{i}^{2}.

(4.20)

A dense benchmark is the quadratic aggregate

T_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}=\frac{T\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}-N}{\{2\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\}^{1/2}},\qquad\hat{\bm{D}}=\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{2},\ldots,\hat{\sigma}_{N}^{2}),\qquad\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}\bm{D}^{-1/2}.

(4.21)

Under suitable Gaussian or sub-Gaussian assumptions, the centered maximum in
(
4.20
) has a type-I extreme-value limit, while the
quadratic form in (
4.21
) is asymptotically normal.
The explicit robust counterpart below replaces
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}
and

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
by sign-based estimators that remain valid under
elliptical heavy tails.

Assumption 4.3

(Gaussian and light-tailed benchmark conditions)
.

Assumption
4.6
holds. In addition, the error vectors

\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
are independent over
t
with
\mathbb{E}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})=\bm{0}
,

\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})=\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}
, and positive diagonal scale matrix

\bm{D}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{N}^{2})
.

Assumption 4.4
.

As
\min(N,T)\to\infty
,

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad\frac{N^{2}}{T^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0,

(4.22)

and either
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
is Gaussian or the standardized coordinates

\sigma_{i}^{-1}\varepsilon_{it}
are uniformly sub-Gaussian.

Assumption 4.5
.

There exists
\rho_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\max_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|r_{ij}|\leq\rho_{0}
.
Moreover, there are sequences
\delta_{N}\downarrow 0
and
\kappa_{N}\downarrow 0
with

\delta_{N}=o\{(\log N)^{-1}\}
such that, writing

B_{N,i}=\{j:|r_{ij}|\geq\delta_{N}\}
and

C_{N}=\{i:|B_{N,i}|\geq N^{\kappa_{N}}\}
,

\frac{|C_{N}|}{N}\longrightarrow 0.

(4.23)

Theorem 4.3

(Gaussian and light-tailed benchmark limits)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.3
–
4.5
hold.
Under the null hypothesis
H_{0,\alpha}
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max,\alpha}-2\log N+\log\log N\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\!\left\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\right\},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.25)

Under local alternatives satisfying

T\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}=O\!\bigl(\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})\bigr),

(4.26)

one has

T_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(\mu_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha},1),\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}=\lim_{\min(N,T)\to\infty}\frac{T\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}},

(4.27)

and the max test is consistent whenever

\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{T}}

(4.28)

for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
.

Let

p_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}=1-\Phi\bigl(T_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}\bigr),\qquad p_{\max,\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}=1-G_{\mathrm{EV}}\bigl(T_{\max,\alpha}-2\log N+\log\log N\bigr),

(4.29)

where
G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}
. The benchmark
adaptive Cauchy combination is

T_{\mathrm{Ada},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}=\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}})\}+\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\max,\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}})\}.

(4.30)

Proposition 4.1

(A benchmark adaptive combination)
.

Suppose, in addition to Assumptions
4.3
–
4.5
,
that
T_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}
and

T_{\max,\alpha}-2\log N+\log\log N
are asymptotically independent under

H_{0,\alpha}
. Then

T_{\mathrm{Ada},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Cauchy}(0,1),

(4.31)

and the induced Cauchy-combination
p
-value is asymptotically valid.

4.3.4
A robust spatial-sign sum-type test

We now review the spatial-sign procedure of

83
. Let

\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,T,

(4.32)

so that under the model (
4.10
),
\bm{Z}_{t}
is a location-shifted
version of the error vector. Let
\bm{D}=\operatorname{diag}(d_{1}^{2},\ldots,d_{N}^{2})
denote the
marginal scale matrix, and let
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}\bm{D}^{-1/2}

be the correlation matrix associated with the error distribution.
Define the spatial sign

U(\bm{x})=\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert},\qquad U(\bm{0})=\bm{0}.

(4.33)

The sign-transformed residuals are

\bm{U}_{t}=U\bigl(\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t})\bigr),\qquad t=1,\ldots,T.

(4.34)

The basic quadratic form proposed in
83
is

Q_{\alpha}=\frac{N}{\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t_{1},t_{2}\leq T\\
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\end{subarray}}h_{t_{1}}h_{t_{2}}\,\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}}.

(4.35)

The variance of
Q_{\alpha}
is asymptotically
2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})
. A leave-two-out
estimator of
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})
is obtained by

\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}=\frac{N^{2}}{(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},t_{2}=1\\
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\end{subarray}}^{T}h_{t_{1}}^{2}h_{t_{2}}^{2}\bigl\{U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{t_{1}}^{(t_{1},t_{2})})^{\top}U(\hat{\bm{D}}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{t_{2}}^{(t_{1},t_{2})})\bigr\}^{2},

(4.36)

where the superscript
(t_{1},t_{2})
indicates that the corresponding observations
are left out in the scale estimation step. The studentized statistic is

T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}=\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{\{2\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\}^{1/2}}.

(4.37)

The assumptions below are stated in the notation of the book and are sufficient
for the null and local-alternative theory. They are adapted from the conditions
used in
83
.

Assumption 4.6
.

The factor process
\{f_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
is strictly stationary with finite second
moments. The matrix
T^{-1}\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{F}
converges to a positive-definite limit

\bm{Q}_{f}
, and

T^{-1}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}\longrightarrow\omega,\qquad\omega=1-\mathbb{E}(f_{t})^{\top}\{\mathbb{E}(f_{t}f_{t}^{\top})\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}(f_{t})\in(0,1].

(4.38)

Assumption 4.7
.

The error vectors satisfy

\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}=\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\bm{W}_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,T,

(4.39)

where
\bm{W}_{t}
are independent and identically distributed spherically symmetric
random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\mathbb{P}(\bm{W}_{t}=\bm{0})=0
. Let

c_{1}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rVert^{-1}\bigr),\qquad c_{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rVert^{-2}\bigr),

(4.40)

which are finite.

Assumption 4.8
.

As
\min(N,T)\to\infty
,

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad\frac{N^{2}}{T^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad\frac{\max_{1\leq i\leq N}r_{ii}^{2}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0.

(4.41)

Assumption 4.9
.

Under a sequence of local alternatives,

\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}=O\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})}{TN}\right),\qquad\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{R}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}=o\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}{TN}\right).

(4.42)

Theorem 4.4
.

Under Assumptions
4.6
–
4.8
and the null
hypothesis
H_{0,\alpha}
,

T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)

(4.43)

as
\min(N,T)\to\infty
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Under Assumptions
4.6
–
4.9
,

T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(\mu_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha},1),

(4.44)

where

\mu_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}=\lim_{\min(N,T)\to\infty}\frac{\phi_{\alpha}^{2}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}},\qquad\phi_{\alpha}^{2}=\omega TNc_{1}^{2}\,\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}.

(4.45)

Consequently, the asymptotic power at one-sided level
\gamma
is

\beta_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}(\gamma)=1-\Phi\{z_{1-\gamma}-\mu_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\}.

(4.46)

The key feature of (
4.45
) is that only the diagonal
scale matrix
\bm{D}
and the elliptical sign constant
c_{1}
appear in the signal
term. The test therefore remains valid even when fourth moments are absent.
This is the essential gain over covariance-based procedures.

4.3.5
Weighted spatial-sign tests and the INST procedure

The unweighted statistic (
4.37
) is optimal only for a specific radial
configuration.
153
broadened the construction to a
class of weighted spatial-sign tests and showed that inverse-norm weighting is locally
optimal within that class. Their work is the direct alpha-testing analogue of the
weighted sign methodology that we studied in Chapter 2.

For a measurable weight function
K:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, define

r_{t}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t})\right\rVert,\qquad\hat{\psi}_{2,K}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}K^{2}(r_{t}),

(4.47)

and the weighted quadratic form

Q_{K,\alpha}=\frac{N}{\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t_{1},t_{2}\leq T\\
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\end{subarray}}h_{t_{1}}h_{t_{2}}K(r_{t_{1}})K(r_{t_{2}})\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}}.

(4.48)

The associated studentized statistic is

T_{K,\alpha}=\frac{Q_{K,\alpha}}{\{2\hat{\psi}_{2,K}^{2}\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\}^{1/2}}.

(4.49)

Let

\psi_{1,K}=\mathbb{E}\{K(r_{t})r_{t}^{-1}\},\qquad\psi_{2,K}=\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{t})\},

(4.50)

where the expectation is taken under the elliptical radial model of
Assumption
4.7
.

Theorem 4.6

(Weighted spatial-sign alpha tests)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.6
–
4.9
hold and that

\psi_{2,K}<\infty
. Then under
H_{0,\alpha}
,

T_{K,\alpha}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(4.51)

Under local alternatives satisfying Assumption
4.9
,

T_{K,\alpha}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(\mu_{K,\alpha},1),\qquad\mu_{K,\alpha}=\lim_{\min(N,T)\to\infty}\frac{\omega TN\psi_{1,K}^{2}\,\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}}{\{2\psi_{2,K}^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}.

(4.52)

Proposition 4.2

(Optimality of inverse-norm weighting)
.

For every admissible weight
K
,

\frac{\psi_{1,K}^{2}}{\psi_{2,K}}\leq\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-2}),

(4.53)

with equality if and only if
K(t)=c\,t^{-1}
almost surely for some nonzero
constant
c
. Consequently, the locally most powerful member of the weighted
spatial-sign family is obtained by setting
K(t)=t^{-1}
, which yields the
inverse-norm sign test (INST)

T_{\mathrm{INST},\alpha}=T_{K,\alpha}\big|_{K(t)=t^{-1}}.

(4.54)

4.3.6
A robust max-type test and its adaptive combination

To gain power under sparse alternatives, one needs a coordinatewise statistic.
The recent paper
154
proceeds by using the
scaled spatial median of the transformed residuals. Let
\bm{Z}_{t}
be defined by
(
4.32
). The pair
(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})
is defined as the
solution to the equations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\bm{\theta})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bm{0},

(4.55)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{diag}\!\left[U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\bm{\theta})\bigr)U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\top}\right]

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\bm{I}_{N}.

(4.56)

The estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
is a robust analogue of the OLS alpha estimator.
The max-type statistic is then

T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}=T\hat{\zeta}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N,

(4.57)

where
\hat{\zeta}
is a consistent estimator of the nuisance constant

\zeta=\frac{N}{\{\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})\}^{2}}\Bigl[1-2\eta\,\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})\mathbb{E}(r_{t})+\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{2})\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-2})\Bigr]^{-1},

(4.58)

with
r_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\bm{\theta})\right\rVert
and

\eta=T^{-1}\bm{1}_{T}^{\top}\bm{F}(\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{F})^{-1}\bm{F}^{\top}\bm{1}_{T}
.
A sample version of (
4.58
) is obtained by replacing the
expectations of
r_{t},r_{t}^{-1},r_{t}^{2},r_{t}^{-2}
with their empirical averages
computed from

\tilde{r}_{t}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\right\rVert.

(4.59)

The assumptions below follow the same scaling convention as in the original
paper, but are stated directly in the notation of this book.

Assumption 4.10
.

The factor process satisfies Assumption
4.6
. In addition,

\log N=o(T^{1/10})
and
\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_{it})
is bounded
uniformly in
(N,T)
.

Assumption 4.11
.

The residual vectors admit the decomposition

\bm{Z}_{t}=\omega_{T}T^{-1}\bm{\alpha}+r_{t}\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{U}_{t},

(4.60)

where
\omega_{T}=\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}/T
,
r_{t}>0
,
\bm{U}_{t}
is uniformly distributed on

\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
, and
r_{t}
is independent of
\bm{U}_{t}
. The moments

\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{a})
are finite for
a\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}
.

Assumption 4.12
.

The correlation matrix
\bm{R}
satisfies

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|r_{ij}|\leq\rho_{0}<1,\qquad\sum_{j=1}^{N}|r_{ij}|^{q}\leq C_{q}\quad\text{for some }q\in[0,1),

(4.61)

uniformly in
i
, and

\min_{1\leq i\leq N}d_{i}^{2}\geq c_{0},\qquad\max_{1\leq i\leq N}d_{i}^{2}\leq C_{0}

(4.62)

for positive constants
c_{0}
and
C_{0}
.

Assumption 4.13
.

Under the null hypothesis,

\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\alpha_{i}|=0.

(4.63)

Under sparse alternatives used for power calculations, there exists a set

\mathcal{A}\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
with cardinality
s_{N}=|\mathcal{A}|
such that

\alpha_{i}\neq 0\iff i\in\mathcal{A},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{T}},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{2}=O\!\left(\frac{N\log N}{T}\right)

(4.64)

for some constant
C>0
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Under Assumptions
4.10
–
4.13
and the null
hypothesis,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\leq x\bigr)\longrightarrow\exp\!\left\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\right\},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.65)

That is, the statistic (
4.57
) converges to a type-I extreme
value distribution.

Theorem 4.8
.

Under Assumptions
4.10
–
4.13
, if the sparse
signal condition in (
4.64
) holds with a sufficiently large
constant
C
, then for every fixed significance level
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}_{H_{1,\alpha}}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}>q_{1-\gamma}^{\mathrm{EV}}\bigr)\longrightarrow 1,

(4.66)

where
q_{1-\gamma}^{\mathrm{EV}}
is the
(1-\gamma)
-quantile of the limit in
(
4.65
).

The asymptotic independence between the dense-oriented statistic

T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}
and the sparse-oriented statistic
T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}

leads to an adaptive procedure. Let

p_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}=1-\Phi(T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}),\qquad p_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}=1-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}),

(4.67)

where
G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}
. The truncated Cauchy
combination is

T_{\mathrm{CC},\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}<1/2)+\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}<1/2).

(4.68)

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4.6
–
4.9
and

4.10
–
4.13
hold, that

\log N=o(T^{1/10})
, and that the sparse alternative in
(
4.64
) is satisfied. Then for every fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\leq x,T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\leq y\bigr)-\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\leq x\bigr)\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\leq y\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.69)

Consequently, the combination statistic (
4.68
) has a valid null
limit given by Theorem
4.1
.

4.3.7
Temporal dependence and
L_{q}
extensions for unconditional models

The basic spatial-sign and max-type procedures have already been extended in
several directions for unconditional linear factor models. Under temporal
dependence of the returns,
94
and

95
replace the independent residual theory
by long-run covariance normalization and still obtain dense-, sparse-, and
adaptive tests. The most recent
L_{q}
-type family of

156
interpolates between sum-type and max-type
statistics by considering

T_{q,\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\hat{\theta}_{i}|^{q},\qquad 2\leq q<\infty,

(4.70)

with
q
controlling the degree of sparsity sensitivity. These extensions fit
naturally into the max–sum paradigm introduced at the beginning of this
chapter.

4.4
Robust mutual fund selection with false discovery rate control

The global alpha tests in Section 4.3 answer the question whether
all
pricing
errors vanish jointly. In empirical fund evaluation, however, the inferential target is
usually finer: one wants to identify the individual funds with positive alpha while
controlling the false discovery rate (FDR). This leads to a large-scale one-sided testing
problem in which the same heavy-tailed features that motivated spatial-sign global testing
also invalidate standard Gaussian multiple-testing procedures.

Suppose again that returns follow the linear factor pricing model

Y_{it}=\alpha_{i}+\bm{\beta}_{i}^{\top}f_{t}+\varepsilon_{it},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,\quad t=1,\ldots,T,

(4.71)

with observable factor vector
f_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
. For mutual fund selection, the relevant
multiple hypotheses are

H_{0i}:\ \alpha_{i}\leq 0,\qquad H_{1i}:\ \alpha_{i}>0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

(4.72)

The one-sided formulation is natural because positive alpha corresponds to superior stock
selection ability, whereas large negative alpha is not a discovery of practical interest.

4.4.1
Observable factors: the spatial-sign BH procedure

Let
\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}=Y_{it}-\hat{\alpha}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\top}f_{t}
be the OLS residuals from
Section 4.3, and write
\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}=(\hat{\varepsilon}_{1t},\ldots,\hat{\varepsilon}_{Nt})^{\top}
. Following the robust alpha-testing literature, estimate a
location vector
\bm{\theta}
and a diagonal scale matrix
\bm{D}
by solving

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}

\displaystyle=\bm{0},

(4.73)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{diag}\Bigl[U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}^{\top}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\bm{I}_{N}.

(4.74)

Let
(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})
denote the solution,
\hat{d}_{i}
the
i
th diagonal entry of

\hat{\bm{D}}
, and

\hat{r}_{t}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\hat{\zeta}_{1}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{r}_{t}^{-1},\qquad\hat{\omega}=T^{-1}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}.

(4.75)

The fundwise spatial-sign statistics are then

Z_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}=\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\zeta}_{1}\,\hat{d}_{i}^{-1/2}\hat{\theta}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

(4.76)

and the corresponding one-sided
p
-values are

p_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}=1-\Phi\bigl(Z_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}\bigr),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

(4.77)

For a target FDR level
q\in(0,1)
, let
p_{(1)}^{\mathrm{SS}}\leq\cdots\leq p_{(N)}^{\mathrm{SS}}

be the ordered
p
-values and define

\hat{k}_{\mathrm{SS}}=\max\Bigl\{1\leq k\leq N:p_{(k)}^{\mathrm{SS}}\leq qk/N\Bigr\},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{SS}}(q)=\Bigl\{i:p_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}\leq p_{(\hat{k}_{\mathrm{SS}})}^{\mathrm{SS}}\Bigr\},

(4.78)

with the convention
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{SS}}(q)=\varnothing
if the maximizing set is empty.
This is the spatial-sign Benjamini–Hochberg procedure (SS-BH).

Assumption 4.14
.

The observable-factor model (
4.71
) holds. The error vectors

\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
are independent over
t
, centered, elliptically symmetric, and satisfy the
same radial-moment and weak cross-sectional dependence conditions as in
Assumptions
4.10
–
4.13
. In particular, the diagonal scale
matrix
\bm{D}
is positive definite and the correlation matrix

\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
obeys the sparse-correlation condition
(
4.23
). Moreover,
\log N=o(T^{1/10})
.

Theorem 4.10

(Observable-factor spatial-sign multiple testing)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.14
holds. Let

\bm{U}_{t}=U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})
, let
\bm{W}=(\bm{1}_{T},\bm{F})
be the full design matrix,
and write
V_{st}
for the
(s,t)
entry of

\bm{P}_{W}=\bm{W}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}
. Then

\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\zeta}_{1}\,\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\hat{\omega}\bm{\alpha})=T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta_{1}^{-1}\Bigl(1-\sum_{s=1}^{T}r_{s}^{-1}r_{t}V_{st}\Bigr)\bm{U}_{t}+\bm{C}_{T},

(4.79)

where
\zeta_{1}=\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})
and

\left\lVert\bm{C}_{T}\right\rVert_{\infty}=o_{P}\bigl((\log N)^{-1/2}\bigr).

(4.80)

Consequently, there exists a Gaussian vector
\bm{G}\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{\Xi})
such that

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\zeta}_{1}\,\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\hat{\omega}\bm{\alpha})\leq z\bigr)-\mathbb{P}(\bm{G}\leq z)\Bigr|\longrightarrow 0,

(4.81)

with
\Xi_{ii}=1
and
\max_{i\neq j}|\Xi_{ij}|\leq\rho_{0}<1
. In particular, under

H_{0i}:\alpha_{i}\leq 0
, each null
p
-value in (
4.77
) is asymptotically
conservative, and the BH procedure (
4.78
) satisfies

\operatorname*{FDR}\bigl\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{SS}}(q)\bigr\}\leq q+o(1).

(4.82)

If, moreover,
\alpha_{i}\geq C\sqrt{(\log N)/T}
on a nonvanishing set of truly skilled funds,
then the corresponding coordinates are selected with probability tending to one.

4.4.2
Latent factors: factor-adjusted spatial-sign BH

When omitted or latent factors are present, directly applying SS-BH to the residuals from the
observable-factor regression may be overly conservative because the resulting
p
-values remain
strongly dependent. The factor-adjusted spatial-sign procedure in

135
therefore combines robust multiple testing with
robust latent-factor extraction. Specifically, suppose the return vector admits the decomposition

\bm{Y}_{t}=\bm{\alpha}+\bm{B}f_{t}+\bm{\Lambda}g_{t}+\bm{u}_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,T,

(4.83)

where
g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is an unobserved factor vector and
\bm{u}_{t}
is the idiosyncratic component.
Under elliptical tails, a robust way to estimate the latent factor space is to apply principal
component analysis to the spatial Kendall’s tau matrix rather than to the sample covariance
matrix, following
62
. Let
\hat{g}_{t}
and

\hat{\bm{\Lambda}}
denote the resulting factor estimates, and define factor-adjusted residuals

\check{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}=\bm{Y}_{t}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{B}}f_{t}-\hat{\bm{\Lambda}}\hat{g}_{t}.

(4.84)

Applying the spatial-sign equations (
4.73
)–(
4.74
) to

\{\check{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
yields a new location estimator

\check{\bm{\theta}}
and diagonal scale matrix
\check{\bm{D}}
, and hence factor-adjusted test statistics

Z_{i}^{\mathrm{FSS}}=\sqrt{T}\,\check{\zeta}_{1}\,\check{d}_{i}^{-1/2}\check{\theta}_{i},\qquad p_{i}^{\mathrm{FSS}}=1-\Phi\bigl(Z_{i}^{\mathrm{FSS}}\bigr),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

(4.85)

The factor-adjusted spatial-sign BH procedure (FSS-BH) is then obtained by applying the BH
step-up rule to the ordered values of
\{p_{i}^{\mathrm{FSS}}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.

Assumption 4.15
.

The latent-factor model (
4.83
) holds with fixed latent factor number

r
. The common component is pervasive, the idiosyncratic errors satisfy the same elliptical
and weak-dependence conditions as in Assumption
4.14
, and the robust factor
estimator obeys

\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\check{\alpha}_{i}-\alpha_{i}|=o_{P}\bigl((T\log N)^{-1/2}\bigr).

(4.86)

Theorem 4.11

(Factor-adjusted spatial-sign multiple testing)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.15
holds. Then the Gaussian approximation
(
4.81
) continues to hold with

\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}},\hat{\zeta}_{1}
replaced by

\check{\bm{\theta}},\check{\bm{D}},\check{\zeta}_{1}
. Consequently, the BH procedure based on the
factor-adjusted
p
-values in (
4.85
) satisfies

\operatorname*{FDR}\bigl\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FSS}}(q)\bigr\}\leq q+o(1).

(4.87)

In heavy-tailed settings with latent dependence, the FSS-BH procedure is therefore asymptotically
valid and typically less conservative than the observable-factor SS-BH procedure.

The main conceptual point is that mutual fund selection is not a different inferential problem
from alpha testing; it is the multiple-testing version of the same location problem. What changes
is the inferential loss function: instead of controlling a single type-I error probability, one
must control the expected proportion of false rejections among all selected funds. The
spatial-sign methodology provides the same robustness benefits in this setting as it does for
global alpha tests.

4.5
Testing alpha in conditional time-varying factor models

4.5.1
Model, spline approximation, and low-dimensional Wald tests

In conditional factor models the pricing error and factor loadings are allowed to vary with
time or with a normalized calendar index. A standard formulation, used in

80
,
4
, and

96
, is

Y_{it}=\alpha_{i}(t/T)+\bm{\beta}_{i}(t/T)^{\top}f_{t}+\varepsilon_{it},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,\quad t=1,\ldots,T.

(4.88)

Write

\delta_{i}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\alpha_{i}(t/T),\qquad\delta_{i}(t/T)=\alpha_{i}(t/T)-\delta_{i}.

(4.89)

Then (
4.88
) becomes

Y_{it}=\delta_{i}+\delta_{i}(t/T)+\bm{\beta}_{i}(t/T)^{\top}f_{t}+\varepsilon_{it},

(4.90)

and the null hypothesis of interest is

H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}:\ \delta_{1}=\cdots=\delta_{N}=0.

(4.91)

Let
B(t/T)=(B_{1}(t/T),\ldots,B_{L}(t/T))^{\top}
be a B-spline basis of order
q
,
let

\tilde{B}_{k}(t/T)=B_{k}(t/T)-T^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{T}B_{k}(s/T),\qquad\tilde{B}(t/T)=(\tilde{B}_{1}(t/T),\ldots,\tilde{B}_{L}(t/T))^{\top},

(4.92)

and define the sieve regressor

Z_{t}=\Bigl(\tilde{B}(t/T)^{\top},\ f_{t}^{\top}\otimes B(t/T)^{\top}\Bigr)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K+1)L}.

(4.93)

Approximating
\delta_{i}(\cdot)
and the components of
\bm{\beta}_{i}(\cdot)
by spline series,
one obtains the working regression

Y_{it}\approx\delta_{i}+\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\top}Z_{t}+\varepsilon_{it},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

(4.94)

with least-squares estimator

\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}=(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})^{-1}\bm{Z}^{\top}Y_{i\cdot},\qquad\bm{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{T})^{\top}.

(4.95)

The residuals are

\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}=Y_{it}-\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\top}Z_{t},\qquad\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}=(\hat{\varepsilon}_{1t},\ldots,\hat{\varepsilon}_{Nt})^{\top}.

(4.96)

When
N
is fixed and
T\to\infty
, the classical nonparametric Wald tests of

80
and

4
are based on the spline or kernel estimator

\hat{\bm{\delta}}=(\hat{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\delta}_{N})^{\top}
and its asymptotic covariance
matrix
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\delta}
. The benchmark statistic is

W_{T,\mathrm{cond}}=T\hat{\bm{\delta}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\delta}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\delta}}.

(4.97)

Under fixed
N
and the usual smoothness and bandwidth conditions,

W_{T,\mathrm{cond}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{N}^{2}
under
(
4.91
). This classical theory breaks down when
N
is of the same order as,
or larger than,
T
.

4.5.2
High-dimensional light-tailed sum, max, and adaptive tests

Following
96
, let

\bm{h}=\bm{M}_{Z}\bm{1}_{T},\qquad\bm{M}_{Z}=\bm{I}_{T}-\bm{Z}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})^{-1}\bm{Z}^{\top},\qquad\omega_{T}=\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}.

(4.98)

The dense-oriented high-dimensional alpha statistic is

J_{NT}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}\Bigr)^{2}.

(4.99)

Writing
\bm{\Omega}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t})
, its standardized version is

T_{\mathrm{HDA},\mathrm{cond}}=\frac{J_{NT}-N^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega})}{\{2N^{-2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})\}^{1/2}}.

(4.100)

A sparse-oriented Gaussian benchmark, used in

93
, is

M_{NT}=\max_{1\leq i\leq N}T^{-1}\hat{\sigma}_{ii}^{-1}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i\cdot}^{\top}\bm{1}_{T})^{2},\qquad\hat{\sigma}_{ij}=\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i\cdot}^{\top}\hat{\varepsilon}_{j\cdot}}{T-K-1}.

(4.101)

The corresponding adaptive benchmark combines the
p
-values of
(
4.100
) and (
4.101
) through the Cauchy rule

p_{\mathrm{Ada},\mathrm{cond}}^{\mathrm{G}}=1-F_{\mathrm{C}}\!\bigl(0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\mathrm{HDA}})\}+0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\mathrm{MNT}})\}\bigr),

(4.102)

where
F_{\mathrm{C}}
is the standard Cauchy distribution function.

Assumption 4.16

(Conditional factor benchmark conditions)
.

The functions
\delta_{i}(\cdot)
and
\beta_{ij}(\cdot)
belong to a Hölder class

\mathcal{H}_{r}
with
r>3/2
. The factor dimension
K
is fixed,

\sup_{t}\left\lVert f_{t}\right\rVert_{2}<\infty
, and
T^{-1}(\bm{1}_{T},\bm{F})^{\top}(\bm{1}_{T},\bm{F})

converges to a positive-definite limit.

Assumption 4.17
.

The error vectors
\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot 1},\ldots,\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot T}
are independent
and identically distributed with mean zero, covariance matrix
\bm{\Omega}
, and uniformly
sub-Gaussian coordinates after marginal standardization. Moreover,

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}\to 0,\qquad\frac{TL^{-2r}N}{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}\to 0,\qquad\frac{\max_{i}\sum_{j}|\omega_{ij}|}{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}\to 0.

(4.103)

Theorem 4.12

(Light-tailed conditional alpha benchmarks)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.16
–
4.17
hold and

H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}
is true. Then

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{HDA},\mathrm{cond}}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.104)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M_{NT}-2\log N+\log\log N\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\!\left\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\right\},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.105)

If, in addition, the centered max statistic and the standardized sum statistic are
asymptotically independent under
H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}
, then the adaptive
combination (
4.102
) is asymptotically valid.

4.5.3
Spatial-sign sum, max, and adaptive procedures

The robust counterpart of
161
starts from the same
residuals (
4.96
) but replaces least-squares quadratic forms by
spatial signs. Let

\bm{U}_{\mathrm{cond}}=\bigl(U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot 1}),\ldots,U(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot T})\bigr)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times N}.

(4.106)

The robust sum-type statistic is

T_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}=\frac{(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{-1}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{U}_{\mathrm{cond}}\bm{U}_{\mathrm{cond}}^{\top}\bm{h}-1}{\{2\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{u}^{2})}\}^{1/2}},

(4.107)

where
\bm{\Sigma}_{u}=\mathbb{E}\{U(\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t})U(\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t})^{\top}\}
and

\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{u}^{2})}
is estimated by the leave-two-out U-statistic of

161
.

To obtain a sparse-oriented robust statistic,
159

proposed to estimate jointly a location vector
\bm{\theta}
and a diagonal scale matrix
\bm{D}

from the residuals by solving

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}

\displaystyle=\bm{0},

(4.108)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{diag}\Bigl[U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\bm{\theta})\}^{\top}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\bm{I}_{N}.

(4.109)

With
\tilde{r}_{t}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\right\rVert
, define

\hat{\varsigma}_{2}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{r}_{t}^{2},\qquad\hat{\varsigma}_{1}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{r}_{t},\qquad\hat{\varsigma}_{-1}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{r}_{t}^{-1},

(4.110)

and

\hat{\zeta}=\frac{N\hat{\varsigma}_{-1}^{2}}{1-2(1-\omega_{T}/T)\hat{\varsigma}_{-1}\hat{\varsigma}_{1}+(1-\omega_{T}/T)\hat{\varsigma}_{2}\hat{\varsigma}_{-1}^{2}}.

(4.111)

The robust max statistic is

T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}=T\hat{\zeta}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N.

(4.112)

The corresponding adaptive Cauchy combination is

p_{\mathrm{CC},\mathrm{cond}}=1-F_{\mathrm{C}}\Bigl[0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\mathrm{CSS}})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\mathrm{CSS}}<1/2)+0.5\tan\{\pi(0.5-p_{\mathrm{CSM}})\}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\mathrm{CSM}}<1/2)\Bigr].

(4.113)

Assumption 4.18

(Robust conditional factor conditions)
.

Assumption
4.16
holds. In addition, the residual vectors admit the
elliptical representation

\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t}=v_{t}\bm{\Gamma}\bm{W}_{t},\qquad\bm{\Omega}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Gamma}^{\top},

(4.114)

where
\bm{W}_{t}
has independent symmetric coordinates with unit variance,
v_{t}\geq 0
is
independent of the spatial sign of
\bm{W}_{t}
, the Orlicz norms of
v_{t}
and the coordinates of

\bm{W}_{t}
are uniformly bounded, and the radial moments

\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-k})
,
k=1,2,3,4
, exist.
Furthermore, with
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Gamma}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
,

\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}|r_{ij}|\leq a_{0}(N),\qquad a_{0}(N)\asymp N^{1-\delta},\qquad 0<\delta\leq\frac{1}{2},

(4.115)

and the sparse-correlation condition (
4.23
) holds with
\bm{R}
in place of the
unconditional correlation matrix.

Theorem 4.13

(Robust tests for conditional time-varying alpha)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.16
and
4.18
hold.
Under
H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.116)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}\leq y\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\!\left\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}\right\},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R},

(4.117)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}\leq x,T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}\leq y\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Phi(x)\exp\!\left\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}\right\}

(4.118)

for every fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consequently, the adaptive
p
-value
(
4.113
) is asymptotically valid.

Under alternatives satisfying

\left\lVert\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{T}},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=O(NT^{-1}L),\qquad\lambda_{\max}(\bm{R})=o\{NL^{-1}(\log N)^{-1}\},

(4.119)

for a sufficiently large constant
C
, the max-type statistic

T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}
is consistent. If, in addition,

\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\delta}=O\!\left(\kappa_{1}^{-2}T^{-1}\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{u}^{2})\}^{1/2}\right),\qquad\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}=o\!\left(\kappa_{1}^{-2}N^{-1}T^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})\right),

(4.120)

where
\kappa_{1}=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t}\right\rVert^{-1}
, then the same asymptotic factorization
holds under the corresponding local alternatives, namely,

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}\leq x,T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}\leq y\bigr)-\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}\leq x\bigr)\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}\leq y\bigr)\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.121)

Consequently, if
\beta_{\mathrm{CSS},\gamma}^{\mathrm{cond}}
,

\beta_{\mathrm{CSM},\gamma}^{\mathrm{cond}}
, and

\beta_{\mathrm{CC},\gamma}^{\mathrm{cond}}
denote the power functions of the sum,
max, and adaptive procedures at level
\gamma
, then for every fixed

\gamma\in(0,1)
,

\beta_{\mathrm{CC},\gamma}^{\mathrm{cond}}\geq\beta_{\mathrm{CSS},\gamma/2}^{\mathrm{cond}}+\beta_{\mathrm{CSM},\gamma/2}^{\mathrm{cond}}-\beta_{\mathrm{CSS},\gamma/2}^{\mathrm{cond}}\beta_{\mathrm{CSM},\gamma/2}^{\mathrm{cond}}+o(1).

(4.122)

The conditional-factor section highlights the same message as the unconditional one: the
sum statistic is the right choice for dense alternatives, the max statistic is the right choice
for sparse alternatives, and the asymptotic independence between the two gives a principled
route to adaptive inference. The difference is that the spline projection introduces an extra
approximation layer, which must be controlled jointly with the high-dimensional probability
bounds.

4.6
High-dimensional change-point inference

4.6.1
Classical low-dimensional change-point tests

Consider first the univariate mean-change model

X_{i}=\mu+\delta\mathbbm{1}(i>\tau)+\varepsilon_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(4.123)

where the unknown location of the change point is
\tau\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Let
S_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
. The classical cumulative-sum (CUSUM) statistic is

C_{n}(k)=n^{-1/2}\bigl(S_{k}-kS_{n}/n\bigr),\qquad k=1,\ldots,n-1.

(4.124)

The test rejects for large values of
\max_{1\leq k\leq n-1}|C_{n}(k)|
. Under
independent mean-zero errors with variance
\sigma^{2}
, one has the Brownian
bridge limit

\sigma^{-1}\max_{1\leq k\leq n-1}|C_{n}(k)|\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\sup_{0<u<1}|B^{\circ}(u)|,

(4.125)

where
B^{\circ}
is a standard Brownian bridge. Variants with weights

\{u(1-u)\}^{-\gamma}
were introduced to improve sensitivity to changes near the
boundaries. Classical references include
106
and the book of

28
.

In the multivariate fixed-dimensional case one applies the same principle to
vector observations
\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, replacing (
4.124
)
by either a quadratic form or a maximum over coordinates. If
p
is fixed, then
continuous mapping applied to the vector-valued partial-sum process yields the
limiting null distribution. When
p
diverges, however, one needs a new
extreme-value or high-dimensional central-limit argument.

4.6.2
High-dimensional Gaussian and light-tailed benchmarks

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
follow the single change-point model

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{\delta}\,\mathbbm{1}(i>\tau)+\bm{\varepsilon}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(4.126)

where
\bm{\delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the mean-shift vector. Write

S_{k,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{ij},\qquad\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\sum_{i=2}^{n}(X_{ij}-X_{i-1,j})^{2},\qquad j=1,\ldots,p.

(4.127)

For
\gamma\in[0,1/2]
, define the weighted coordinatewise CUSUM statistic by

C_{\gamma,j}(k)=\Bigl\{\frac{k}{n}\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}^{-\gamma}\frac{n^{-1/2}\{S_{k,j}-kS_{n,j}/n\}}{\hat{\sigma}_{j}},\qquad 1\leq k\leq n-1.

(4.128)

The sparse-oriented maxima are

M_{n,p}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\max_{1\leq k\leq n-1}|C_{0,j}(k)|,\qquad M_{n,p}^{\dagger}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}|C_{1/2,j}(k)|,

(4.129)

where
\lambda_{n}\to\infty
and
\lambda_{n}/n\to 0
. A dense-oriented aggregate is
obtained by

S_{n,p}=\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}C_{0,j}^{2}(k).

(4.130)

The data-adaptive DMS strategy of
131
uses the
standardized version of (
4.130
) together with

M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
.

Assumption 4.19
.

The error vectors
\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}
are independent and identically distributed
with mean zero and covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}=(\sigma_{jj^{\prime}})_{1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq p}
.
For some constants
0<c<C<\infty
,

c\leq\min_{1\leq j\leq p}\sigma_{jj}^{1/2}\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sigma_{jj}^{1/2}\leq C.

(4.131)

Assumption 4.20
.

Either the coordinates of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}
are Gaussian, or they are uniformly
sub-Gaussian in the sense that there exists
\zeta>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\exp(t\varepsilon_{ij})\leq\exp(\zeta t^{2})\qquad\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ 1\leq j\leq p.

(4.132)

Furthermore, the correlation matrix
\bm{R}=(\rho_{jj^{\prime}})
satisfies

\max_{j\neq j^{\prime}}|\rho_{jj^{\prime}}|\leq\rho_{0}<1,\qquad c\leq\lambda_{\min}(\bm{R})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\bm{R})\leq C.

(4.133)

Assumption 4.21
.

Let
h_{n}=n/\lambda_{n}
. As
(n,p)\to\infty
,

\log p=o(n^{1/3}),\qquad\log h_{n}=o(n^{1/3}),\qquad\lambda_{n}\to\infty,\qquad\lambda_{n}/n\to 0.

(4.134)

Theorem 4.14
.

Under Assumptions
4.19
–
4.21
and the null
hypothesis
\bm{\delta}=\bm{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(A(\log p)M_{n,p}-D(\log p)\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\{-e^{-x}\},

(4.135)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(A(\log(ph_{n}))M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(\log(ph_{n}))\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\{-e^{-x}\},

(4.136)

where

A(x)=(2\log x)^{1/2},\qquad D(x)=2\log x+\frac{1}{2}\log\log x-\frac{1}{2}\log\pi.

(4.137)

Theorem
4.14
is the extreme-value backbone of the sparse
change-point theory. To achieve power against dense alternatives,

131
combine a sum-
L_{2}
statistic with the
weighted max statistic. Let

T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{cp}}=\frac{S_{n,p}-(n+2)p}{V_{n,p}^{1/2}},

(4.138)

where
V_{n,p}
is a consistent estimator of
\operatorname{Var}(S_{n,p})
constructed from
finite differences. Define the max-
L_{\infty}

p
-value by

p_{\max,\mathrm{cp}}=1-G\{A(\log(ph_{n}))M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(\log(ph_{n}))\},

(4.139)

where
G(x)=\exp\{-e^{-x}\}
, and let

p_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{cp}}=1-\Phi(T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{cp}}).

(4.140)

The DMS combination is obtained by either the Fisher rule or the Cauchy rule
introduced earlier.

Theorem 4.15
.

Under Assumptions
4.19
–
4.21
and the null
hypothesis,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(A(\log(ph_{n}))M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(\log(ph_{n}))\leq x,T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{cp}}\leq y\bigr)\longrightarrow\exp\{-e^{-x}\}\,\Phi(y)

(4.141)

for every fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consequently, the DMS combination based on the
two associated
p
-values is asymptotically valid.

4.6.3
Spatial-sign max, sum, and adaptive change-point procedures

The robust change-point procedures of
85

replace sample means by scaled spatial medians and replace raw quadratic forms by
statistics built from spatial signs. This is crucial when the coordinates are heavy tailed,
when fourth moments fail to exist, or when a few large outliers contaminate local CUSUM
aggregates.

For integers
1\leq a\leq b\leq n
, let
(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{a:b},\hat{\bm{D}}_{a:b})
be the solution to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{b-a+1}\sum_{i=a}^{b}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\}

\displaystyle=\bm{0},

(4.142)

\displaystyle\frac{p}{b-a+1}\operatorname{diag}\sum_{i=a}^{b}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\}U\{\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})\}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{p}.

(4.143)

These are the segmentwise scaled spatial median equations. Denote the full-sample solution by

(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})=(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1:n},\hat{\bm{D}}_{1:n})
, let

\hat{r}_{i}=\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\hat{\zeta}_{1}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{r}_{i}^{-1},\qquad h_{n}=\left\{\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}\right)^{-1}-1\right\}^{2},

(4.144)

and write
\zeta_{1}=\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-1})
for the population counterpart.

The max-
L_{\infty}
spatial-sign CUSUM statistics are

\bm{C}_{\gamma}(k)=\Bigl\{\frac{k}{n}\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}^{1-\gamma}n^{1/2}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\bigl(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1:k}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1:n}\bigr),\qquad\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n},

(4.145)

with

M_{n,p}=(1-n^{-1/2})\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\left\lVert\bm{C}_{0}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty},\qquad M_{n,p}^{\dagger}=(1-n^{-1/2})\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\left\lVert\bm{C}_{1/2}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(4.146)

The unweighted statistic
M_{n,p}
is natural for interior changes, while the weighted statistic

M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
stabilizes the variance near the boundaries.

A dense-oriented spatial-sign analogue is built from the full-sample sign process

\hat{\bm{U}}_{i}=U\{\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\theta}})\},\qquad\hat{\bm{S}}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\bm{U}}_{i},\qquad k=1,\ldots,n,

(4.147)

and the weighted partial-sum transforms

\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{\gamma}(k)=\Bigl\{\frac{k}{n}\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}^{-\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\left(\hat{\bm{S}}_{k}-\frac{k}{n}\hat{\bm{S}}_{n}\right),\qquad\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}.

(4.148)

The corresponding max-
L_{2}
statistics are

\displaystyle S_{n,p}

\displaystyle=(1-n^{-1/2})\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\left\{\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{0}(k)^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{0}(k)-\frac{k(n-k)}{n^{2}}p\right\},

(4.149)

\displaystyle S_{n,p}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(1-n^{-1/2})\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\left\{\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{1/2}(k)^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{1/2}(k)-p\right\}.

(4.150)

These
L_{2}
-type procedures are designed for dense changes and provide the robust analogue of
the sum statistic in the Gaussian benchmark section.

Assumption 4.22

(Robust change-point conditions)
.

The observations follow the single change-point model
(
4.126
), and the error vectors
\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}
are independent and
identically distributed from an elliptically symmetric distribution with positive diagonal scale
matrix
\bm{D}
and correlation matrix
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
. The radial moments

\mathbb{E}(r_{i}^{-k})
,
k=1,2,3,4
, exist, and the weak cross-sectional dependence condition
(
4.23
) holds with
p
in place of
N
.

Assumption 4.23
.

For the max-
L_{\infty}
statistics,

\lambda_{n}\asymp n^{\lambda}
for some
0<\lambda<1
,

\log^{7}n=o\bigl(p^{1/6\wedge\eta_{0}/2}\bigr),\qquad\log^{2}p=o\bigl(n^{1/5\wedge\lambda/3}\bigr),

(4.151)

where
\eta_{0}
is the correlation-sparsity exponent in
(
4.23
). For the max-
L_{2}
statistics,

\log p=o(n),\qquad\lambda_{n}\to\infty,\qquad\lambda_{n}/n\to 0.

(4.152)

Theorem 4.16

(Spatial-sign max-
L_{\infty}
limits)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.22
–
4.23
hold.
Under the null hypothesis
\bm{\delta}=\bm{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(2p\zeta_{1}^{2}M_{n,p}^{2}-\log(2p)\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G(x),

(4.153)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(p^{1/2}\zeta_{1}A(p\log h_{n})M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(p\log h_{n})\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G(x),

(4.154)

where
G(x)=\exp\{-\exp(-x)\}
and the functions
A(\cdot)
and
D(\cdot)
are defined in
(
4.137
). Hence the null
p
-values are

p_{M_{n,p}}=1-G\bigl(2p\hat{\zeta}_{1}^{2}M_{n,p}^{2}-\log(2p)\bigr),\qquad p_{M_{n,p}^{\dagger}}=1-G\bigl(p^{1/2}\hat{\zeta}_{1}A(p\log h_{n})M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(p\log h_{n})\bigr).

(4.155)

The max-
L_{2}
statistics have non-Gaussian null limits. Let
V(t)
be a centered Gaussian
process on
[0,1]
with covariance kernel

\operatorname{Cov}\{V(t),V(s)\}=(1-t)^{2}s^{2},\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq 1,

(4.156)

and let
F_{V}
denote the distribution function of
\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}V(t)
. Then

85
proved the following result.

Theorem 4.17

(Spatial-sign max-
L_{2}
limits)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.22
–
4.23
hold and let

\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
. Under the null hypothesis,

\displaystyle\frac{pS_{n,p}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}V(t),

(4.157)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(A\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\frac{pS_{n,p}^{\dagger}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}-D\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\{-2e^{-x}\}.

(4.158)

Accordingly, one may use

p_{S_{n,p}}=1-F_{V}\left(\frac{pS_{n,p}}{2\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}}\right),\qquad p_{S_{n,p}^{\dagger}}=1-G_{2}\Biggl(A\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\frac{pS_{n,p}^{\dagger}}{2\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}}-D\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\Biggr),

(4.159)

where
G_{2}(x)=\exp\{-2e^{-x}\}
and
\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}
is a ratio-consistent estimator of

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})
.

The max-
L_{\infty}
and max-
L_{2}
statistics are complementary: the former targets sparse shifts,
whereas the latter targets dense alternatives. Their adaptive combination therefore mirrors the
same max–sum logic used for alpha testing and white-noise testing.

Theorem 4.18

(Adaptive spatial-sign change-point tests)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.22
–
4.23
hold. Under the null
hypothesis,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(2p\zeta_{1}^{2}M_{n,p}^{2}-\log(2p)\leq x,\frac{pS_{n,p}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\leq y\Bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G(x)F_{V}(y),

(4.160)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl(p^{1/2}\zeta_{1}A(p\log h_{n})M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(p\log h_{n})\leq x,A\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\frac{pS_{n,p}^{\dagger}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}-D\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\leq y\Biggr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G(x)G_{2}(y).

(4.161)

Consequently, the Fisher combinations

p_{M,S}=1-F_{\chi_{4}^{2}}\bigl[-2\{\log p_{M_{n,p}}+\log p_{S_{n,p}}\}\bigr],\qquad p_{M^{\dagger},S^{\dagger}}=1-F_{\chi_{4}^{2}}\bigl[-2\{\log p_{M_{n,p}^{\dagger}}+\log p_{S_{n,p}^{\dagger}}\}\bigr]

(4.162)

are asymptotically valid.

Theorem 4.19

(Local-alternative asymptotic independence)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.22
–
4.23
hold and there is a single
change at location
\tau_{n}
with jump vector
\bm{\delta}_{n}
. Let

A_{n}=\{j:\delta_{n,j}\neq 0\}
be the support of the jump vector. If

|A_{n}|=o\left\{\frac{p}{(\log\log p)^{2}}\wedge\frac{p\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}{\log n}\right\},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\delta}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=o\left(\frac{p^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}{n^{2}}\right),

(4.163)

then the factorization in (
4.160
) and (
4.161
)
continues to hold under the corresponding local alternatives. Hence the Fisher combination
retains its adaptive power advantage under the sparse local alternatives for which the individual
sum- and max-type components remain nondegenerate.

4.6.4
Functional and temporally dependent extensions

The idea of combining sparse and dense evidence is not limited to i.i.d.
observations. The functional method of
147

replaces scalar or vector CUSUMs by adjacent-deviation subspace statistics, while

139
extends adaptive change-point inference to
temporally dependent high-dimensional time series by pairing a max-
L_{2}
test
with an existing max-
L_{\infty}
procedure. These extensions reinforce the
central message of the chapter: the correct normalization changes from problem to
problem, but the max–sum design principle remains stable.

4.7
High-dimensional white-noise testing

4.7.1
Classical low-dimensional portmanteau tests

Let
\{\bm{X}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{n}
be a
p
-variate weakly stationary time series with mean
zero and autocovariance matrices

\bm{\Gamma}(h)=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{t+h}),\qquad h\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.164)

The white-noise hypothesis is

H_{0,\mathrm{WN}}:\ \bm{\Gamma}(h)=\bm{0}\text{ for all }h\geq 1.

(4.165)

When
p=1
, the classical portmanteau tests are the Box–Pierce statistic

Q_{\mathrm{BP}}=n\sum_{h=1}^{L}\hat{\rho}_{h}^{2},

(4.166)

and the Ljung–Box refinement

Q_{\mathrm{LB}}=n(n+2)\sum_{h=1}^{L}\frac{\hat{\rho}_{h}^{2}}{n-h},

(4.167)

where
\hat{\rho}_{h}
is the sample autocorrelation at lag
h
.
Under fixed
L
and white noise,

Q_{\mathrm{BP}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{L}^{2},\qquad Q_{\mathrm{LB}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{L}^{2}.

(4.168)

See
13
and
90
. In multivariate fixed-

p
settings one similarly aggregates traces of sample autocorrelation matrices.
The difficulty in the high-dimensional regime is that both the number of
coordinates and the number of lagged cross-correlations are large.

4.7.2
Gaussian and light-tailed high-dimensional benchmarks

Write the sample lag-
h
autocovariance matrix as

\hat{\bm{\Gamma}}(h)=\frac{1}{n-h}\sum_{t=1}^{n-h}\bm{X}_{t}\bm{X}_{t+h}^{\top},\qquad h=1,\ldots,L.

(4.169)

A dense-oriented benchmark is

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}=\sum_{h=1}^{L}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Gamma}}(h)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

(4.170)

with standardized version

T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}^{\circ}=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}-\mu_{\mathrm{WN}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{WN}}},

(4.171)

where
\mu_{\mathrm{WN}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{WN}}^{2}
are the null mean and variance. The
sparse-oriented benchmark is based on the largest standardized lagged sample correlation. Let

\hat{\rho}_{ij}(h)=\frac{\hat{\gamma}_{ij}(h)}{(\hat{\gamma}_{ii}(0)\hat{\gamma}_{jj}(0))^{1/2}},\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq p,\qquad 1\leq h\leq L,

(4.172)

and define

T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}=\max_{1\leq h\leq L}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p}\sqrt{n}\,|\hat{\rho}_{ij}(h)|.

(4.173)

These two statistics are the white-noise analogues of the dense and sparse alpha tests in
Section 4.3.

Assumption 4.24
.

Under the null hypothesis,
\{\bm{X}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{n}
is a
p
-variate white-noise sequence with
independent mean-zero observations and covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
. After componentwise
standardization, the coordinates are uniformly sub-Gaussian and satisfy

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})}\to 0,\qquad\log(p^{2}L)=o(n^{1/3}).

(4.174)

Theorem 4.20

(Gaussian and light-tailed white-noise benchmarks)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.24
holds and
L=L_{n}\to\infty
with

L=O(n^{\kappa})
for some
0<\kappa<1/4
. Under the null hypothesis of white noise,

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.175)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L)\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

(4.176)

where
G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}
. In particular,

p_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}=1-\Phi\bigl(T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}^{\circ}\bigr),\qquad p_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}=1-G_{\mathrm{EV}}\bigl(T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L)\bigr)

(4.177)

are asymptotically valid null
p
-values.

4.7.3
Adaptive testing by Fisher combination

42
propose an adaptive white-noise test
that combines the sum-type and max-type benchmarks through their asymptotic independence.
Using the
p
-values in (
4.177
), define

T_{\mathrm{F},\mathrm{WN}}=-2\log p_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}-2\log p_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}.

(4.178)

Assumption 4.25
.

Assumption
4.24
holds. In addition,
p^{2}L\to\infty
and

L\to\infty,\qquad L=O(n^{\kappa}),\qquad\log p=o(n^{1/7})

(4.179)

for some
0<\kappa<1/4
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Under Assumption
4.25
and the null hypothesis of white noise,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}^{\circ}\leq x,T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L)\leq y\bigr)-\Phi(x)G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\longrightarrow 0

(4.180)

for every fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Hence

T_{\mathrm{F},\mathrm{WN}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{4}^{2}.

(4.181)

4.7.4
Spatial-sign and rank-based robust white-noise tests

The sign-test monograph of
107
showed that the
high-dimensional sign paradigm extends beyond one-sample location testing and, in particular,
provides robust portmanteau tests for i.i.d.-ness against serial dependence. Their statistic is a
sign-portmanteau sum over lagged inner products of spatial signs and is valid under weak
symmetry assumptions without requiring finite fourth moments. This result provides the
conceptual precursor for the recent elliptical white-noise procedures discussed next.

Let
\bm{U}_{t}=U(\bm{X}_{t})
denote the spatial sign of the observation at time
t
and write

T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{1}{n-h}\sum_{h+1\leq s<t\leq n}\bm{U}_{s-h}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t-h}\,\bm{U}_{s}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t},

(4.182)

where
H
is the lag truncation level. The statistic (
4.182
), proposed by

152
, is the direct spatial-sign analogue of the sum-type
white-noise statistic. Let

\bm{\Omega}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{t}\bm{U}_{t}^{\top}),\qquad\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq s<t\leq n}(\bm{U}_{s}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t})^{2},\qquad\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}^{2}=H^{2}\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}^{2}.

(4.183)

Assumption 4.26
.

The observations are i.i.d. from a centered elliptically symmetric distribution with density

\det(\bm{\Xi})^{-1/2}g(\left\lVert\bm{\Xi}^{-1/2}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2})
and scatter matrix
\bm{\Xi}
. Moreover,

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})}\to 0,\qquad\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{4}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})}\exp\left\{-\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})}{128p\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0})}\right\}\to 0,

(4.184)

where
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X}_{t})
.

Theorem 4.22

(Spatial-sign white-noise test)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.26
holds. Under the null hypothesis of white noise,

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),\qquad\sigma_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}^{2}=H^{2}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2}),

(4.185)

so that one rejects for large values of

T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}/\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}
.

Under the first-order autoregressive alternative

\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{A}_{0}r_{t}\bm{u}_{t}+\bm{A}_{1}r_{t-1}\bm{u}_{t-1},

(4.186)

with
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{A}_{0}^{\top}\bm{A}_{0}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}=\bm{A}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{1}
, the asymptotic power
for
H=1
satisfies

\beta_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}(\alpha)=\Phi\left(-z_{\alpha}+\frac{c_{1}^{2}n\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}\bm{\Sigma}_{1})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\right)+o(1),

(4.187)

which shows that the spatial-sign procedure enjoys the same inverse-norm efficiency gain as in
other elliptical testing problems.

21
develop a considerably broader rank-based
framework. Instead of committing to one specific correlation measure, it treats three families of
lagged dependence measures: simple linear rank statistics, non-degenerate rank-based

U
-statistics, and degenerate rank-based
U
-statistics. The methodology is built so that the same
max, sum, and adaptive-combination ideas apply to all three families.

For a fixed lag
k\in\{1,\ldots,H\}
and component pair
(i,j)
, define the lagged pair

\bm{W}_{t}^{ij}(k)=(\varepsilon_{t,i},\varepsilon_{t+k,j})^{\top}
for
t=1,\ldots,n-k
. The first class is the
simple linear rank family. Let
Q_{n-k,t}^{i}(k)
be the rank of
\varepsilon_{t,i}
among

\varepsilon_{1,i},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n-k,i}
, let
\widetilde{Q}_{n-k,t+k}^{j}(k)
be the rank of

\varepsilon_{t+k,j}
among
\varepsilon_{k+1,j},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n,j}
, and let

R_{n-k,t+k}^{ij}(k)
be the
relative rank
of
\varepsilon_{t+k,j}
with respect to the ordering of

\varepsilon_{t,i}
. Then the simple linear rank statistic is

V_{ij}(k)=(n-k)^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{n-k}c_{n-k,t}g\!\left\{\frac{R_{n-k,t+k}^{ij}(k)}{n-k+1}\right\},

(4.188)

where
g
is a Lipschitz score function and
\{c_{n-k,t}\}
is a deterministic regression-constant
array. The max-type and sum-type statistics are

L_{V,H}=\max_{1\leq k\leq H}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p}|V_{ij}(k)|,\qquad S_{V,H}=\sum_{k=1}^{H}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}\{V_{ij}^{2}(k)-\mathbb{E}_{0}V_{ij}^{2}(k)\}.

(4.189)

The second class uses non-degenerate rank-based
U
-statistics of order
m
,

U_{ij}(k)=\binom{n-k}{m}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}\leq n-k}h\bigl(\bm{W}_{t_{1}}^{ij}(k),\ldots,\bm{W}_{t_{m}}^{ij}(k)\bigr),

(4.190)

where the kernel
h
is bounded and non-degenerate in the Hoeffding sense. Kendall’s tau is the
canonical example, obtained with
m=2
and

h_{\tau}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})=\operatorname{sign}\{(w_{11}-w_{21})(w_{12}-w_{22})\}.

(4.191)

The induced statistics are

L_{U,H}=\max_{1\leq k\leq H}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p}|U_{ij}(k)|,\qquad S_{U,H}=\sum_{k=1}^{H}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}\{U_{ij}^{2}(k)-\mathbb{E}_{0}U_{ij}^{2}(k)\}.

(4.192)

The third class uses completely degenerate rank-based
U
-statistics,

D_{ij}(k)=\binom{n-k}{m}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}\leq n-k}h_{0}\bigl(\bm{W}_{t_{1}}^{ij}(k),\ldots,\bm{W}_{t_{m}}^{ij}(k)\bigr),

(4.193)

with the first Hoeffding projection equal to zero. Hoeffding’s
D
, the
Blum–Kiefer–Rosenblatt statistic
R
, and Bergsma–Dassios–Yanagimoto’s
\tau^{\ast}
all belong
to this class and are used in
21
as nonlinear rank
alternatives for detecting non-monotone serial dependence.

The max, sum, and max–sum structures are then defined family by family. For the
non-degenerate
U
-statistics, for instance,

p_{L_{U}}=1-G_{\mathrm{EV}}\bigl(L_{U,H}^{2}/\sigma_{L,U}^{2}-2\log(Hp^{2})+\log\log(Hp^{2})\bigr),\qquad p_{S_{U}}=1-\Phi(S_{U,H}/\sigma_{S,U}),

(4.194)

where
\sigma_{L,U}^{2}=(n-k)\operatorname{Var}_{0}\{U_{ij}(k)\}
and
\sigma_{S,U}^{2}
is the null variance of

S_{U,H}
. The adaptive statistic is

T_{C,U,\mathrm{WN}}=-2\log p_{L_{U}}-2\log p_{S_{U}}.

(4.195)

The same construction applies to the linear-rank and degenerate-
U
families.

Assumption 4.27
.

Let
N_{H}=Hp^{2}
. The following hold for the chosen rank family.

(i)

The componentwise lagged statistics are centered under the null and have common null
variance
\sigma_{0,H}^{2}
up to
1+o(1)
factors.

(ii)

The correlation matrix of the studentized collection satisfies the weak-dependence
condition used in Chapter 2: there exists
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that all off-diagonal
correlations are bounded by
\varepsilon
, and the set of indices with more than

N_{H}^{\kappa}
neighbors exceeding a vanishing threshold has cardinality
o(N_{H})
.

(iii)

For the linear-rank family the score function is Lipschitz and the regression-constant array
satisfies the usual bounded-energy and third-moment conditions. For the non-degenerate and
degenerate
U
-statistic families the kernels are bounded and satisfy the corresponding
Hoeffding non-degeneracy or complete degeneracy conditions.

Theorem 4.23

(Rank-based max, sum, and adaptive white-noise tests)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.27
holds for one of the three rank families. Under the
null hypothesis of white noise,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\bullet,H}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log(Hp^{2})+\log\log(Hp^{2})\leq y\Bigr\}\longrightarrow G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y),

(4.196)

\frac{S_{\bullet,H}}{\sigma_{S,\bullet}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.197)

where the placeholder
\bullet\in\{V,U,D\}
stands for the chosen family. Moreover,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\bullet,H}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log(Hp^{2})+\log\log(Hp^{2})\leq y,S_{\bullet,H}/\sigma_{S,\bullet}\leq x\Bigr\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\Phi(x)\longrightarrow 0,

(4.198)

so the adaptive statistic
T_{C,\bullet,\mathrm{WN}}=-2\log p_{L_{\bullet}}-2\log p_{S_{\bullet}}
satisfies

T_{C,\bullet,\mathrm{WN}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{4}^{2}.

(4.199)

Under a sparse local alternative of the form

\max_{1\leq k\leq H}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p}\bigl|\mathbb{E}\{\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ij}(k)\}\bigr|\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\log(Hp^{2})}{n}},

(4.200)

for a sufficiently large constant
c
, the max-type and max–sum tests are consistent; under dense
alternatives with

\frac{1}{\sigma_{S,\bullet}}\sum_{k=1}^{H}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{2}\{\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ij}(k)\}\longrightarrow\infty,

(4.201)

the sum-type and adaptive tests are consistent as well.

The importance of Theorem
4.23
is twofold. First, it shows that the
Chen–Song–Feng paper is not merely a one-statistic refinement: it gives an entire rank-based
library for high-dimensional white-noise testing, ranging from Spearman-type linear ranks to
Kendall-type non-degenerate
U
-statistics and fully nonlinear degenerate rank statistics. Second,
it establishes the same max–sum complementarity that runs throughout the book, and it does so
without any elliptical or moment assumptions.

4.8
High-dimensional independence testing

4.8.1
Classical low-dimensional benchmarks

There are several classical independence problems. For two Gaussian random
vectors
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, the null hypothesis of independence is
equivalent to vanishing cross-covariance,

H_{0,\mathrm{ind}}:\ \operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\bm{0}.

(4.202)

If
n
i.i.d. observations are available and
p,q
are fixed, Wilks’ likelihood
ratio based on canonical correlations provides the classical benchmark. Let

\hat{\rho}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\rho}_{m}
be the sample canonical correlations,

m=\min(p,q)
, and define

\Lambda=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(1-\hat{\rho}_{\ell}^{2}).

(4.203)

Under Gaussianity and
H_{0,\mathrm{ind}}
,

-\Bigl\{n-1-\frac{p+q+1}{2}\Bigr\}\log\Lambda\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{pq}^{2}.

(4.204)

See
3
. In panel data, a fixed-dimensional
benchmark is the Pesaran CD statistic, which tests cross-sectional independence
through the average pairwise sample correlation,

CD=\sqrt{\frac{2T}{N(N-1)}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\hat{\rho}_{ij},

(4.205)

and is asymptotically standard normal under the null; see

111
.

4.8.2
Panel cross-sectional independence: max, sum and Fisher combination

Consider a panel of residuals
\{\varepsilon_{it}:1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T\}
.
The null hypothesis of cross-sectional independence is

H_{0,\mathrm{panel}}:\ \operatorname{Corr}(\varepsilon_{it},\varepsilon_{jt})=0\quad\text{for all }i\neq j.

(4.206)

Let

\hat{\rho}_{ij}=\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}\hat{\varepsilon}_{jt}}{\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\varepsilon}_{jt}^{2}\right)^{1/2}},\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,

(4.207)

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}
are residuals after removing the relevant within-
unit effects or regressors. The sparse- and dense-oriented statistics are

L_{N}=\max_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\hat{\rho}_{ij}|,\qquad S_{N}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2}.

(4.208)

The max–sum test of
39
and the Fisher
combination test of
132
are both built on the
joint asymptotic theory of
L_{N}
and
S_{N}
.

Assumption 4.28
.

Under the null hypothesis, the panel units are cross-sectionally independent.
For each
i
, the time series
\{\varepsilon_{it}\}_{t=1}^{T}
is weakly
stationary with mean zero, finite fourth moment, and absolutely summable
autocovariance function.

Assumption 4.29
.

Let
\bm{\Sigma}=(\sigma_{ts})_{1\leq t,s\leq T}
be the temporal covariance matrix of a
single unit under the null. Its eigenvalues satisfy

0<c\leq\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq C<\infty,

(4.209)

and the effective temporal dimension satisfies

\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}\to\infty,\qquad\log N=o\!\left(\Bigl\{\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}\Bigr\}^{1/7}\right).

(4.210)

Theorem 4.24
.

Under Assumptions
4.28
–
4.29
and the null
hypothesis
H_{0,\mathrm{panel}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}L_{N}^{2}-4\log N+\log\log N

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}G_{\mathrm{EV}},

(4.211)

\displaystyle\frac{S_{N}-\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.212)

where
G_{\mathrm{EV}}
has distribution function

G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)=\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}
, and
\mu_{N},\sigma_{N}^{2}
are the
null mean and variance of
S_{N}
.

Let

p_{L_{N}}=1-G_{\mathrm{EV}}\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}L_{N}^{2}-4\log N+\log\log N\right),\qquad p_{S_{N}}=1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{S_{N}-\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}\right).

(4.213)

The Fisher combination statistic is

T_{\mathrm{F},\mathrm{panel}}=-2\log p_{L_{N}}-2\log p_{S_{N}}.

(4.214)

Theorem 4.25
.

Under Assumptions
4.28
–
4.29
and the null
hypothesis,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}L_{N}^{2}-4\log N+\log\log N\leq x,\frac{S_{N}-\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}\leq y\right\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)\Phi(y)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.215)

Consequently,

T_{\mathrm{F},\mathrm{panel}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{4}^{2}.

(4.216)

4.8.3
Mutual independence of high-dimensional random vectors

Now suppose that for each subject
s=1,\ldots,n
we observe a collection of
random vectors

\bm{Z}_{s}=(\bm{Z}_{s1}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{sm}^{\top})^{\top},\qquad\bm{Z}_{sa}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}}.

(4.217)

The null hypothesis of mutual independence is

H_{0,\mathrm{mut}}:\ \bm{Z}_{s1},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{sm}\text{ are mutually independent.}

(4.218)

133
develop a genuinely high-dimensional
rank-based max–sum framework for this problem. Its key idea is to first build, for each pair of
blocks
(a,b)
, a blockwise dependence score from one of several rank-correlation classes, and
then to aggregate those pairwise block scores by a max statistic, a sum statistic, or an adaptive
combination.

To make the construction concrete, consider one block pair
(a,b)
with dimensions
d_{a}
and

d_{b}
. For each component pair
(r,\ell)
, let
T^{ab}_{r\ell}
denote a rank-based dependence score.
In the simple linear-rank family one may take

T^{ab}_{r\ell}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{n}f\!\left\{\frac{R^{(a,r)}_{s}}{n+1}\right\}g\!\left\{\frac{R^{(b,\ell)}_{s}}{n+1}\right\},

(4.219)

where
R^{(a,r)}_{s}
is the rank of the
r
th component of
\bm{Z}_{sa}
among the
n
observations in
block
a
, and similarly for block
b
. In the non-degenerate
U
-statistic family one instead uses

T^{ab}_{r\ell}=\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}<\cdots<s_{m}\leq n}h\bigl((Z_{s_{1},ar},Z_{s_{1},b\ell}),\ldots,(Z_{s_{m},ar},Z_{s_{m},b\ell})\bigr),

(4.220)

with a bounded non-degenerate kernel
h
; Kendall’s tau is the representative example. The
third family uses completely degenerate kernels such as Hoeffding’s
D
, the
Blum–Kiefer–Rosenblatt statistic, or Bergsma–Dassios–Yanagimoto’s
\tau^{\ast}
.

For the pair
(a,b)
, the paper forms a blockwise max-type summary

L_{ab}=\max_{1\leq r\leq d_{a},\,1\leq\ell\leq d_{b}}|T^{ab}_{r\ell}|,

(4.221)

and a blockwise sum-type summary

S_{ab}=\sum_{r=1}^{d_{a}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{b}}\Bigl\{(T^{ab}_{r\ell})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{0}(T^{ab}_{r\ell})^{2}\Bigr\}.

(4.222)

Across all block pairs, define

L_{\mathrm{mut}}=\max_{1\leq a<b\leq m}L_{ab},\qquad S_{\mathrm{mut}}=\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq m}S_{ab}.

(4.223)

The associated adaptive statistic is

T_{C,\mathrm{mut}}=-2\log p_{L,\mathrm{mut}}-2\log p_{S,\mathrm{mut}},

(4.224)

where
p_{L,\mathrm{mut}}
and
p_{S,\mathrm{mut}}
are the max-type and sum-type
p
-values obtained
from their limiting null laws.

Assumption 4.30
.

Let
M_{m}=\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq m}d_{a}d_{b}
denote the total number of componentwise block-pair
comparisons. The studentized pairwise rank statistics satisfy the weak-dependence condition of
Chapter 2: there exists
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that all off-diagonal correlations are bounded by

\varepsilon
, and the set of indices having more than
M_{m}^{\kappa}
neighbors with correlations
exceeding a vanishing threshold has cardinality
o(M_{m})
for some
\kappa\in(0,1)
. Moreover,
for the chosen rank family, the kernels or score functions are bounded and satisfy the regularity
conditions guaranteeing uniform Gaussian approximation of the sum statistic.

Theorem 4.26

(Rank-based max–sum tests for mutual independence)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.30
