holds. Under the null hypothesis
(
4.218
),

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\mathrm{mut}}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\mathrm{mut}}^{2}-2\log M_{m}+\log\log M_{m}\leq y\Bigr\}\longrightarrow G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y),

(4.225)

\frac{S_{\mathrm{mut}}}{\sigma_{S,\mathrm{mut}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.226)

and the two statistics are asymptotically independent:

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\mathrm{mut}}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\mathrm{mut}}^{2}-2\log M_{m}+\log\log M_{m}\leq y,S_{\mathrm{mut}}/\sigma_{S,\mathrm{mut}}\leq x\Bigr\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\Phi(x)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.227)

Consequently,

T_{C,\mathrm{mut}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{4}^{2}.

(4.228)

If, in addition, the sparse alternative satisfies

\max_{1\leq a<b\leq m}\max_{1\leq r\leq d_{a},\,1\leq\ell\leq d_{b}}\bigl|\mathbb{E}(T^{ab}_{r\ell})\bigr|\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\log M_{m}}{n}},

(4.229)

for a sufficiently large constant
c
, then the max-type and adaptive tests are consistent. Under
dense alternatives with

\sigma_{S,\mathrm{mut}}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq m}\sum_{r=1}^{d_{a}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{b}}\mathbb{E}^{2}(T^{ab}_{r\ell})\longrightarrow\infty,

(4.230)

the sum-type and adaptive tests are consistent as well.

Theorem
4.26
is the key reason why the max–sum independence methodology of
133

belongs in this chapter rather than being treated as a brief side remark. It is not only a robust
mutual-independence test, but a full high-dimensional max, sum, and adaptive testing framework
for vector-valued blocks, with theory covering both sparse and dense alternatives.

4.8.4
Independence between two high-dimensional random vectors

Finally consider i.i.d. observations
(\bm{X}_{1},\bm{Y}_{1}),\ldots,(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})
with

\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\qquad\bm{Y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{q},\qquad H_{0,XY}:\ \bm{X}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\bm{Y}_{i}.

(4.231)

134
study this problem by constructing max-type,
sum-type, and max–sum tests from three classes of rank-based correlations. The resulting
procedures cover both monotone and non-monotone dependence, and remain fully distribution
free.

Simple linear rank correlations.

For each component pair
(a,b)
, let
R^{X}_{ia}
be the rank of
X_{ia}
among

X_{1a},\ldots,X_{na}
and let
R^{Y}_{ib}
be the rank of
Y_{ib}
among
Y_{1b},\ldots,Y_{nb}
. The
simple linear rank statistic is

\widetilde{V}_{ab}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f\!\left\{\frac{R^{X}_{ia}}{n+1}\right\}g\!\left\{\frac{R^{Y}_{ib}}{n+1}\right\},\qquad 1\leq a\leq p,\ 1\leq b\leq q,

(4.232)

where
f
and
g
are Lipschitz score functions. Spearman’s
\rho
corresponds to the centered
linear score choice
f(u)=g(u)=u-1/2
.

Non-degenerate rank-based
U
-statistics.

A second family is obtained by

\widetilde{U}_{ab}=\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}\leq n}h\bigl((X_{i_{1}a},Y_{i_{1}b}),\ldots,(X_{i_{m}a},Y_{i_{m}b})\bigr),

(4.233)

with a bounded non-degenerate kernel
h
. Kendall’s tau is the most important example, with

m=2
and kernel

h_{\tau}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})=\operatorname{sign}\{(w_{11}-w_{21})(w_{12}-w_{22})\}.

(4.234)

Accordingly,

\tilde{\tau}_{ab}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\operatorname{sign}\{(X_{ia}-X_{ja})(Y_{ib}-Y_{jb})\}.

(4.235)

Degenerate rank-based
U
-statistics.

The third family uses completely degenerate kernels,

\widetilde{D}_{ab}=\binom{n}{m_{0}}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m_{0}}\leq n}h_{0}\bigl((X_{i_{1}a},Y_{i_{1}b}),\ldots,(X_{i_{m_{0}}a},Y_{i_{m_{0}}b})\bigr),

(4.236)

which includes Hoeffding’s
D
, the Blum–Kiefer–Rosenblatt statistic, and
Bergsma–Dassios–Yanagimoto’s
\tau^{\ast}
.

For each class, the max-type and sum-type statistics are

L_{\bullet,XY}=\max_{1\leq a\leq p,\,1\leq b\leq q}|\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab}|,\qquad S_{\bullet,XY}=\sum_{a=1}^{p}\sum_{b=1}^{q}\Bigl\{(\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{0}(\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab})^{2}\Bigr\},

(4.237)

where again
\bullet\in\{V,U,D\}
identifies the chosen rank family. The adaptive combination is

T_{C,\bullet,XY}=-2\log p_{L,\bullet}-2\log p_{S,\bullet}.

(4.238)

Assumption 4.31
.

Let
N_{pq}=pq
. The studentized family
\{\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab}\}
satisfies the same weak
dependence condition used in the white-noise and mutual-independence sections. In addition, the
score functions or kernels are bounded and regular enough to yield Gaussian approximation for the
sum statistic and extreme-value approximation for the maximum.

Theorem 4.27

(Rank-based max–sum tests for vector independence)
.

Suppose Assumption
4.31
holds. Under the null hypothesis
(
4.231
),

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\bullet,XY}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log(pq)+\log\log(pq)\leq y\Bigr\}\longrightarrow G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y),

(4.239)

\frac{S_{\bullet,XY}}{\sigma_{S,\bullet}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.240)

and

\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{L_{\bullet,XY}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log(pq)+\log\log(pq)\leq y,S_{\bullet,XY}/\sigma_{S,\bullet}\leq x\Bigr\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\Phi(x)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.241)

Hence

T_{C,\bullet,XY}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi_{4}^{2}.

(4.242)

Under sparse alternatives of the form

\max_{1\leq a\leq p,\,1\leq b\leq q}\bigl|\mathbb{E}(\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab})\bigr|\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\log(pq)}{n}},

(4.243)

for sufficiently large
c
, the max-type and adaptive tests are consistent. Under dense
alternatives satisfying

\sigma_{S,\bullet}^{-1}\sum_{a=1}^{p}\sum_{b=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}^{2}(\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ab})\to\infty,

(4.244)

the sum-type and adaptive tests are consistent. Moreover, for the sparse local alternative
(
4.243
), the asymptotic independence in
(
4.241
) continues to hold after replacing the null distribution of

S_{\bullet,XY}
by its shifted Gaussian limit.

Theorem
4.27
shows why the Chen-type brief summary was not enough: the
paper is a complete method-theory-development for vector independence, not just a remark that
rank correlations can be used. It develops explicit max, sum, and adaptive tests for three distinct
families of rank measures, proves their null limits, and then shows how sparse- and dense-signal
power regimes are captured by the different aggregation rules.

Appendix to Chapter 4: Detailed Proofs

In this appendix we give detailed proofs of the main statements formulated in
the chapter. The proofs are organized so that the reusable arguments — Wishart
reduction, Hájek-type projection, Gaussian approximation for maxima, and product
limit calculations under asymptotic independence — are clearly separated.

A4.1. Proof of Theorem
4.1

Let

U_{n,p}=p_{\max},\qquad V_{n,p}=p_{\mathrm{sum}}.

(4.245)

Under Assumption
4.1
, both variables are asymptotically
uniform on
(0,1)
, and the joint convergence in
(
4.6
) implies

\sup_{u,v\in[0,1]}\left|\mathbb{P}(U_{n,p}\leq u,V_{n,p}\leq v)-uv\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.246)

Hence
(U_{n,p},V_{n,p})\Rightarrow(U,V)
with
U,V
i.i.d.

\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)
. By the continuous mapping theorem,

-2\log U_{n,p}-2\log V_{n,p}\Rightarrow-2\log U-2\log V.

(4.247)

Since
-2\log U
and
-2\log V
are independent
\chi^{2}_{2}
random variables, the
sum has the
\chi^{2}_{4}
distribution, proving
(
4.7
).

For the Cauchy combination, define

g(u)=\frac{1}{2}\tan\{\pi(1/2-u)\}\mathbbm{1}(u<1/2),\qquad u\in(0,1).

(4.248)

Then
T_{\mathrm{C}}=g(U_{n,p})+g(V_{n,p})
. The function
g
is continuous on

(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}
and the singularity at
1/2
is removable because of the
indicator truncation. Therefore

T_{\mathrm{C}}\Rightarrow g(U)+g(V).

(4.249)

The right-hand side defines the truncated Cauchy limit in
(
4.8
). This proves the theorem. ∎

A4.2. Proof of Theorem
4.2

Let
\bm{X}=(\bm{1}_{T},\bm{F})\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times(K+1)}
and write the regression model in
matrix form as

\bm{Y}^{\top}=\bm{X}\bm{C}+\bm{E},

(4.250)

where
\bm{C}=(\bm{\alpha},\bm{B}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K+1)\times N}
and the rows of
\bm{E}

are i.i.d.
N_{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})
. Under
H_{0,\alpha}
the first row of

\bm{C}
vanishes. Standard multivariate regression theory yields

\hat{\bm{C}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{Y}^{\top},\qquad\hat{\bm{E}}=\bm{Y}^{\top}-\bm{X}\hat{\bm{C}},\qquad(T-K-1)\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}=\hat{\bm{E}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{E}}.

(4.251)

Moreover,

\hat{\bm{C}}\sim MN_{K+1,N}\!igl(\bm{C},(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1},\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}\bigr),\qquad(T-K-1)\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}\sim W_{N}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon},T-K-1),

(4.252)

and the two matrices are independent.

Let
\bm{e}_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
. Then

\hat{\bm{\alpha}}=\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}\hat{\bm{C}},\qquad\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}\mid\bm{X})=(\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{e}_{1})\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}.

(4.253)

A direct block-matrix inversion yields

\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{e}_{1}=\frac{1+\bar{f}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{f}^{-1}\bar{f}}{T}.

(4.254)

Therefore, under
H_{0,\alpha}
,

W:=\frac{T}{1+\bar{f}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{f}^{-1}\bar{f}}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}\sim\chi^{2}_{N}.

(4.255)

Also, since
(T-K-1)\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}\sim W_{N}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon},T-K-1)
,
we introduce the residual scatter matrix

\bm{S}_{\varepsilon}:=(T-K-1)\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}.

(4.256)

The multivariate regression version of Hotelling’s
T^{2}
theorem states that,
conditionally on
\bm{X}
,

\frac{T-N-K}{N(T-K-1)}\frac{T\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}}{1+\bar{f}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{f}^{-1}\bar{f}}\sim F_{N,T-N-K}.

(4.257)

This is exactly the statistic defined in (
4.17
). Hence
(
4.18
) holds. This proves the result. ∎

A4.3. Proof of Theorem
4.4

We prove the theorem by a Hájek projection argument. Write

\bm{H}=\frac{\bm{h}\bm{h}^{\top}}{\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}},\qquad Q_{\alpha}=N\sum_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}}H_{t_{1}t_{2}}\,\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}}.

(4.258)

Under
H_{0,\alpha}
, the sign vectors
\bm{U}_{t}
are centered and exchangeable, and

\mathbb{E}(Q_{\alpha})=0.

(4.259)

To compute the variance, expand

Q_{\alpha}^{2}=N^{2}\sum_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}}\sum_{s_{1}\neq s_{2}}H_{t_{1}t_{2}}H_{s_{1}s_{2}}(\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}})(\bm{U}_{s_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{s_{2}}).

(4.260)

Only index configurations with
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
contribute to the
leading order. Using independence across time and the spherical symmetry of the
signs,

\mathbb{E}\{(\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}})^{2}\}=N^{-2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})+O\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{N^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right).

(4.261)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(Q_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=2N^{2}\sum_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}}H_{t_{1}t_{2}}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\{(\bm{U}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t_{2}})^{2}\}+O\!\left(\frac{N^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{T\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right)

(4.262)

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})+O\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}+\frac{N^{2}}{T^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right),

(4.263)

where we used

\sum_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}}H_{t_{1}t_{2}}^{2}=1-\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}h_{t}^{4}}{(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{2}}=1+O(T^{-1}).

(4.264)

Assumption
4.8
implies that the remainder in
(
4.263
) is
o\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}
.

Next decompose
Q_{\alpha}
into a martingale array. Let

\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma(\bm{U}_{1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t}),\qquad D_{t}=2N\sum_{s<t}H_{st}\,\bm{U}_{s}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t},\qquad t=2,\ldots,T.

(4.265)

Then
Q_{\alpha}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{t}
and

\mathbb{E}(D_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=0
. The conditional variance is

\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbb{E}(D_{t}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})+R_{1,T},

(4.266)

where

\frac{R_{1,T}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0.

(4.267)

This follows by a direct fourth-moment calculation using
Assumption
4.8
. In addition,

\frac{1}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbb{E}(D_{t}^{4})\leq C\left\{\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}+\frac{N^{2}}{T^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right\}\longrightarrow 0,

(4.268)

so the Lyapunov condition for the martingale CLT holds. Consequently,

\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(4.269)

Finally, Proposition 2 of
83
in the notation of the
book gives

\frac{\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1.

(4.270)

Slutsky’s theorem then yields (
4.43
). ∎

A4.4. Proof of Theorem
4.5

Under the local alternative, write

\bm{Z}_{t}=\omega_{T}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{t},\qquad\omega_{T}=T^{-1}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}.

(4.271)

A first-order expansion of the spatial sign around
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
gives

U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\omega_{T}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})\bigr)=U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})+c_{1}\bm{A}_{t}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{r}_{t},

(4.272)

where

\bm{A}_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rVert^{-1}\left\{\bm{I}_{N}-U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})^{\top}\right\},

(4.273)

and the remainder satisfies

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bm{r}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\leq CT\,\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{R}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}=o\bigl(\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})\bigr)

(4.274)

by Assumption
4.9
. Substitute the expansion into
(
4.35
). The leading linear term in
\bm{\alpha}
has mean

\mathbb{E}(Q_{\alpha})=\omega TNc_{1}^{2}\,\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}+o(1)=\phi_{\alpha}^{2}+o(1),

(4.275)

while the variance remains
2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}
. More explicitly,

Q_{\alpha}=\phi_{\alpha}^{2}+M_{T}+R_{T},

(4.276)

where

\frac{M_{T}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),\qquad\frac{R_{T}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0.

(4.277)

Therefore,

\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(\mu_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha},1).

(4.278)

Consistency of
\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}
and Slutsky’s theorem complete the proof.
The power formula (
4.46
) is immediate. ∎

A4.5. Proof of Theorem
4.7

We first control the Bahadur expansion of the scaled spatial median defined by
(
4.55
)–(
4.56
). Let

\Psi_{T}(\bm{\theta},\bm{D})=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\bm{Z}_{t}-\bm{\theta})\bigr),

(4.279)

so that
\Psi_{T}(\hat{\bm{\theta}},\hat{\bm{D}})=\bm{0}
. A Taylor expansion at the null
parameter
(\bm{0},\bm{D})
yields

\bm{0}=\Psi_{T}(\bm{0},\bm{D})-\zeta^{-1}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}+\bm{R}_{T},

(4.280)

where

\left\lVert\bm{R}_{T}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{\log N}{T}\right).

(4.281)

Hence

\sqrt{T\zeta}\,\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\xi_{t}+\bm{r}_{T},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{T}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{\log N}{\sqrt{T}}\right),

(4.282)

where the coordinates of
\xi_{t}
are weakly dependent and asymptotically
standardized.

Let

W_{i}=\sqrt{T\zeta}\,\hat{\theta}_{i}/d_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

(4.283)

By Gaussian comparison and the correlation sparsity in
Assumption
4.12
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}W_{i}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N\leq x\Bigr)-\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\}\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.284)

Because
T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}=\max_{i}W_{i}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N+o_{p}(1)
and
more precisely the approximation error is of order

O_{p}\{(\log N)^{3/2}/\sqrt{T}\}
, we obtain the limit
(
4.65
). ∎

A4.6. Proof of Theorem
4.8

Under the sparse alternative in (
4.64
), there exists

i_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
such that

|\alpha_{i_{0}}|\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{T}}.

(4.285)

The Bahadur expansion (
4.282
) now has nonzero mean,
namely

\mathbb{E}(W_{i_{0}})=\sqrt{T\zeta}\,\alpha_{i_{0}}/d_{i_{0}}+O(T^{-1/2})\geq c_{1}C\sqrt{\log N}

(4.286)

for some constant
c_{1}>0
. Therefore,

W_{i_{0}}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N\geq(c_{1}^{2}C^{2}-2)\log N+O_{p}(\sqrt{\log N}).

(4.287)

If
C
is large enough so that
c_{1}^{2}C^{2}>2
, the right-hand side diverges to

+\infty
in probability. Since

T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\geq W_{i_{0}}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N+o_{p}(1)
, the rejection
probability tends to one. This proves
(
4.66
). ∎

A4.7. Proof of Theorem
4.9

Write

T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}=A_{T}+B_{T},\qquad T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}=C_{T}+D_{T},

(4.288)

where
A_{T}
and
C_{T}
are the leading Gaussian or Hájek projection terms, while

B_{T}
and
D_{T}
are remainders of orders

B_{T}=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{3/2}(\bm{R}^{2})}+\frac{N^{2}}{T^{2}\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right),\qquad D_{T}=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{(\log N)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(4.289)

The leading term
A_{T}
is a degenerate quadratic form in the sign process,
whereas
C_{T}
depends only on the coordinatewise maxima of the linear Bahadur
expansion. Their covariance is of smaller order:

\operatorname{Cov}(A_{T},\mathbbm{1}\{C_{T}\leq y\})=O\!\left(\frac{\log N}{T^{1/2}}+\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\right)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.290)

Using the asymptotic-independence theorem of

38
, applied to the leading quadratic and
maximal components, we obtain

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\leq x,T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\leq y)-\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathrm{SS},\alpha}\leq x)\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathrm{SM},\alpha}\leq y)\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.291)

This is exactly (
4.69
). The validity of the
Cauchy combination then follows from
Theorem
4.1
. ∎

A4.8. Proof of Theorem
4.14

For
1\leq j\leq p
and
0\leq u\leq 1
, define the standardized bridge process

G_{n,j}(u)=\sigma_{j}^{-1}\left[n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nu\rfloor}\varepsilon_{ij}-un^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{ij}\right].

(4.292)

Then

C_{0,j}(k)=G_{n,j}(k/n)+\Delta_{n,j}(k),\qquad C_{1/2,j}(k)=\frac{G_{n,j}(k/n)}{\{(k/n)(1-k/n)\}^{1/2}}+\Delta^{\dagger}_{n,j}(k),

(4.293)

where the variance-estimation error terms satisfy

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\max_{1\leq k\leq n-1}|\Delta_{n,j}(k)|=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),\qquad\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}|\Delta^{\dagger}_{n,j}(k)|=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(ph_{n})}{n}}\right).

(4.294)

Under Assumptions
4.19
–
4.21
, Donsker’s theorem and the
Komlós–Major–Tusnády strong approximation imply that one may construct Brownian bridges

\{B_{j}^{\circ}(u):0\leq u\leq 1\}_{j=1}^{p}
on a common probability space such that

\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}|G_{n,j}(u)-B_{j}^{\circ}(u)|=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right).

(4.295)

Hence,

M_{n,p}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sup_{0<u<1}|B_{j}^{\circ}(u)|+o_{P}\{A(\log p)^{-1}\},

(4.296)

and similarly

M_{n,p}^{\dagger}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sup_{\lambda_{n}/n\leq u\leq 1-\lambda_{n}/n}\frac{|B_{j}^{\circ}(u)|}{\{u(1-u)\}^{1/2}}+o_{P}\{A(\log(ph_{n}))^{-1}\}.

(4.297)

Let

Y_{j}=\sup_{0<u<1}|B_{j}^{\circ}(u)|,\qquad Y_{j}^{\dagger}=\sup_{\lambda_{n}/n\leq u\leq 1-\lambda_{n}/n}\frac{|B_{j}^{\circ}(u)|}{\{u(1-u)\}^{1/2}}.

(4.298)

The classical tail expansions for Brownian-bridge suprema yield

\,\mathbb{P}\{Y_{j}>z\}=2e^{-2z^{2}}\{1+o(1)\},\qquad z\to\infty,

(4.299)

and

\mathbb{P}\{Y_{j}^{\dagger}>z\}=\frac{\exp(-z^{2}/2)}{\sqrt{\pi\log(ph_{n})}}\{1+o(1)\},\qquad z\to\infty.

(4.300)

With the normalizing sequence

m_{p}(x)=\frac{x+D(\log p)}{A(\log p)},\qquad m_{p,h}(x)=\frac{x+D(\log(ph_{n}))}{A(\log(ph_{n}))},

(4.301)

we therefore have

p\mathbb{P}\{Y_{1}>m_{p}(x)\}\to e^{-x},\qquad p\mathbb{P}\{Y_{1}^{\dagger}>m_{p,h}(x)\}\to e^{-x}.

(4.302)

The sparse-correlation condition in Assumption
4.20
allows one to apply the
standard Poisson approximation for maxima of weakly dependent arrays; thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Y_{j}\leq m_{p}(x)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\to\exp\{-e^{-x}\},

(4.303)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Y_{j}^{\dagger}\leq m_{p,h}(x)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\to\exp\{-e^{-x}\}.

(4.304)

Combining (
4.296
)–(
4.304
) yields
(
4.135
)–(
4.136
). ∎

A4.9. Proof of Theorem
4.15

Let

Z_{n,p}=A(\log(ph_{n}))M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(\log(ph_{n})),\qquad W_{n,p}=T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{cp}}.

(4.305)

We first treat the Gaussian case. Write
\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}=\bm{\Sigma}^{1/2}\eta_{i}
with

\eta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
. The weighted max statistic depends on
coordinatewise bridge suprema and hence is measurable with respect to the sigma-field generated by

\Bigl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{k,i}\eta_{ij}:1\leq j\leq p,\ \lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}\Bigr\},

(4.306)

where
a_{k,i}=n^{-1/2}\{\mathbbm{1}(i\leq k)-k/n\}/\{(k/n)(1-k/n)\}^{1/2}
. On the other hand,
the sum statistic has the quadratic-form representation

W_{n,p}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}b_{k}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{k,i}\eta_{ij}\right)^{2}-\mu_{n,p}+r_{n,p},\qquad\frac{r_{n,p}}{V_{n,p}^{1/2}}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0,

(4.307)

for deterministic coefficients
b_{k},c_{k,i}
satisfying

\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}b_{k}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{k,i}^{4}=o(1),\qquad\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}|b_{k}|\sum_{i=1}^{n}|c_{k,i}|^{3}=o(1).

(4.308)

Thus
W_{n,p}
is driven by a large collection of moderate Gaussian quadratic terms, whereas

Z_{n,p}
is driven by the extreme upper tail of the maxima in (
4.306
).

To make this separation quantitative, truncate the max field at level
m_{p,h}(x)
from
(
4.301
) and write

\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)=\left\{Z_{n,p}\leq x\right\}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{p}\bigcap_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\{|\mathcal{B}_{j,k}|\leq m_{p,h}(x)\},

(4.309)

where
\mathcal{B}_{j,k}
denotes the weighted bridge coordinate. Since

\mathbb{P}\{\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)^{c}\}=O(p^{-1})
, the covariance bound

\bigl|\operatorname{Cov}\{\mathbbm{1}(\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)),W_{n,p}\}\bigr|\leq\{\operatorname{Var}(W_{n,p})\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)^{c})\}^{1/2}=o(1)

(4.310)

shows that conditioning on the extremal event only perturbs the centered quadratic statistic by an
amount negligible relative to its standard deviation. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\{W_{n,p}\leq y\mid\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)\}-\mathbb{P}\{W_{n,p}\leq y\}\to 0,

(4.311)

uniformly over bounded
(x,y)
.

Now combine (
4.311
) with the extreme-value limit for
Z_{n,p}
and
with the asymptotic normality of
W_{n,p}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{n,p}\leq x,W_{n,p}\leq y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\{W_{n,p}\leq y\mid\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)\}\Pr\{\mathcal{E}_{n,p}(x)\}

(4.312)

\displaystyle=\{\Phi(y)+o(1)\}\{\exp(-e^{-x})+o(1)\}=\Phi(y)\exp(-e^{-x})+o(1).

(4.313)

For sub-Gaussian coordinates, apply the Gaussian approximation for maxima and quadratic forms to
replace
(Z_{n,p},W_{n,p})
by their Gaussian counterpart with an
o(1)
error uniformly over
rectangles. This proves (
4.141
). ∎

A4.10. Proof of Theorem
4.21

Let

Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}=T_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}^{\circ},\qquad Z_{\max,n}=T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L).

(4.314)

By Theorem
4.20
,

Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),\qquad Z_{\max,n}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}G_{\mathrm{EV}}.

(4.315)

We now verify the joint factorization.

Write
\hat{\gamma}_{ij}(h)=n^{-1/2}W_{ijh}+r_{ijh}
, where the Gaussian approximation of

20
yields

\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p\atop 1\leq h\leq L}|r_{ijh}|=o_{P}\!\left((\log(p^{2}L))^{-1/2}\right),

(4.316)

and the array
\{W_{ijh}\}
is centered Gaussian with covariance matching that of the lagged
sample autocovariances under the null. Then

Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}=\sum_{h=1}^{L}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}a_{ijh}(W_{ijh}^{2}-1)+o_{P}(1),

(4.317)

for deterministic weights
a_{ijh}
satisfying

\sum_{h=1}^{L}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}a_{ijh}^{2}=1+o(1),\qquad\max_{i,j,h}|a_{ijh}|=o(1),

(4.318)

whereas

Z_{\max,n}=\max_{i,j,h}|W_{ijh}|^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L)+o_{P}(1).

(4.319)

Thus the sum statistic is a smooth quadratic aggregate of the whole Gaussian field, while the max
statistic depends only on the most extreme order statistics of the same field.

Let
u_{p,L}(y)
satisfy

u_{p,L}^{2}(y)=2\log(p^{2}L)-\log\log(p^{2}L)+y.

(4.320)

Define the exceedance event

\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)=\Bigl\{\max_{i,j,h}|W_{ijh}|\leq u_{p,L}(y)\Bigr\}.

(4.321)

Since
\Pr\{\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)^{c}\}=O\bigl((p^{2}L)^{-1}\bigr)
uniformly over bounded
y
, we have

\bigl|\operatorname{Cov}\{\mathbbm{1}(\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)),Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\}\bigr|\leq\{\operatorname{Var}(Z_{\mathrm{sum},n})\Pr(\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)^{c})\}^{1/2}=o(1),

(4.322)

which implies

\Pr\{Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\leq x\mid\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)\}=\Pr\{Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\leq x\}+o(1)=\Phi(x)+o(1).

(4.323)

Combining (
4.323
) with the Gumbel limit of
Z_{\max,n}
gives

\displaystyle\Pr(Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\leq x,Z_{\max,n}\leq y)

\displaystyle=\Pr\{Z_{\mathrm{sum},n}\leq x\mid\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)\}\Pr\{\mathcal{A}_{n}(y)\}

(4.324)

\displaystyle=\Phi(x)G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)+o(1),

(4.325)

which is exactly (
4.180
). Since

-2\log p_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}
and
-2\log p_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}
are asymptotically independent

\chi_{2}^{2}
variables, their sum converges to
\chi_{4}^{2}
, proving
(
4.181
). ∎

A4.11. Proof of Theorem
4.24

The proof proceeds in two parts. For the maximum statistic, let

Z_{ij}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}}\hat{\rho}_{ij},\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N.

(4.326)

Under the null,
Z_{ij}
are approximately standard normal and weakly dependent.
An extreme-value calculation therefore yields

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i<j}Z_{ij}^{2}-4\log N+\log\log N\leq x\right)\longrightarrow\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\},

(4.327)

which is (
4.211
).

For the sum statistic, write

S_{N}=\sum_{i<j}\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2}=\mu_{N}+\sum_{i<j}(\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2}).

(4.328)

The second term is a sum of weakly dependent centered variables. Its variance is

\sigma_{N}^{2}=\sum_{i<j}\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2})+2\sum_{(i,j)\neq(k,\ell)}\operatorname{Cov}(\hat{\rho}_{ij}^{2},\hat{\rho}_{k\ell}^{2}),

(4.329)

which is of order
N^{2}\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}/\operatorname{tr}^{4}(\bm{\Sigma})
. A Lyapunov-type
argument based on Assumption
4.29
gives the asymptotic normal
limit (
4.212
). ∎

A4.12. Proof of Theorem
4.25

Let

Z_{L,N}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma})}{\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}L_{N}^{2}-4\log N+\log\log N,\qquad Z_{S,N}=\frac{S_{N}-\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}.

(4.330)

The proof of
132
shows that

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(Z_{L,N}\leq x,Z_{S,N}\leq y)-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x)\Phi(y)\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.331)

The argument combines a Poisson approximation for exceedances of the largest
pairwise correlations with a central limit theorem for the sum of squared
correlations. Since the exceedance event depends only on the far upper tail of
one or a few coordinates, whereas the sum statistic averages over all

\binom{N}{2}
pairs, the overlap is asymptotically negligible. Formula
(
4.215
) follows immediately.

Now define the null
p
-values as in (
4.213
). By the
continuous mapping theorem and Theorem
4.1
,

T_{\mathrm{F},\mathrm{panel}}=-2\log p_{L_{N}}-2\log p_{S_{N}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{4}.

(4.332)

This proves (
4.216
). ∎

A4.13. Proof of Theorem
4.3

Under
H_{0,\alpha}
we have

\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\bm{\alpha}}=\hat{\omega}^{1/2}\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{R}^{1/2}\bm{z}+\bm{r}_{T},\qquad\bm{z}\sim N_{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{N}),\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{T}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{tr}^{1/4}(\bm{R}^{2})\bigr),

(4.333)

where
\hat{\omega}=T(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{-1}\to\omega^{-1}
by
Assumption
4.3
. Therefore

\displaystyle T\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\top}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{R}\bm{z}+o_{P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})\bigr),

(4.334)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{R}\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R})=N,\qquad\operatorname{Var}(\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{R}\bm{z})=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2}).

(4.335)

Assumption
4.4
implies the Lyapunov ratio

\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{4})}{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0,

(4.336)

so the quadratic-form CLT yields

\frac{\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{R}\bm{z}-\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(4.337)

Combining (
4.334
) and
(
4.337
) proves
(
4.24
).

For the max statistic, let

\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{N})^{\top}=\hat{\omega}^{-1/2}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\bm{\alpha}}.

(4.338)

Then
\bm{\xi}
is asymptotically Gaussian with covariance
\bm{R}
. Hence

T_{\max,\alpha}=\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\xi_{i}^{2}+o_{P}(1).

(4.339)

Assumption
4.5
is exactly the standard weak-dependence condition used
in Gaussian extreme-value theory: no pairwise correlation approaches
1
and the set of
strongly dependent coordinates is asymptotically negligible. Therefore

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\xi_{i}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N\leq x\right)\to\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\},

(4.340)

which together with (
4.339
) proves
(
4.25
). Under
(
4.26
), the centered quadratic form acquires the mean shift

T\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}
, proving
(
4.27
). The sparse-detection claim follows because
(
4.28
) implies that at least one coordinate of

\sqrt{T}\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\alpha}}
exceeds the null extreme-value threshold by a diverging
margin. ∎

A4.14. Proof of Proposition
4.1

Under the proposition assumptions, both

p_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}
and
p_{\max,\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}
are asymptotically
uniform on
(0,1)
and asymptotically independent. Therefore

\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\mathrm{sum},\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}})\}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Cauchy}(0,1),\qquad\tan\{\pi(1/2-p_{\max,\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}})\}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Cauchy}(0,1),

(4.341)

and the sum of two independent standard Cauchy variables is again Cauchy after scaling by

1/2
. Hence
(
4.31
) follows immediately. ∎

A4.15. Proof of Theorem
4.6

Let

\bm{W}_{t}=K(r_{t})\bm{U}_{t},\qquad\bar{\bm{\Sigma}}_{K}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{W}_{t}\bm{W}_{t}^{\top})=\psi_{2,K}p^{-1}\bm{R}+o(p^{-1}),

(4.342)

where the last identity follows from elliptical symmetry and the same SSCM approximation
used in Chapter 1. Then

Q_{K,\alpha}=\frac{N}{\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h}}\sum_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}}h_{t_{1}}h_{t_{2}}\bm{W}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\bm{W}_{t_{2}}.

(4.343)

Under
H_{0,\alpha}
,
\mathbb{E}(\bm{W}_{t})=\bm{0}
and therefore
\mathbb{E}(Q_{K,\alpha})=0
. By the same
pairing argument used in the proof of Theorem
4.4
,

\operatorname{Var}(Q_{K,\alpha})=2\psi_{2,K}^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}.

(4.344)

The martingale decomposition from the proof of
Theorem
4.4
applies verbatim after replacing
\bm{U}_{t}
by
\bm{W}_{t}
,
and the Lyapunov ratio is unchanged up to the multiplicative constant
\psi_{2,K}^{2}
.
Hence

\frac{Q_{K,\alpha}}{\{2\psi_{2,K}^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(4.345)

Since
\hat{\psi}_{2,K}\to\psi_{2,K}
and

\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}/\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\to 1
in probability,
(
4.51
) follows by Slutsky’s theorem.

Under local alternatives, the first-order elliptical expansion gives

K(r_{t})U\bigl(\bm{D}^{-1/2}(\omega_{T}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})\bigr)=K(r_{t})\bm{U}_{t}+\psi_{1,K}\bm{A}_{t}\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{r}_{t},

(4.346)

where
\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bm{r}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=o(T^{-1}\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2}))
and the matrices
\bm{A}_{t}
are the same
as in the proof of Theorem
4.5
. Substituting
(
4.346
) into (
4.343
) yields

\mathbb{E}(Q_{K,\alpha})=\omega TN\psi_{1,K}^{2}\,\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}+o\{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{R}^{2})\},

(4.347)

while the variance remains (
4.344
). Therefore
(
4.52
) follows from the same martingale CLT. ∎

A4.16. Proof of Proposition
4.2

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\psi_{1,K}^{2}=\Bigl[\mathbb{E}\{K(r_{t})r_{t}^{-1}\}\Bigr]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\{K^{2}(r_{t})\}\,\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-2})=\psi_{2,K}\,\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-2}).

(4.348)

Dividing both sides by
\psi_{2,K}
proves
(
4.53
). Equality in
(
4.348
) holds if and only if
K(r_{t})
is proportional to
r_{t}^{-1}
almost
surely, which is equivalent to
K(t)=ct^{-1}
on the support of the radial variable. Since the
noncentrality parameter in (
4.52
) is increasing in

\psi_{1,K}^{2}/\psi_{2,K}
, inverse-norm weighting is locally optimal. ∎

A4.17. Proof of Theorem
4.12

For the sum statistic, write

\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}=T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\cdot t}.

(4.349)

Under
H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}
and the spline approximation conditions of
Assumption
4.16
, the estimation error introduced by

\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}
is of smaller order than
\operatorname{tr}^{1/4}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})
, so that

J_{NT}=N^{-1}\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}+o_{P}\!\bigl(N^{-1}\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})\bigr).

(4.350)

Since
\mathbb{E}(\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}})=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega})
and

\operatorname{Var}(\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}})=2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})
, the same quadratic-form CLT
as in (
4.337
) yields
(
4.104
).

For the max statistic, let

\xi_{i}=T^{-1/2}\hat{\sigma}_{ii}^{-1/2}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i\cdot}^{\top}\bm{1}_{T},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

(4.351)

Then
M_{NT}=\max_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}+o_{P}(1)
. Under the sub-Gaussian and sparse-dependence
assumptions in (
4.103
), the Gaussian approximation for maxima implies

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\xi_{i}^{2}-2\log N+\log\log N\leq x\right)\to\exp\{-\pi^{-1/2}e^{-x/2}\},

(4.352)

which proves (
4.105
). The adaptive-combination claim is then an
immediate consequence of the asymptotic independence assumption and the generic Cauchy
argument already used in the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

A4.18. Proof of Theorem
4.13

The proof follows the decomposition developed in the supplementary arguments of the
uploaded preprint on robust spatial-sign testing for conditional factor models. Let

\bm{U}_{t}=U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{\cdot t}),\qquad V_{t}=\bm{Z}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})^{-1}Z_{t},

(4.353)

and let
\zeta_{1}=\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})
. The Bahadur expansion of the scaled spatial median yields

T^{1/2}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}=T^{-1/2}\zeta_{1}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Bigl(1-\sum_{s=1}^{T}r_{s}^{-1}r_{t}V_{st}\Bigr)\bm{U}_{t}+\bm{C}_{T},

(4.354)

where

\left\lVert\bm{C}_{T}\right\rVert_{\infty}=o_{P}\!\bigl((\log N)^{-1/2}\bigr)

(4.355)

under Assumptions
4.16
and
4.18
. Equation
(
4.354
) is the conditional-factor analogue of the max-type Bahadur
representation already used in the unconditional linear factor model.

For the sum statistic, let

Q_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}=(\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{h})^{-1}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{U}_{\mathrm{cond}}\bm{U}_{\mathrm{cond}}^{\top}\bm{h}.

(4.356)

Under
H_{0,\mathrm{cond}}
, the Hoeffding decomposition of
(
4.356
) gives

\frac{Q_{\mathrm{CSS},\mathrm{cond}}-1}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{u}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.357)

while the leave-two-out variance estimator is ratio consistent. This proves
(
4.116
).

For the max statistic, combine (
4.354
) with a Gaussian
approximation over hyperrectangles. Let

\bm{G}\sim N\Bigl(\bm{0},\ \zeta^{-1}\eta_{\omega}^{-1}\bm{R}/N\Bigr),\qquad\zeta=N\{\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})\}^{2}/\eta_{\omega},

(4.358)

where
\eta_{\omega}=1-2\eta\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})\mathbb{E}(r_{t})+\eta\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{2})\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1})^{2}
.
Then

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\bigl(T\hat{\zeta}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq x\bigr)-\mathbb{P}\bigl(\zeta\left\lVert\bm{G}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq x\bigr)\Bigr|\to 0.

(4.359)

The weak-dependence part of Assumption
4.18
guarantees that the maximum
of
\bm{G}
satisfies the same type-I extreme-value limit as in
(
4.340
). Consequently,
(
4.117
) holds.

For the joint limit, write the sum statistic as a degenerate U-statistic plus a negligible spline
remainder and the max statistic as the maximum of the Gaussian approximation in
(
4.359
). The covariance between the degenerate U-statistic part
and each coordinate of the Gaussian approximation is of order

o\{\operatorname{tr}^{1/2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{u}^{2})\}
, so their standardized limits are asymptotically independent.
Hence (
4.118
) follows. Under
(
4.119
), the deterministic centering term in the Bahadur expansion
pushes at least one coordinate of
\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{\theta}}
above the null threshold, giving
consistency of
T_{\mathrm{CSM},\mathrm{cond}}
. Finally,
(
4.120
) is exactly the local-signal scaling that keeps the mean shift of
the sum statistic bounded while preserving the same covariance-decoupling argument, so the
joint factorization remains valid under the corresponding local alternatives. ∎

A4.19. Proof of Theorems
4.10
and
4.11

We first prove the observable-factor result. Let
\bm{W}=(\bm{1}_{T},\bm{F})
and write

\bm{P}_{W}=\bm{W}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}=(V_{st})_{1\leq s,t\leq T}
. Under the elliptical model,
let

\bm{U}_{t}=U(\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}),\qquad r_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\zeta_{1}=\mathbb{E}(r_{t}^{-1}).

(4.360)

Arguing exactly as in the proof of Theorem
4.7
but with the full
regression design
\bm{W}
in place of the global-alpha design, the estimating equations
(
4.73
)–(
4.74
) admit the Bahadur expansion

\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\zeta}_{1}\,\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\hat{\omega}\bm{\alpha})=T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta_{1}^{-1}\Bigl(1-\sum_{s=1}^{T}r_{s}^{-1}r_{t}V_{st}\Bigr)\bm{U}_{t}+\bm{C}_{T},

(4.361)

with remainder satisfying

\left\lVert\bm{C}_{T}\right\rVert_{\infty}=o_{P}\bigl((\log N)^{-1/2}\bigr).

(4.362)

The coefficient array in (
4.361
) has bounded row sums because the factor
dimension is fixed and the sample Gram matrix of
\bm{W}
is well conditioned. Hence the Gaussian
approximation for maxima over hyperrectangles gives a Gaussian vector
\bm{G}\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{\Xi})

such that

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\left|\Pr\bigl(\sqrt{T}\,\hat{\zeta}_{1}\,\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\hat{\omega}\bm{\alpha})\leq z\bigr)-\Pr(\bm{G}\leq z)\right|\to 0.

(4.363)

Under
H_{0i}:\alpha_{i}\leq 0
, the
i
th coordinate is asymptotically dominated by a standard normal,
so the one-sided
p
-value
p_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}
is conservative. The weak-correlation condition

\max_{i\neq j}|\Xi_{ij}|\leq\rho_{0}<1
and the sparse-neighborhood condition inherited from
(
4.23
) imply that the null
p
-values are asymptotically PRDS in the sense
needed for the Benjamini–Hochberg argument. Therefore,

\operatorname*{FDR}\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{SS}}(q)\}\leq\frac{q}{N}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{H}_{0}}\Pr\{p_{i}^{\mathrm{SS}}\leq q\}+o(1)\leq q+o(1),

(4.364)

where
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is the set of true null hypotheses. If
\alpha_{i}\geq C\sqrt{(\log N)/T}
, then the
mean shift of the
i
th Gaussian approximation in (
4.363
) diverges at the same
rate as the BH screening threshold, so that the rejection probability tends to one.

For the latent-factor case, write the factor-adjusted residuals as

\check{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{t}=\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}+\bm{\rho}_{t},\qquad\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\rho_{it}|=o_{P}\bigl((T\log N)^{-1/2}\bigr),

(4.365)

where (
4.86
) guarantees that the contamination term
\bm{\rho}_{t}
is uniformly
smaller than the Gaussian-approximation scale. Repeating the derivation of
(
4.361
) with
\check{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{t}
in place of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
yields the same
expansion with an additional remainder of order
o_{P}\{(\log N)^{-1/2}\}
. Hence the same Gaussian
approximation and the same BH argument continue to hold, giving
(
4.87
). ∎

A4.20. Proof of Theorem
4.16

For each
k
, let

\Delta_{k}=n^{1/2}\hat{\bm{D}}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1:k}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1:n}).

(4.366)

The scaled spatial median admits the intervalwise Bahadur representation

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1:k}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}+\frac{1}{k\zeta_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{-1}\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{r}_{1,k},

(4.367)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1:n}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}+\frac{1}{(n-k)\zeta_{1}}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{U}_{i}+\bm{r}_{2,k},

(4.368)

with

\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{r}_{1,k}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\bm{r}_{2,k}\right\rVert_{\infty}\bigr\}=o_{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{np\log p}}\right).

(4.369)

Substituting (
4.367
)–(
4.368
) into
(
4.145
) gives

\bm{C}_{\gamma}(k)=\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}}\Bigl\{\frac{k}{n}\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}^{1-\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{-1}\bm{U}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}r_{i}^{-1}\bm{U}_{i}\right\}+o_{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p\log p}}\right),

(4.370)

uniformly in
k
. Under
H_{0}
, the leading term is a Gaussian bridge array with marginal variance

(2p\zeta_{1}^{2})^{-1}
, so the maximum over
p
coordinates and admissible
k
values obeys the same
Poisson approximation as a maximum of weakly dependent Gaussian variables. Therefore,

\Pr\Bigl(2p\zeta_{1}^{2}M_{n,p}^{2}-\log(2p)\leq x\Bigr)=\exp\{-e^{-x}\}+o(1).

(4.371)

For
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
, use the same expansion with
\gamma=1/2
and note that the effective number
of scanning points is of order
p\log h_{n}
. The weighted bridge tail then leads to

\Pr\Bigl(p^{1/2}\zeta_{1}A(p\log h_{n})M_{n,p}^{\dagger}-D(p\log h_{n})\leq x\Bigr)=\exp\{-e^{-x}\}+o(1),

(4.372)

which proves (
4.153
)–(
4.154
). The
p
-value
formulas in (
4.155
) are then immediate. ∎

A4.21. Proof of Theorem
4.17

The statistic
S_{n,p}
is built from the sign partial sums

\hat{\bm{S}}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\bm{U}}_{i}
. Under the null hypothesis,

\hat{\bm{U}}_{i}=\bm{U}_{i}+o_{P}(1),\qquad\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{i})=\bm{0},\qquad\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{U}_{i})=p^{-1}\bm{R}.

(4.373)

Therefore,

\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{0}(k)=\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbbm{1}(i\leq k)-\frac{k}{n}\right)\bm{U}_{i}+o_{P}(1).

(4.374)

After centering by
k(n-k)p/n^{2}
, the leading term is a quadratic form in the Gaussian limit of the
sign process. Writing
\bm{R}=\bm{\Gamma}\bm{\Gamma}^{\top}
and
\bm{Z}_{i}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
, one may
represent the limit as

\frac{pS_{n,p}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}=\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\Gamma}\{\bm{B}^{\circ}(t)-t\bm{B}^{\circ}(1)\}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-tp}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}+o_{P}(1),

(4.375)

where
\bm{B}^{\circ}(t)
is a
p
-vector Brownian bridge. Since the centered quadratic form has covariance
kernel
(1-t)^{2}s^{2}
after normalization, it converges to
\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}V(t)
, proving
(
4.157
).

For
S_{n,p}^{\dagger}
, replace
\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{0}(k)
by

\{k/n(1-k/n)\}^{-1/2}\widetilde{\bm{C}}_{0}(k)
. The resulting field is approximately the maximum of
independent centered
\chi_{p}^{2}
variables indexed by
k
, and the classical extreme-value calculus gives

\Pr\Biggl(A\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\frac{pS_{n,p}^{\dagger}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}-D\!\left\{\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right)\right\}\leq x\Biggr)=\exp\{-2e^{-x}\}+o(1).

(4.376)

Finally, the ratio consistency of
\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}
follows from the U-statistic law of large
numbers applied to separated boundary blocks of the sign sequence, and plugging it into the
normalizations gives the stated
p
-values. ∎

A4.22. Proof of Theorems
4.18
and
4.19

Let

Z_{1,n}=2p\zeta_{1}^{2}M_{n,p}^{2}-\log(2p),\qquad Z_{2,n}=\frac{pS_{n,p}}{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})}.

(4.377)

Under
H_{0}
, Theorems
4.16
and
4.17
provide the marginal
limits
Z_{1,n}\Rightarrow G
and
Z_{2,n}\Rightarrow F_{V}
. To prove joint convergence, decompose the
sign array into extreme and bulk parts. The max statistic depends on the exceedance indicators

I_{j,k}(x)=\mathbbm{1}\{\|\bm{C}_{0}(k)\|_{\infty}>u_{p}(x)\},\qquad u_{p}(x)=\bigl\{\log(2p)+x\bigr\}^{1/2}/(\sqrt{2p}\zeta_{1}),

(4.378)

whereas the
L_{2}
statistic is a smooth quadratic functional of the entire sign process. Because the
number of exceedances is tight and the contribution of any fixed coordinate to
S_{n,p}
is of order

o\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\}
, one obtains

\operatorname{Cov}\bigl\{\mathbbm{1}(Z_{1,n}\leq x),Z_{2,n}\bigr\}=o(1).

(4.379)

The same argument applies to the pair
(M_{n,p}^{\dagger},S_{n,p}^{\dagger})
, which proves the null
factorizations in (
4.160
)–(
4.161
).

Under the local alternatives in (
4.163
), write the sign process as

\bm{U}_{i}=\bar{\bm{U}}_{i}+\bm{\Delta}_{n}\mathbbm{1}(i>\tau_{n})+\bm{r}_{i,n},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{r}_{i,n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=o_{P}\{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{R}^{2})\},

(4.380)

where
\bar{\bm{U}}_{i}
obeys the null model. The support-size condition in
(
4.163
) ensures that the nonzero mean shift only affects
o(p)
coordinates, so the
covariance-decoupling argument leading to (
4.379
) remains valid. Hence the
same joint limits hold under the local alternatives. Since Fisher’s method only requires the product
limit of the two
p
-values, (
4.162
) is asymptotically valid under
H_{0}
, and the
same local-alternative factorization implies the adaptive power statement. ∎

A4.23. Proof of Theorem
4.20

For each lag
h
, let

W_{h}=\sqrt{n-h}\,\mathrm{vec}\{\hat{\bm{\Gamma}}(h)\}.

(4.381)

Under the white-noise null,
\mathbb{E}(W_{h})=\bm{0}
and the vectors
W_{1},\ldots,W_{L}
are asymptotically
independent Gaussian vectors with covariance matrices depending only on
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
. Therefore,

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}=\sum_{h=1}^{L}(n-h)^{-1}\left\lVert W_{h}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(4.382)

A standard quadratic-form central limit theorem then yields

\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{sum},\mathrm{WN}}-\mu_{\mathrm{WN}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{WN}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(4.383)

provided
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{4})=o\{\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})\}
.

For the maximum statistic, the Gaussian approximation gives

T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}=\max_{1\leq h\leq L}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq p}|Z_{ij}(h)|+o_{P}\{(\log(p^{2}L))^{-1/2}\},

(4.384)

where
\{Z_{ij}(h)\}
is a weakly dependent Gaussian array with unit variances. The standard tail
calculation for maxima of Gaussian arrays gives

\Pr\bigl(T_{\max,\mathrm{WN}}^{2}-2\log(p^{2}L)+\log\log(p^{2}L)\leq x\bigr)\to G_{\mathrm{EV}}(x),

(4.385)

which is (
4.176
). Together with (
4.383
), this proves
Theorem
4.20
. ∎

A4.24. Proof of Theorem
4.22

Let
\bm{U}_{t}=U(\bm{X}_{t})
and set

H_{st}(h)=\bm{U}_{s-h}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t-h}\,\bm{U}_{s}^{\top}\bm{U}_{t},\qquad h+1\leq s<t\leq n.

(4.386)

Then

T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{1}{n-h}\sum_{h+1\leq s<t\leq n}H_{st}(h).

(4.387)

Under the null of white noise, the kernels
H_{st}(h)
are centered and degenerate. Their second
moment is

\mathbb{E}\{H_{st}(h)^{2}\}=\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2}),\qquad\bm{\Omega}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{U}_{t}\bm{U}_{t}^{\top}),

(4.388)

while all mixed terms with disjoint time indices vanish. Consequently,

\operatorname{Var}\{T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}\}=H^{2}\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})\{1+o(1)\}=\sigma_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}^{2}\{1+o(1)\}.

(4.389)

Since the kernel is bounded by one in absolute value, the Lindeberg condition for degenerate
U-statistics follows from Assumption
4.26
, and hence

\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

(4.390)

The estimator of
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})
in (
4.183
) is an ordinary second-order
U-statistic, so

\frac{\widehat{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}^{2})}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1,\qquad\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}^{2}}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1.

(4.391)

Therefore (
4.185
) follows.

Under the autoregressive alternative (
4.186
) with
H=1
, the first-order
Hoeffding projection is no longer centered. A direct calculation gives

\mathbb{E}\{T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}\}=\frac{c_{1}^{2}n\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}\bm{\Sigma}_{1})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})\}^{1/2}}+o(1),

(4.392)

while the variance remains asymptotically the same as in (
4.389
). Hence

\Pr\left(\frac{T_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}{\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SS},\mathrm{WN}}}>z_{\alpha}\right)=\Phi\left(-z_{\alpha}+\frac{c_{1}^{2}n\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}\bm{\Sigma}_{1})}{\{2\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{2})\}^{1/2}}\right)+o(1),

(4.393)

which is exactly (
4.187
). ∎

A4.25. Proof of Theorem
4.23

The proof follows the three-step pattern used repeatedly in this chapter. For the max statistic,
let
\hat{T}_{s}^{(\bullet)}
enumerate the studentized family
\{\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ij}(k):1\leq k\leq H,1\leq i,j\leq p\}
and write
N_{H}=Hp^{2}
. Under Assumption
4.27
, the
correlation matrix of
(\hat{T}_{1}^{(\bullet)},\ldots,\hat{T}_{N_{H}}^{(\bullet)})
satisfies the weak local
dependence condition used in Chapter 2. Therefore the extreme-value argument of Jiang-type
maxima gives

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{L_{\bullet,H}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log N_{H}+\log\log N_{H}\leq y\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\right|\to 0.

For the sum statistic, write

S_{\bullet,H}=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{H}}\bigl\{(\hat{T}_{s}^{(\bullet)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{0}(\hat{T}_{s}^{(\bullet)})^{2}\bigr\}=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{H}}\xi_{s}.

By the boundedness of the score functions or kernels and the Gaussian-approximation condition in
Assumption
4.27
,

\sum_{s=1}^{N_{H}}\mathbb{E}_{0}(\xi_{s})=0,\qquad\sum_{s=1}^{N_{H}}\operatorname{Var}_{0}(\xi_{s})=\sigma_{S,\bullet}^{2},\qquad\sum_{s=1}^{N_{H}}\mathbb{E}_{0}|\xi_{s}|^{2+\delta}=o(\sigma_{S,\bullet}^{2+\delta})

for some
\delta>0
. Hence Lyapunov’s theorem yields

S_{\bullet,H}/\sigma_{S,\bullet}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

To prove the joint factorization, decompose the sum statistic as

S_{\bullet,H}=S_{\bullet,H}^{\mathcal{I}(z)}+S_{\bullet,H}^{\mathcal{I}(z)^{c}},\qquad\mathcal{I}(z)=\{s:\ |\hat{T}_{s}^{(\bullet)}|>z\sigma_{L,\bullet}\}

with
z=(2\log N_{H}-\log\log N_{H}+y)^{1/2}
. The event determining the maximum depends only on

S_{\bullet,H}^{\mathcal{I}(z)}
, whereas the contribution of the exceedance set to the normalized sum is
negligible:

\frac{S_{\bullet,H}^{\mathcal{I}(z)}}{\sigma_{S,\bullet}}\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}0.

Because the complement
S_{\bullet,H}^{\mathcal{I}(z)^{c}}
is asymptotically Gaussian and independent of
the rare exceedance process generating the Gumbel limit, (
4.198
)
follows. Finally, under the sparse alternative (
4.200
),

\max_{s\leq N_{H}}|\mathbb{E}(\hat{T}_{s}^{(\bullet)})|\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{H}}{n}}

with
c
sufficiently large implies

\mathbb{P}\{L_{\bullet,H}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log N_{H}+\log\log N_{H}>q_{\alpha}\}\to 1.

Under the dense alternative (
4.201
), the mean shift of the normalized
sum diverges:

\frac{\mathbb{E}(S_{\bullet,H})}{\sigma_{S,\bullet}}=\sigma_{S,\bullet}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{H}\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{2}\{\hat{T}^{(\bullet)}_{ij}(k)\}\longrightarrow\infty,

which proves consistency of the sum-type and adaptive tests.

A4.26. Proof of Theorem
4.26

Enumerate all blockwise component-pair statistics by

\{\hat{T}_{s}^{\mathrm{mut}}:1\leq s\leq M_{m}\}
. Under the null hypothesis of mutual independence,
all these centered statistics have mean zero. The max statistic can be rewritten as

L_{\mathrm{mut}}=\max_{1\leq s\leq M_{m}}|\hat{T}_{s}^{\mathrm{mut}}|.

Assumption
4.30
gives exactly the same weak-dependence condition as in the
proof of Theorem
4.23
; therefore

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{L_{\mathrm{mut}}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\mathrm{mut}}^{2}-2\log M_{m}+\log\log M_{m}\leq y\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\right|\to 0.

For the sum statistic,

S_{\mathrm{mut}}=\sum_{s=1}^{M_{m}}\{(\hat{T}_{s}^{\mathrm{mut}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{0}(\hat{T}_{s}^{\mathrm{mut}})^{2}\}

has variance
\sigma_{S,\mathrm{mut}}^{2}
of the same order as the sum of pairwise covariances. The
bounded-kernel/score assumption and the Gaussian approximation built into
Assumption
4.30
imply a Lyapunov condition, and hence

S_{\mathrm{mut}}/\sigma_{S,\mathrm{mut}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

The asymptotic independence in (
4.227
) follows by the same rare-
exceedance decomposition used in A4.25. Under the sparse alternative
(
4.229
), the maximal mean shift dominates the extreme-value threshold;
under the dense alternative (
4.230
), the mean of the normalized sum
explodes. These two facts prove consistency of the max-type, sum-type, and adaptive tests in the
respective regimes.

A4.27. Proof of Theorem
4.27

The proof is parallel to that of Theorem
4.26
. Let

N_{pq}=pq
and enumerate the statistics by

\{\hat{T}_{s}^{XY}:1\leq s\leq N_{pq}\}
. Under
Assumption
4.31
, the studentized vector satisfies the same weak-dependence
condition required by the extreme-value approximation. Therefore,

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\{L_{\bullet,XY}^{2}/\sigma_{L,\bullet}^{2}-2\log N_{pq}+\log\log N_{pq}\leq y\}-G_{\mathrm{EV}}(y)\right|\to 0.

For the sum statistic,

S_{\bullet,XY}=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{pq}}\{(\hat{T}_{s}^{XY})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{0}(\hat{T}_{s}^{XY})^{2}\}

obeys a central limit theorem because the standardized third moments are summable:

\sum_{s=1}^{N_{pq}}\mathbb{E}_{0}|\hat{T}_{s}^{XY}|^{2+\delta}=o(\sigma_{S,\bullet}^{2+\delta}).

This yields (
4.240
). For the joint limit, let

\mathcal{J}(z)=\{s:\ |\hat{T}_{s}^{XY}|>z\sigma_{L,\bullet}\},\qquad z=(2\log N_{pq}-\log\log N_{pq}+y)^{1/2},

and decompose
S_{\bullet,XY}
into the exceedance and non-exceedance parts. The exceedance part
is
o_{P}(\sigma_{S,\bullet})
, while the non-exceedance part is asymptotically Gaussian and decouples
from the Poisson-type limit driving the extreme-value law. Hence
(
4.241
) and (
4.242
) follow. Under
(
4.243
), the maximal mean dominates the Gumbel threshold; under
(
4.244
), the normalized sum has diverging mean. Finally, when the sparse
alternative holds but the sum statistic is centered at its shifted Gaussian mean rather than the null
mean, the same decoupling argument gives the H1 asymptotic independence stated after
(
4.244
).

Chapter 5
Classification under Elliptical Symmetry: LDA, QDA, and Sparse High-Dimensional Rules

5.1
Introduction

Classification is one of the core methodological themes of modern data analysis.
Given a feature vector
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and a class label
L\in\{1,2\}
, the goal
is to construct a decision rule
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\{1,2\}
with small
misclassification probability. In low dimensions, the classical theory is built
around Fisher’s linear discriminant analysis (LDA) and quadratic discriminant
analysis (QDA); see
3
. In high dimensions, however, the
sample covariance matrix is singular or unstable, and the direct plug-in
versions of LDA and QDA no longer work. This gave rise to a large literature on
high-dimensional sparse discriminant analysis under Gaussian or light-tailed
models, including the independence rule, FAIR, thresholded LDA, direct sparse
LDA, direct sparse QDA, and related penalized formulations; see

9
;
31
;
119
;
16
;
98
;
142
;
25
;
78
;
69
;
19
.

The purpose of this chapter is to place discriminant analysis into the same
elliptical-symmetry framework that has guided the rest of the book. The guiding
message is parallel to that of Chapters 2–4. First, one should understand the
classical fixed-
p
discriminant rules, their statistics, and the asymptotic
behavior of their plug-in versions. Second, one should review the main
high-dimensional Gaussian benchmarks in explicit algebraic form. Third, one
should replace fragile mean and covariance estimators by robust location and
shape estimators when the populations are elliptically symmetric and may be
heavy tailed.

In accordance with the writing principle adopted after Chapter 2, this chapter
is not written as a brief survey. For each main topic we explicitly record the
classification statistic, the corresponding risk functional, the assumptions used
for the asymptotic theory, and the main proof mechanism. The chapter is
organized as follows.

(i)

Section
5.2
formulates the binary classification problem,
introduces the oracle LDA and QDA scores, and explains how these rules extend
from Gaussian populations to elliptical populations.

(ii)

Section
5.3
reviews the classical fixed-dimensional
theory. This includes the exact Bayes error of Fisher’s rule and the
fixed-
p
asymptotic normality of the plug-in LDA and QDA scores.

(iii)

Section
5.4
reviews the main high-dimensional
Gaussian and light-tail benchmark methods with their explicit optimization
formulas and risk rates.

(iv)

Section
5.5
collects the robust building blocks
needed later: classwise spatial medians, spatial-sign covariance matrices,
trace estimation, and covariance surrogates.

(v)

Sections
5.6
and
5.7
develop the two main
robust high-dimensional procedures of this chapter: spatial-sign based sparse
linear discriminant analysis (SSLDA) and spatial-sign based sparse quadratic
discriminant analysis (SSQDA).

A second organizing principle is that the classification chapter, unlike the
preceding testing chapters, is naturally phrased in terms of
risk
rather
than null distributions. Nevertheless, the proof skeleton remains very similar:
one first proves estimation rates for the key discriminant objects, then obtains
uniform control of the induced score error, and finally translates score error
into excess-risk error by a margin or density condition near the decision
boundary.

5.2
Problem formulation, notation, and oracle discriminant rules

5.2.1
Binary classification and the risk functional

Throughout the chapter we begin with the two-class problem. Let

L\in\{1,2\}
be the class label and let
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be the feature vector.
The conditional distribution of
\bm{Z}
given
L=k
is denoted by
P_{k}
and has
density
f_{k}
with respect to Lebesgue measure. The prior probabilities are

\pi_{k}=\mathbb{P}(L=k)
with
\pi_{1}+\pi_{2}=1
. For a measurable classifier

G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\{1,2\}
, the misclassification risk is

R(G)=\pi_{1}\mathbb{P}_{1}\{G(\bm{Z})=2\}+\pi_{2}\mathbb{P}_{2}\{G(\bm{Z})=1\}.

(5.1)

The Bayes classifier is

G_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{z})=\begin{cases}1,&\pi_{1}f_{1}(\bm{z})\geq\pi_{2}f_{2}(\bm{z}),\\
2,&\pi_{1}f_{1}(\bm{z})<\pi_{2}f_{2}(\bm{z}),\end{cases}

(5.2)

and it uniquely minimizes (
5.1
) up to
P_{1}+P_{2}
-null sets.

We will use the following notational conventions throughout the chapter. The
location difference is

\bm{\delta}=\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2},\qquad\bm{\mu}=\frac{\bm{\mu}_{1}+\bm{\mu}_{2}}{2},\qquad\bar{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\bm{\mu}_{1}+\bm{\mu}_{2}}{2}.

(5.3)

When a covariance matrix exists, it is denoted by
\bm{\Sigma}_{k}
in class
k
and

\bm{\Omega}_{k}=\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}
denotes the corresponding precision matrix. In the
homoscedastic case we write
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}=\bm{\Sigma}_{2}=\bm{\Sigma}
and

\bm{\Omega}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
. Under elliptical symmetry, we distinguish the covariance
matrix from the shape matrix: when
\bm{\Sigma}_{k}
exists and

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})>0
, we write

\bm{\Lambda}_{k}=\frac{p\bm{\Sigma}_{k}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})},\qquad\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{k})=p.

(5.4)

5.2.2
Oracle LDA under Gaussian populations

Suppose first that

\bm{Z}\mid(L=k)\sim N_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{k},\bm{\Sigma}),\qquad k=1,2,

(5.5)

with common positive definite covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
. The log-likelihood
ratio is, up to an additive constant,

q_{F}(\bm{z})=\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\mu}_{2}\bigr)+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.6)

Equivalently,

q_{F}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.7)

The Fisher classifier is therefore

G_{F}(\bm{z})=\begin{cases}1,&q_{F}(\bm{z})\geq 0,\\
2,&q_{F}(\bm{z})<0.\end{cases}

(5.8)

When
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
, this is precisely Fisher’s linear discriminant rule.

The signal strength parameter for LDA is the Mahalanobis separation

\Delta_{F}^{2}=\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}.

(5.9)

This quantity will repeatedly appear in both the low-dimensional and
high-dimensional theories.

5.2.3
Oracle QDA under Gaussian populations

When the two classes have different covariance matrices,

\bm{Z}\mid(L=k)\sim N_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{k},\bm{\Sigma}_{k}),\qquad k=1,2,

(5.10)

with positive definite
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}
, the Bayes rule compares the
quadratic score

q_{Q}(\bm{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|+\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{2}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.11)

It is often convenient to rewrite the score in the form emphasized by

69
and
19
. Define

\bm{D}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}-\bm{\Omega}_{1},\qquad\bm{\beta}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}(\bm{\mu}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{1}),\qquad\bar{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\bm{\mu}_{1}+\bm{\mu}_{2}}{2}.

(5.12)

Then (
5.11
) is algebraically equivalent to

q_{Q}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{D}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})-2\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{\mu}})-\eta,\qquad\eta=\log\frac{|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|}-2\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.13)

The QDA classifier is
G_{Q}(\bm{z})=1\{q_{Q}(\bm{z})\geq 0\}+2\{q_{Q}(\bm{z})<0\}
.

The representation (
5.13
) is crucial in high dimensions,
because it shows that the truly relevant parameters are not the full covariance
matrices themselves but rather the quadratic interaction matrix
\bm{D}
and the
linear discriminant direction
\bm{\beta}
.

5.2.4
Elliptical populations and generalized discriminant scores

The Gaussian formulas above remain the correct point of departure under
elliptical symmetry. Suppose now that class
k
has density

f_{k}(\bm{z})=|\bm{\Lambda}_{k}|^{-1/2}g_{k}\bigl(d_{k}(\bm{z})\bigr),\qquad d_{k}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{k}),\qquad k=1,2,

(5.14)

where
g_{k}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a decreasing radial generator and

\bm{\Lambda}_{k}
is positive definite. The Bayes rule compares

q_{E}(\bm{z})=\log\pi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}|+\log g_{1}\{d_{1}(\bm{z})\}-\log\pi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{2}|-\log g_{2}\{d_{2}(\bm{z})\}.

(5.15)

Two special cases are central for this chapter.

(a)

If
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
and
\bm{\Lambda}_{1}=\bm{\Lambda}_{2}=\bm{\Lambda}
, then monotonicity of

g
implies that the Bayes rule is equivalent to the linear score

(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}+\log(\pi_{1}/\pi_{2})
.

(b)

If
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
but
\bm{\Lambda}_{1}eq\bm{\Lambda}_{2}
, then the Bayes rule is a
generalized QDA rule comparing
\log g\{d_{1}(\bm{z})\}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}|

and
\log g\{d_{2}(\bm{z})\}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{2}|
.

Thus, the linear-versus-quadratic dichotomy persists well beyond the Gaussian
model. What changes under elliptical symmetry is that one should estimate
location and shape robustly, and one should not insist on using raw second
moments when the radial distribution may be heavy tailed.

Theorem 5.1

(Oracle discriminant score under elliptical symmetry)
.

Assume that the class-conditional densities satisfy
(
5.14
). Then the Bayes classifier is obtained by
thresholding the score (
5.15
). In particular:

(i)

if
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
and
\bm{\Lambda}_{1}=\bm{\Lambda}_{2}=\bm{\Lambda}
, then
(
5.15
) is equivalent to the linear score

q_{E,F}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}};

(5.16)

(ii)

if
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
but
\bm{\Lambda}_{1}eq\bm{\Lambda}_{2}
, then the Bayes classifier is
a generalized QDA rule comparing

\log g\{d_{1}(\bm{z})\}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}|+\log\pi_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\log g\{d_{2}(\bm{z})\}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{2}|+\log\pi_{2}.

(5.17)

5.3
Classical low-dimensional discriminant analysis

5.3.1
Fisher’s linear discriminant rule and its Bayes risk

We first return to the fixed-dimensional Gaussian benchmark
(
5.5
). In that setting, Fisher’s statistic is the linear
score
q_{F}(\bm{z})
in (
5.6
). The exact error probability under
equal priors has a closed form.

Theorem 5.2

(Exact Bayes error of Fisher’s rule)
.

Assume (
5.5
) with
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=1/2
and

\bm{\Sigma}
positive definite. Let
\Delta_{F}^{2}=\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}
.
Then the oracle Fisher classifier (
5.8
) satisfies

R(G_{F})=\Phi\!\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right),

(5.18)

where
\Phi
denotes the standard normal distribution function.

5.3.2
Plug-in LDA in fixed dimension

Suppose now that
p
is fixed while
n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty
. Let

\bar{\bm{X}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\bm{X}_{i},\qquad\bar{\bm{Y}}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\bm{Y}_{j},

(5.19)

and let the pooled covariance matrix be

\bm{S}_{p}=\frac{1}{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})(\bm{X}_{i}-\bar{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}(\bm{Y}_{j}-\bar{\bm{Y}})(\bm{Y}_{j}-\bar{\bm{Y}})^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigg].

(5.20)

The classical plug-in Fisher score is

\hat{q}_{F}(\bm{z})=\left(\bm{z}-\frac{\bar{\bm{X}}+\bar{\bm{Y}}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bar{\bm{Y}})+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.21)

Theorem 5.3

(Fixed-
p
asymptotic normality of the plug-in LDA score)
.

Assume (
5.5
) and let
p
be fixed. If

n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty
with
n_{1}/(n_{1}+n_{2})\to\rho\in(0,1)
, then for each fixed

\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\sqrt{n_{1}+n_{2}}\,\bigl\{\hat{q}_{F}(\bm{z})-q_{F}(\bm{z})\bigr\}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N\{0,\tau_{F}^{2}(\bm{z})\},

(5.22)

where

\tau_{F}^{2}(\bm{z})=\rho^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Omega}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})+(1-\rho)^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Omega}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})+\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}.

(5.23)

The exact algebraic form of
\tau_{F}^{2}(\bm{z})
is not the main issue in the book; the
important point is the proof mechanism. One expands
\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}
around

\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
and jointly linearizes the sample means and pooled covariance.
This fixed-
p
delta-method argument is the baseline that later breaks down when

p
is comparable to or larger than
n
.

5.3.3
Plug-in QDA in fixed dimension

In the heteroscedastic Gaussian model (
5.10
), let

\bm{S}_{k}=\frac{1}{n_{k}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}(\bm{Z}_{ki}-\bar{\bm{Z}}_{k})(\bm{Z}_{ki}-\bar{\bm{Z}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad k=1,2,

(5.24)

where
\bm{Z}_{1i}=\bm{X}_{i}
,
\bm{Z}_{2j}=\bm{Y}_{j}
,
\bar{\bm{Z}}_{1}=\bar{\bm{X}}
, and

\bar{\bm{Z}}_{2}=\bar{\bm{Y}}
. The plug-in QDA score is

\hat{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{S}_{1}|+\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{S}_{2}|-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{1}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{X}})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{Y}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{2}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{Y}})+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

(5.25)

Theorem 5.4

(Fixed-
p
asymptotic normality of the plug-in QDA score)
.

Assume (
5.10
) and let
p
be fixed. If

n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty
with
n_{1}/(n_{1}+n_{2})\to\rho\in(0,1)
, then for each fixed

\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\sqrt{n_{1}+n_{2}}\,\bigl\{\hat{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})-q_{Q}(\bm{z})\bigr\}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N\{0,\tau_{Q}^{2}(\bm{z})\},

(5.26)

where
\tau_{Q}^{2}(\bm{z})
is the asymptotic variance obtained by jointly
linearizing
\bar{\bm{X}}
,
\bar{\bm{Y}}
,
\bm{S}_{1}
,
\bm{S}_{2}
, and the
log-determinant term.

The theorem has two immediate interpretations. First, in fixed dimension the
plug-in QDA score is still asymptotically regular even though it depends on two
covariance matrices and a log-determinant term. Second, the difficulty of QDA
in high dimensions is not a lack of smoothness but a lack of estimability: the
quadratic score depends on too many second-order parameters.

5.3.4
Elliptical populations in fixed dimension

The fixed-
p
literature on elliptical discriminant analysis is older than the
high-dimensional literature and deserves a brief but explicit review.

36
showed that the Fisher rule remains optimal in the
homoscedastic elliptical model with common generator, while
130

studied the fixed-dimensional asymptotics of discriminant analysis under
elliptical populations. In the heteroscedastic setting,

12
proposed a generalized quadratic discriminant
analysis rule, and
55
developed a robust
version designed to reduce sensitivity to outliers. Robust fixed-
p
linear
rules based on high-breakdown estimators were earlier studied by

26
;
27
.

The common fixed-dimensional model is (
5.14
). The
statistic remains the generalized log-density score
q_{E}(\bm{z})
in
(
5.15
). If the generator is known, the plug-in version
is obtained by replacing
\bm{\mu}_{k}
and
\bm{\Lambda}_{k}
by robust estimators such as
M-estimators, S-estimators, the minimum covariance determinant estimator, or,
in the present book’s preferred language, the spatial median and a robust shape
estimator. The fixed-
p
asymptotic theory again follows from a delta-method
argument:

\sqrt{n}\bigl\{\hat{q}_{E}(\bm{z})-q_{E}(\bm{z})\bigr\}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,\tau_{E}^{2}(\bm{z})),

(5.27)

provided the classwise location and shape estimators admit joint asymptotic
linear representations. In the remainder of this chapter we no longer work in
fixed dimension, but the fixed-
p
review above explains why the high-dimensional
robust methods should focus on direct estimation of the discriminant objects
rather than on full covariance reconstruction.

5.4
High-dimensional Gaussian and light-tail benchmarks

This section reviews the main benchmark methods that form the high-dimensional
background of the robust procedures developed later in the chapter. The point is
not to reproduce the entire benchmark literature, but to state explicitly the
statistics that are being replaced by robust elliptical analogues.

5.4.1
The independence rule and FAIR

9
showed that the naive plug-in Fisher rule based on the full
sample covariance matrix can deteriorate badly when
p
is comparable to or
larger than
n
, even under Gaussian assumptions. Their proposed replacement is
the
independence rule
, which ignores off-diagonal covariance terms and
classifies according to

G_{\mathrm{ind}}(\bm{z})=1\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\bar{X}_{j}-\bar{Y}_{j}}{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}\left(z_{j}-\frac{\bar{X}_{j}+\bar{Y}_{j}}{2}\right)\geq 0\right\}+2\{\cdot<0\},

(5.28)

where
\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}
is a pooled marginal variance estimate.
31
’s
feature-annealed independence rule (FAIR) further screens coordinates before
applying the independence rule. If
\mathcal{J}\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
denotes the
selected coordinate set, FAIR uses

G_{\mathrm{FAIR}}(\bm{z})=1\left\{\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\frac{\bar{X}_{j}-\bar{Y}_{j}}{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}\left(z_{j}-\frac{\bar{X}_{j}+\bar{Y}_{j}}{2}\right)\geq 0\right\}+2\{\cdot<0\}.

(5.29)

The key asymptotic message of FAIR is that if most coordinates are nondiscriminant,
then aggressive screening can substantially reduce the accumulation of noise in the
linear score.

5.4.2
Plug-in sparse-covariance and sparse-precision approaches for LDA

A second major route is to estimate the discriminant ingredients themselves and then plug them
into the classical LDA score. Let

\hat{g}_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}(\bm{z})=\bigl(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}\bigr)^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}\hat{\bm{\delta}},\qquad\hat{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}+\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}}{2},\qquad\hat{\bm{\delta}}=\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2},

(5.30)

where
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}
is obtained either by inverting a sparse covariance estimator or by directly
estimating a sparse precision matrix. In thresholding-based plug-in LDA one first constructs

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{T}=(\hat{\sigma}_{ij}\mathbbm{1}\{|\hat{\sigma}_{ij}|>\tau_{n}\})_{1\leq i,j\leq p}

(5.31)

and then sets
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{T}=\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{T}^{-1}
whenever the thresholded estimator is positive
definite. When only the mean difference is sparse, a companion thresholding step is

\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{T}=(\hat{\delta}_{j}\mathbbm{1}\{|\hat{\delta}_{j}|>\eta_{n}\})_{1\leq j\leq p}.

(5.32)

The resulting classifier is

G_{\mathrm{T-LDA}}(\bm{z})=1\{(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{T}\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{T}\geq 0\}+2\{\cdot<0\}.

(5.33)

This plug-in philosophy underlies the thresholded LDA rules of

119
and connects naturally to the covariance and precision estimation
methods reviewed in Chapter 3; see also the broad overview of

32
.

A closely related variant estimates the precision matrix directly by sparse inverse-covariance
methods such as CLIME, graphical Lasso, TIGER, or other penalized likelihood procedures and then
forms the same score (
5.30
). Algebraically this route remains a plug-in
LDA rule, even though the precision matrix is estimated directly rather than through inversion of a
covariance estimator. The main theoretical requirement is that the score error induced by

(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2},\hat{\bm{\Omega}})
is asymptotically smaller than the Bayes margin.

Proposition 5.1

(Generic plug-in LDA consistency)
.

Assume the common-covariance Gaussian model and suppose that

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{P}(1),\qquad\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1).

(5.34)

Then, for every deterministic sequence
\bm{z}_{n}
with
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
,

\hat{g}_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{L}(\bm{z}_{n})=o_{P}(1).

(5.35)

If, in addition, the Bayes margin is separated away from zero on the decision boundary, then the
misclassification risk of
G_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}
converges to the Bayes risk.

The proposition is deliberately generic: it covers thresholded-covariance LDA, sparse-precision
plug-in LDA, and ridge-stabilized versions of the same idea. Its value in this chapter is that it
makes clear how the large-matrix estimation theory of Chapter 3 feeds directly into high-
dimensional classification.

5.4.3
Direct sparse LDA

The central algebraic object in homoscedastic LDA is the discriminant direction

\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}
.
16
’s linear programming discriminant
(LPD) rule estimates this vector directly instead of separately estimating

\bm{\Omega}
and
\bm{\delta}
. The estimator is

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{LPD}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\gamma}-\hat{\bm{\delta}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{n}\bigr\},

(5.36)

where
\hat{\bm{\delta}}=\bar{\bm{X}}-\bar{\bm{Y}}
and
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
is a pooled covariance
estimate. The classifier is then

G_{\mathrm{LPD}}(\bm{z})=1\Bigl\{\bigl(\bm{z}-(\bar{\bm{X}}+\bar{\bm{Y}})/2\bigr)^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{LPD}}\geq 0\Bigr\}+2\{\cdot<0\}.

(5.37)

Under sparsity of
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}
and standard eigenvalue and balance conditions,
16

derived both consistency and relative-risk convergence rates.

98
proposed another direct sparse LDA route by recasting the
LDA problem as a penalized least-squares regression. Their DSDA estimator is
characterized by

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{DSDA}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Biggl\{\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-a-\bm{x}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\gamma})^{2}+\lambda\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}\right\rVert_{1}\Biggr\},

(5.38)

for a suitable coding
y_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
.
142
and, separately,

25
developed closely related penalized Fisher and sparse
optimal-scoring formulations.
61
further showed how Gaussian-copula
transformations can extend this line of work beyond strict normality while still relying on
light-tail or latent-Gaussian structure.

5.4.4
Plug-in and regularized QDA via sparse classwise matrices

In QDA, a natural benchmark route is to estimate each class-specific covariance or precision
matrix and then insert those estimates into the quadratic score. If

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1},\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}
and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are classwise sparse estimators, the
plug-in QDA score is

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{plugQDA}}(\bm{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1})}{\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2})}-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}).

(5.39)

Equivalently, one can estimate sparse classwise precision matrices
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k}
and use

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{plugQDA}}(\bm{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{2})}{\det(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{1})}-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{1}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}).

(5.40)

This is the route taken by the sparse QDA procedures of
78
, where sparsity is
imposed on class-specific means and covariance matrices, together with positive-definiteness or
regularization constraints ensuring invertibility. The main theoretical task is to control the error of
each quadratic form and the log-determinant contrast.

Proposition 5.2

(Generic plug-in QDA consistency)
.

Assume the heteroscedastic Gaussian model and suppose that

\sum_{k=1}^{2}\bigl\{\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\bigr\}=o_{P}(1),

(5.41)

with each
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}
positive definite. Then for every deterministic sequence
\bm{z}_{n}
with

\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
,

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{plugQDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{Q}(\bm{z}_{n})=o_{P}(1).

(5.42)

If the Bayes quadratic margin is separated, then the misclassification risk of the plug-in QDA rule
converges to the Bayes risk.

5.4.5
Direct high-dimensional QDA benchmarks

QDA is more difficult than LDA because the discriminant score depends on two
precision matrices and therefore on
O(p^{2})
unknown parameters.
78
’s sparse
QDA approach imposes sparsity on class-specific means and covariance matrices.
69

observed that the QDA score depends more fundamentally on the interaction matrix

\bm{D}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}-\bm{\Omega}_{1}
and the linear term
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}(\bm{\mu}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{1})
, and
proposed the direct estimator

\hat{\bm{D}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{D}}\bigl\{\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\bm{D}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\bm{D}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}+\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{1,n}\bigr\},

(5.43)

\hat{\bm{\beta}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\beta}}\bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\bm{\beta}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}+\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{2,n}\bigr\}.

(5.44)

19
sharpened this direct approach and established minimax-optimal
rates, up to logarithmic factors, over sparse parameter spaces. Their SDAR rule
will be the direct Gaussian benchmark for the SSQDA development below.

The benchmark literature therefore leaves us with two complementary templates. In
plug-in LDA/QDA, estimate sparse means and sparse covariance or precision matrices and then
insert them into the classical score. In direct sparse LDA/QDA, estimate the discriminant
objects
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}
,
\bm{D}
, and
\bm{\beta}
directly. The robust elliptical theory of this chapter
