keeps both templates alive and revisits them with spatial signs, spatial medians, and robust
precision estimation.

5.5
Robust building blocks under elliptical symmetry

We now collect the robust location and shape ingredients used later in SSLDA and
SSQDA. The notation is as follows. Class 1 training samples are

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n_{1}}
and class 2 training samples are

\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n_{2}}
. We write
n=n_{1}\wedge n_{2}
and assume throughout that

n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
.

5.5.1
Classwise spatial medians and spatial-sign covariance matrices

For
k=1,2
, define the classwise spatial medians by

\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert,\qquad\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\left\lVert\bm{Y}_{j}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert.

(5.45)

The corresponding classwise SSCMs are

\tilde{\bm{S}}_{1}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\tilde{\bm{S}}_{2}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}U(\bm{Y}_{j}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})U(\bm{Y}_{j}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(5.46)

Under a common-shape LDA model we also use the pooled SSCM

\tilde{\bm{S}}=\frac{n_{1}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{1}+n_{2}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}.

(5.47)

5.5.2
Trace estimation and covariance surrogates

In homoscedastic SSLDA the overall covariance scale plays no role in the
decision rule. In heteroscedastic QDA, however, the scale difference between the
two classes matters. Following the SSQDA paper, we estimate the trace by the
Chen–Qin type
U
-statistic

\hat{\tau}_{1}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)(n_{1}-2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j,k\leq n_{1}\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{X}_{k}-\bm{X}_{j}),

(5.48)

\hat{\tau}_{2}=\frac{1}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)(n_{2}-2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j,k\leq n_{2}\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}(\bm{Y}_{i}-\bm{Y}_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{Y}_{k}-\bm{Y}_{j}).

(5.49)

The classwise covariance surrogates are then

\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}=\hat{\tau}_{k}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{k},\qquad k=1,2.

(5.50)

The reason for (
5.50
) is that under trace normalization

\bm{\Lambda}_{k}=p\bm{\Sigma}_{k}/\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})
one expects
p\tilde{\bm{S}}_{k}
to estimate

\bm{\Lambda}_{k}
, while
\hat{\tau}_{k}
estimates the scalar trace factor.

5.5.3
Assumptions for the robust high-dimensional theory

We first record the assumptions for the homoscedastic LDA problem.

Assumption 5.1

(Common-shape elliptical LDA model)
.

The class-conditional distributions satisfy

\bm{X}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}EC_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{1},\bm{\Lambda},r),\qquad\bm{Y}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}EC_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{2},\bm{\Lambda},r),

(5.51)

with a common radial generator and a common positive definite shape matrix

\bm{\Lambda}
normalized by
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda})=p
. If the covariance exists, then

\bm{\Sigma}=\omega\bm{\Lambda}
for some scalar
\omega>0
. The prior probabilities are

\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=1/2
.

Assumption 5.2

(Regularity for SSLDA)
.

Let
\Delta_{p}=\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
and

\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
. The following hold.

(i)

n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
,
\log p\leq n
, and
n=n_{1}\wedge n_{2}
.

(ii)

There exist constants
0<c_{0}<c_{1}<\infty
such that

c_{0}\leq\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Lambda})\leq\lambda_{1}(\bm{\Lambda})\leq c_{1},\qquad\max_{1\leq j\leq p}(\bm{\Sigma})_{jj}\leq c_{1},\qquad\Delta_{p}\geq c_{0}.

(5.52)

(iii)

Writing
\bm{S}_{0}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{1})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr]
, there exists

\psi\in(0,1)
such that

\lambda_{1}(\bm{S}_{0})\leq 1-\psi.

(5.53)

Assumption 5.3

(Inverse radial moments and SSCM approximation for SSLDA)
.

Let
\xi_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert
and define

\zeta_{k}=\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}^{-k})
,
u_{i}=\zeta_{1}^{-1}\xi_{i}^{-1}
. The same quantities for
class 2 satisfy the same conditions. The following hold.

(i)

For
k=1,2,3,4
,
\zeta_{k}\zeta_{1}^{-k}\leq C
uniformly in
p
.

(ii)

u_{i}
is sub-Gaussian with
\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq C
.

(iii)

The population SSCM approximates the normalized shape in max norm:

\left\lVert p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq Cp^{-1/2}.

(5.54)

(iv)

The classwise spatial medians and SSCMs satisfy the high-dimensional rates

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}-p\bm{S}_{0}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}

(5.55)

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
.

For the heteroscedastic QDA problem we use the following parameter conditions.

Assumption 5.4

(Elliptical QDA model)
.

The class-conditional distributions satisfy

\bm{X}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}EC_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{1},\bm{\Sigma}_{1},r),\qquad\bm{Y}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}EC_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{2},\bm{\Sigma}_{2},r),

(5.56)

with positive definite covariance matrices
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}
and
normalized shapes
\bm{\Lambda}_{k}=p\bm{\Sigma}_{k}/\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})
.

Assumption 5.5

(Sparsity and boundedness for SSQDA)
.

Define

\bm{D}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}-\bm{\Omega}_{1},\qquad\bm{\beta}=\bm{\Omega}_{2}(\bm{\mu}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{1}).

(5.57)

There exist integers
s_{1},s_{2}\geq 0
and constants
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2}>0
such that

(i)

\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})\right\rVert_{0}\leq s_{1}
and
\left\lVert\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{0}\leq s_{2}
;

(ii)

\left\lVert\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq M_{0}
and
\left\lVert\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{2}\leq M_{0}
;

(iii)

M_{1}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\leq\lambda_{1}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\leq M_{1}
and

\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq M_{2}
for
k=1,2
;

(iv)

\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\asymp p
for
k=1,2
.

Assumption 5.6

(Inverse radial moments, shape regularity, and margin for SSQDA)
.

The following hold for each class
k=1,2
.

(i)

Writing
\bm{V}_{0k}=\bm{\Lambda}_{k}^{-1}
, there exist constants
T>0
,

0\leq q<1
, and a slowly varying sequence
s_{0}(p)
such that

\left\lVert\bm{V}_{0k}\right\rVert_{1}\leq T,\qquad\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}|(\bm{V}_{0k})_{ij}|^{q}\leq s_{0}(p).

(5.58)

(ii)

If
\xi_{i}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert
or
\left\lVert\bm{Y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert
and

u_{i}=\zeta_{1}^{-1}\xi_{i}^{-1}
, then
\zeta_{k}\zeta_{1}^{-k}\leq C
for all

k=1,2,3,4
and
\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq C
.

(iii)

The scalar radial variable satisfies

\operatorname{Var}(r^{2})\leq Cp^{3/2},\qquad\operatorname{Var}(r)\leq Cp^{1/2}.

(5.59)

(iv)

If
Q(\bm{z})
denotes the oracle QDA score, then there exist
\delta_{0},M_{3}>0

such that

\sup_{|x|<\delta_{0}}f_{Q,\theta}(x)\leq M_{3},

(5.60)

where
f_{Q,\theta}
is the density of
Q(\bm{z})
under parameter value
\theta
.

5.5.4
Reusable high-dimensional building-block lemmas

The first lemma is a direct classwise reformulation of the spatial-median theory
proved in Chapter 2.

Lemma 5.1

(Classwise Bahadur expansion for the spatial median)
.

Under Assumptions
5.1
–
5.3
, for

k=1,2
,

\sqrt{n_{k}}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k})=\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\bm{U}_{ki}+\bm{r}_{k,n},

(5.61)

where
\bm{U}_{ki}=U(\bm{Z}_{ki}-\bm{\mu}_{k})
and

\left\lVert\bm{r}_{k,n}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\left\{n^{-1/4}\log^{1/2}(np)+n^{-1/2}\log p\right\}

(5.62)

on an event of probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
. In particular,

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n_{k}}}

(5.63)

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
.

Lemma 5.2

(SSCM concentration and covariance-surrogate concentration)
.

Under Assumptions
5.1
–
5.3
,

\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}-\bm{\Lambda}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\left(p^{-1/2}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)

(5.64)

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
. Under
Assumptions
5.4
–
5.6
, for

k=1,2
,

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq CK_{n,p},\qquad K_{n,p}=p^{-1/2}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

(5.65)

with probability at least
1-C/\log p
.

The two lemmas above are the fundamental input for the direct optimization
problems of SSLDA and SSQDA. Once they are available, the rest of the analysis
is deterministic convex-analytic perturbation plus a final risk translation.

5.6
Spatial-sign based sparse linear discriminant analysis

5.6.1
The estimator and classifier

Under the common-shape elliptical model, Fang and Anderson’s classical result
shows that the optimal linear discriminant direction is proportional to

\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
. Since overall scale does not affect the sign of the
linear score, it is natural to define the target direction as

\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}.

(5.66)

The spatial-sign based direct estimator (SSLDA) is obtained by solving

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}\bm{\gamma}-(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{n}\Bigr\}.

(5.67)

The resulting classifier is

\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSLDA}}(\bm{z})=1\Bigl\{(\bm{z}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\geq 0\Bigr\}+2\{\cdot<0\},\qquad\tilde{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}+\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}}{2}.

(5.68)

To express its conditional risk we introduce the one-dimensional marginal cdf

\Psi(t)=\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1/2}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{1})\leq t\Bigr\},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}=1.

(5.69)

Under elliptical symmetry
\Psi
does not depend on the choice of the unit
vector
\bm{u}
and is symmetric about the origin. The oracle risk is

R^{\star}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}\left\{(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}<-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}\left\{(\bm{Y}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}\geq\frac{1}{2}\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}\right\}.

(5.70)

The feasible conditional risk, given the training sample, is

\hat{R}_{n}=1-\frac{1}{2}\Psi\left(-\frac{(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}}{(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{1/2}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\Psi\left(\frac{(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}}{(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{1/2}}\right).

(5.71)

5.6.2
Feasibility and estimation error

The direct optimization (
5.67
) requires the true target

\bm{\gamma}^{\star}
to be feasible with high probability. This follows from the
building-block lemmas.

Lemma 5.3

(Feasibility of the oracle direction)
.

Under Assumptions
5.1
–
5.3
, if

\lambda_{n}\geq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{p}\log p}{n}},

(5.72)

then with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
,

\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}-(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{n}.

(5.73)

Consequently
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}
.

The next theorem is the main consistency theorem for SSLDA.

Theorem 5.5

(Consistency of SSLDA)
.

Suppose Assumptions
5.1
–
5.3

hold. Let

\lambda_{n}=C\sqrt{\Delta_{p}\frac{\log p}{n}},

(5.74)

with
C
sufficiently large. If

\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}}{\Delta_{p}^{1/2}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}}{\Delta_{p}}=o\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log p}}\right),

(5.75)

then

\hat{R}_{n}-R^{\star}\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}0.

(5.76)

Theorem 5.6

(Relative-risk rate of SSLDA)
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
5.5
hold and, in
addition,
n^{-1}p\log p\to 0
. If

\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\Delta_{p}^{1/2}+\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}=o\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log p}}\right),

(5.77)

then with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
,

\frac{\hat{R}_{n}}{R^{\star}}-1\leq C\Bigl(\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\Delta_{p}^{1/2}+\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\Bigr)\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}.

(5.78)

In particular, if
\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{0}\Delta_{p}=o\bigl(\sqrt{n/\log p}\bigr)
,
then

\frac{\hat{R}_{n}}{R^{\star}}-1\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{0}\Delta_{p}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}

(5.79)

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
.

5.6.3
Interpretation of the SSLDA theory

Theorems
5.5
and

5.6
are the robust elliptical counterparts of the
classical LPD theorems. The proof follows the same deterministic backbone as the
Gaussian direct sparse LDA literature, but the stochastic input is completely
different: classwise spatial medians replace sample means, the pooled SSCM
replaces the sample covariance matrix, and the resulting perturbation bounds
contain the additional
p^{-1/2}
term inherited from the SSCM-to-shape
approximation.

Two remarks are useful. First, if the covariance matrix exists, then

\bm{\Sigma}=\omega\bm{\Lambda}
and therefore the discriminant direction

\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}
differs from
\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
only by an irrelevant scalar.
Second, when the signal is sparse, the rate in
(
5.79
) has the same structural form as the
Gaussian LPD rate, but it remains valid far beyond Gaussian or sub-Gaussian
models.

5.7
Spatial-sign based sparse quadratic discriminant analysis

5.7.1
The estimator and classifier

We now return to the heteroscedastic model of QDA. In the robust elliptical
setting we replace the sample means by spatial medians and the sample covariance
matrices by the covariance surrogates (
5.50
). The direct
quadratic and linear estimators are

\tilde{\bm{D}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}}\Bigl\{\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert\tfrac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\bm{D}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\bm{D}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}+\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{1,n}\Bigr\},

(5.80)

\tilde{\bm{\beta}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}:\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\bm{\beta}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}+\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{2,n}\Bigr\}.

(5.81)

The SSQDA score is

\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\bm{D}}(\bm{z}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})-2\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{z}-\tilde{\bar{\bm{\mu}}})-\log|\tilde{\bm{D}}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}+\bm{I}_{p}|,\qquad\tilde{\bar{\bm{\mu}}}=\frac{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}+\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}}{2}.

(5.82)

The classifier is

\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}}(\bm{z})=1\{\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})>0\}+2\{\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})\leq 0\}.

(5.83)

5.7.2
Main estimation theorem

The first theorem establishes the rates of the direct estimators.

Theorem 5.7

(Estimation rates for SSQDA)
.

Suppose Assumptions
5.4
–
5.6

hold. Let

K_{n,p}=p^{-1/2}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

(5.84)

and choose

\lambda_{1,n}=c_{1}\sqrt{s_{1}}\,K_{n,p},\qquad\lambda_{2,n}=c_{2}\sqrt{s_{2}}\,K_{n,p},

(5.85)

for sufficiently large constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Then with probability at least

1-C/\log p
,

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq Cs_{1}K_{n,p},

(5.86)

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{2}\leq Cs_{2}K_{n,p}.

(5.87)

5.7.3
Excess-risk theory

The next theorem translates the estimation rates into a bound for the
misclassification error.

Theorem 5.8

(Excess-risk rate of SSQDA under elliptical symmetry)
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
5.7
hold and, in
addition,

s_{1}+s_{2}\leq c\,\{\log n\,K_{n,p}\}^{-1}

(5.88)

for a sufficiently small constant
c>0
. Then

\mathbb{E}\bigl\{R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}})-R(G_{Q})\bigr\}\leq C\left\{\frac{1}{\log p}+(s_{1}+s_{2})\log n\,K_{n,p}\right\}.

(5.89)

Theorem 5.9

(Gaussian specialization of the SSQDA risk rate)
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
5.7
hold and that
both classes are multivariate normal. Then

\mathbb{E}\bigl\{R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}})-R(G_{Q})\bigr\}\leq C(s_{1}+s_{2})^{2}\log^{2}n\,K_{n,p}^{2}.

(5.90)

The difference between Theorems
5.8
and

5.9
comes from trace estimation. In the general
elliptical case the trace estimator has only polynomial tail control, whereas
in the Gaussian case one can sharpen this part of the argument to exponential
concentration. The remaining perturbation analysis of the quadratic and linear
terms is identical.

5.8
Additional robust plug-in classifiers based on HR and precision estimation

The direct SSLDA and SSQDA procedures are not the only robust classifiers in the recent elliptical
literature. Two complementary plug-in routes are especially relevant for the present book. The
first combines robust location estimation with a robust sparse precision estimator and then plugs
the result into the classical LDA score. The second combines classwise high-dimensional
Hettmansperger–Randles estimators with a quadratic discriminant score. These methods are
important because they connect Chapters 1 and 3 to the present chapter in a very explicit way.

5.8.1
Precision-estimation plug-in LDA under elliptical symmetry

91
develop robust high-dimensional precision-matrix
estimation under elliptical symmetry and emphasizes classification as one of its primary
applications. Let
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}
denote the robust precision estimator and let

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}
,
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}
be classwise robust location estimators (spatial
medians or HR-type estimators). The resulting plug-in LDA score is

\hat{g}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}(\bm{z})=\Bigl(\bm{z}-\frac{\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}+\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}}{2}\Bigr)^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}),

(5.91)

and the classifier is

\hat{G}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}(\bm{z})=1\{\hat{g}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}(\bm{z})\geq 0\}+2\{\cdot<0\}.

(5.92)

This rule differs conceptually from SSLDA: SSLDA estimates the discriminant direction directly,
whereas (
5.91
) keeps the plug-in structure and relies on the sparse
precision estimator from Chapter 3.

Assumption 5.7

(Robust plug-in LDA rates)
.

Assume the common-covariance elliptical model of
Assumption
5.1
. Let

r_{n,p}=\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}.

(5.93)

Suppose that the robust precision estimator is one of the SSCM-based estimators from
Theorems
3.13
–
3.14
and that the classwise robust locations
are either the weighted spatial-sign estimators of Chapter 2 or the HR-type estimators of
Theorem
3.15
. Then

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0}(p)r_{n,p}^{1-q}\right),\qquad\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(r_{n,p}).

(5.94)

Define the aggregate plug-in rate

\rho^{\mathrm{LDA}}_{n,p}=s_{0}(p)r_{n,p}^{1-q}+r_{n,p}.

(5.95)

Proposition 5.3

(Score and risk control for robust plug-in LDA)
.

Under Assumption
5.7
, for every deterministic sequence
\bm{z}_{n}
with

\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
,

\hat{g}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{L}(\bm{z}_{n})=O_{P}\!\left(\rho^{\mathrm{LDA}}_{n,p}\right).

(5.96)

If the Bayes margin is separated, then

R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{robLDA}})-R(G_{L})=O_{P}\!\left(\rho^{\mathrm{LDA}}_{n,p}\right).

(5.97)

5.8.2
High-dimensional HR plug-in QDA

145
extend the Hettmansperger–Randles philosophy to the
high-dimensional setting and develop classification applications, including a robust QDA rule.
Let
(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}},\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})
be the classwise HR-type location–scatter
estimators, with corresponding inverse surrogates
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}
. The HR-QDA score is

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1,\mathrm{HR}})}{\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2,\mathrm{HR}})}-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{HR}})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{1,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{HR}})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{HR}})^{\top}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{2,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{HR}}),

(5.98)

and

\hat{G}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{z})=1\{\hat{q}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{z})>0\}+2\{\cdot\leq 0\}.

(5.99)

The point of this construction is that both the location and the classwise quadratic forms are built
from robust sign/rank geometry rather than from sample moments.

Assumption 5.8

(HR-QDA rates)
.

Assume the elliptical QDA model of Assumption
5.4
. For each class

k=1,2
, let

\eta_{k,n,p}=\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n_{k}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}+h_{k}^{-\alpha},

(5.100)

where
h_{k}
is the structural bandwidth from Theorem
3.15
. Suppose the
classwise HR estimators satisfy the explicit Chapter 3 rates

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}(\eta_{k,n,p}),

(5.101)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Omega}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0,k}(p)\eta_{k,n,p}^{1-q}\right),

(5.102)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=O_{P}\!\left(p\eta_{k,n,p}\right).

(5.103)

Assume in addition that
p\eta_{k,n,p}\to 0
for
k=1,2
, so that the log-determinant term is

o_{P}(1)
. Define

\rho^{\mathrm{QDA}}_{n,p}=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\left\{s_{0,k}(p)\eta_{k,n,p}^{1-q}+\eta_{k,n,p}\right\}.

(5.104)

Proposition 5.4

(Score and risk control for HR-QDA)
.

Under Assumption
5.8
, for every deterministic sequence
\bm{z}_{n}
with

\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
,

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{Q}(\bm{z}_{n})=O_{P}\!\left(\rho^{\mathrm{QDA}}_{n,p}\right).

(5.105)

If the Bayes quadratic margin is separated, then

R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}})-R(G_{Q})=O_{P}\!\left(\rho^{\mathrm{QDA}}_{n,p}\right).

(5.106)

These two plug-in rules are useful complements to SSLDA and SSQDA. The direct methods estimate
the discriminant objects themselves and are often sharper under sparsity. The plug-in rules keep
closer contact with classical discriminant analysis and make it easier to import the robust matrix
estimators developed elsewhere in the book.

5.9
Bibliographic notes

The fixed-
p
Gaussian material is classical; see
3
. The
extension from Gaussian to elliptical populations was developed in the monograph
of
36
and in the fixed-dimensional asymptotic analysis of

130
. Robust fixed-dimensional discriminant analysis using
high-breakdown estimators was developed by
26
and further
studied by
27
. The generalized QDA viewpoint
under elliptical symmetry was developed by
12
; a
robustified version was given by
55
.

The benchmark high-dimensional Gaussian literature includes the independence
rule of
9
, FAIR of
31
, thresholded sparse
LDA of
119
, direct sparse LDA of

16
;
98
, penalized Fisher and optimal-scoring
methods of
142
;
25
, copula-based
discriminant analysis of
61
, and sparse QDA procedures of

78
;
69
;
19
.

The robust high-dimensional theory developed in this chapter is built on the
spatial-sign program of the book. For the spatial median and SSCM in high
dimensions, see Chapter 2 and the related papers

79
;
157
;
91
. The
present chapter’s robust classification thread is based on the recent SSLDA and
SSQDA papers
(
162
;
120
)
. As in the previous
chapters, our presentation has adopted the same writing principle: classical
fixed-
p
methods first, then high-dimensional Gaussian benchmarks, and finally
elliptical robust methods with explicit optimization formulas, assumptions,
proofs, and rates.

Appendix to Chapter 5: Detailed Proofs

This appendix collects detailed proofs of the theorem-level results of
Chapter 5. In the spirit established after Chapter 2, the arguments are written
in the unified notation of the book and avoid referring the reader back to the
original papers. When we invoke a result from an earlier chapter, we explicitly
state the chapter-and-theorem number and rewrite the resulting bound in the
present notation.

A. Proofs of the fixed-
p
classical results

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.1
.

The Bayes classifier compares
\pi_{1}f_{1}(\bm{z})
and
\pi_{2}f_{2}(\bm{z})
. Under
(
5.14
),

\log\{\pi_{1}f_{1}(\bm{z})\}-\log\{\pi_{2}f_{2}(\bm{z})\}=\log\pi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}|+\log g_{1}\{d_{1}(\bm{z})\}-\log\pi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\log|\bm{\Lambda}_{2}|-\log g_{2}\{d_{2}(\bm{z})\}.

This is exactly the score in (
5.15
). Hence the Bayes
classifier is obtained by thresholding that score at zero.

If
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
and
\bm{\Lambda}_{1}=\bm{\Lambda}_{2}=\bm{\Lambda}
, then the two log-determinant
terms cancel and, because
g
is decreasing,

q_{E}(\bm{z})\geq 0\iff d_{1}(\bm{z})-d_{2}(\bm{z})\leq 2\log(\pi_{1}/\pi_{2})\times 0,

that is,

(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})-(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\leq-2\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

Expanding the quadratic forms gives

-2\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2})+\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\mu}_{2}\leq-2\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.

Rearranging yields

(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}+\log\frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}\geq 0,

which is (
5.16
). Part (ii) is simply the statement of
the threshold rule for the general heteroscedastic common-generator model.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.2
.

Under class 1,

q_{F}(\bm{Z})=(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}=(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}.

Since
\bm{Z}\mid L=1\sim N_{p}(\bm{\mu}_{1},\bm{\Sigma})
,

(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}\sim N\bigl(0,\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}\bigr)=N(0,\Delta_{F}^{2}).

Therefore

\mathbb{P}_{1}\{G_{F}(\bm{Z})=2\}=\mathbb{P}_{1}\{q_{F}(\bm{Z})<0\}=\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right).

Under class 2,

q_{F}(\bm{Z})=(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta},

so the same calculation yields

\mathbb{P}_{2}\{G_{F}(\bm{Z})=1\}=\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right).

Because
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=1/2
,

R(G_{F})=\frac{1}{2}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right)=\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{F}}{2}\right).

∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.3
.

Let
N=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Because
p
is fixed and the model is Gaussian,

\sqrt{n_{1}}(\bar{\bm{X}}-\bm{\mu}_{1})\overset{d}{\to}N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}),\qquad\sqrt{n_{2}}(\bar{\bm{Y}}-\bm{\mu}_{2})\overset{d}{\to}N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}),

and

\sqrt{N}(\bm{S}_{p}-\bm{\Sigma})=O_{P}(1)

in every matrix norm. By the standard matrix inverse expansion,

\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}=-\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{S}_{p}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}+O_{P}(N^{-1}).

(5.107)

Write

\hat{q}_{F}(\bm{z})-q_{F}(\bm{z})=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

where

T_{1}=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}-\bm{\Omega})\bm{\delta},

T_{2}=-(\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta},\qquad\hat{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\bar{\bm{X}}+\bar{\bm{Y}}}{2},

T_{3}=(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{p}^{-1}(\hat{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta}),\qquad\hat{\bm{\delta}}=\bar{\bm{X}}-\bar{\bm{Y}}.

Using (
5.107
) and the joint asymptotic normality of the
sample means and pooled covariance,
\sqrt{N}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
converges jointly to a
centered Gaussian vector. Therefore
\sqrt{N}\{\hat{q}_{F}(\bm{z})-q_{F}(\bm{z})\}
is
asymptotically normal. Collecting the first-order mean-estimation terms gives
exactly (
5.23
); the pooled-covariance contribution is of
the same order and is absorbed into
\tau_{F}^{2}(\bm{z})
. This yields
(
5.22
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Let
N=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Since
p
is fixed,

\sqrt{n_{k}}(\bar{\bm{Z}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k})=O_{P}(1),\qquad\sqrt{n_{k}}(\bm{S}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k})=O_{P}(1),\qquad k=1,2.

The maps

\bm{A}\mapsto\bm{A}^{-1},\qquad\bm{A}\mapsto\log|\bm{A}|,\qquad(\bm{a},\bm{A})\mapsto(\bm{z}-\bm{a})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{a})

are continuously differentiable on the set of positive definite matrices.
Applying the multivariate delta method to the vector

(\bar{\bm{X}},\bar{\bm{Y}},\bm{S}_{1},\bm{S}_{2})

therefore yields

\sqrt{N}\{\hat{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})-q_{Q}(\bm{z})\}\overset{d}{\to}N\{0,\tau_{Q}^{2}(\bm{z})\},

for some finite variance
\tau_{Q}^{2}(\bm{z})
. Expanding the Fréchet derivatives of

\log|\bm{A}|
and
\bm{A}^{-1}
gives the explicit formula, which is omitted here
because it is not used elsewhere in the book. The important point is that all
terms enter linearly at first order and therefore the plug-in QDA score is
asymptotically normal in fixed dimension.
∎

B. Proofs of the building-block lemmas

Detailed proof of Lemma
5.1
.

Apply the ordinary spatial-median Bahadur expansion established in Chapter 2 separately to the two
classes. For class
k
one has

\sqrt{n_{k}}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k})=\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\bm{U}_{ki}+\bm{r}_{k,n},

with the same Jacobian form as in Chapter 2. The proof there is based on the
Taylor expansion of the classwise estimating equation

\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}U(\bm{Z}_{ki}-\bm{\theta})=\bm{0}

about
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\mu}_{k}
. The empirical Jacobian converges uniformly on a shrinking
neighborhood of
\bm{\mu}_{k}
, and the second-order remainder is bounded by the same
arguments as in the Chapter 2 proof of the ordinary spatial median. Rewriting
that proof in the present notation yields the max-norm remainder bound
(
5.62
). Dividing by
\sqrt{n_{k}}
gives
(
5.63
).
∎

Detailed proof of Lemma
5.2
.

We first prove (
5.64
). Decompose

p\tilde{\bm{S}}-\bm{\Lambda}=p(\tilde{\bm{S}}-\bm{S}_{0})+(p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda}).

By Assumption
5.3
(
5.54
),

\left\lVert p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq Cp^{-1/2}.

For the empirical term, the proof of the SSCM concentration result used in the
SSPCA program yields

\left\lVert p(\tilde{\bm{S}}-\bm{S}_{0})\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
. Combining the two bounds gives
(
5.64
).

Next consider the covariance surrogate. For each class
k
,

\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}=(\hat{\tau}_{k}-\tau_{k})\tilde{\bm{S}}_{k}+\tau_{k}(\tilde{\bm{S}}_{k}-\bm{S}_{0k})+\tau_{k}\bm{S}_{0k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k},

where
\tau_{k}=\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})
. By the trace-estimation argument of the SSQDA
paper and the proof reproduced below in Theorem
5.7
,

|\hat{\tau}_{k}-\tau_{k}|\leq CK_{n,p}

with probability at least
1-C/\log p
. By the first part of the lemma,

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{S}}_{k}-\bm{S}_{0k}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq CK_{n,p}/p.

Finally, the deterministic approximation
\tau_{k}\bm{S}_{0k}=\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+O(p^{-1/2})

in max norm follows from the same SSCM-to-shape approximation. Therefore

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq CK_{n,p}

with probability at least
1-C/\log p
, proving (
5.65
).
∎

C. Proofs for SSLDA

Detailed proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Write
\tilde{\bm{\delta}}=\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
. By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}-\tilde{\bm{\delta}}\right\rVert_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(p\tilde{\bm{S}}-p\bm{S}_{0})\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert(p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda})\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.108)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}.

(5.109)

Since
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
,

\left\lVert(p\tilde{\bm{S}}-p\bm{S}_{0})\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}-p\bm{S}_{0}\right\rVert_{\max}\,\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1},

\left\lVert(p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda})\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\left\lVert p\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{\Lambda}\right\rVert_{\max}\,\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

By Assumption
5.3
, the first coefficient is bounded by

C\sqrt{(\log p)/n}
and the second by
Cp^{-1/2}
. The classwise median errors
are bounded by
C\sqrt{(\log p)/n}
by Lemma
5.1
.
Hence

\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}-\tilde{\bm{\delta}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\left(p^{-1/2}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+C\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}.

Under Assumption
5.2
,
\Delta_{p}\geq c_{0}
, so the right
side is bounded by
\lambda_{n}
once (
5.72
) holds and

C
is chosen sufficiently large. This proves
(
5.73
). Since
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}
minimizes the
\ell_{1}
norm
over the feasible set and
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}
is feasible, one also has

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}
.
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.5
.

Set
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\bm{\delta}
. By the definition of
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}
and
Lemma
5.3
,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.110)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle p(\bm{\gamma}^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\bm{S}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-(\bm{\gamma}^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\bm{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1},

(5.111)

by feasibility of
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}
, while feasibility of
\bm{\gamma}^{\star}
gives

\displaystyle p(\bm{\gamma}^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\bm{S}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\max}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}

(5.112)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.113)

Subtracting these two inequalities and using (
5.110
) yields

\bigl|(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\delta}\bigr|\leq 4\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.114)

Now examine the numerator of the first argument in (
5.71
):

\displaystyle\left|(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right|

(5.115)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{\max}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}+2\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}

(5.116)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+2\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.117)

The same bound holds for the second argument with
\bm{\mu}_{2}
.

For the denominator, note that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\bm{\Sigma}-p\tilde{\bm{S}})\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\tilde{\bm{\delta}}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.118)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}-p\tilde{\bm{S}}\right\rVert_{\max}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}+2\lambda_{n}

(5.119)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+2\lambda_{n},

(5.120)

where we used (
5.64
), the relation

\bm{\Sigma}=\omega\bm{\Lambda}
, and (
5.110
). Hence

\displaystyle\bigl|\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\delta}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{1}\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.121)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+2\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.122)

Combining (
5.114
) and (
5.121
) gives

\left|\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right|\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+6\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}.

(5.123)

Since
\bm{\delta}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\gamma}^{\star}=\Delta_{p}
, we conclude that

\left|\frac{(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{1/2}}{\Delta_{p}^{1/2}}-1\right|\leq C\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}}{\Delta_{p}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}}{\Delta_{p}^{1/2}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}.

(5.124)

The consistency condition (
5.75
) implies that the
right-hand side is
o(1)
. Therefore the two arguments of
\Psi
in
(
5.71
) equal
\mp\Delta_{p}^{1/2}/2+o(1)
uniformly on an event of
probability tending to one. Since
\Psi
is continuous,

\hat{R}_{n}-R^{\star}\to 0

in probability. This proves (
5.76
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Under the extra condition
n^{-1}p\log p\to 0
, the SSCM approximation sharpens
to

\left\lVert p\tilde{\bm{S}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

with probability at least
1-Cp^{-1}
. Repeating the proof of
Theorem
5.5
with this sharper bound yields

\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\star})\right\rVert_{\max}\leq C\lambda_{n},

(5.125)

and therefore

\left|\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\Delta_{p}\right|\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\lambda_{n}.

(5.126)

Using (
5.114
) and the numerator expansion from the previous
proof, we obtain

\left|(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{p}\right|+\left|(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}_{2})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{p}\right|\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\Delta_{p}^{1/2}\lambda_{n}.

(5.127)

Let

r_{n}=C\left(\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\Delta_{p}^{1/2}+\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}.

Then (
5.126
)–(
5.127
) imply that the two arguments
of
\Psi
in (
5.71
) equal

-\frac{\Delta_{p}^{1/2}}{2}+O(r_{n})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\Delta_{p}^{1/2}}{2}+O(r_{n}),

respectively. A first-order Taylor expansion of
\Psi
around

\pm\Delta_{p}^{1/2}/2
gives

\hat{R}_{n}=R^{\star}\{1+O(r_{n})\},

which is exactly (
5.78
). If

\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{0}\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{2}
and

\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq C\Delta_{p}
by the eigenvalue bound on
\bm{\Lambda}
,
then

\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}\Delta_{p}^{1/2}+\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\leq C\left\lVert\bm{\gamma}^{\star}\right\rVert_{0}\Delta_{p},

which yields (
5.79
).
∎

D. Proofs for SSQDA

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.7
.

Set

\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}=\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1})-\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}),\qquad\bm{\sigma}=\operatorname{vec}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1})-\operatorname{vec}(\bm{\Sigma}_{2}),

and define the linear operators

\bm{V}=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\otimes\bm{\Sigma}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\otimes\bm{\Sigma}_{1},\qquad\tilde{\bm{V}}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\otimes\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}.

Since
\bm{V}\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})=\bm{\sigma}
, the true parameter
\bm{D}
is feasible with high
probability once

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{V}}\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})-\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\lambda_{1,n}.

Using Lemma
5.2
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{V}}\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})-\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\right\rVert_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\tilde{\bm{V}}-\bm{V})\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\bm{\sigma}-\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.128)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{V}}-\bm{V}\right\rVert_{\max}\,\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\bm{D})\right\rVert_{1}+2\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}+2\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}-\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.129)

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{s_{1}}K_{n,p},

(5.130)

with probability at least
1-C/\log p
. Hence
\bm{D}
is feasible for
(
5.80
). Because
\tilde{\bm{D}}
minimizes the
\ell_{1}
norm over
that feasible set,

\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D})\right\rVert_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{s_{1}}\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}.

(5.131)

Now use restricted strong convexity. By Assumption
5.5

and the eigenvalue bounds on
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}
,

\lambda_{\min}(\bm{V})\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1})\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{2})\lambda_{p}(\bm{\Sigma}_{1})\geq M_{1}^{-2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}^{2}\bigl|\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{V}\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D})\bigr|

(5.132)

\displaystyle=M_{1}^{2}\bigl|\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{V}\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}})-\bm{\sigma})\bigr|.

(5.133)

Insert and subtract the empirical operator:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}^{2}\,\left\lVert\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D})\right\rVert_{1}\Bigl[\left\lVert(\bm{V}-\tilde{\bm{V}})\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}})\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{V}}\operatorname{vec}(\tilde{\bm{D}})-\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}-\bm{\sigma}\right\rVert_{\max}\Bigr].

(5.134)

The middle term is bounded by
\lambda_{1,n}
by feasibility of
\tilde{\bm{D}}
.
The first and third terms are both
O(K_{n,p})
by
Lemma
5.2
. Using
(
5.131
) therefore gives

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq C\sqrt{s_{1}}\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,K_{n,p},

which implies (
5.86
).

The proof for
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}
is analogous but simpler. Since

\bm{\Sigma}_{2}\bm{\beta}=\bm{\mu}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{1}
, feasibility of
\bm{\beta}
follows from

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\bm{\beta}-(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})\right\rVert_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}-\bm{\Sigma}_{2})\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.135)

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{s_{2}}K_{n,p},

(5.136)

with probability at least
1-C/\log p
. Hence

\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\beta}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}
and the error vector belongs to the
same standard cone. By the eigenvalue bound on
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}\,|(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta})|

(5.137)

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}\,\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}\Bigl[\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}-\bm{\Sigma}_{2})\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{\max}+\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{\max}\Bigr]

(5.138)

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{s_{2}}\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}\right\rVert_{2}K_{n,p}.

(5.139)

This proves (
5.87
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.8
.

Let
Q(\bm{z})
denote the oracle QDA score and
\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{z})
the feasible SSQDA
score. Write

M(\bm{z})=Q(\bm{z})-\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{z}).

Then, with equal priors,

\displaystyle R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}})-R(G_{Q})

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\tilde{q}_{Q}>0,\,Q\leq 0\}}(\pi_{1}f_{1}-\pi_{2}f_{2})(\bm{z})\,d\bm{z}

(5.140)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{f_{1}}\left[\Bigl(1-e^{Q_{E}(\bm{Z})/2}\Bigr)1\{\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{Z})>0,\,0\leq Q(\bm{Z})\leq M(\bm{Z})\}\right],

(5.141)

where
Q_{E}(\bm{z})
is the exact elliptical log-density score. Therefore it suffices
to control
M(\bm{Z})
and the mass of the event on which
Q(\bm{Z})
lies within a
small neighborhood of zero.

Decompose
M(\bm{z})
into the quadratic, linear, and log-determinant pieces:

\displaystyle M(\bm{z})

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{D}-\tilde{\bm{D}})(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})

(5.142)

\displaystyle\quad-2(\bm{\beta}-\tilde{\bm{\beta}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{z}-\bar{\bm{\mu}})

(5.143)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl\{\log|\bm{D}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}+\bm{I}_{p}|-\log|\tilde{\bm{D}}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}+\bm{I}_{p}|\bigr\}

(5.144)

\displaystyle\quad+\text{terms involving }(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}).

(5.145)

By Lemma
5.1
and
Theorem
5.7
, each location term is bounded by

C(s_{1}+s_{2})K_{n,p}
in expectation. For the log-determinant term, use the matrix
identity

\log|\bm{A}|-\log|\bm{B}|=\log|\bm{I}+\bm{B}^{-1}(\bm{A}-\bm{B})|

and the bound
|\log|\bm{I}+\bm{H}||\leq C\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{F}
whenever
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
is
small. Assumption
5.5
and
Theorem
5.7
then yield

\mathbb{E}\bigl|\log|\bm{D}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}+\bm{I}_{p}|-\log|\tilde{\bm{D}}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}+\bm{I}_{p}|\bigr|\leq Cs_{1}K_{n,p}.

For the quadratic term, write
\bm{Z}=\bm{\mu}_{1}+r\bm{\Gamma}_{1}\bm{u}
under class 1. Then

(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{D}-\tilde{\bm{D}})(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})=r^{2}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Gamma}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{D}-\tilde{\bm{D}})\bm{\Gamma}_{1}\bm{u}.

Using Assumption
5.6
, the spectral bound on

\bm{\Gamma}_{1}
, and (
5.86
),

\mathbb{E}\bigl|(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{D}-\tilde{\bm{D}})(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})\bigr|\leq Cs_{1}K_{n,p}.

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\bigl|(\bm{\beta}-\tilde{\bm{\beta}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{Z}-\bar{\bm{\mu}})\bigr|\leq Cs_{2}K_{n,p}.

Combining all pieces gives

\mathbb{E}|M(\bm{Z})|\leq C(s_{1}+s_{2})K_{n,p}.

(5.146)

Now split according to whether
|Q(\bm{Z})|\leq\delta_{n}
or not, with

\delta_{n}=(s_{1}+s_{2})\log n\,K_{n,p}
. By the margin condition
(
5.60
),

\mathbb{P}\{|Q(\bm{Z})|\leq\delta_{n}\}\leq C\delta_{n}.

On the complement, the indicator event

\{\tilde{q}_{Q}(\bm{Z})>0,\,Q(\bm{Z})\leq 0\}
implies
|M(\bm{Z})|\geq\delta_{n}
, so by
Markov’s inequality and (
5.146
),

\mathbb{P}\{|M(\bm{Z})|\geq\delta_{n}\}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}|M(\bm{Z})|}{\delta_{n}}\leq\frac{C}{\log p},

for sufficiently large
n
under (
5.88
). Combining the
two parts yields

\mathbb{E}\{R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}})-R(G_{Q})\}\leq C\left\{\frac{1}{\log p}+(s_{1}+s_{2})\log n\,K_{n,p}\right\},

which is (
5.89
).
∎

Detailed proof of Theorem
5.9
.

When both classes are Gaussian, the trace estimator in
(
5.48
) admits exponential concentration. Consequently,
all occurrences of the polynomial tail term
1/\log p
in the proof of
Theorem
5.8
can be replaced by terms of order
K_{n,p}^{2}
.
Moreover, the quadratic-form fluctuation term can be bounded in second moment by

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{D}-\tilde{\bm{D}})(\bm{Z}-\bm{\mu}_{1})\bigr]^{2}\leq C\left\lVert\tilde{\bm{D}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

so that, using Theorem
5.7
,

\mathbb{E}|M(\bm{Z})|\leq C(s_{1}+s_{2})K_{n,p}.

Running the same margin argument with the sharpened Gaussian concentration gives

\mathbb{E}\{R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{SSQDA}})-R(G_{Q})\}\leq C(s_{1}+s_{2})^{2}\log^{2}n\,K_{n,p}^{2},

which is exactly (
5.90
).
∎

E. Proofs for additional plug-in classifiers

Detailed proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Write

\bm{\mu}=\frac{\bm{\mu}_{1}+\bm{\mu}_{2}}{2},\qquad\hat{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}+\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}}{2},\qquad\bm{\delta}=\bm{\mu}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{2},\qquad\hat{\bm{\delta}}=\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}.

Then

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{L}(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}\hat{\bm{\delta}}-(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}

(5.147)

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega})\hat{\bm{\delta}}+(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}(\hat{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta})+(\bm{\mu}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}.

(5.148)

By assumption,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{\mu}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1),

and similarly
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1)
. Since
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
and

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
, we have
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
. Moreover,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}(1)
because

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O(1)+o_{P}(1)=O_{P}(1).

Therefore, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega})\hat{\bm{\delta}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1),

(5.149)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}(\hat{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1),

(5.150)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{\mu}-\hat{\bm{\mu}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1).

(5.151)

Substituting these bounds into (
5.148
) yields
(
5.35
).

For the risk statement, let

\mathcal{D}=\{\bm{z}:q_{L}(\bm{z})=0\}

be the Bayes decision boundary. If the margin is separated in the sense that there exists

c_{0}>0
such that
|q_{L}(\bm{z})|\geq c_{0}
off an arbitrarily small neighborhood of
\mathcal{D}
,
then (
5.35
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\{\operatorname{sign}(\hat{g}_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}(\bm{Z}))\neq\operatorname{sign}(q_{L}(\bm{Z}))\}\to 0.

Since the excess risk is bounded by the probability of disagreeing with the Bayes rule, the
misclassification risk of
G_{\mathrm{plugLDA}}
converges to the Bayes risk.
∎

Detailed proof of Proposition
5.2
.

For
k=1,2
, define

Q_{k}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{k}),\qquad\hat{Q}_{k}(\bm{z})=(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{z}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}).

Then

\hat{q}_{\mathrm{plugQDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{Q}(\bm{z}_{n})=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}(-1)^{k}\Bigl[\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\Bigr]-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}(-1)^{k}\bigl[\hat{Q}_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})\bigr].

(5.152)

We first control the inverse matrices. Since
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{P}(1)
and

\bm{\Sigma}_{k}
is positive definite with eigenvalues bounded away from zero,

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}=\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k})\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1},

(5.153)

which implies

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{P}(1),\qquad\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}(1).

Next, by the identity

\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})=\log\det\Bigl(\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1/2}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1/2}\Bigr),

and the continuity of
\log\det(\bm{I}_{p}+\bm{H})
at
\bm{H}=0
, we obtain

\left\lvert\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\right\rvert=o_{P}(1).

(5.154)

For the quadratic forms, write

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1})(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})

(5.155)

\displaystyle\quad+(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})+(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\mu}_{k}).

(5.156)

Because
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
and
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1)
, we have

\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
. Hence

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1})(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{P}(1),

(5.157)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}=o_{P}(1),

(5.158)

and the same bound holds for the last term in (
5.156
). Therefore,

\hat{Q}_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})=o_{P}(1)
for
k=1,2
. Together with
(
5.154
), equation (
5.152
) yields
(
5.42
).

The risk convergence follows from the same argument as in the LDA case: if the Bayes quadratic
margin is separated away from zero outside an arbitrarily small neighborhood of the Bayes
boundary, then the plug-in QDA and oracle QDA disagree with probability tending to zero, and
hence the plug-in QDA risk converges to the Bayes risk.
∎

Detailed proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Let

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}=\frac{\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}+\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}}{2},\qquad\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}=\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1,\mathrm{rob}}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2,\mathrm{rob}}.

Then

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{L}(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\Omega})\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}

(5.159)

\displaystyle\quad+(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}(\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\delta})+(\bm{\mu}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}.

(5.160)

Assumption
5.7
implies

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(r_{n,p}),\qquad\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(r_{n,p}),

and therefore
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
. Also,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}(1)
because

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O(1)+O_{P}\!\left(s_{0}(p)r_{n,p}^{1-q}\right)=O_{P}(1).

Since
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
, we have
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
. Hence

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\Omega})\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0}(p)r_{n,p}^{1-q}\right),

(5.161)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}(\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\delta})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(r_{n,p}),

(5.162)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{\mu}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}\bm{\delta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{rob}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(r_{n,p}).

(5.163)

Substituting these bounds into (
5.160
) gives
(
5.96
). If the Bayes margin is separated, the disagreement probability
between
\hat{G}_{\mathrm{robLDA}}
and
G_{L}
is of the same order as the score perturbation, which
implies (
5.97
).
∎

Detailed proof of Proposition
5.4
.

For
k=1,2
, define

\hat{Q}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}_{n})=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}),\qquad Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})=(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\mu}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{k}(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\mu}_{k}).

Then

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{z}_{n})-q_{Q}(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}(-1)^{k}\Bigl[\log\det(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})-\log\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k})\Bigr]

(5.164)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}(-1)^{k}\Bigl[\hat{Q}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})\Bigr].

(5.165)

For each
k
,

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Omega}_{k})(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})

(5.166)

\displaystyle\quad+(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{k}(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})+(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{k}(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\mu}_{k}).

(5.167)

Under Assumption
5.8
,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Omega}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0,k}(p)\eta_{k,n,p}^{1-q}\right),\qquad\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(\eta_{k,n,p}),

and
\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(1)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Omega}_{k})(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\Omega}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0,k}(p)\eta_{k,n,p}^{1-q}\right),

(5.168)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Omega}_{k}(\bm{\mu}_{k}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{\Omega}_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{P}(\eta_{k,n,p}),

(5.169)

and the same bound holds for the last term in (
5.167
). Thus,

\hat{Q}_{k,\mathrm{HR}}(\bm{z}_{n})-Q_{k}(\bm{z}_{n})=O_{P}\!\left(s_{0,k}(p)\eta_{k,n,p}^{1-q}+\eta_{k,n,p}\right).

Together with the log-determinant control in
(
5.103
) and the condition
p\eta_{k,n,p}=o(1)
, equation
(
5.165
) gives (
5.105
) with rate

\rho^{\mathrm{QDA}}_{n,p}
.

If the Bayes quadratic margin is separated, then the event on which

\hat{G}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}}(\bm{Z})\neq G_{Q}(\bm{Z})
is contained in the event that the score perturbation
crosses the margin. Consequently,

R(\hat{G}_{\mathrm{HRQDA}})-R(G_{Q})=O_{P}\!\left(\rho^{\mathrm{QDA}}_{n,p}\right),

which is exactly (
5.106
).
∎

Chapter 6
Principal Component Analysis, Factor Models, and Canonical Correlation under Elliptical Symmetry

6.1
Introduction

Principal component analysis (PCA), factor analysis, and canonical correlation
analysis (CCA) are three of the most classical devices for extracting latent
structure from multivariate data. In fixed dimension they are usually taught as
separate topics. PCA diagonalizes a covariance matrix and produces orthogonal
directions of maximal variance; factor analysis decomposes the covariance matrix
into a low-rank common part and a structured idiosyncratic part; CCA studies the
leading singular vectors of a normalized cross-covariance matrix and identifies
maximally correlated linear combinations of two random vectors. In modern
high-dimensional problems these three topics are tightly connected. All of them
require the estimation of an eigen- or singular-subspace, and all of them become
unstable when the sample covariance matrix is singular, ill-conditioned, or
unduly affected by heavy tails.

The goal of this chapter is to develop a unified treatment of these topics under
elliptical symmetry. The chapter follows exactly the writing principle adopted
in the later version of Chapter 2.

(i)

We first review the classical low-dimensional theory. We write down the
estimators and test statistics explicitly, record the fixed-
p
asymptotic
distributions that form the classical benchmark, and explain the basic
perturbation formulas behind the theory.

(ii)

We then review the main high-dimensional Gaussian or light-tailed
benchmark methods, including the spiked covariance model, sparse PCA,
approximate factor models, POET, and sparse CCA.

(iii)

Finally, we turn to the central theme of the book: robust dimension
reduction under elliptical symmetry. The core objects are the spatial-sign
covariance matrix, generalized weighted sign covariance matrices, robust
principal subspaces, elliptical factor models, and spatial-sign based sparse
CCA.

The guiding message of the chapter is simple but important. Under elliptical
symmetry, radial magnitudes are often the least reliable aspect of the data,
whereas directional information remains highly informative. Consequently, the
right analogues of covariance-based eigenanalysis are often based on shape,
direction, and self-normalized transformations. This idea already appeared in
fixed-dimensional robust PCA; in your recent work it becomes fully high-
dimensional and connects naturally with the previous chapters on location,
matrix estimation, and classification. The chapter therefore serves as the
natural conclusion of the monograph: once robust location, robust scatter, and
robust inverse matrices are available, one can also build robust latent-structure
learning procedures under the same geometric framework. See

125
;
60
;
158
;
140
;
144
;
112
.

As in Chapter 2 and Chapter 3, all assumptions needed by theorem-level
statements are written inside the chapter. Bibliographic notes are followed by
an appendix containing detailed proofs. In the proofs we avoid vague
“
o_{p}(1)
-type” summaries whenever explicit orders can be displayed.

6.2
Low-dimensional PCA, factor analysis, and CCA

6.2.1
Population and sample PCA

Let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be centered with covariance matrix

\bm{\Sigma}=\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0,

(6.1)

where
\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{p}
form an orthonormal basis. The
j
th principal
component score is
\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{X}
, and the
r
-dimensional principal
subspace is
\operatorname{span}(\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r})
. The population directions may be
characterized variationally by the Rayleigh–Ritz principle:

\bm{v}_{1}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1}\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{v},\qquad\bm{v}_{j}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\ \bm{v}\perp\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{j-1}}\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{v}.

(6.2)

The corresponding optimal values are the ordered eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}
.

Given centered observations
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, the sample covariance matrix
is

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\bm{X}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.3)

Its eigen-decomposition is written as

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{p}\geq 0.

(6.4)

The ordinary PCA estimator of the leading direction is
\hat{\bm{v}}_{1}
, and the
sample
r
-dimensional principal subspace is

\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{r}=\operatorname{span}(\hat{\bm{v}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{v}}_{r}).

(6.5)

In fixed dimension, consistency of sample PCA follows from consistency of

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
and continuity of eigenvalues and eigenvectors under spectral
separation. The next theorem records the explicit perturbation expansions that
underlie both the classical fixed-
p
theory and much of the high-dimensional
analysis later in the chapter.

Assumption 6.1
.

Let
q\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
be fixed. Assume that the covariance matrix in
(
6.1
) satisfies

\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{q}>\lambda_{q+1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0.

(6.6)

For each
1\leq j\leq q
, define the spectral gap

g_{j}=\min_{k\neq j}|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}|.

(6.7)

Theorem 6.1

(Deterministic perturbation expansion for eigenpairs)
.

Assume Assumption
6.1
. Let

\bm{E}=\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}
. If

\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{g_{j}}{8}

(6.8)

for some
j\leq q
, then the
j
th sample eigenvalue and eigenvector admit the
expansions

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}

\displaystyle=\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}+R_{\lambda,j},

(6.9)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}-\bm{v}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}\,\bm{v}_{k}+\bm{r}_{j},

(6.10)

where the remainders satisfy

|R_{\lambda,j}|\leq\frac{8\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{g_{j}},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{r}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{32\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{g_{j}^{2}}.

(6.11)

Theorem
6.1
is the basic bridge between covariance
estimation and eigenspace estimation. In particular, if

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
in fixed dimension, then the
leading linear terms in (
6.9
) and
(
6.10
) determine the limiting distributions.

Theorem 6.2

(Fixed-
p
Gaussian asymptotics for PCA)
.

Assume Assumption
6.1
and let

\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma})
. Then for every

1\leq j\leq q
,

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,2\lambda_{j}^{2}),

(6.12)

and

\sqrt{n}\bigl(\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}-\bm{v}_{j}\bigr)\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N_{p}\!\left(\bm{0},\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}\bm{v}_{k}\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\right).

(6.13)

The covariance matrix in (
6.13
) is singular in the

\bm{v}_{j}
direction, which reflects the unit-norm constraint on the eigenvector.

The limiting laws in Theorem
6.2
are classical;
see
3
;
101
. They also remain valid under much
weaker moment assumptions with modified covariance formulas. Theorem

6.1
shows why the structure of the result is so
stable: the eigenvalue fluctuation comes from a quadratic form,

\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}
, whereas the eigenvector fluctuation is a
weighted sum of off-diagonal quadratic forms,

\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}
for
k\neq j
.

6.2.2
Low-dimensional factor analysis

The classical
K
-factor model is

\bm{X}=\bm{B}\bm{f}+\bm{u},\qquad\mathbb{E}(\bm{f})=\bm{0},\qquad\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{f})=\bm{I}_{K},\qquad\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{u})=\bm{\Psi},

(6.14)

where
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times K}
is the loading matrix and

\bm{\Psi}
is typically diagonal or approximately diagonal. The covariance
matrix then decomposes as

\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{B}\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}+\bm{\Psi}.

(6.15)

When the eigenvalues associated with the common factors are separated from the
noise spectrum, the loading space is identified by the leading eigenspace of

\bm{\Sigma}
. This is the conceptual link between PCA and factor analysis.

In low dimension, maximum-likelihood factor analysis under Gaussianity yields
likelihood-ratio tests for the number of factors and asymptotically normal
estimators of loadings; see
3
. For the purposes of this
monograph, the following deterministic eigenspace statement is the most useful
fixed-
p
fact.

Proposition 6.1

(Classical factor subspace recovery by eigenanalysis)
.

Let
\bm{\Sigma}
be given by (
6.15
), and let

\mathcal{V}_{K}=\operatorname{span}(\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{K})
be the leading
K
-dimensional
eigenspace of
\bm{\Sigma}
. Assume that

\delta_{K}:=\lambda_{K}(\bm{\Sigma})-\lambda_{K+1}(\bm{\Sigma})>0.

(6.16)

Let
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
be any symmetric estimator and

\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{K}
its leading
K
-dimensional eigenspace. If

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}<\delta_{K}/2
, then

\left\|\sin\Theta(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{K},\mathcal{V}_{K})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{\delta_{K}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(6.17)

Proposition
6.1
already contains the main
mathematical principle behind principal-components-based factor estimation. In
fixed dimension it is almost trivial because

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
; in high dimension the real work is
to construct a matrix estimator that still concentrates fast enough.

6.2.3
Low-dimensional CCA as a related spectral problem

Let
(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
be a pair of centered random vectors of dimensions
p_{x}
and

p_{y}
with covariance blocks

\operatorname{Cov}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{X}\\
\bm{Y}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{xx}&\bm{\Sigma}_{xy}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{yx}&\bm{\Sigma}_{yy}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bm{\Sigma}_{yx}=\bm{\Sigma}_{xy}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.18)

Classical CCA, introduced by
67
, solves

\max_{\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{x}},\,\bm{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{y}}}\frac{\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{xy}\bm{b}}{\sqrt{\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{xx}\bm{a}}\,\sqrt{\bm{b}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Sigma}_{yy}\bm{b}}}.

(6.19)

Writing

\bm{K}=\bm{\Sigma}_{xx}^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{xy}\bm{\Sigma}_{yy}^{-1/2},

(6.20)

the canonical correlations are the singular values of
\bm{K}
, and the canonical
loading vectors are obtained from the corresponding singular vectors after
unwhitening:

\bm{a}_{j}=\bm{\Sigma}_{xx}^{-1/2}\bm{u}_{j},\qquad\bm{b}_{j}=\bm{\Sigma}_{yy}^{-1/2}\bm{v}_{j},\qquad\bm{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{\min(p_{x},p_{y})}\rho_{j}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.21)

Under Gaussianity and fixed dimensions, the likelihood-ratio test for

H_{0}:\rho_{1}=\cdots=\rho_{r}=0
is based on Wilks’ Lambda,

\Lambda_{\mathrm{Wilks}}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-\hat{\rho}_{j}^{2}),

(6.22)

and the Bartlett approximation gives

-\left(n-1-\frac{p_{x}+p_{y}+1}{2}\right)\log\Lambda_{\mathrm{Wilks}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\chi^{2}_{p_{x}p_{y}}

(6.23)

under the null; see
3
. We record these formulas here because
later, in the high-dimensional and elliptical parts of the chapter, the same
spectral problem reappears but with covariance surrogates and sparsity
constraints replacing the classical sample covariance matrices.

6.3
High-dimensional Gaussian and light-tailed benchmarks

6.3.1
The spiked covariance model and the failure of ordinary PCA

A canonical high-dimensional benchmark is the spiked covariance model

\bm{\Sigma}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{p}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}>1.

(6.24)

When
p/n\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
, ordinary PCA exhibits the familiar phase
transition phenomenon. In the rank-one case, if
\lambda_{1}>1+\sqrt{\gamma}
,
then the largest sample eigenvalue separates from the bulk and the leading sample
eigenvector has nontrivial overlap with the population spike; more precisely,

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\lambda_{1}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda_{1}-1}\right),

(6.25)

\displaystyle|\hat{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{v}_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{1-\gamma/(\lambda_{1}-1)^{2}}{1+\gamma/(\lambda_{1}-1)}.

(6.26)

If
\lambda_{1}\leq 1+\sqrt{\gamma}
, then the overlap tends to zero. These
formulas, established in varying forms by

72
;
109
;
102
, provide the classical benchmark
for high-dimensional PCA. They show that ordinary PCA may fail even under
perfect Gaussian assumptions unless the signal eigenvalues exceed a nontrivial
threshold.

6.3.2
Sparse PCA benchmarks

A common remedy is to assume that the leading eigenvectors are sparse. The
basic optimization problem is

\hat{\bm{v}}_{\ell_{0}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\,\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s}\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{v}.

(6.27)

The estimator in (
6.27
) is combinatorial but it
clarifies the statistical target. A second route relaxes the problem to a
semidefinite or Fantope program,

\hat{\bm{H}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\bm{H}\succeq 0}\bigl\langle\hat{\bm{\Sigma}},\bm{H}\bigr\rangle-\tau\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{1}\quad\text{subject to}\quad 0\preceq\bm{H}\preceq\bm{I},\ \operatorname{tr}(\bm{H})=r,

(6.28)

from which the sparse principal subspace is extracted. An algorithmically
simple alternative is the penalized matrix decomposition of

141
.

For one leading component, the minimax benchmark under sparsity is of order

\inf_{\tilde{\bm{v}}}\sup_{\bm{v}_{1}:\,\|\bm{v}_{1}\|_{0}\leq s}\mathbb{E}\Bigl\{\sin^{2}\angle(\tilde{\bm{v}},\bm{v}_{1})\Bigr\}\asymp\frac{s\log(ep/s)}{n\,\theta^{2}},

(6.29)

where
\theta
denotes the spike strength or eigengap parameter. See

2
;
129
;
18
. This rate will serve as the
benchmark for the robust sparse PCA procedures developed later in the chapter.

6.3.3
Approximate factor models and POET

The high-dimensional approximate factor model is

\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{B}\bm{f}_{t}+\bm{u}_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,n,

(6.30)

with
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times K}
, latent factor vector
\bm{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, and
idiosyncratic component
\bm{u}_{t}
. The covariance decomposes as

\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{B}\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{f}_{t})\bm{B}^{\mathsf{T}}+\bm{\Sigma}_{u}.

(6.31)

When the factors are pervasive, the leading eigenvalues of the common component
are of order
p
, and the leading sample principal components estimate the
factor loading space; see
6
. To exploit the additional sparsity of

\bm{\Sigma}_{u}
,
34
proposed the POET estimator,

\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathrm{POET}}=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}+\mathcal{T}_{\tau}(\hat{\bm{R}}_{u}),

(6.32)

where
\hat{\bm{R}}_{u}
is the residual covariance matrix after removing the first
K

principal components and
\mathcal{T}_{\tau}(\cdot)
denotes thresholding.
Under conditional sparsity of
\bm{\Sigma}_{u}
, POET attains sharp rates under both
operator and max norms. In the present monograph, Chapter 3 already treated the
robust covariance-estimation side of elliptical factor models; the present
chapter focuses on the corresponding eigenspace and latent-structure viewpoint.

Factor-number selection also has a large literature. The information criteria of

5
and the eigenratio criterion of
1

are the two most widely used classical benchmarks. Later in the chapter we will
formulate a sign-based eigenratio rule under elliptical symmetry.

6.3.4
High-dimensional sparse CCA benchmarks

When
p_{x}
and
p_{y}
are large, the classical CCA problem based on
(
6.19
) is no longer feasible. Sparse CCA methods impose
sparsity on the canonical vectors. A representative formulation is the
penalized matrix decomposition of
141
,

(\hat{\bm{u}},\hat{\bm{v}})=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\|\bm{u}\|_{2}\leq 1,\,\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\leq 1}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{xy}\bm{v}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{x},\ \left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{y}.

(6.33)

More refined procedures allow general within-view covariance structures and
attain minimax-optimal rates; see

53
;
54
. A representative minimax rate
for one canonical pair is of order

\frac{s_{x}\log(ep_{x}/s_{x})+s_{y}\log(ep_{y}/s_{y})}{n\,\rho_{1}^{2}},

(6.34)

where
s_{x},s_{y}
are the sparsity levels and
\rho_{1}
is the leading canonical
correlation. The robust SSCCA procedure studied later in the chapter is the
elliptical analogue of these Gaussian benchmarks.

6.4
Population sign geometry under elliptical symmetry

The robust methods in this chapter are built on a simple but fundamental
population fact: under elliptical symmetry, directional covariance matrices share
eigenvectors with the underlying shape matrix.

6.4.1
The spatial-sign covariance matrix

Let
\bm{X}
follow the elliptical model

\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+\xi\bm{A}\bm{u},\qquad\bm{u}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1}),\qquad\xi\geq 0,

(6.35)

where
\xi
and
\bm{u}
are independent. Let

\bm{\Theta}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\bm{\Lambda}=\frac{p\bm{\Theta}}{\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Theta})}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}>0,

(6.36)

be the normalized shape matrix. Define the population spatial-sign covariance
matrix by

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr\}.

(6.37)

The next theorem is one of the main geometric facts behind robust PCA under
ellipticity.

Theorem 6.3

(Eigenspace preservation for the SSCM)
.

Let
\bm{\Lambda}
be given by (
6.36
). Then

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\eta_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\eta_{j}=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right),

(6.38)

where
\bm{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p})^{\mathsf{T}}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
. Consequently,

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
have the same eigenvectors. Moreover, if

\lambda_{j}>\lambda_{k}
, then
\eta_{j}>\eta_{k}
.

The eigenvalue formula in (
6.38
) shows that spatial
signs discard radial magnitude but preserve the ordering of principal
directions. This is why SSCM-based PCA is meaningful under elliptical
symmetry. A second robust route is based on the multivariate Kendall matrix,
which is the starting point of elliptical component analysis (ECA) in

60
. The difference between the two approaches is structural:
ECA uses a pairwise U-statistic and is therefore naturally translation
invariant, whereas SSCM-based PCA uses a first-order sign covariance and is
computationally cheaper.

6.4.2
The multivariate Kendall’s tau matrix and ECA

Let
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
be an independent copy of
\bm{X}
. The population multivariate
Kendall’s tau matrix is

\bm{K}_{\tau}=\mathbb{E}\left\{U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})U(\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\}.

(6.39)

Under elliptical symmetry,
\bm{X}-\widetilde{\bm{X}}
has the same eigenvectors as the population
shape matrix. Therefore, exactly as in Chapter 1,
