\bm{K}_{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\kappa_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\kappa_{j}=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right),

(6.40)

where
\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
. Hence
\bm{K}_{\tau}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
share the same
principal subspace.

Given observations
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
, define the sample Kendall matrix

\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{X}_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.41)

and let
\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{ECA}}_{r}
be the span of its first
r
eigenvectors.
Following
60
, one may combine (
6.41
) with a sparse
constraint or a truncated-power iteration to obtain sparse ECA estimators.

Theorem 6.4

(A basic ECA subspace bound)
.

Assume that the leading
r
-dimensional Kendall eigenspace is separated by

\delta^{\tau}_{r}=\kappa_{r}-\kappa_{r+1}>0.

(6.42)

If

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}-\bm{K}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),

(6.43)

then

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{ECA}}_{r},\mathcal{V}_{r}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{P}\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\delta^{\tau}_{r}}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right).

(6.44)

Theorem
6.4
is the book-level summary of ECA. In words,
Kendallization replaces the sample covariance matrix by a pairwise directional
matrix that keeps the principal eigenspace of the elliptical shape matrix while
remaining valid under heavy tails and even under the broader transelliptical
family. The computational cost is the main trade-off: evaluating
(
6.41
) requires a second-order U-statistic, so the raw
cost is
O(n^{2}p^{2})
rather than the
O(np^{2})
cost of SSCM-based PCA.

6.4.3
Generalized spatial-sign covariance matrices and radial weights

The plain spatial sign uses the radial weight
r\mapsto 1/r
. To interpolate
between ordinary covariance and hard sign normalization,
117

proposed a generalized spatial-sign covariance matrix (GSSCM). Write

w_{\xi}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})=(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\,\xi(\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}),

(6.45)

where
\xi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a radial weight. Equivalently, if one defines

K(r)=r\xi(r),\qquad U_{K}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}):=K(\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2})U(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})=(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\,\xi(\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}),

(6.46)

then the generalized spatial-sign covariance matrix is simply the second moment of the
weighted signs,

\bm{M}_{K}=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{U_{K}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})U_{K}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr\}.

(6.47)

This notation matches the weighted sign statistics already used in Chapter 2.

The main practical weight families are the following.

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{Win}}(r)

\displaystyle=\min\left(1,\frac{Q_{2}}{r}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{Quad}}(r)

\displaystyle=\min\left(1,\frac{Q_{2}^{2}}{r^{2}}\right),

(6.48)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{Ball}}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\{r\leq Q_{2}\},

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{Shell}}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\{Q_{1}<r\leq Q_{3}\},

(6.49)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{LR}}(r)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&r\leq Q_{2},\\[3.00003pt]
\dfrac{Q_{3}^{\ast}-r}{Q_{3}^{\ast}-Q_{2}},&Q_{2}<r\leq Q_{3}^{\ast},\\[8.00003pt]
0,&r>Q_{3}^{\ast},\end{cases}

(6.50)

where
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{3}^{\ast}
are robust cutoff radii. Following

117
and
140
, these cutoffs are
obtained from the Wilson–Hilferty transformed radii
D_{i}=R_{i}^{2/3}
via

Q_{1}=\{\mathrm{hmed}(D_{i})-\mathrm{hmad}(D_{i})\}^{3/2},\quad Q_{2}=\{\mathrm{hmed}(D_{i})\}^{3/2},\quad Q_{3}=\{\mathrm{hmed}(D_{i})+\mathrm{hmad}(D_{i})\}^{3/2},\quad Q_{3}^{\ast}=\{\mathrm{hmed}(D_{i})+1.4826\,\mathrm{hmad}(D_{i})\}^{3/2}.

(6.51)

Winsor and Quadratic-Winsor keep central observations almost unchanged while shrinking the
outer tail. Ball retains only the inner bulk, Shell targets an annulus to reduce central
contamination, and the linearly redescending weight is a smooth compromise between hard
rejection and soft attenuation.

Theorem 6.5

(Generalized weighted sign matrices share the shape eigenvectors)
.

Let
K
be measurable with

\mathbb{E}\!\left\{K^{2}(\left\lVert\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2})\right\}<\infty.

(6.52)

Then

\bm{M}_{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\eta_{K,j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\eta_{K,j}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}K^{2}\!\left(\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\right],

(6.53)

where
\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
. Consequently,
\bm{M}_{K}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
have the
same eigenvectors. If
\lambda_{j}>\lambda_{k}
and
K
is induced by one of the monotone
weight families (
6.48
)–(
6.50
), then

\eta_{K,j}>\eta_{K,k}
, so the ordering of the principal directions is preserved.

Theorem
6.5
is the population foundation of generalized
spatial-sign PCA. It is also the precise statement behind
140
:
the weight function may change efficiency and robustness, but it does not change the target
eigenspace under elliptical symmetry.

6.5
Spatial-sign PCA in fixed and increasing dimension

6.5.1
Estimator and standing assumptions

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}
be independent copies of
\bm{X}
. The sample spatial
median is

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}\right\rVert_{2}.

(6.54)

The sample SSCM is

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.55)

Let

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\hat{\eta}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\hat{\eta}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\hat{\eta}_{p}\geq 0,

(6.56)

and define the SPCA estimator of the top
r
principal subspace by

\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{SPCA}}_{r}=\operatorname{span}(\hat{\bm{v}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{v}}_{r}).

(6.57)

The next assumption isolates the two ingredients needed for a clean
nonasymptotic analysis: concentration of the spatial median and radial separation
of the observations from the center.

Assumption 6.2
.

There exist positive constants
c_{\mu}
and
c_{0}
such that, for every
t>0
, the
event

\mathcal{E}_{t}=\left\{\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leq c_{\mu}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{n}},\qquad\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\geq c_{0}\sqrt{p}\right\}

(6.58)

satisfies

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{t})\geq 1-2e^{-t}.

(6.59)

In addition, the sample size is large enough that

c_{\mu}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{n}}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{4}\sqrt{p}.

(6.60)

Assumption
6.2
is deliberately written in the notation of
this book rather than imported from an article. The first inequality is exactly
the type of spatial-median bound established in Chapter 2. The second says that
all observations stay a macroscopic distance away from the center; in high
dimension this is natural for concentrated radial models. The condition can be
weakened by replacing the minimum radius by an averaged inverse-radius bound, but
(
6.58
) leads to a cleaner presentation.

6.5.2
A nonasymptotic operator-norm bound

The matrix

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}

contains two errors: the empirical fluctuation of sign outer products and the
error caused by estimating the location. The next theorem separates these two
sources explicitly.

Theorem 6.6

(Operator-norm bound for the sample SSCM)
.

Assume Assumption
6.2
. Then for every
t>0
, with
probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}.

(6.61)

The first two terms in (
6.61
) are the matrix-Bernstein
fluctuation of the bounded summands

U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
The last term is the price paid for estimating the location. Since the minimum
radius is of order
\sqrt{p}
, that last term reduces to order
n^{-1/2}
under
Assumption
6.2
. This is precisely the mechanism stressed
in the SPCA paper: location estimation matters, but in many high-dimensional
regimes it is of smaller order than the principal empirical fluctuation. See

158
.

As in ordinary PCA, a Davis–Kahan argument immediately converts the matrix error
bound into a principal-subspace bound.

Corollary 6.1

(Subspace error for nonsparse SPCA)
.

Let
1\leq r<p
and define the sign-eigenvalue gap

\delta_{r}^{\mathrm{S}}:=\eta_{r}-\eta_{r+1}>0.

(6.62)

Assume Assumption
6.2
. Then for every
t>0
, with
probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
,

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{SPCA}}_{r},\mathcal{V}_{r}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2r}}{\delta_{r}^{\mathrm{S}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}\right],

(6.63)

where
\mathcal{V}_{r}=\operatorname{span}(\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r})
is the leading
r
-dimensional
shape eigenspace.

6.5.3
Fixed-
p
asymptotic review for sign-based PCA

In fixed dimension the asymptotic behavior of SSCM eigenvectors has long been
studied. If
p
is fixed, the center is estimated at rate
n^{-1/2}
and the
matrix
\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}
has the linearization

\sqrt{n}\bigl(\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\right]+o_{p}(1).

(6.64)

Combining (
6.64
) with the perturbation expansion in
Theorem
6.1
yields the asymptotic normality of the
sign-based eigenvectors. See
125
for a robust PCA
perspective and the earlier low-dimensional SSCM literature cited there. We do
not formalize this fixed-
p
result as a theorem in the present chapter because
our main focus is the explicit nonasymptotic and high-dimensional theory above.

6.6
Sparse spatial-sign PCA

6.6.1
Support-constrained estimator

The sparse version of SPCA is obtained by constraining the support of the leading
vector. Let

\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\ \|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s}\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}.

(6.65)

This is the direct spatial-sign analogue of the oracle sparse PCA estimator based
on the sample covariance matrix. It matches the combinatorial program analyzed
in
158
.

Assumption 6.3
.

The leading shape eigenvector satisfies

\|\bm{v}_{1}\|_{0}\leq s,\qquad\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}:=\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}>0.

(6.66)

The central technical quantity is the restricted operator norm

\|\bm{A}\|_{(m)}:=\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\ \|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq m}|\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}\bm{v}|.

(6.67)

The next theorem is the sparse analogue of
Theorem
6.6
.

Theorem 6.7

(Restricted concentration of the sample SSCM)
.

Assume Assumption
6.2
. Then for every
t>0
, with
probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
,

\|\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\|_{(2s)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}.

(6.68)

The first two terms are obtained by applying the matrix Bernstein inequality on
each coordinate subset of size
2s
and then union-bounding over all such
subsets. The location-estimation term is the same as in the nonsparse bound
because support restriction does not change the center-estimation error.

Theorem 6.8

(Estimation rate for sparse SPCA)
.

Assume Assumptions
6.2
and

6.3
. Then for every
t>0
, with probability at least

1-4e^{-t}
,

\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}},\bm{v}_{1})\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{8\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}\right].

(6.69)

In particular, if

\frac{s\log(ep/s)}{n}\to 0,\qquad\frac{p}{n}=O(1),

(6.70)

and
\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}
is bounded away from zero, then

\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}},\bm{v}_{1})\to 0
in probability.

The rate in (
6.69
) has the same structure as the Gaussian
sparse PCA benchmark: the dominant statistical term is of order

\{s\log(ep/s)/n\}^{1/2}
, multiplied by the inverse eigengap. In that sense,
SSPCA preserves the sparse-PCA efficiency pattern while gaining robustness to
heavy tails.

6.6.2
A computationally efficient truncated-power iteration

The estimator (
6.65
) is not directly computable in large
problems. Following the general sparse-PCA literature, a practical alternative
is to use a truncated-power iteration. Given an initial unit vector

\bm{v}^{(0)}
, iterate

\displaystyle\bm{w}^{(m+1)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}^{(m)},

(6.71)

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{v}}^{(m+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{w}^{(m+1)}}{\left\lVert\bm{w}^{(m+1)}\right\rVert_{2}},

(6.72)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{(m+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{v}}^{(m+1)})}{\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{v}}^{(m+1)})\right\rVert_{2}},

(6.73)

where
\mathcal{T}_{s}(\cdot)
keeps the
s
largest coordinates in absolute value
and sets the others to zero. As in the Gaussian sparse-PCA literature, the key
practical issues are the choice of initialization and the choice of
s
. Two
natural initializations are:

(a)

the leading eigenvector of
\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}
itself;

(b)

a sparse warm start from a Fantope-type relaxation.

The theoretical analysis of the full nonconvex iteration follows the same logic
as in the covariance-based case: one needs a basin condition on the initial
vector and a deterministic contraction inequality for the truncated power map.
Since the purpose of the chapter is to state a self-contained robust sparse-PCA
methodology rather than to repeat every algorithmic detail from the sparse-PCA
literature, we use (
6.65
) as the principal theoretical
object and record (
6.71
)–(
6.73
) as the
computational implementation.

6.7
Generalized spatial-sign PCA

The family (
6.46
) leads to a corresponding class
of PCA procedures. Define the weighted sample matrix

\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.74)

Let
\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\hat{\eta}_{K,j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{K,j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{K,j}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
The resulting generalized SPCA estimator of the leading
r
-dimensional subspace
is

\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{(K)}_{r}=\operatorname{span}(\hat{\bm{v}}_{K,1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{v}}_{K,r}).

(6.75)

This includes ordinary SPCA as the special case
K\equiv 1
.

Assumption 6.4
.

The weight function
K
is Lipschitz continuous with constant
L_{K}
and bounded
by
K_{\max}
:

0\leq K(r)\leq K_{\max},\qquad|K(r)-K(s)|\leq L_{K}|r-s|,\qquad r,s\geq 0.

(6.76)

Theorem 6.9

(Nonasymptotic bound for generalized SPCA)
.

Assume Assumptions
6.2
and

6.4
. Then for every
t>0
, with probability at least

1-4e^{-t}
,

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}-\bm{M}_{K}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq K_{\max}^{2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}\right]+\frac{8K_{\max}L_{K}c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}.

(6.77)

Consequently, if

\delta_{K,r}:=\eta_{K,r}-\eta_{K,r+1}>0
, then

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{(K)}_{r},\mathcal{V}_{r}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2r}}{\delta_{K,r}}\left[K_{\max}^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}\right\}+\frac{8K_{\max}L_{K}c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}\right].

(6.78)

Theorem
6.9
is the book-level version of generalized
spatial-sign PCA. It makes completely explicit how the radial weight affects
the statistical error: the empirical fluctuation is scaled by
K_{\max}^{2}
, and
the location-estimation term is scaled by
K_{\max}L_{K}
. This decomposition is
useful in practice because it makes the robustness-efficiency trade-off visible
at the theorem level.

6.8
Elliptical factor models and robust principal subspace recovery

6.8.1
Model and principal subspace

We now return to factor models, but this time under elliptical symmetry. A
convenient book-level model is

\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{B}\bm{f}_{t}+\bm{u}_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,n,

(6.79)

where the joint vector
(\bm{f}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}},\bm{u}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}
is elliptically
symmetric, the loading matrix
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times K}
has rank
K
, and the
idiosyncratic covariance matrix of
\bm{u}_{t}
is sparse or approximately sparse.
The corresponding covariance or scatter matrix has the familiar low-rank plus
structured-noise form. The robust matrix-estimation consequences of this model
were already treated in Chapter 3. The emphasis here is the recovery of the
common principal subspace.

Let
\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}
be the population SSCM of
\bm{X}_{t}
. By
Theorem
6.3
, the leading eigenspace of

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}
coincides with the leading eigenspace of the population shape
matrix. Therefore the robust factor-loading space can be estimated from the top

K
eigenvectors of
\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.

Assumption 6.5
.

The elliptical factor model (
6.79
) satisfies the
following conditions.

(1)

The leading sign-eigenvalue gap is positive:

\delta_{K}^{\mathrm{S}}:=\eta_{K}-\eta_{K+1}>0.

(6.80)

(2)

Assumption
6.2
holds for the sample spatial median.

(3)

The number of factors
K
is fixed.

Theorem 6.10

(Robust factor-subspace recovery by SPCA)
.

Assume Assumption
6.5
. Let

\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{SPCA}}_{K}
be the leading
K
-dimensional eigenspace of

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}
. Then for every
t>0
, with probability at least

1-4e^{-t}
,

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{SPCA}}_{K},\mathcal{V}_{K}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{\delta_{K}^{\mathrm{S}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}\right].

(6.81)

In particular, if
\delta_{K}^{\mathrm{S}}
is bounded away from zero and

\log p=o(n)
, then the robust factor-loading space is consistently estimated.

Theorem
6.10
should be read together with the
matrix-estimation results of Chapter 3. The message is that the same SSCM or
Tyler-type robustification that stabilizes matrix estimation also yields a
stable principal subspace for latent-factor recovery.

6.8.2
Estimating the number of factors

Once the robust principal subspace is available, the next question is the number
of factors. Let
\hat{\eta}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\hat{\eta}_{p}
be the eigenvalues of

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}
, and let
K_{\max}
be a deterministic upper bound. A
robust eigenratio estimator is

\hat{K}_{\mathrm{ER}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{1\leq j\leq K_{\max}}\frac{\hat{\eta}_{j}}{\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}.

(6.82)

This is the sign-based analogue of the eigenratio rules used in ordinary factor
analysis.

Theorem 6.11

(Consistency of the robust eigenratio estimator)
.

Assume Assumption
6.5
. Suppose in addition that

\eta_{K}>\eta_{K+1}>0,\qquad\frac{\eta_{K}}{\eta_{K+1}}\geq 1+\gamma_{0}

(6.83)

for some
\gamma_{0}>0
, and that for some
t>0
,

\tau_{n,p}(t):=\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}\leq\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4(2+\gamma_{0})}\eta_{K+1}.

(6.84)

Then, with probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
,

\hat{K}_{\mathrm{ER}}=K.

(6.85)

Theorem
6.11
is intentionally transparent:
it reduces factor-number consistency to a perturbation inequality for ordered
eigenvalues. The condition (
6.84
) states that the
stochastic eigenvalue error must be smaller than the deterministic ratio gap at
the true factor boundary.

6.8.3
Kendall-based factor analysis without moment constraints

A complementary route replaces the first-order SSCM by the multivariate Kendall
matrix. This is the approach developed by
62

for factor analysis without moment constraints. Using
(
6.41
), let

\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\hat{\kappa}_{j}\,\hat{\bm{u}}^{(\tau)}_{j}(\hat{\bm{u}}^{(\tau)}_{j})^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\hat{\kappa}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\hat{\kappa}_{p}\geq 0,

(6.86)

and define the Kendall-based estimator of the loading space by

\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{K}=\operatorname{span}\bigl(\hat{\bm{u}}^{(\tau)}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{u}}^{(\tau)}_{K}\bigr).

(6.87)

Since
\bm{K}_{\tau}
and the population shape matrix share eigenvectors, this
estimator targets the same common-factor subspace as SPCA, but with pairwise
translation invariance built in from the outset.

Theorem 6.12

(Kendall-based factor-subspace recovery)
.

Assume that the elliptical factor model (
6.79
)
has a
K
-dimensional leading eigenspace and that the Kendall eigengap satisfies

\delta^{\tau}_{K}:=\kappa_{K}-\kappa_{K+1}>0.

(6.88)

If

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}-\bm{K}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),

(6.89)

then

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{K},\mathcal{V}_{K}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{P}\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\delta^{\tau}_{K}}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right).

(6.90)

Moreover, if for some
\gamma^{\tau}_{0}>0
,

\frac{\kappa_{K}}{\kappa_{K+1}}\geq 1+\gamma^{\tau}_{0},

(6.91)

and if

\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\leq\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}\kappa_{K+1},

(6.92)

then the Kendall eigenratio estimator

\hat{K}^{(\tau)}_{\mathrm{ER}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{1\leq j\leq K_{\max}}\frac{\hat{\kappa}_{j}}{\hat{\kappa}_{j+1}}

(6.93)

is consistent, that is,

\hat{K}^{(\tau)}_{\mathrm{ER}}=K\text{ with probability tending to one.}

(6.94)

Theorem
6.12
is the book-level summary of the
He–Kong–Yu–Zhang route: Kendallization produces a factor estimator that does
not require finite fourth moments and still achieves the standard eigengap-type
subspace rate. Relative to SSCM-based SPCA, the gain is automatic translation
invariance and broader robustness; the cost is the second-order U-statistic
computation already noted in the ECA discussion.

6.9
Sparse CCA under elliptical symmetry

6.9.1
A sign-based cross-covariance surrogate

We now return to CCA. Let

\bm{Z}_{i}=(\bm{X}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}},\bm{Y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{x}+p_{y}}
,

i=1,\ldots,n
, be paired observations from an elliptically symmetric
distribution centered at
(\bm{\mu}_{x}^{\mathsf{T}},\bm{\mu}_{y}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}
. Partition the
population sign-based matrix as

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}^{(Z)}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{M}_{xx}&\bm{M}_{xy}\\
\bm{M}_{yx}&\bm{M}_{yy}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.95)

The corresponding whitened cross-structure is

\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}=\bm{M}_{xx}^{-1/2}\bm{M}_{xy}\bm{M}_{yy}^{-1/2}.

(6.96)

Let its singular value decomposition be

\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\rho_{j}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\rho_{1}>\rho_{2}\geq 0.

(6.97)

The vectors
\bm{u}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{1}
are the robust canonical directions in the
sign-normalized geometry.

In practice we use a sample counterpart

\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}=\hat{\bm{M}}_{xx}^{-1/2}\hat{\bm{M}}_{xy}\hat{\bm{M}}_{yy}^{-1/2},

(6.98)

with blocks extracted from the joint sample SSCM. The spatial-sign sparse CCA
estimator is then defined by the constrained optimization problem

(\hat{\bm{u}},\hat{\bm{v}})\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1,\ \left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1,\ \left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{x},\ \left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{y}.

(6.99)

This is the robust analogue of the Gaussian sparse-CCA program
(
6.33
). It is also the natural book-level summary
of the SSCCA methodology in
112
.

Assumption 6.6
.

The sign-based CCA problem satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

The leading singular value is separated:

\Delta_{\mathrm{C}}:=\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}>0.

(6.100)

(2)

The true leading singular vectors are feasible:

\left\lVert\bm{u}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\bm{v}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}=1,\qquad\left\lVert\bm{u}_{1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{x},\qquad\left\lVert\bm{v}_{1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{y}.

(6.101)

(3)

For some deterministic quantity
\varepsilon_{n,p}
and every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\varepsilon_{n,p}\bigr\}\geq 1-e^{-t}.

(6.102)

The max-norm error bound (
6.102
) can be derived from the
blockwise sign-covariance concentration results in Chapter 3 together with
matrix perturbation for inverse square roots. For the theorem below it is
convenient to state the error directly at the level of

\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}
.

Theorem 6.13

(Error bound for spatial-sign sparse CCA)
.

Assume Assumption
6.6
. Let
(\hat{\bm{u}},\hat{\bm{v}})
be any global
maximizer of (
6.99
). On the event

\{\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\varepsilon_{n,p}\}
,

\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{C}}}{2}\left\{\sin^{2}\angle(\hat{\bm{u}},\bm{u}_{1})+\sin^{2}\angle(\hat{\bm{v}},\bm{v}_{1})\right\}\leq 2c_{x}c_{y}\varepsilon_{n,p}.

(6.103)

Consequently,

\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{u}},\bm{u}_{1})+\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{v}},\bm{v}_{1})\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{2c_{x}c_{y}\varepsilon_{n,p}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{C}}}}.

(6.104)

Furthermore, if the signs of the inner products are chosen so that

\hat{\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}_{1}\geq 0
and
\hat{\bm{v}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{v}_{1}\geq 0
, then

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{u}}-\bm{u}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\left(\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{u}},\bm{u}_{1})+\sin\angle(\hat{\bm{v}},\bm{v}_{1})\right).

(6.105)

Theorem
6.13
is a clean illustration of the max-norm
philosophy that has appeared repeatedly throughout the book. Because the
objective is bilinear, the perturbation of the objective function is naturally
controlled by the product of the
\ell_{1}
radii and the matrix max norm. The
spectral gap
\Delta_{\mathrm{C}}
then turns the objective bound into an
angle bound. This is the direct sign-based counterpart of the Gaussian sparse
CCA theory.

6.10
Bibliographic notes

The low-dimensional PCA, factor-analysis, and CCA theory may be found in

3
;
101
. The high-dimensional PCA benchmark based on
the spiked covariance model was shaped by the works of

72
;
109
;
102
. Sparse PCA was developed through a
large literature; for the optimization and minimax benchmarks used in this
chapter, see
2
;
129
;
18
;
141
. Approximate factor models and POET are classical
benchmarks in high-dimensional econometrics and statistics; see

6
;
5
;
34
. For Gaussian sparse CCA and its
minimax theory, see
141
;
53
;
54
.

On the robust side, sign-based PCA in fixed dimension goes back to the robust
multivariate literature summarized by
104
and further developed for
PCA by
125
. The generalized sign-covariance
perspective is developed in
117
. In high
dimensions,
60
established the ECA route based on the
multivariate Kendall matrix, while
158
and

140
developed the spatial-sign and generalized
spatial-sign PCA procedures emphasized in this chapter. Elliptical factor-model
estimation appears in
35
; factor analysis via the multivariate
Kendall matrix under weak moment conditions is developed by

62
; and matrix estimation under
elliptical factor models is extended along the present research line by

144
. The sign-based
precision tools used in the CCA discussion connect to
91
.
Finally, the spatial-sign sparse CCA direction is represented by

112
.

Appendix to Chapter 6: Detailed Proofs

This appendix contains formula-level proofs for the theorem statements in the
chapter. The deterministic perturbation arguments are developed explicitly so
that later chapters can reuse them without re-derivation.

A. Proof of Theorem
6.1

Write
\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}=\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{E}
and fix
j\leq q
. Let

\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}=\alpha_{j}\bm{v}_{j}+\bm{w}_{j},\qquad\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{w}_{j}=0,\qquad\alpha_{j}=\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{v}_{j}\geq 0.

(6.106)

Since
\left\lVert\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}=1
, one has

\alpha_{j}^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=1.

(6.107)

The eigenequation for
\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}
is

(\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{E})\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}.

(6.108)

Premultiplying by
\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
yields

\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}+\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}\alpha_{j}.

(6.109)

Hence

\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}=\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}+\alpha_{j}^{-1}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\bm{w}_{j}.

(6.110)

Therefore, to prove (
6.9
) it suffices to control

\bm{w}_{j}
and
\alpha_{j}
.

By Weyl’s inequality,

|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|\leq\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq g_{j}/8.

(6.111)

Now project (
6.108
) onto the orthogonal complement of

\bm{v}_{j}
. Let
\bm{P}_{j}=\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
and
\bm{Q}_{j}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{j}
. Since

\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{v}_{j}=\bm{0}
and
\bm{Q}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}=\bm{w}_{j}
,

\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)\bm{w}_{j}=-\alpha_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}-\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{E}\bm{w}_{j}.

(6.112)

On the subspace
\operatorname{Range}(\bm{Q}_{j})
, the operator

\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}
is diagonal in the basis

\{\bm{v}_{k}:k\neq j\}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}
. Using
(
6.111
),

|\lambda_{k}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}|\geq|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}|-|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|\geq g_{j}-g_{j}/8=7g_{j}/8.

(6.113)

Thus the inverse exists on
\operatorname{Range}(\bm{Q}_{j})
and

\left\|\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{8}{7g_{j}}.

(6.114)

Applying (
6.114
) to
(
6.112
) gives

\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{8}{7g_{j}}\bigl(\alpha_{j}\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\bigr).

(6.115)

Since
\alpha_{j}\leq 1
and
\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq g_{j}/8
,

\left(1-\frac{1}{7}\right)\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{7g_{j}}8\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

(6.116)

which yields

\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{4\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{3g_{j}}<\frac{1}{2}.

(6.117)

By (
6.107
),

\alpha_{j}=\sqrt{1-\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\geq\sqrt{3}/2>1/2.

(6.118)

Returning to (
6.110
), we obtain

|R_{\lambda,j}|\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{8\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{g_{j}},

(6.119)

which proves (
6.9
) and the first half of
(
6.11
).

We next derive the linear expansion of
\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}
. From
(
6.112
),

\bm{w}_{j}=-\alpha_{j}\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}-\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{E}\bm{w}_{j}.

(6.120)

Define the first-order term

\bm{w}_{j}^{(1)}:=-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{E}\bm{v}_{j}}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}\,\bm{v}_{k}.

(6.121)

Subtracting (
6.121
) from
(
6.120
), and using the identity

\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}-\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\lambda_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}=(\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j})\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\lambda_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1},

(6.122)

we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}-\bm{w}_{j}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq|1-\alpha_{j}|\left\|\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\quad+|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|\left\|\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\lambda_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\bigl(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}\bm{Q}_{j}\bigr)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}.

(6.123)

Using

|1-\alpha_{j}|\leq\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
,
(
6.114
),

\|(\bm{Q}_{j}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{Q}_{j}-\lambda_{j}\bm{Q}_{j})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq g_{j}^{-1}
,
(
6.111
), and (
6.117
), each term on the
right-hand side of (
6.123
) is bounded by a
constant multiple of
\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}/g_{j}^{2}
. Collecting the numerical constants
gives

\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}-\bm{w}_{j}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{32\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{g_{j}^{2}}.

(6.124)

Finally,

\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}-\bm{v}_{j}=(\alpha_{j}-1)\bm{v}_{j}+\bm{w}_{j}
,
and
|\alpha_{j}-1|\leq\left\lVert\bm{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}/g_{j}^{2}
, so the same
order holds for the full vector remainder. This proves
(
6.10
) and the second bound in
(
6.11
). ∎

B. Proof of Theorem
6.2

Fix
j\leq q
. Under the Gaussian model,

\bm{X}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}Z_{ik}\bm{v}_{k},\qquad Z_{ik}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}N(0,1).

(6.125)

Hence

\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}(Z_{ij}^{2}-1).

(6.126)

Because
\operatorname{Var}(Z_{ij}^{2}-1)=2
, the classical central limit theorem yields

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,2\lambda_{j}^{2}).

(6.127)

The remainder term in (
6.9
) is of order

O_{p}(n^{-1})
by Theorem
6.1
, since

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
in fixed dimension. Therefore
(
6.12
) follows.

For the eigenvector, observe that for
k\neq j
,

\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{j}}\,Z_{ik}Z_{ij}.

(6.128)

Since
Z_{ik}Z_{ij}
has mean
0
and variance
1
,

\sqrt{n}\,\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}).

(6.129)

Moreover, for different
k\neq\ell
with both distinct from
j
, the Gaussian
moments imply

\operatorname{Cov}\{Z_{ik}Z_{ij},Z_{i\ell}Z_{ij}\}=0.

(6.130)

Hence the vector of off-diagonal quadratic forms converges jointly to a centered
Gaussian vector with diagonal covariance matrix. Applying the linear expansion
(
6.10
) gives

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}-\bm{v}_{j})=-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\sqrt{n}\,\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma})\bm{v}_{j}}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}\bm{v}_{k}+o_{p}(1).

(6.131)

The covariance matrix of the Gaussian limit in
(
6.131
) is therefore

\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\bm{v}_{k}\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}},

(6.132)

which is exactly (
6.13
). ∎

C. Proof of Proposition
6.1

The result is a direct application of the Davis–Kahan sin
\Theta
theorem.
Let
\bm{P}=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
and

\hat{\bm{P}}=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
denote the projection matrices
onto
\mathcal{V}_{K}
and
\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{K}
. Because the spectral gap at the

K
th eigenvalue is
\delta_{K}
, the Davis–Kahan theorem yields

\|\hat{\bm{P}}-\bm{P}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\delta_{K}}\sqrt{K}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}-\bm{\Sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(6.133)

Since

\|\sin\Theta(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{K},\mathcal{V}_{K})\|_{\mathrm{F}}=2^{-1/2}\|\hat{\bm{P}}-\bm{P}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
,
we obtain (
6.17
). ∎

D. Proof of Theorem
6.3

Let
\bm{\Lambda}=\bm{V}\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})\bm{V}^{\mathsf{T}}
and write

\bm{Z}\sim N_{p}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p})
. Since

\bm{u}=\bm{Z}/\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{2}
is uniformly distributed on the unit sphere and
independent of
\xi
, the direction of
\bm{X}-\bm{\mu}
may be represented as

U(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=\frac{\bm{V}\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}}.

(6.134)

Therefore

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}=\bm{V}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right]\bm{V}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.135)

For
j\neq k
, the
(j,k)
entry of the expectation in
(
6.135
) is

\sqrt{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{Z_{j}Z_{k}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right)=0

(6.136)

because the integrand is odd in
Z_{j}
(or in
Z_{k}
). Hence the expectation is
diagonal and

\eta_{j}=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right),

(6.137)

which proves (
6.38
).

It remains to prove the ordering. Fix
j\neq k
and let

R_{jk}=\sum_{\ell\notin\{j,k\}}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}
. Using the
exchangeability of
(Z_{j}^{2},Z_{k}^{2})
, we may write

\displaystyle\eta_{j}-\eta_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}-\lambda_{k}Z_{k}^{2}}{R_{jk}+\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}+\lambda_{k}Z_{k}^{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{j}Z_{k}^{2}-\lambda_{k}Z_{j}^{2}}{R_{jk}+\lambda_{j}Z_{k}^{2}+\lambda_{k}Z_{j}^{2}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{R_{jk}(Z_{j}^{2}+Z_{k}^{2})+2(\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{k})Z_{j}^{2}Z_{k}^{2}}{(R_{jk}+\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}+\lambda_{k}Z_{k}^{2})(R_{jk}+\lambda_{j}Z_{k}^{2}+\lambda_{k}Z_{j}^{2})}\right].

(6.138)

The random fraction in (
6.138
) is nonnegative and positive
with positive probability. Hence

\eta_{j}-\eta_{k}
has the same sign as
\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}
. In particular,
if
\lambda_{j}>\lambda_{k}
, then
\eta_{j}>\eta_{k}
. ∎

E. Proof of Theorem
6.5

Using the representation (
6.134
),

U_{K}(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})=K\!\left(\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\frac{\bm{V}\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}}.

(6.139)

Thus

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{K}

\displaystyle=\bm{V}\,\mathbb{E}\Biggl[K^{2}\!\left(\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\frac{\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\mathsf{T}}\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{p}})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}Z_{\ell}^{2}}\Biggr]\bm{V}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.140)

Again, the off-diagonal entries vanish by oddness, because the additional factor

K^{2}(\cdot)
depends only on the squared coordinates through the radius. The
diagonal entries are exactly those displayed in
(
6.53
). Hence
\bm{M}_{K}
and
\bm{\Lambda}
share the
same eigenvectors. ∎

F. Proof of Theorem
6.6

Write

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}=\bigl\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\mu})\bigr\}+\bigl\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bigr\},

(6.141)

where

\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\theta}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.142)

We bound the two terms separately.

Step 1: empirical fluctuation with known location.

Let

\bm{Y}_{i}=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}.

(6.143)

Then
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i})=\bm{O}
and

\left\lVert\bm{Y}_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2
. Moreover,

\bm{Y}_{i}^{2}=\bigl(U_{i}U_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bigr)^{2},\qquad U_{i}:=U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}),

(6.144)

and since
U_{i}U_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
is an idempotent rank-one matrix with operator norm
1
,
we have

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}^{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq n.

(6.145)

Applying matrix Bernstein inequality in the form of
126
gives,
for every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}\right]\leq 2e^{-t}.

(6.146)

Step 2: error caused by location estimation.

For nonzero vectors
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
,

\|U(\bm{x})U(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}-U(\bm{y})U(\bm{y})^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2\|U(\bm{x})-U(\bm{y})\|_{2}.

(6.147)

Indeed,

\displaystyle U(\bm{x})U(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}-U(\bm{y})U(\bm{y})^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\{U(\bm{x})-U(\bm{y})\}U(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}+U(\bm{y})\{U(\bm{x})-U(\bm{y})\}^{\mathsf{T}},

and both unit vectors have Euclidean norm
1
. Next, on the event

\mathcal{E}_{t}
and under (
6.60
),

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\qquad\text{for all }i,

(6.148)

so the map
\bm{x}\mapsto U(\bm{x})
is Lipschitz along the segment joining

\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}
with derivative bounded by

2/\|\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\|_{2}
. Therefore,

\left\lVert U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{4\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}}\leq\frac{4}{c_{0}\sqrt{p}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}.

(6.149)

Combining (
6.147
) and
(
6.149
) yields

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{8}{c_{0}\sqrt{p}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}.

(6.150)

The last inequality uses Assumption
6.2
.

Step 3: combine the two bounds.

Intersecting the event in (
6.146
) with

\mathcal{E}_{t}
and using (
6.141
), we obtain
(
6.61
). The probability bound is at least
1-4e^{-t}
.
∎

G. Proof of Corollary
6.1

The result follows from Theorem
6.6
and the sin
\Theta

version of the Davis–Kahan theorem. Indeed, letting

\hat{\bm{P}}_{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
and

\bm{P}_{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we have

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{SPCA}}_{r},\mathcal{V}_{r}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|\hat{\bm{P}}_{r}-\bm{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2r}}{\delta_{r}^{\mathrm{S}}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

(6.151)

which, combined with (
6.61
), gives
(
6.63
). ∎

H. Proof of Theorem
6.7

Fix a subset
S\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
with
|S|\leq 2s
. Let

\bm{A}_{SS}
denote the principal submatrix indexed by
S
. By applying
Theorem
6.6
to the
|S|
-dimensional subvectors

\bm{X}_{i,S}
, we obtain that with probability at least
1-2e^{-(t+\log N_{S})}
,

\left\lVert\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\}_{SS}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2|S|)+t+\log N_{S}\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2|S|)+t+\log N_{S}\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}},

(6.152)

where
N_{S}
is the number of candidate subsets. Since

|S|\leq 2s
and

\binom{p}{2s}\leq\exp\{2s\log(ep/2s)\}
, a union bound over all
S
yields

\sup_{|S|\leq 2s}\left\lVert\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\}_{SS}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{2s\log(ep/2s)+t\}}{3n}+\frac{8c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}

(6.153)

with probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
. By the definition of the restricted norm
(
6.67
), the left-hand side of
(
6.153
) is exactly

\|\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\|_{(2s)}
. This proves
(
6.68
). ∎

I. Proof of Theorem
6.8

Because
\bm{v}_{1}
is feasible for the optimization problem
(
6.65
), we have

\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}\geq\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}_{1}.

(6.154)

Subtracting

\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}

from both sides gives

\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}_{1}-\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}\leq\bigl|\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}})\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}\bigr|+\bigl|\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}})\bm{v}_{1}\bigr|.

(6.155)

Both vectors on the right-hand side are at most
s
-sparse, so their support
union has size at most
2s
. Hence

\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}_{1}-\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}\leq 2\|\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\|_{(2s)}.

(6.156)

Now decompose

\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}=\cos\theta\,\bm{v}_{1}+\sin\theta\,\bm{u},\qquad\bm{u}\perp\bm{v}_{1},\qquad\theta=\angle(\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}},\bm{v}_{1}).

(6.157)

Since

\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}=\eta_{1}\bm{v}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}+\sum_{j\geq 2}\eta_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{\mathrm{SS}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{1}\cos^{2}\theta+\eta_{2}\sin^{2}\theta\

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}-(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\sin^{2}\theta=\eta_{1}-\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}\sin^{2}\theta.

(6.158)

Combining (
6.156
) and
(
6.158
), and using

\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}_{1}=\eta_{1}
, yields

\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}\sin^{2}\theta\leq 2\|\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\|_{(2s)}.

(6.159)

Using the simple inequality

\sin\theta\leq\sqrt{2a/\delta}
whenever
\delta\sin^{2}\theta\leq 2a
,
we obtain

\sin\theta\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{S}}}\|\hat{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{M}_{\mathrm{S}}\|_{(2s)}.

(6.160)

Finally, substitute the restricted concentration bound from
Theorem
6.7
. This proves
(
6.69
). ∎

J. Proof of Theorem
6.9

Write

\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}-\bm{M}_{K}=\bigl\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\bm{\mu})\bigr\}+\bigl\{\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{M}_{K}\bigr\},

(6.161)

where

\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\bm{\theta}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(6.162)

For the empirical fluctuation term, note that

\|U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})U_{K}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq K_{\max}^{2}
.
Hence the same matrix-Bernstein argument as in the proof of
Theorem
6.6
gives

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{M}_{K}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq K_{\max}^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{8\{\log(2p)+t\}}{n}}+\frac{4\{\log(2p)+t\}}{3n}\right\}

(6.163)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
.

For the location term, observe that for any nonzero vectors
\bm{x},\bm{y}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert U_{K}(\bm{x})-U_{K}(\bm{y})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq|K(\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2})-K(\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2})|\,\left\lVert U(\bm{x})\right\rVert_{2}+K_{\max}\left\lVert U(\bm{x})-U(\bm{y})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{K}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}+K_{\max}\left\lVert U(\bm{x})-U(\bm{y})\right\rVert_{2}.

(6.164)

Using (
6.148
) and the same derivative bound as in
(
6.149
),

\left\lVert U(\bm{X}_{i}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-U(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{4}{c_{0}\sqrt{p}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}.

(6.165)

Combining (
6.164
) and
(
6.165
), and then using the outer-product
bound as in (
6.147
), yields

\left\lVert\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}})-\hat{\bm{M}}_{K}(\bm{\mu})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{8K_{\max}L_{K}}{c_{0}\sqrt{p}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathrm{SM}}-\bm{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{8K_{\max}L_{K}c_{\mu}}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{np}}.

(6.166)

Intersecting this event with (
6.163
) proves
(
6.77
). The subspace bound
(
6.78
) is again an immediate Davis–Kahan
consequence. ∎

K. Proof of Theorem
6.10

This is Corollary
6.1
with
r=K
. The only point
worth emphasizing is that the target space is the common principal subspace of
the elliptical factor model, which coincides with the leading shape eigenspace by
Theorem
6.3
. Therefore
(
6.81
) follows directly from
(
6.63
). ∎

L. Proof of Theorem
6.11

By Weyl’s inequality and Theorem
6.6
, on an event of
probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
,

|\hat{\eta}_{j}-\eta_{j}|\leq\tau_{n,p}(t)\qquad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq p.

(6.167)

Hence

\frac{\hat{\eta}_{K}}{\hat{\eta}_{K+1}}\geq\frac{\eta_{K}-\tau_{n,p}(t)}{\eta_{K+1}+\tau_{n,p}(t)}.

(6.168)

Using (
6.83
) and (
6.84
),

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{K}-\tau_{n,p}(t)}{\eta_{K+1}+\tau_{n,p}(t)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1+\gamma_{0})\eta_{K+1}-\tau_{n,p}(t)}{\eta_{K+1}+\tau_{n,p}(t)}>1+\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}.

(6.169)

For
j\neq K
, because there is no larger ratio gap than the one at
K
in the
population sequence, one has

\frac{\hat{\eta}_{j}}{\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}\leq\frac{\eta_{j}+\tau_{n,p}(t)}{\eta_{j+1}-\tau_{n,p}(t)}<1+\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}.

(6.170)

The inequality (
6.170
) follows from the same algebra
as (
6.169
). Therefore the maximizer of the empirical
ratio in (
6.82
) is exactly
K
. ∎

M. Proof of Theorem
6.13

Let

\mathcal{F}:=\{(\bm{u},\bm{v}):\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1,\ \left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1,\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{x},\ \left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{y}\}.

(6.171)

By assumption,
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{v}_{1})\in\mathcal{F}
, and by optimality of

(\hat{\bm{u}},\hat{\bm{v}})
for (
6.99
),

\hat{\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}\geq\bm{u}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}\bm{v}_{1}.

(6.172)

On the event

\{\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq\varepsilon_{n,p}\}
,
for any
(\bm{u},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{F}
,

\bigl|\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}(\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}})\bm{v}\bigr|\leq\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert_{1}\,\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\max}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{1}\leq c_{x}c_{y}\varepsilon_{n,p}.

(6.173)

Therefore

\hat{\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}\geq\rho_{1}-2c_{x}c_{y}\varepsilon_{n,p}.

(6.174)

Now expand
\hat{\bm{u}}
and
\hat{\bm{v}}
in the singular-vector bases of

\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}
:

\hat{\bm{u}}=\cos\theta_{u}\,\bm{u}_{1}+\sin\theta_{u}\,\bm{u}_{\perp},\qquad\hat{\bm{v}}=\cos\theta_{v}\,\bm{v}_{1}+\sin\theta_{v}\,\bm{v}_{\perp},

(6.175)

where
\bm{u}_{\perp}\perp\bm{u}_{1}
,
\bm{v}_{\perp}\perp\bm{v}_{1}
,

\left\lVert\bm{u}_{\perp}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\bm{v}_{\perp}\right\rVert_{2}=1
, and

\theta_{u}=\angle(\hat{\bm{u}},\bm{u}_{1})
,

\theta_{v}=\angle(\hat{\bm{v}},\bm{v}_{1})
. Since

\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}=\rho_{1}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}+\sum_{j\geq 2}\rho_{j}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{v}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{K}_{\mathrm{S}}\hat{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{1}\cos\theta_{u}\cos\theta_{v}+\rho_{2}\sin\theta_{u}\sin\theta_{v}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sin^{2}\theta_{u}+\sin^{2}\theta_{v}}{2}\right)+\rho_{2}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta_{u}+\sin^{2}\theta_{v}}{2}

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}-\frac{\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}}{2}\bigl\{\sin^{2}\theta_{u}+\sin^{2}\theta_{v}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}-\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{C}}}{2}\bigl\{\sin^{2}\theta_{u}+\sin^{2}\theta_{v}\bigr\}.

(6.176)

Combining (
6.174
) and
(
6.176
) proves
(
6.103
). The inequality
(
6.104
) follows immediately. Finally, for any unit
vectors
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
with
\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b}\geq 0
,

\left\lVert\bm{a}-\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=2\{1-\bm{a}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b}\}\leq 2\sin^{2}\angle(\bm{a},\bm{b}),

(6.177)

which implies (
6.105
). ∎

N. Proof of Theorem
6.12

The first assertion follows immediately from Davis–Kahan. Indeed, on the event
(
6.89
),

\left\|\sin\Theta\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{K},\mathcal{V}_{K}\bigr)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{\delta^{\tau}_{K}}\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}-\bm{K}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(6.178)

Substituting (
6.89
) into
(
6.178
) yields (
6.90
).

For factor-number consistency, Weyl’s inequality gives

|\hat{\kappa}_{j}-\kappa_{j}|\leq\left\lVert\hat{\bm{K}}_{\tau}-\bm{K}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{P}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)\qquad(1\leq j\leq p).

(6.179)

Hence, on the event

\mathcal{E}_{\tau}=\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq K_{\max}+1}|\hat{\kappa}_{j}-\kappa_{j}|\leq\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}\kappa_{K+1}\right\},

(6.180)

we have

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\kappa}_{K}}{\hat{\kappa}_{K+1}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{K}-\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}\kappa_{K+1}}{\kappa_{K+1}+\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}\kappa_{K+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{K}/\kappa_{K+1}-\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}}{1+\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4(2+\gamma^{\tau}_{0})}}\geq 1+\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{2},

(6.181)

where the last inequality uses (
6.91
). For any
j\neq K
,
there is no larger population ratio gap than the one at
K
, so similarly,

\frac{\hat{\kappa}_{j}}{\hat{\kappa}_{j+1}}\leq 1+\frac{\gamma^{\tau}_{0}}{4}\qquad(j\neq K).

(6.182)

Therefore the maximizer in (
6.93
) is exactly
K
on

\mathcal{E}_{\tau}
. Since (
6.92
) implies

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\tau})\to 1
, we obtain
(
6.94
). ∎

Appendix A
Auxiliary Probability Results

This appendix collects several probability results that are repeatedly used in
high-dimensional inference. The statements are written in forms tailored to the
arguments developed in the main chapters. Throughout this appendix,
C,c>0

denote generic constants whose values may change from line to line.

A.1
Scalar, vector, and matrix Bernstein inequalities

The first group of tools controls partial sums of independent random variables,
random vectors, and random matrices. In later chapters, the scalar version is
used to control individual coordinates, the vector version is used for

\ell_{\infty}
-type errors, and the matrix version is used for operator-norm
bounds of sample scatter and covariance matrices.

Theorem A.1

(Scalar Bernstein inequality)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent real-valued random variables such that

\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=0
and
\left\lvert X_{i}\right\rvert\leq M
almost surely for every
i
. Define

S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},\qquad V_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{2}).

Then, for every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\{S_{n}\geq t\}\leq\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{n}+Mt/3)}\right\},

(A.1)

and consequently,

\mathbb{P}\{\left\lvert S_{n}\right\rvert\geq t\}\leq 2\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{n}+Mt/3)}\right\}.

(A.2)

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in(0,3/M)
. For every real number
x\leq M
, the power-series
expansion of
e^{\theta x}
together with the inequality

\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\frac{(\theta x)^{m}}{m!}\leq\frac{\theta^{2}x^{2}}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\theta M}{3}\Big)^{m}=\frac{\theta^{2}x^{2}}{2(1-\theta M/3)}

implies

e^{\theta x}\leq 1+\theta x+\frac{\theta^{2}x^{2}}{2(1-\theta M/3)}.

(A.3)

Applying (
A.3
) with
x=X_{i}
and using

\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=0
yields

\mathbb{E}e^{\theta X_{i}}\leq 1+\frac{\theta^{2}\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{2})}{2(1-\theta M/3)}\leq\exp\!\left\{\frac{\theta^{2}\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{2})}{2(1-\theta M/3)}\right\}.

Since the
X_{i}
are independent,

\mathbb{E}e^{\theta S_{n}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}e^{\theta X_{i}}\leq\exp\!\left\{\frac{\theta^{2}V_{n}}{2(1-\theta M/3)}\right\}.

(A.4)

By Markov’s inequality and (
A.4
),

\mathbb{P}\{S_{n}\geq t\}=\mathbb{P}\{e^{\theta S_{n}}\geq e^{\theta t}\}\leq e^{-\theta t}\mathbb{E}e^{\theta S_{n}}\leq\exp\!\left\{-\theta t+\frac{\theta^{2}V_{n}}{2(1-\theta M/3)}\right\}.

Choose

\theta=\frac{t}{V_{n}+Mt/3}.

Then
0<\theta<3/M
, and direct substitution gives

-\theta t+\frac{\theta^{2}V_{n}}{2(1-\theta M/3)}=-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{n}+Mt/3)}.

This proves (
A.1
). The two-sided bound follows by
applying (
A.1
) to
S_{n}
and
-S_{n}
.
∎

Corollary A.1

(Vector Bernstein inequality in max norm)
.

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\dots,\bm{X}_{n}
be independent random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with

\mathbb{E}(\bm{X}_{i})=\bm{0}
. Write

\bm{X}_{i}=(X_{i1},\dots,X_{ip})^{\mathsf{T}}
, assume that

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M\qquad\text{almost surely},

and define

V_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(X_{ij}^{2}).

Then, for every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\Big\|_{\infty}\geq t\right\}\leq 2p\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{\infty}+Mt/3)}\right\}.

(A.5)

Proof.

For each coordinate
j
,

S_{n,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}

is a sum of independent centered random variables satisfying

\left\lvert X_{ij}\right\rvert\leq M
almost surely and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(X_{ij}^{2})\leq V_{\infty}
. By
Theorem
A.1
,

\mathbb{P}\{\left\lvert S_{n,j}\right\rvert\geq t\}\leq 2\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{\infty}+Mt/3)}\right\}.

Since

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\Big\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left\lvert S_{n,j}\right\rvert,

a union bound yields

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\Big\|_{\infty}\geq t\right\}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}\{\left\lvert S_{n,j}\right\rvert\geq t\}\leq 2p\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{\infty}+Mt/3)}\right\}.

∎

Corollary A.2

(Entrywise matrix Bernstein inequality)
.

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\dots,\bm{X}_{n}
be independent random matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}

with
\mathbb{E}(\bm{X}_{i})=\bm{O}
. Assume

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}\right\rVert_{\max}\leq M\qquad\text{almost surely},

and define

V_{\max}=\max_{1\leq r\leq p\atop 1\leq s\leq q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{i})_{rs}^{2}\big].

Then, for every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\right\rVert_{\max}\geq t\right\}\leq 2pq\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(V_{\max}+Mt/3)}\right\}.

(A.6)

Proof.

Apply Corollary
A.1
to the vector obtained by stacking
all
pq
entries of each
\bm{X}_{i}
.
∎

Theorem A.2

(Self-adjoint matrix Bernstein inequality)
.

Let
\bm{Y}_{1},\dots,\bm{Y}_{n}
be independent self-adjoint random matrices in

\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i})=\bm{O}
and

\lambda_{\max}(\bm{Y}_{i})\leq L\qquad\text{almost surely for every }i.

Define the matrix variance proxy

v(\bm{Y})=\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}^{2})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(A.7)

Then, for every
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\lambda_{\max}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Y}_{i}\Big)\geq t\right\}\leq p\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(v(\bm{Y})+Lt/3)}\right\}.

(A.8)

If, in addition,
\lambda_{\max}(-\bm{Y}_{i})\leq L
almost surely for every
i
,
then

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Y}_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\geq t\right\}\leq 2p\exp\!\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(v(\bm{Y})+Lt/3)}\right\}.

(A.9)

Proof.

Set
\bm{S}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Y}_{i}
. For every
\theta>0
, the matrix Laplace
transform bound gives

\mathbb{P}\{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{S}_{n})\geq t\}\leq e^{-\theta t}\,\operatorname{tr}\left[\exp\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\mathbb{E}e^{\theta\bm{Y}_{i}}\Big\}\right].

(A.10)

We next bound
\mathbb{E}e^{\theta\bm{Y}_{i}}
. Since
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{Y}_{i})\leq L
,
functional calculus implies

e^{\theta\bm{Y}_{i}}\preceq\bm{I}+\theta\bm{Y}_{i}+\frac{e^{\theta L}-\theta L-1}{L^{2}}\bm{Y}_{i}^{2}.

(A.11)

Taking expectation and using
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i})=\bm{O}
yield

\mathbb{E}e^{\theta\bm{Y}_{i}}\preceq\bm{I}+\frac{e^{\theta L}-\theta L-1}{L^{2}}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}^{2})\preceq\exp\!\left\{\frac{e^{\theta L}-\theta L-1}{L^{2}}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}^{2})\right\}.

Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\mathbb{E}e^{\theta\bm{Y}_{i}}\preceq g(\theta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}^{2}),\qquad g(\theta)=\frac{e^{\theta L}-\theta L-1}{L^{2}}.

Substituting this into (
A.10
) and using

\operatorname{tr}(e^{\bm{A}})\leq p\exp\{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{A})\}
for self-adjoint
\bm{A}
, we
obtain

\mathbb{P}\{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{S}_{n})\geq t\}\leq p\exp\{-\theta t+g(\theta)v(\bm{Y})\}.

For
0<\theta<3/L
,

g(\theta)=\frac{e^{\theta L}-\theta L-1}{L^{2}}\leq\frac{\theta^{2}}{2(1-\theta L/3)}.

Therefore

\mathbb{P}\{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{S}_{n})\geq t\}\leq p\exp\!\left\{-\theta t+\frac{\theta^{2}v(\bm{Y})}{2(1-\theta L/3)}\right\},\qquad 0<\theta<3/L.

Choosing

\theta=\frac{t}{v(\bm{Y})+Lt/3}

produces (
A.8
). Applying the same bound to

-\bm{Y}_{i}
and then using a union bound proves
(
A.9
).
∎

Remark A.1

(A convenient specialization)
.

If the matrices in Theorem
A.2
are uniformly bounded by

L
in operator norm, then for the sample average

\bar{\bm{Y}}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Y}_{i}
we have

\mathbb{P}\!\left\{\left\lVert\bar{\bm{Y}}_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\geq x\right\}\leq 2p\exp\!\left\{-\frac{n^{2}x^{2}}{2(v(\bm{Y})+nLx/3)}\right\}.

This is the form most often used to bound the operator norm of centered sample
scatter or covariance matrices.

A.2
Martingale central limit theorem

A second family of tools enters the proofs of U-statistic decompositions,
Hoeffding projections, quadratic-form expansions, and adaptive combination
statistics.

Definition A.1

(Martingale difference array)
.

Let
\{\mathcal{F}_{n,k}:0\leq k\leq k_{n}\}
be an increasing family of

\sigma
-fields. A triangular array
\{\xi_{n,k}:1\leq k\leq k_{n}\}
is called a
martingale difference array with respect to

\{\mathcal{F}_{n,k}\}
if
\xi_{n,k}
is

\mathcal{F}_{n,k}
-measurable,
\mathbb{E}\left\lvert\xi_{n,k}\right\rvert<\infty
, and

\mathbb{E}(\xi_{n,k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1})=0\qquad\text{a.s. for every }k.

Theorem A.3

(Martingale central limit theorem)
.

Let
\{\xi_{n,k},\mathcal{F}_{n,k}:1\leq k\leq k_{n}\}
be a martingale
difference array, and set

S_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\xi_{n,k},\qquad V_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{n,k}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}).

Assume that:

(i)

V_{n}\to^{P}1
;

(ii)

for every
\varepsilon>0
,

L_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\xi_{n,k}^{2}\mathbbm{1}(\left\lvert\xi_{n,k}\right\rvert>\varepsilon)\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}\right]\to^{P}0.

(A.12)

Then

S_{n}\to^{d}N(0,1).

(A.13)

Remark A.2
.

Theorem
A.3
is the form used most frequently in this
book. In applications,
S_{n}
is usually the sum of martingale increments
obtained after a filtration-based decomposition of a quadratic form,
U-statistic, or leave-one-out expansion. The key tasks are therefore to verify
conditional variance convergence and a Lindeberg condition.

Corollary A.3

(Fourth-moment criterion)
.

Let
\{\xi_{n,k},\mathcal{F}_{n,k}:1\leq k\leq k_{n}\}
be a martingale
difference array. Assume

V_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{n,k}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1})\to^{P}\sigma^{2},\qquad\sigma^{2}>0,

(A.14)

and

\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{n,k}^{4})\to 0.

(A.15)

Then

\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\xi_{n,k}\to^{d}N(0,\sigma^{2}).

(A.16)

Proof.

Set
\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}=\sigma^{-1}\xi_{n,k}
. Then

\{\tilde{\xi}_{n,k},\mathcal{F}_{n,k}\}
is again a martingale difference array,
and

\tilde{V}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1})=\sigma^{-2}V_{n}\to^{P}1.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. The conditional Lindeberg term satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{n}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}^{2}\mathbbm{1}(\left\lvert\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}\right\rvert>\varepsilon)\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-2}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\!\left(\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}^{4}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}\right)\qquad\text{a.s.}

by the inequality
x^{2}\mathbbm{1}(\left\lvert x\right\rvert>\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon^{-2}x^{4}
.
Taking expectations and using (
A.15
),

\mathbb{E}\tilde{L}_{n}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon^{-2}\sigma^{-4}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{n,k}^{4})\to 0.

Hence
\tilde{L}_{n}(\varepsilon)\to^{P}0
by Markov’s inequality. Applying
Theorem
A.3
to
\{\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}\}
yields

\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}\tilde{\xi}_{n,k}\to^{d}N(0,1)
, which is equivalent to
(
A.16
).
∎

A.3
Extreme-value limits and Gaussian tails

Max-type tests in high dimensions are typically calibrated by type-I extreme
value limits. The standard derivations reduce to two ingredients: a sharp tail
approximation for the normal distribution and the identity

\mathbb{P}\Big\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}W_{j}\leq u\Big\}=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}\{W_{j}\leq u\}

under independence.

Lemma A.1

(Mills ratio for the standard normal tail)
.

Let
\Phi
and
\phi
denote the distribution and density functions of the
standard normal law. Then, for every
x>0
,

\frac{1}{x+x^{-1}}\phi(x)\leq 1-\Phi(x)\leq\frac{1}{x}\phi(x).

(A.17)

In particular,

1-\Phi(x)=\frac{\phi(x)}{x}\{1+o(1)\}\qquad\text{as }x\to\infty.

(A.18)

Proof.

Since

1-\Phi(x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}\phi(t)\,dt,

integration by parts gives

1-\Phi(x)=\frac{\phi(x)}{x}-\int_{x}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt.

(A.19)

The upper bound in (
A.17
) follows immediately from
(
A.19
). For the lower bound, note that

t^{-2}\leq x^{-2}
for
t\geq x
, so

\int_{x}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt\leq\frac{1}{x^{2}}\int_{x}^{\infty}\phi(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\{1-\Phi(x)\}.

Substituting this into (
A.19
) yields

1-\Phi(x)\geq\frac{\phi(x)}{x}-\frac{1}{x^{2}}\{1-\Phi(x)\},

which rearranges to

\Big(1+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\Big)\{1-\Phi(x)\}\geq\frac{\phi(x)}{x}.

This is equivalent to the lower bound in (
A.17
). Finally,
combining the upper and lower bounds gives

\frac{x^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\leq\frac{x\{1-\Phi(x)\}}{\phi(x)}\leq 1,

which implies (
A.18
).
∎

Proposition A.1

(Normal-tail calibration for maxima)
.

Define

u_{p}(y)=\big(2\log p-\log\log p+y\big)^{1/2},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.20)

Then, as
p\to\infty
,

p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}e^{-y/2},

(A.21)

and therefore

2p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y/2}.

(A.22)

Proof.

By Lemma
A.1
,

1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))=\frac{\phi(u_{p}(y))}{u_{p}(y)}\{1+o(1)\}.

Now

\phi(u_{p}(y))=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{u_{p}(y)^{2}}{2}\right\}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\!\left\{-\log p+\frac{1}{2}\log\log p-\frac{y}{2}\right\}=\frac{(\log p)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,p}e^{-y/2}.

Moreover,

u_{p}(y)=(2\log p)^{1/2}\{1+o(1)\}.

Combining the last two displays gives

p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}=p\frac{\phi(u_{p}(y))}{u_{p}(y)}\{1+o(1)\}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}e^{-y/2}.

This proves (
A.21
), and
(
A.22
) follows by multiplication by
2
.
∎

Theorem A.4

(Type-I extreme-value limit for Gaussian maxima)
.

Let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p}
be independent
N(0,1)
random variables, and define

M_{p}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Z_{j},\qquad M_{p}^{*}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left\lvert Z_{j}\right\rvert.

With
u_{p}(y)
defined in (
A.20
), the following hold as

p\to\infty
:

\mathbb{P}\{M_{p}\leq u_{p}(y)\}\to\exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}e^{-y/2}\right\},

(A.23)

and

\mathbb{P}\{(M_{p}^{*})^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq y\}\to\exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y/2}\right\}.

(A.24)

Equivalently, if

b_{p}=\sqrt{2\log p}-\frac{\log\log p+\log(4\pi)}{2\sqrt{2\log p}},\qquad a_{p}=\sqrt{2\log p},

(A.25)

then

\mathbb{P}\big\{a_{p}(M_{p}-b_{p})\leq x\big\}\to\exp\{-e^{-x}\},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.26)

Proof.

By independence,

\mathbb{P}\{M_{p}\leq u\}=\Phi(u)^{p}=\big[1-\{1-\Phi(u)\}\big]^{p}.

Take
u=u_{p}(y)
. By Proposition
A.1
,

p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\to\lambda(y):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}e^{-y/2}.

Since
1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\to 0
,

\log\Phi(u_{p}(y))^{p}=p\log\big[1-\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\big]=-p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}+o(1)\to-\lambda(y).

Exponentiating proves (
A.23
).

Next,

\mathbb{P}\{M_{p}^{*}\leq u\}=\{2\Phi(u)-1\}^{p}=\big[1-2\{1-\Phi(u)\}\big]^{p}.

Again taking
u=u_{p}(y)
and using
(
A.22
), we obtain

2p\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y/2}.

Therefore

\log\mathbb{P}\{M_{p}^{*}\leq u_{p}(y)\}=p\log\big[1-2\{1-\Phi(u_{p}(y))\}\big]\to-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y/2},

which proves (
A.24
). The affine-normalization form
(
A.26
) is equivalent to
(
A.23
) after the change of variables

y=2x-\log(4\pi)
and the expansion

u_{p}(2x-\log(4\pi))=b_{p}+x/a_{p}+o(a_{p}^{-1})
.
∎

Corollary A.4

(Extreme-value limit for maxima of squared normals)
.

If
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p}
are independent
N(0,1)
random variables and

Q_{p}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Z_{j}^{2},

then, for every
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}\{Q_{p}-2\log p+\log\log p\leq y\}\to\exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y/2}\right\}.

(A.27)

Proof.

Since
Q_{p}=(M_{p}^{*})^{2}
, the conclusion is immediate from
(
A.24
).
∎

Remark A.3

(Dependent maxima)
.

The independent limits in
Theorem
A.4
and
Corollary
A.4
serve as the basic benchmark for the
max-type statistics studied throughout the book. In the chapter-specific proofs,
independence is often replaced by weak dependence conditions that guarantee the
same type-I extreme-value limit. Those conditions are method-specific and are
therefore developed in the corresponding chapters rather than in this general
appendix.

Bibliographic notes

The scalar Bernstein inequality and its vectorized consequences are classical.
The operator-norm matrix Bernstein inequality stated in
Theorem
A.2
follows the matrix Laplace transform approach
of
126
. The martingale central limit theorem in
Theorem
A.3
is standard; a comprehensive treatment may be
found in
57
. Classical references for extreme-value theory
include
75
and

29
.
